Long time justification of wave turbulence theory
Yu Deng, Zaher Hani

Abstract
. In a series of previous works [ 19 , 20 , 22 ] , we gave a rigorous derivation of the homogeneous wave kinetic equation (WKE) up to small multiples of the kinetic timescale , which corresponds to short time solutions to the wave kinetic equation. In this work, we extend this justification to arbitrarily long times that cover the full lifespan of the WKE. This is the first long-time derivation ever obtained in any large data nonlinear (particle or wave) collisional kinetic limit.



1.
Introduction

The problem of rigorously justifying the laws of kinetic theory and more generally statistical physics, starting from Hamiltonian first principles, was first raised to prominence by Hilbert in his famous list of problems announced at the ICM in 1900.

More than a century has passed since Hilbert announced this problem—now called
Hilbert’s Sixth Problem
—and during this time, a tremendous amount of progress has been achieved towards its end. We shall review this progress more thoroughly in Section
1.2
, but it can be summarized briefly as follows:
In the classical context of particle kinetic theory, the turning point was Lanford’s result
[
54
]
in 1975 which justified Boltzmann’s kinetic theory up to small kinetic times; this result was later completed and developed upon in many works, most notably
[
35
]
. In the context of wave kinetic theory (also known as wave turbulence theory), where colliding particles are replaced by nonlinearly interacting waves, the parallel results were only recently achieved by the authors
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
following several important developments after the turn of the century in linear or equilibrium settings. However, for all this time, one of the biggest challenges in kinetic theory remained open, which is to resolve Hilbert’s sixth problem for a nonlinear system (such as classical Boltzmann or wave turbulence) over arbitrarily long time intervals in the kinetic timescale.

In this paper, we give the
first ever large data long-time justification result
of a kinetic limit for a nonlinear Hamiltonian (particle or wave) collisional system: we derive the homogeneous wave kinetic equation (WKE), from the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation, up to
large
multiples of the kinetic time
T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, that cover the
full lifespan
of the WKE. We also obtain associated results such as propagation of chaos, density evolution, and the hierarchical evolution of higher moments over the same long time range. This extends the results of
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
, which concern only sufficiently small multiples of
T_{\mathrm{kin}}
.

1.1.
Setup and the main result

We start by describing the setup and main result of this paper, before moving to the background and discussions in Section
1.2
. Following
[
22
]
, in dimension
d\geq 3
, consider the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation

\left\{\begin{split}&(i\partial_{t}-\Delta)u+\alpha|u|^{2}u=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d}=[0,L]^{d},\\
&u(0,x)=u_{\mathrm{in}}(x)\end{split}\right.

(NLS)

on the square torus
1
1

1

All results and proofs extend without change to arbitrary rectangular tori as long as
0<\gamma<1
(see below). For
\gamma=1
we need a genericity condition on the torus.

\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d}=[0,L]^{d}
of size
L
. Here
\alpha
is a parameter indicating the strength of the nonlinearity, and

\Delta:=\frac{1}{2\pi}(\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}+\cdots+\partial_{x_{d}}^{2})

is the normalized Laplacian. Let
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}:=(L^{-1}\mathbb{Z})^{d}
be the dual of
\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d}
, and fix the space Fourier transform as

\widehat{u}(t,k)=\frac{1}{L^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}}u(t,x)e^{-2\pi ik\cdot x}\,dx,\qquad u(t,x)=\frac{1}{L^{d/2}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\widehat{u}(t,k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot x}.

(1.1)

Assume the initial data of (
NLS
) is given by

u_{\mathrm{in}}(x)=\frac{1}{L^{d/2}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\widehat{u_{\mathrm{in}}}(k)e^{2\pi ik\cdot x},\quad\widehat{u_{\mathrm{in}}}(k)=\sqrt{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k)}\cdot\mathfrak{g}_{k},

(DAT)

where
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
is a given Schwartz function, and
\{\mathfrak{g}_{k}\}
is a collection of i.i.d. random variables. For concreteness, we will assume each
\mathfrak{g}_{k}
is a standard normalized Gaussian. The results remain true with suitable modifications in non-Gaussian cases, see Remark
1.5
and
[
20
]
.

Define the
kinetic (or Van Hove) time

T_{\mathrm{kin}}:=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{2}}.

In this paper we will study the dynamics of (
NLS
) under the limit
L\to\infty,\alpha\to 0
. We assume that they are related by
\alpha=L^{-\gamma}
, so in particular
T_{\mathrm{kin}}=\frac{1}{2}L^{2\gamma}
, where
\gamma\in(0,1]
is a fixed value called the
scaling law
between
L
and
\alpha
. This is the full range of admissible scaling laws for the problem as we explain in Remark
1.4
below.

1.1.1.
The wave kinetic equation

The wave kinetic equation is given by:

\left\{\begin{split}&\partial_{\tau}\varphi(\tau,k)=\mathcal{K}(\varphi(\tau),\varphi(\tau),\varphi(\tau))(k),\\
&\varphi(0,k)=\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k),\end{split}\right.

(WKE)

where
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}
is as above and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and the nonlinearity
\mathcal{K}
(i.e. cubic collision operator) is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})(k)

\displaystyle=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\big\{\varphi_{1}(k_{1})\varphi_{2}(k_{2})\varphi_{3}(k_{3})-\varphi_{1}(k)\varphi_{2}(k_{2})\varphi_{3}(k_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\varphi_{1}(k_{1})\varphi_{2}(k)\varphi_{3}(k_{3})-\varphi_{1}(k_{1})\varphi_{2}(k_{2})\varphi_{3}(k)\big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\bm{\delta}(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k)\bm{\delta}(|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}.

(COL)

Here and below
\bm{\delta}
denotes the Dirac delta, and we define

|k|^{2}:=\langle k,k\rangle,\quad\langle k,\ell\rangle:=k^{1}\ell^{1}+\cdots+k^{d}\ell^{d},

where
k=(k^{1},\cdots,k^{d})
and
\ell=(\ell^{1},\cdots,\ell^{d})
are
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
or
\mathbb{R}^{d}
vectors.

The local well-posedness of (
WKE
) is proved in
[
36
]
. In general, global well-posedness is not true; there are known examples of finite-time blowup
[
31
,
32
,
9
]
. In fact such blowup, in the form of
\delta
singularity, is expected under mild conditions on initial data, and is connected to the formation of condensates. See Section
1.5.3
for more related discussions.

1.1.2.
The main result

Our main result is as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
d\geq 3
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
(or assume
\gamma=1
but replace
\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d}
by a rectangular torus and assume a genericity condition on its aspect ratios), and fix Schwartz initial data
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}\geq 0
. We also fix
\tau_{*}\in(0,\tau_{\mathrm{max}})
, where
\tau_{\max}
is the maximal time of existence for (
WKE
) (which may be finite or
\infty
), see Proposition
3.2
for the precise definition. Consider the equation (
NLS
) with random initial data (
DAT
), and assume
\alpha=L^{-\gamma}
so that
T_{\mathrm{kin}}=\frac{1}{2}L^{2\gamma}
.

Then, for sufficiently large
L
, (
NLS
) has a smooth solution
u(t,x)
up to time

T=\tau_{*}\cdot\frac{L^{2\gamma}}{2}=\tau_{*}\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},

with probability
\geq 1-e^{-(\log L)^{40d}}
. Moreover we have

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\left|\mathbb{E}\,|\widehat{u}(t,k)|^{2}-\varphi\bigg(\frac{t}{T_{\mathrm{kin}}},k\bigg)\right|\leq L^{-\theta},

(1.2)

where
\theta>0
depends only on
(d,\gamma)
(see the line following (
3.19
) in Section
3.3.2
),
\widehat{u}
is as in (
1.1
), and
\varphi(\tau,k)
is the solution to (
WKE
). In (
1.2
) and below we understand that the expectation
\mathbb{E}
is taken assuming (
NLS
) has a smooth solution on
[0,T]
, which is an event with overwhelming probability.

Remark 1.2
.

The choice of the equation (
NLS
) is not essential, and ideas of the proof work for any semilinear dispersive equation modulo technicalities
2
2

2

For equations with derivative nonlinearity or quasilinear equations (such as water wave), it is physically natural to add vanishing dissipations to the system, or to put ultraviolet cutoffs at very high frequencies that converge to the identity in the kinetic limit. With such considerations, our method of proof also work for these equations.
. The reason we choose to work with (
NLS
) is that it can be viewed as the universal Hamiltonian equation, in the sense that any other Hamiltonian equation reduces to (
NLS
) in a certain limit regime of solutions (see
[
71
]
). Moreover, the kinetic theory for (
NLS
) is closely related to both classical and quantum Boltzmann equations. In fact, the integration domain in the collision kernel in (
COL
) is the same as that in the Boltzmann equation (see Section
1.2
below for further discussions). All this makes (NLS) the perfect choice as the underlying system in our studies.

Remark 1.3
.

A major significance of Theorem
1.1
is that it allows all the rich long-time dynamics of (
WKE
), see Section
1.5.3
, to manifest via (
NLS
). In the case when (
WKE
) has finite time blowup, Theorem
1.1
is clearly optimal (unless we want to extend beyond the blowup time). When (
WKE
) has global solution, we can allow the
\tau_{*}
in Theorem
1.1
to grow with
L
, and an interesting question is to determine the optimal dependence of
\tau_{*}
on
L
. In general this depends on the long-time behavior of the solution to (
WKE
); for uniformly bounded solutions, our proof yields
\tau_{*}\sim\log\log L
. This may be improved to
\tau_{*}\sim\log L/\log\log L
by refining the arguments in the proof (we made no such effort in this paper).

Remark 1.4
.

The role of the scaling law
\gamma
has been vague in early physics literature, but has recently been clarified by the authors in
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
. For the equation (
NLS
) on an arbitrary torus (i.e. without imposing genericity condition on its aspect ratios), the admissible range of scaling laws is
\gamma\in(0,1)
, with the endpoint
\gamma=0
also being admissible in the
discrete
setting (see
[
55
]
) and
\gamma=1
also being admissible if we impose genericity condition on the torus
[
19
]
. This full range is covered in
[
22
]
and the current paper. Two specific scaling laws are of particular importance:
\gamma=1
(
T_{\mathrm{kin}}\sim L^{2}
) which is linked to the notable Gibbs measure invariance problem in dimension
d=3
, and
\gamma=1/2
(
T_{\mathrm{kin}}\sim L
), which corresponds to the Boltzmann-Grad scaling in the particle case, and is a natural scaling law in the inhomogeneous setting. See
[
21
,
22
]
for more discussions.

Remark 1.5
.

As in the earlier papers
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, we can also obtain the accompanying results to Theorem
1.1
pertaining to higher order statistics, using the same arguments in
[
20
]
. These include propagation of chaos (which in fact directly follows from the proof of Theorem
1.1
), probability density evolution in the case of non-Gaussian initial data, and derivation of the wave kinetic hierarchy; see
[
20
]
for more details.

Remark 1.6
.

Unlike in
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, here the Schwartz assumption in Theorem
1.1
is necessary (at least the level of regularity and decay required for
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}
should depend on
\tau_{*}
), because in our inductive argument (Section
3.5
), the norms involved in the bounds get successively weaker with each iteration.

1.2.
Background and history

The work of Boltzmann
[
7
]
in the 1870s marked the formal beginning of
kinetic theory
. In general, it studies the evolution of macroscopic
3
3

3

In some of the literature, a distinction is made between the
mesoscopic
observables studied in kinetic theory (such as the density function), and the macroscopic ones obtained by taking averages of the mescoscopic quantitites (such as fluid velocity).
observables in the kinetic limit, where certain statistical averages of a given microscopic system (e.g.
N
colliding particles) are studied in the limit of infinite system size (
N\to\infty
). In the classical case of colliding particles, the microscopic dynamics is Newtonian and the corresponding macroscopic limit is given by the
Boltzmann equation
.

The parallel theory for waves, under the name of
wave kinetic theory
or
wave turbulence theory
, was initiated in the work of Peierls
[
60
]
in the 1920s. Here particles are replaced by wave modes (or wave packets), collisions are replaced by nonlinear wave interactions such as (
NLS
), and the Boltzmann equation is replaced by the wave kinetic equation. With advances in the last century, the wave kinetic theory has now become a vast subject, which provides systematic treatments of wave interactions and has major scientific applications across various disciplines
[
46
,
47
,
74
,
2
,
73
,
76
,
3
,
16
,
34
,
75
,
65
,
51
]
, see Nazarenko
[
57
]
for a relatively modern survey. In particular, we mention the influential papers of Hasselmann
[
46
,
47
]
and Zakharov
[
74
]
: the former initiates the study of kinetic theory for water waves, leading to highly successful applications to oceanography. The latter one discovers the
Zakharov spectrum
in analogy to the Kolmogorov spectrum in hydrodynamic turbulence, which gives the theory its common name of
wave turbulence
.

Another aspect of wave kinetic theory appears when we replace classical particles in the Boltzmann picture with quantum particles (Bosons or Fermions). This leads to the quantum Boltzmann or
Boltzmann-Nordheim equation
, see the papers of Nordheim
[
58
]
and Uehling-Uhlenbeck
[
72
]
in the 1920–30s. The quantum Boltzmann equation is closely related to both the classical Boltzmann equation and the wave kinetic equation (
WKE
) associated with (
NLS
); in fact its collision operator is an exact linear combination of the other two, and all three collision operators are given by integrations in the same domain. On another hand, one can also consider classical or quantum particle systems where the nonlinear interactions is rescaled by the size of the system, in what is called the
mean field limit
. This leads to Vlasov-type equations in the classical setting, and nonlinear Schrödinger equations in the quantum setting. Such mean-field limits are relatively better understood, and we shall not elaborate on them in our discussion here (cf.
[
68
,
29
,
53
,
64
,
50
,
42
,
40
]
for a sample of results and some review articles).

Mathematically speaking, the main challenge is to rigorously derive the kinetic equations, as well as subsequent diffusion and other asymptotic limits, from the underlying microscopic systems. For classical particles, this was explicitly stated in
Hilbert’s Sixth Problem
back in 1900
[
43
]
. The state of art in this particle setting has been the rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation for
short time
(cf. Lanford
[
54
]
, King
[
52
]
, which have been completed and improved more recently in Gallagher-Saint-Raymond-Texier
[
35
]
) or
small data
(Illner-Pulvirenti
[
48
,
49
]
, Pulvirenti
[
61
]
); see also
[
62
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
for some recent developments. The next step, which would be the justification of Boltzmann equation for arbitrarily long time, has been a major open problem in this subject since the 1975 paper of Lanford
[
54
]

4
4

4

More can be said when the microscopic particle system is replaced by a random collision model as in
[
59
]
.
.

On the wave turbulence side, the mathematical progress started much slower, but is quickly catching up in recent years. Earlier results such as Spohn
[
67
]
and Erdös-Yau
[
30
]
focused on linear problems where the Feynman diagram expansion has a simpler structure. These were then extended beyond the kinetic time scale in the breakthrough of Erdös-Salmhofer-Yau
[
28
]
.

The next major advancement was Lukkarinen-Spohn
[
55
]
. Though only involving equilibrium settings, it was the first one to treat the full nonlinear problem, and inspired many of the subsequent studies. In nonlinear, out-of-equilibrium settings, partial results were obtained by several different groups including the authors
[
8
,
18
,
14
,
15
,
17
]
, which covered successively better time scales but still fell short of the kinetic time
T_{\mathrm{kin}}
.

The state of the art prior to this work has been the authors’ recent papers
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
. These provide the first rigorous derivation of (
WKE
) from (
NLS
) that reaches (a small multiple of)
T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, thus matching the state of the art in the particle case
[
35
]
. We also mention a few results concerning the inhomogeneous setting and stochastic variants etc., see
[
33
,
25
,
70
,
41
,
45
,
26
,
1
,
56
]
, and concerning the solution theory to (
WKE
), see
[
31
,
32
,
9
,
36
,
66
,
13
]
. Finally, the derivation of the Boltzmann-Nordheim equation (see
[
27
]
for precise formulation) is still completely open, even for short time (for recent progress, cf.
[
12
,
10
,
11
]
and references therein).

We conclude that, in all the
large data, nonlinear
settings discussed above, the results have been limited to short time, even for the equilibrium result in
[
55
]
. In this context, the current paper provides the first rigorous derivation of any collisional nonlinear kinetic equation (particle or wave) that goes beyond the small data or short time perturbative setting.

1.3.
The difficulty

As discussed in Section
1.2
, in both particle and wave settings, the justification of the kinetic approximation has been limited to short time, and extending this to arbitrarily long time has been a major unsolved problem. The only exception is Erdös-Salmhofer-Yau
[
28
]
(extending Erdös-Yau
[
30
]
), which concerns a
linear
equation.

The fundamental difficulty involved in these long-time problems is the
divergence of Taylor series
, which we explain below. In general, there are two approaches to the short-time justification of the kinetic approximation: the
hierarchical approach

[
54
,
52
,
35
,
28
,
55
]
, which relies on the infinite hierarchy system satisfied by the
n
-th order statistics (i.e.
n
-th moments or
n
-particle distributions),
and the
dynamical approach

[
8
,
18
,
14
,
15
,
19
,
20
,
22
]
, which relies on approximating the dynamics of the microscopic system.

In the dynamical approach, the solution to the microscopic system (such as
NLS
)) is approximated by high order Duhamel expansions; this is essentially a Taylor series in time, which will have to converge if we want this approach to be valid. In the hierarchical approach, a key challenge is to obtain exponential a priori bounds for high order target quantities (i.e.
n
-th order statistics for very large
n
), which also requires the absolute convergence of a certain Taylor-type series expansion. Thus in either case,
as long as the approach is based on the structure of time
0
data, the result cannot go beyond the radius of convergence of the Taylor series at time
0
.

For
linear
equations such as
[
28
]
this is not a problem, because under suitable assumptions the above radius of convergence is
always

\infty
. However, even for the simplest nonlinear equation, it is very likely that a regular solution exists on a longer time interval, but the radius of convergence of the Taylor expansion at time
0
is much smaller. In such cases, it is not possible to go beyond this radius of convergence, by simply setting up an argument “based on time
0
”. On the other hand, if we want to exploit any “base time”
t_{*}
greater than 0, then we are faced with the difficulty that the data at time
t_{*}
does not have independent states or modes, and the behavior of the target quantities at time
t_{*}
relies on the full nonlinear dynamics on
[0,t_{*}]
. This difficulty is intimately linked to the emergence of
time irreversibility
, which we will discuss in Sections
1.4
and
2.4
below.

1.4.
The strategy

Our main theorem, Theorem
1.1
, resolves the difficulty in Section
1.3
and completes the long-time justification of the kinetic limit in the wave turbulence setting. This relies on the following strategy, which is summarized here, and expanded upon in Section
2
below.

Idea 1: Shifting base times.
We follow the dynamical approach. As discussed in Section
1.3
, to go beyond the radius of convergence, we need to shift the base time, and perform expansions at later times
t>0
. In practice, for fixed
\tau_{*}
as in Theorem
1.1
, we may choose
\delta
small enough depending on
\tau_{*}
, such that
\tau_{*}=\mathfrak{D}\delta
with
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the solution
\varphi(\tau,k)
has a convergent Taylor expansion on
[p\delta,(p+1)\delta]
with base point
p\delta
, for each
0\leq p<\mathfrak{D}
. This formally allows us to expand
u(\tau\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
as a Taylor series of
u(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
for
\tau\in[p\delta,(p+1)\delta]
, so
if
u(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
were to have independent Fourier coefficients as
u(0)=u_{\mathrm{in}}
does in (
DAT
)
, then repeating the same arguments as in
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
would immediately yield (
1.2
) for
t\in[p\delta,(p+1)\delta]
. Of course, such independence is not true due to the nonlinear evolution (
NLS
) on
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
, so the first central component of our proof is to
examine the sense in which the Fourier coefficients of
u(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
can be viewed as nearly independent.
This near independence condition is to be proved
inductively in
p
, which means it will get successively weaker and involve successively lower order quantities as
p
increases; at each fixed
p
it has to be strong enough to imply the validity of kinetic approximation, but also has to be verified by the actual time evolution of (
NLS
). More importantly, this condition has to recognize the irreversibility of the kinetic equation, which means that, at each
p
it has to indicate a
unique preferred direction
of time in which the derivation can proceed. This emergence of time irreversibility is a subtle point on which we shall elaborate below and in Section
2.4
.

Idea 2: Quantifying “near independence”.
Now the question becomes how to measure the dependence of Fourier coefficients of
u(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
. One possibility, suggested by the proof of propagation of chaos in
[
20
]
, is to express this in terms of mixed moments, or more precisely
cumulants
, which quantify how far the behavior of the mixed higher order moments is from independence and Gaussianity.

Recall the definition of cumulants
\mathbb{K}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{r})
(see
[
55
]
, or Definition
3.1
below). Now consider

\mathbb{K}\big(\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{1})^{\zeta_{1}}\,,\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{2})^{\zeta_{2}}\,,\cdots,\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{r})^{\zeta_{r}}\big),

(1.3)

where
k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}
(indicating possible conjugates, see Section
3.3.3
below). By gauge and space translation invariance, it is easy to see that (
1.3
) vanishes unless
\sum_{j}\zeta_{j}=\sum_{j}\zeta_{j}k_{j}=0
(so
r
must be even); moreover (
1.3
) equals
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}\approx\varphi(p\delta,k)
if
r=2
and
k_{1}=k_{2}=k
. As such, we know that the independence (and Gaussianity) of the Fourier coefficients of
u(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
exactly corresponds to the vanishing of the cumulants (
1.3
) for
r\geq 4
.

Now, for near independence, it is natural to think that such vanishing should be replaced by suitable smallness or decay conditions for (
1.3
). Indeed, it follows from the proof in
[
20
]
that

|(\ref{introcm})|\leq L^{-cr},\quad\forall r\geq 4

(1.4)

for some small value
c>0
. Unfortunately, this is too weak as we shall see in an example below. On the other hand, in the Gibbs measure case
[
55
]
, (
1.4
) is replaced by the much stronger
physical space

L^{1}
bound for cumulants; however, this is too strong and a calculation shows that such bounds cannot hold away from equilibrium (see Appendix
B
).

In fact, there is a deep reason why the bound (
1.4
) or the one in
[
55
]
, or
any norm upper bound
for that matter, will
not be enough to run a successful inductive argument
following Idea 1 above. This is because such a norm upper bound does not indicate an intrinsic direction of time that would allow the derivation to proceed
only
in that direction but not in the other.

More precisely, if such a norm bound for cumulants at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
was sufficient to derive (
WKE
) on the time interval
[p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
, then since (
NLS
) is time-reversible, the same argument would also give a derivation of the
time-reversed
version of (
WKE
) on the time interval
[(p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
. But this would contradict the fact that we actually have the
original
(
WKE
) on the time interval
[(p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
. Such a “paradox” is well-known in the kinetic literature, and presents a major challenge to any long-time derivation attempt for kinetic equations following Idea 1 above.

In the context of (
NLS
) and (
WKE
), this difficulty can be demonstrated by a concrete example, whose details we leave to Section
2.1
as it requires some preliminaries. Basically, for
p=1
, we can find a term
\mathbb{K}_{0}
in the Taylor series expansion of (
1.3
) with
r=4
, such that when we expand
\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
into a Taylor series consisting of multilinear expressions of
\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
, the contribution of
\mathbb{K}_{0}
to
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}
is a term that
does not appear in
the leading term approximation
\varphi(2\delta,k)
. However, this contribution has the same order of magnitude as this leading term! This anomaly, presented by
\mathbb{K}_{0}
, still vanishes in the limit, but only because of the cancellations coming from the
exact structure
of
\mathbb{K}_{0}
, namely its own Taylor series expansion on the interval
[0,\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
.

In other words, such cancellation relies crucially on the fact that
\mathbb{K}_{0}
comes from a forward-in-time expansion of the (
NLS
) flow. If we choose to run the
backward
evolution of (
NLS
) from time
\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
to time
0
(instead of from time
\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
to time
2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
), this cancellation will fail and we will get a different effective equation due to the contribution of
\mathbb{K}_{0}
. This is explained in more detail in Sections
2.1
and
2.4
.

Idea 3: Setting up the ansatz.
We now need to set up the precise ansatz for the cumulants (
1.3
) at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, which involves not only upper bounds but also exact structures. This ansatz will contain
a separate expansion
for each term (
1.3
) up to some high order, plus a remainder that is sufficiently small. As explained in Idea 2 above, such expansion at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
also needs to distinguish between going forward to time
(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
and going backward to time
(p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
. This is achieved by having this expansion
memorize the whole history of interactions by (
NLS
) on the time interval
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
. More precisely, the expansion will be a suitable iteration of short-time Taylor expansions on time intervals
[q,\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},(q+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
for
0\leq q<p
.

Schematically, we may choose
R_{p}\gg R_{p+1}
, and consider the ansatz

\mathbb{K}\big(\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{1})^{\zeta_{1}}\,,\cdots,\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{r})^{\zeta_{r}}\big)=\sum(\mathrm{terms\ at\ step\ }p)+(\mathrm{small\ remainder})

(1.5)

for all
r\leq R_{p}
; the inductive argument amounts to proving that if (
1.5
) is true for
p
, then it is true for
p+1
. Of course, this is based on the fact that
\widehat{u}((p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
can be expanded into Taylor series consisting of multilinear expressions of
\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
. As in all our previous works, this Taylor series is organized into ternary trees; after using the technical Lemma
A.1
, we then obtain a “pre-Feynman diagram” expansion of the cumulants (
1.3
) for
p+1
in terms of those for
p
, which is organized into multiple ternary trees.

The exact ansatz (
1.5
) is then determined by inductively iterating the above pre-expansion. By looking at the first few
p
’s, we may guess that (
1.5
) can be organized into
gardens
(i.e. multiple trees with their leaves paired, see
[
20
]
or Definition
3.4
below). These gardens
\mathcal{G}
at level
p+1
(which we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{p+1}
) can be constructed by induction as follows. Consider the multiple ternary trees occurring in the pre-expansion above; upon using Lemma
A.1
, their leaves are divided into two-element pairs (corresponding to the two-point cumulant
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})|^{2}
) and four-or-more-element subsets (corresponding to higher order cumulants (
1.3
) for
p
with
r\geq 4
). We then construct the garden
\mathcal{G}_{p+1}
by keeping all the leaf pairs, and replacing each four-or-more-element subset by a garden of form
\mathcal{G}_{p}
.

This strategy can be described as a “partial time series expansion”, i.e. we stop the time series expansion at time
p\delta
if we encounter a two-point cumulant, and continue with the expansion if we encounter higher order cumulants. We replace the two-point cumulant
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})|^{2}
by its approximation
\varphi(p\delta,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, and replace the higher order cumulants by the induction hypothesis in (
1.5
). This partial time series expansion has critical importance in our proof, as it allows us to make use of the uniform bound of
\varphi

outside the radius of convergence at
t=0
, and avoid falling into the trap of divergent Taylor series.

Eventually, these iteration steps lead to
completely new combinatorial structures, which we call layered gardens (Definition
3.6
) and canonical layered gardens (Definition
3.7
)
. We need to work out all the properties of these objects from scratch, including the combinatorial ones (Propositions
4.8
–
4.10
,
7.19
,
8.2
–
8.3
) and the analytic ones (Propositions
5.1
,
6.1
–
6.3
,
9.5
,
10.4
–
10.5
). These are the most central parts of this paper.

Finally, once the ansatz for the cumulants is proved inductively for all
p
, we can conclude that the main contribution to
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}
comes from the order
r=2
cumulants in (
1.3
) from the previous time step
(p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, which are themselves approximated by the solution
\varphi((p-1)\delta,k)
of the (
WKE
). Following the arguments in
[
19
,
22
]
, the resulting contribution of these two-point correlations can be shown to sum up to the power series expansion of
\varphi(p\delta,k)
in terms of
\varphi((p-1)\delta,k)
, thus establishing the approximation of
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}
with
\varphi(p\delta,k)
by induction.

For a detailed discussion of the example mentioned in Idea 2 above and emergence of the arrow of time, the motivation of the ansatz, the main components of the proof, and the relation to the previous works
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, see Section
2
below.

1.5.
Future horizons

Theorem
1.1
and its proof opens a door to many problems that were previously out of reach. In this section we only mention the three most important avenues that should be now open for investigation.

1.5.1.
Extension of Lanford’s theorem

With the analogy drawn between particle and wave kinetic theory, it is natural to expect that the particle counterpart of Theorem
1.1
, i.e. the long-time extension of Lanford’s theorem
[
54
,
52
,
35
]
, would also be true. The authors are investigating this in an upcoming paper
[
23
]
jointly with Xiao Ma.

1.5.2.
Fluctuations and large deviations

The cumulant estimates in this paper should be sufficient to obtain the fluctuation and large deviation dynamics around the wave kinetic limit given by (
WKE
), over the same long time interval as in Theorem
1.1
. Such fluctuation and large deviation dynamics were described from a physics point of view in
[
38
]
. The parallel results in the particle setting were proved recently in
[
4
]
for short times.

1.5.3.
Diffusion and other asymptotic limits

Once the kinetic approximation is justified for arbitrarily long time, the next step in the Hilbert’s Sixth Problem, in both particle and wave settings, is then to reach the time scale for diffusion and other asymptotic limits.

More precisely, in the case of Boltzmann equation, it is known that certain rescaled limits of solutions converge to solutions to fluid equations, such as the Euler and Navier-Stokes equations, see
[
39
,
63
,
37
]
and references therein. Deriving those fluid equations directly from Newtonian dynamics would then require extending Lanford’s theorem to the adequate time scale of this limit.

In the case of
inhomogeneous
wave kinetic equation or Boltzmann-Nordheim equation
5
5

5

We remark that the derivation of these inhomogeneous equations has not been complete, at least in the setting without noise (see
[
1
,
45
,
44
]
for some related progress); however we expect this to follow from similar arguments as in
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
and the current paper, modulo technical differences.
, i.e. when
\varphi=\varphi(\tau,x,k)
in (
WKE
) and
\partial_{\tau}\varphi
is replaced by
(\partial_{\tau}+k\cdot\nabla_{x})\varphi
, it is not clear whether a diffusion limit similar to Boltzmann exists. What is the possible asymptotic behavior of the kinetic equation, and what this may imply about the behavior of (
NLS
)
beyond the kinetic time scale
, is a major open avenue of investigation in this subject.

Finally, for the
homogeneous
wave kinetic equation or the Boltzmann-Nordheim equation, there are known finite time blowups where
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}<\infty
with formation of
\delta
singularity (see
[
31
,
32
,
9
]
). In fact this is expected to be fairly general, and corresponds to the formation of condensates. It may be possible to continue (
WKE
) weakly after the blowup time, but the sense in which the approximation (
1.2
) holds probably needs to be modified. How to make this rigorous is another outstanding open problem.

There also exist (formal) global power-law solutions to (
WKE
) known as the Kolmogorov-Zakharov cascade spectra, which were recently investigated in
[
13
]
. These solutions are constant-flux cascade solutions for (
WKE
), and their rigorous understanding is yet another fascinating question. Once this cascade behavior is understood at the level of (
WKE
), Theorem
1.1
will immediately allow to prove energy cascade at the level of (
NLS
), i.e. rigorously establish the generic cascade phenomenon for nonlinear Schrödinger equations. This links to the extensively studied, yet still open, question of growth of Sobolev norms for (
NLS
) and similar nonlinear dispersive equations.

2.
Description of main ideas

In this section we detail out some of the key ideas leading to the main ansatz (Proposition
3.9
), which was briefly stated in Section
1.4
. Throughout this section we will use the (now standard) concepts of trees, couples, gardens and decorations etc., see Figure
1
; the reader may consult the authors’ earlier papers
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
for the definitions of these terms, or see Definitions
3.3
–
3.5
below.

Figure 1.
From left to right: a tree, a couple and a garden of
4
trees, with corresponding decorations (leaves of same color indicate pairings). See Definitions
3.3
–
3.5
below or
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
for precise definitions.

2.1.
An example

We start by discussing the example mentioned in Section
1.4
, Idea 2. Recall that the Fourier coefficients
\widehat{u}(0,k)
are independent for different
k
, but
\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)
are no longer independent due to the nonlinear evolution. The first deviation from independence is characterized by the order
4
cumulant (all odd ordered cumulants vanish as shown in Section
1.4
), say

\mathbb{K}\big(\overline{\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{1})},\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{2}),\overline{\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{3})},\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{4})\big).

(2.1)

Recall that
\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
can be expanded into a Taylor series consisting of multilinear expressions of
\widehat{u}(0)
, where these terms are organized using ternary trees (see Definition
3.3
, and (
3.33
) and (
3.35
) below). If all four factors in (
2.1
) are replaced by the
linear
evolution, then the independence at time
0
will carry over and the resulting cumulant will be
0
; thus the first nonzero contribution to (
2.1
) is given by the case when three of the four factors are replaced by the linear evolution, and the remaining one is replaced by the cubic term (i.e. the first iterate, which corresponds to the ternary tree with only one branching node).

We know the tree expansion is calculated by iterating the nonlinearity in (
NLS
), for example

\widehat{u}(t,k_{1})\sim e^{2\pi i|k_{1}|^{2}t}\bigg[\widehat{u}(0,k_{1})+iL^{-d}\alpha\sum_{k_{2}-k_{3}+k_{4}=k_{1}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{2\pi i\Omega s}\,\mathrm{d}s\cdot\widehat{u}(0,k_{2})\overline{\widehat{u}(0,k_{3})}\widehat{u}(0,k_{4})\bigg]

(2.2)

(we only show the first two terms in the expansion), where
\Omega:=|k_{2}|^{2}-|k_{3}|^{2}+|k_{4}|^{2}-|k_{1}|^{2}
. Now, if we replace the first factor in (
2.1
) by the cubic term, and apply Lemma
A.1
, we would obtain (part of) the first nonzero contribution to (
2.1
), which has the form

\mathbb{K}_{0}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}):=e^{2\pi i\Omega\cdot\delta T_{\mathrm{kin}}}\cdot L^{-d-\gamma}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k_{4}=0}\cdot\prod_{j=2}^{4}\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{j})\cdot\int_{0}^{\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}}e^{-2\pi i\Omega s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(2.3)

Note that this term (as well as the other terms in the full cumulant expansion (
2.1
)) is bounded pointwise by
L^{-(d-\gamma)}
(using that
T_{\mathrm{kin}}=L^{2\gamma}
); however, knowing
only
this upper bound will not be sufficient, which is what we will demonstrate through this example.

To see this, consider the expansion similar to (
2.2
) but with starting time
\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
rather than 0. Now we calculate the quantity
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}
, in which we replace one of the two factors by the linear evolution of
\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
, and the other factor by the term that is cubic in
\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
. This then leads to the contribution

\mathbb{Y}:=L^{-d-\gamma}\sum_{k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}=k}e^{-2\pi i\Omega\cdot\delta T_{\mathrm{kin}}}\cdot\int_{\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}}^{2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}}e^{2\pi i\Omega t}\,\mathrm{d}t\cdot\mathbb{K}_{0}(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

(2.4)

where
\Omega
is defined for
(k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
. In this sum we may restrict to the region
|\Omega|\leq L^{-2\gamma}
(in other words
|t\Omega|\leq 1
), so the
\mathrm{d}t
integral has saturated size
\sim T_{\mathrm{kin}}
; a dimension counting argument then yields that the number of choices of
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
satisfying
k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}=k
and
|\Omega|\leq L^{-2\gamma}
for fixed
k
is at most
L^{2(d-\gamma)}
(see Lemma
A.9
). Now, if we only have the upper bound
L^{-(d-\gamma)}
for
\mathbb{K}_{0}
, then restricting the sum in (
2.4
) to the region
|\Omega|\leq L^{-2\gamma}
yields a sum whose upper bound is
L^{-d-\gamma}\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}\cdot L^{2(d-\gamma)}\cdot L^{-(d-\gamma)}\sim 1
, which has
the same size
as the leading term; however it is clearly
not
a part of the leading term approximation to
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}
.

The conclusion is that, it is impossible to exclude the “fake leading term”
\mathbb{Y}
and obtain the correct asymptotics, if we only use the upper bound for
\mathbb{K}_{0}
defined in (
2.3
). To eliminate
\mathbb{Y}
in the limit, we need to use the exact structure (
2.3
), which (after a time rescaling) leads to the expression

\mathbb{Y}=L^{-2(d-\gamma)}\sum_{k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}=k}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{j})\cdot\int_{0<s<\delta<t<2\delta}e^{\pi i\Omega\cdot L^{2\gamma}(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s\to 0\,\,\,(L\to\infty).

(2.5)

Now
\mathbb{Y}
disappears in the limit, because for fixed
(t,s)
, the asymptotics of the summation in
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
contains a factor
\bm{\delta}(t-s)
, whose integral in
(t,s)
then vanishes due to the restriction
s<\delta<t
. In other words, the contribution of
\mathbb{K}_{0}
vanishes in the limit only if we take into account its own structure (
2.3
), which memorizes the interaction history on the time interval
[0,\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
.

The above cancellation leading to the vanishing of
\mathbb{Y}
is due to the fact that the intervals
[0,\delta]
and
[\delta,2\delta]
have no common interior; this draws interesting analogy with the properties of Markov processes (for example, the Brownian motion whose increments on disjoint intervals are independent and have vanishing correlation). In fact, what the above analysis demonstrates is exactly a kind of
Markovian property
; namely, to the first order, the two-point correlations at time
2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
depend only on the two-point correlations at time
\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, but not on the nonlinear evolution history on
[0,\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
which is recorded by the higher order cumulants
\mathbb{K}_{0}
. Nevertheless, such Markovian property holds only if we go forward in time, and is clearly false if we go backward in time and replace
t\in[\delta,2\delta]
by
t\in[0,\delta]
in (
2.5
), see Section
2.4
.

2.2.
Building up gardens: A naive (but incorrect) attempt

Recall the notations introduced in Section
1.4
and our choice of
\delta
so that the interval
[0,\tau_{*}]
is split into
\mathfrak{D}
subintervals of length
\delta
. As explained in Section
1.4
, a central step in estimating
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}
, and hence in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, is to analyze and control the exact structure of the cumulants of form (
1.3
) (these cumulants are related to the corresponding moments as in Definition
3.1
below).

As explained in Section
1.4
, Idea 2 and Idea 3, such structure should also memorize the full interaction history of (
NLS
) on the time interval
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
. The example in Section
2.1
already illustrates a step in the construction of such history, namely calculating moments and cumulants at time
(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, using their Taylor expansions at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
or earlier. The same example also shows that the information carried by such cumulant expansion is crucial to showing that their contribution vanishes in the limit. Generalizing this time iterative step then naturally leads to the construction of
layered gardens and couples
(i.e. each node is in a unique time layer indicated by nonnegative integers), as briefed in Section
1.4
, Idea 3. To motivate, in this section we will first describe a naive but incorrect attempt, and leave the real construction, which is only one step away, to Section
2.3
.

Recall the
R_{p}
described in Section
1.4
; let
N_{p}
be such that
N_{p}\gg R_{p}\gg N_{p+1}
. For each
p<\mathfrak{D}
, we can always perform an order
N_{p+1}
Duhamel expansion of the solution at time
(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, which expresses it as a sum of iterates of the time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
data plus a remainder term. The iterates are multilinear expressions in the solution at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
and can be arranged as sums of ternary trees whose order (i.e. number of branching nodes) equal the order of the iteration (cf. equations (
3.36
), (
3.33
), and Definitions
3.3
and
3.5
). Note that for
p=0
, all the cumulants

\mathbb{K}\big(\widehat{u}(0,k_{1})^{\zeta_{1}}\,,\widehat{u}(0,k_{2})^{\zeta_{2}}\,,\cdots,\widehat{u}(0,k_{r})^{\zeta_{r}}\big)

at time
0
and of order
r\geq 4
should vanish due to exact independence and Gaussianity in (
DAT
).

For
p=1
, by performing the above expansion, and applying Lemma
A.1
, we can write the main (non-remainder) contribution to the cumulant

\mathbb{K}\big(\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{1})^{\zeta_{1}}\,,\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{2})^{\zeta_{2}}\,,\cdots,\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{r})^{\zeta_{r}}\big)

(2.6)

as a sum over
irreducible gardens

\mathcal{G}
of width
r
. Here an irreducible garden is a garden of width
r
(i.e. a collection of
r
trees whose leaves are completely paired with each other, see Definition
3.4
below), such that no proper subset of these
r
trees have their leaves completely paired. Note that this irreducibility property precisely corresponds to the definition of the cumulant
\mathbb{K}
in (
2.6
); instead, the corresponding moment in (
2.6
) would consist of the sum over all (reducible and irreducible) gardens. This gives the explicit structure of the cumulants at time
\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
as a sum over irreducible gardens
\mathcal{G}
, which we denote by
6
6

6

We use the notion
\mathscr{N}
because the construction here is
not
the correct ansatz, but a naive attempt; see Section
2.3
for more discussions.

\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{N}_{1}
. We shall put all the nodes in this garden
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{N}_{1}
into layer
0
; then each term occurring in this summation is of form
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
which consists of a summation over all decorations
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
and an integration over time variables
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})
defined by the layering (which are all
0
for
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{N}_{1}
). See (
3.24
) in Definition
3.8
below for the exact expression
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
; note that all input functions
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
should be replaced by
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})=\varphi(0,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
.

Now let us move to
p=2
. Again we express the solution
\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)
as a Duhamel expansion, but now
with initial time

\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
. Up to a remainder term that we ignore for now, this writes
\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)
as a sum of terms associated with ternary trees whose leaves are associated with
\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})
. As such, this allows to express the cumulant

\mathbb{K}\big(\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{1})^{\zeta_{1}},\cdots,\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{r})^{\zeta_{r}}\big)=\sum\mathbb{K}\big((\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}})_{k_{1}},\cdots,(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}_{r}})_{k_{r}}\big)

(2.7)

where the sum is taken over all trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}
of sign
\zeta_{j}
and order at most
N_{2}
, and each
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}_{j}}
is the Duhamel iterate associated with the interaction history described by
\mathcal{T}_{j}
, whose exact expression is as in (
3.33
) below. Then, the cumulants in the sum in (
2.7
) can be written as linear combinations of

\mathbb{K}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})^{\sigma_{\mathfrak{l}}},\cdots,\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{r}}\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})^{\sigma_{\mathfrak{l}}}\bigg),

(2.8)

where
\mathcal{L}_{j}
denotes the set of leaf nodes of the tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
and
\sigma_{\mathfrak{l}}\in\{\pm\}
is the sign of each leaf
\mathfrak{l}
. By applying Lemma
A.1
again, we an express (
2.8
) as a sum of form

\sum_{\mathcal{P}}\prod_{B\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{K}\big((\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})^{\sigma_{\mathfrak{l}}})_{\mathfrak{l}\in B}\big).

(2.9)

Here the summation in (
2.9
) is taken over all irreducible partitions
\mathcal{P}
of the set
\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\cdots\mathcal{L}_{r}
(i.e. no nontrivial union of sets in
\mathcal{P}
equals any nontrivial union of sets
\mathcal{L}_{j}
), and the cumulant for each
B
involves the random variables
\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})^{\sigma_{\mathfrak{l}}}
for
\mathfrak{l}\in B
.

Now, we can use the formula deduced above for the cumulants (
2.6
) at time
\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, namely the expansion into terms associated with
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{N}_{1}
, to replace each individual term in (
2.9
), and subsequently obtain an expression for (
2.7
). Combinatorially, this expression is associated with a new, bigger garden
\mathcal{G}_{2}
, which is obtained as follows: start from the collection of trees
(\mathcal{T}_{j}:1\leq j\leq r)
, then for each set
B\in\mathcal{P}
of leaves, we attach a garden
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(B)\in\mathscr{N}_{1}
at these leaves, so that these leaves become the roots of the trees in this garden. Moreover, we put all
branching nodes
of the trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}
into layer
1
, and keep all nodes of each
\mathcal{G}(B)
in layer
0
, to make
\mathcal{G}_{2}
a layered garden, and denote all such layered gardens by
\mathscr{N}_{2}
. Analytically, by the definitions of
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
(see (
3.33
) and (
3.24
) below), it is easy to see that the expression associated with
\mathcal{G}_{2}
is precisely
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}}
in (
3.24
) below, where again the time integration is defined by the layering, and all inputs
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
should be replaced by
\varphi(0,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
.

A simple instance of the above construction is already contained in the example described in Section
2.1
, where we have two trees
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})
with one being trivial (only one leaf) and the other having exactly one branch and three leaves, and attach the garden in the right of Figure
1
, to obtain the couple in the middle of Figure
1
with suitable layering; note that the garden we attach has
width
4
. Another instance of this construction is given in Figure
2
, where we have two trees
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})
each with two branches, and each of the five gardens in
\mathscr{N}_{1}
we attach has
width
2
(i.e. is a couple) with three being trivial and two being nontrivial.

Figure 2.
An example of a layered couple as described in Section
2.2
which appears in the expansion of
\widehat{u}(2\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}})
(the numbers [0] or [1] denote the layer of each node). This is a regular couple (see Definition
4.1
below) but is
not
canonical (see Section
2.3
). In the modified construction in Section
2.3
, this will be replaced by the canonical regular couple in Figure
3
.

At this point, it is now clear how to continue this process inductively and construct
\mathscr{N}_{p+1}
from
\mathscr{N}_{p}
etc., which ultimately gives (up to acceptable remainder terms) an exact
structural
description of the cumulants at any time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, as a sum of terms of form
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
over all irreducible layered gardens
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{N}_{p}
. This clearly accounts for the example in Section
2.1
; however this naive attempt still does not give the correct ansatz, due to one fatal weakness. We discuss this weakness, as well as the one simple step to fix it, in Section
2.3
below.

2.3.
Canonical layering and the true ansatz

An indication why the construction in Section
2.2
does not work is that, by this construction, all leaf nodes
must
be in layer
0
(in other words, all the inputs in
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
in (
3.24
) must be replaced by the initial data
\varphi(0,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
), which is exactly what we want to avoid in Section
1.3
(i.e. base all the analysis on time
0
data).

To see an instance of this major issue, we take a closer look at the layered couple
\mathcal{Q}
in Figure
2
. Note that it is a
regular couple
(see Definition
4.1
below), and does not incorporate the cancellation shown in the example in Section
2.1
(i.e. it is
coherent
in the terminology of Definition
4.7
below); thus there is no gain of powers of
L
in the associated
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
expression. Moreover, if the layer
1
nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
are replaced by layer
p
, then the roots of the two sub-couples with dashed lines can have
arbitrary
layer between
0
and
p
while still being consistent with the construction in Section
2.2
. Therefore, the
number of layerings
of a given regular couple with order
2n
can be as large as
\mathfrak{D}^{n}
. Since the
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
quantity for each individual regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
gains only a factor of
(C_{1}\delta)^{n}
(where
C_{1}
is a large constant depending on initial data
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}
, in the same way as in
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
), this will lead to serious divergence issues as
(C_{1}\mathfrak{D}\delta)^{n}=(C_{1}\tau_{*})^{n}
grows in
n
unless
\tau_{*}
is sufficiently small.

Of course, the above weakness comes from the fact that we are trying to perform the Duhamel expansion all the way to time
0

in every case
. Indeed, whenever we encounter the
two-point correlation

\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})|^{2}
in the expression, we should simply replace it by its (expected) approximation
\varphi(p\delta,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, instead of further Duhamel-expanding it by earlier time data and going all the way to time
0
. In this way we can exploit the fact that
\varphi(p\delta,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
is uniformly bounded in
p
, despite that it cannot be expanded as a power series involving only
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}=\varphi(0,\cdot)
.

Now, recall the reduction and construction process described in Section
2.2
. By Lemma
A.1
, it is clear that, we would encounter the two-point correlation
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})|^{2}

only when
one of the sets
B\in\mathcal{P}
as in Section
2.2
has exactly cardinality
2
. This leads to the following minor but crucial modification to the construction process in Section
2.2
, which is consistent with the brief description in Section
1.4
, Idea 3: namely, in reducing (
2.9
) (and same with
\delta
replaced by arbitrary
p\delta
), we further expand the cumulant
\mathbb{K}\big(((\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}}))^{\sigma_{\mathfrak{l}}})_{\mathfrak{l}\in B}\big)
using earlier time ansatz
only when

|B|\geq 4
. When
|B|=2
, we simply replace the cumulant, which is now just
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})|^{2}
, by
\varphi(\delta,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
plus a decaying sub-leading term. Note that this minor modification addresses both issued mentioned above: now we are stopping expansion at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
whenever we encounter a two-point correlation at this time, and a combinatorial argument allows us to control the number of coherent regular couples (see Section
2.5
below).

Combinatorially, the new construction leads to a new class of layered gardens (and couples)
\mathcal{G}
, which we refer to as
canonical
layered gardens or
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p}
, as follows: for
p=1
,
\mathscr{G}_{1}
still denotes the set of all irreducible gardens with all nodes in layer
0
(which is same as
\mathscr{N}_{1}
in Section
2.2
); for
p\geq 1
,
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
is constructed from
\mathscr{G}_{p}
in the same way as
\mathscr{N}_{p+1}
is constructed from
\mathscr{N}_{p}
in Section
2.2
, except that we attach the garden
\mathcal{G}(B)\in\mathscr{G}_{p}

only when

|B|\geq 4
, while for
|B|=2
we simply
turn these two leaves into a leaf pair and put both of them in layer
p
.

For example, the example described in Section 2.1 is canonical, because the only garden we attach has width
4
; on the other hand, the layered garden in Figure
2
is not canonical, as the attached gardens all have width
2
. Indeed, starting from two trees each with 2 branches as in Section
2.2
, instead of attaching the five width
2
gardens (i.e. couples), the new construction procedure requires to turn each two-leaf set
B
into a leaf pair in layer
1
, which results in the garden as illustrated in Figure
3
, which is a canonical layered garden in
\mathscr{G}_{2}
. See also Figures
4
and
5
for a canonical garden in
\mathscr{G}_{3}
and its construction process.

Figure 3.
The canonical (regular) couple we get, if we start from the same setup in Figure
2
in Section
2.2
, but follow the new construction procedure in Section
2.3
. Here we do not attach any width
2
garden (i.e. couple) but turn any two-leaf subset
B
into a leaf pair in layer
1
.

It can be shown, see Proposition
4.8
, that a layered garden is canonical if and only if for any two nodes
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
such that all their descendant leaves are completely paired, we always have
\max(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\geq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}},\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})
, where
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}
is the layer of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{n}
etc. This provides another easy way to test if a given layered garden is canonical (for example the garden in Figure
2
clearly violates this condition), and also allows to bound the number of layerings satisfying the coherent condition in Definition
4.7
, see Proposition
4.10
.

Analytically, with the new reduction process, and by similar arguments as in Section
2.2
, we see that the cumulants at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
can be expressed as a sum of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
over all canonical layered gardens
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
now is defined exactly as in (
3.24
), with time integration defined by the layering, and the inputs given by
7
7

7

Compared to Section
2.2
, here the inputs are not replaced by the initial data
\varphi(0,\cdot)
because we may not be expanding all the way to time
0
.

F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
. Here
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}
is the layer of
\mathfrak{l}
and in practice
F_{p}(k)
equals
\varphi(p\delta,k)
plus a decaying sub-leading term. For an example (associated with the garden in Figures
4
and
5
) of deducing the expression
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
for
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{3}
from expressions
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{2}
, see Section
3.6
.

2.4.
Emergence of the arrow of time

With the detailed description of the ansatz in Sections
2.1
–
2.3
, the following statement should now become clear:
the structure of the cumulants in (
1.3
) at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
depends on (and memorizes) the whole interaction history of (
NLS
) on the time interval
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
.
Indeed, the ansatz is constructed in terms of canonical layered gardens, which are formed by carefully designed iteration procedures involving the short-time Taylor expansions of (
NLS
) on
[q\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},(q+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
for
0\leq q<p
, and thus sees the whole interaction history on
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
.

Now we can demonstrate, with such an ansatz that memorizes the interaction history, how an intrinsic arrow of time is created and resolves the “paradox” described in Section
1.4
, Idea 2. In fact, suppose we start at time
p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
. The full statistics of
\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)
is then given by two-point correlations, which match the solution
\varphi(p\delta,k)
of (
WKE
), plus the cumulant terms
\mathbb{K}_{0}
, which are lower order but memorize the full history on
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
.

Note that, regardless of whether we go forward to time
(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
or go backward to time
(p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, the contribution of the two-point correlation terms
\varphi(p\delta,k)
to the new two-point correlations will
always be

\varphi((p+1)\delta,k)
, which matches the solution to the original (
WKE
) if we go forward in time, and matches the solution to the
time-reversed
(
WKE
) if we go backward in time. However, whether such approximation is valid still depends on the contribution of the cumulant terms
\mathbb{K}_{0}
to the new two-point correlations, which we denote by
\mathbb{Y}
.

Now, if we go forward, then the terms
\mathbb{Y}
will be combinations of the
existing history
on
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
already seen by
\mathbb{K}_{0}
, and the
new history
coming from interactions of (
NLS
) on
[p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
. As the existing history and the new history belong to
disjoint time intervals

[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
and
[p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
, we know
\mathbb{Y}
will vanish in the limit, as illustrated in Section
2.1
, where
\mathbb{K}_{0}
is as in (
2.3
) and
\mathbb{Y}
as in (
2.5
). Therefore, the two-point correlations in the full statistics of
\widehat{u}((p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)
still matches the solution
\varphi((p+1)\delta,k)
to (
WKE
). It can be shown that the new cumulant terms are still lower order, but they will memorize the the full history of
[0,(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
, which is built up by both
[0,p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
and
[p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
, and the induction goes on.

If instead we go backward to time
(p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}
, then the terms
\mathbb{Y}

will not vanish in the limit
, because we are repeating existing history on
[(p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
instead of exploring new history on
[p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},(p+1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}}]
. The Markovian property in Section
2.1
then fails because
\mathbb{K}_{0}
“recognizes” the history it has experienced; see for example (
2.5
), where the leading term of
\mathbb{Y}
coming from
\bm{\delta}(t-s)
will
not be zero
if the “new history”
t\in[\delta,2\delta]
is changed to
t\in[0,\delta]
and “repeats the existing history”
s\in[0,\delta]
. In other words, instead of (
2.5
), we now have

\mathbb{Y}=-L^{-2(d-\gamma)}\sum_{k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}=k}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{j})\cdot\int_{0<s<\delta;\,0<t<\delta}e^{\pi i\Omega\cdot L^{2\gamma}(t-s)}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s\not\to 0\,\,\,(L\to\infty).

(2.10)

In such cases, due to these contributions
\mathbb{Y}
, the two-point correlations in the full statistics of
\widehat{u}((p-1)\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)
will
not
match
\varphi((p+1)\delta,k)
given by the time-reversed (
WKE
).

In summary, we have demonstrated how our canonical layered garden ansatz has played a crucial role in creating the arrow of time and resolving the time irreversibility “paradox”. It is also clear that the iteration steps in the current proof are
fundamentally different from
the short-time proof in our earlier works
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, as the latter just corresponds to the special case
p=0
, which has
empty history
.

2.5.
Main components of the proof

With the key definition of canonical layered gardens introduced and the ansatz for cumulants (
1.3
) fixed in Section
2.3
, we now describe the main components of the rest of the proof. The proof requires to (i) estimate and analyze the expressions
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
for individual canonical layered gardens
\mathcal{G}
(or suitable combinations thereof), and (ii) control the number of canonical layered gardens under suitable assumptions.

•

Non-regular couples:
It is known since
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
that the
regular couples

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}
(see Definition
4.1
) provide the main contributions to
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
. For non-regular couples
\mathcal{G}
, and non-regular parts of arbitrary gardens and couples (apart from the special structures of
vines
discussed below), the corresponding
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
can be estimated by applying a particular counting algorithm. This algorithm is a major component in the proofs of
[
19
,
22
]
, and exactly the same algorithm and proof applies also in this paper. This is summarized in the
rigidity theorem
(Proposition
11.7
), which we will use as a black box.

•

Non-coherent regular couples:
Now we restrict to regular couples. A key new notion in this paper is that of
incoherency
, see Definition
4.7
below. This notion of incoherency relies on the layering structure, and basically corresponds to the time disjointness property exhibited in the example in Section
2.1
; heuristically, such incoherency will occur in regular couples each time we attach a garden of width at least
4
to a set
B
of leave as described in Section
2.2
and above. Due to the Markovian property observed in Section
2.1
, we can prove that each occurrence of incoherency leads to a gain of power
L^{-c}
for some absolute constant
c
(see Proposition
5.1
), which suffices for our proof.

•

Coherent regular couples:
Turning to regular couples with no incoherency (i.e. coherent), we can prove a structure theorem for them (see Propositions
4.8
–
4.9
), which essentially reduces them to regular couples in a single layer (as the one in Figure
3
) which are already treated in
[
19
,
22
]
. This allows us to obtain the full asymptotics and cancellation for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
associated with such couples
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}
, see Propositions
6.1
–
6.3
. In particular, the leading contribution in
\mathbb{E}|\widehat{u}(p\delta\cdot T_{\mathrm{kin}},k)|^{2}
can be precisely calculated using these asymptotics, which is then shown to be
\varphi(p\delta,k)
by induction in time.

In addition, we can show that, for any coherent regular part of arbitrary gardens and couples that has size
n
, the number of choices for this part, including possible layerings, is bounded by
C_{0}^{n}
where
C_{0}
is a constant
independent
of
\delta
(see Proposition
4.10
). This is a key property that relies on the modified construction process described above (and is certainly not true in the setting of Section
2.2
), and allows to avoid the divergence in Section
2.2
caused by the factor
\mathfrak{D}^{n}
.

•

Vines and twists:
The structure of
vines
and
vine chains
, which was discovered in
[
22
]
(with a special case contained in
[
19
]
), is the main obstacle in the analysis of non-regular couples. Such structures require a delicate cancellation argument, where we combine the
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
terms for different gardens
\mathcal{G}
, that are related by a special combinatorial operation called
twist
.

In this paper, all these arguments need to be adapted to the new layering structure (consistency between twisting and layering structures is proved in Proposition
7.19
). We also need to introduce similar incoherency notions for vines and vine chains (Definition
8.1
), and establish similar gains for each occurrence of incoherency, as with regular couples above, see Proposition
9.5
. The number of coherent vines and vine chains can again be controlled, see Proposition
8.3
.

•

Ladders:
The
ladders
are neutral objects observed in
[
19
,
22
]
that do not gain or lose any powers of
L
. As such, in this paper we only need to avoid
\mathfrak{D}^{n}
type loss in Section
2.2
; just like for regular couples and vines, we can define the incoherency notion for ladders (Definition
8.1
), and prove similar decay estimates and counting bounds for the given number of occurrences of incoherency, see Propositions
8.2
and
10.4
. Finally we need an
L^{1}
estimate for time integrals, similar to
[
19
,
22
]
but adapted to the layered case, see Proposition
10.5
.

2.5.1.
Structure of the paper

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
3
we introduce the relevant notations (couples, gardens etc.), including the fundamental objects of layered gardens and canonical layered gardens, and write down the main ansatz. Then Sections
4
–
6
are devoted to the study of regular couples: Section
4
contains the combinatorial results, Section
5
contains the decay estimate for incoherent regular couples, and Section
6
contains the asymptotics for the coherent ones. In Sections
7
–
8
we define the notions of molecules, vines and twists, and prove relevant combinatorial results for layerings of these objects. In Section
9
we prove the main estimate for vines and vine chains. Putting these together, in Sections
10
–
11
we perform the necessary reduction steps to the molecule associated with a given garden
\mathcal{G}
, and apply the blackbox algorithm in
[
19
,
22
]
to complete the desired estimates for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
. Finally, in Section
12
we provide the estimates for the linearization operator and the remainder term, and complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
. In the whole proof process we will need various auxiliary lemmas (combinatorial, analytical, number theoretic and counting), which are listed in Appendix
A
. Then Appendix
B
illustrates the failure of some physical space
L^{1}
bound in
[
55
]
(discussed in Section
1.4
, Idea 2), Appendix
C
proves the persistence of regularity for solutions to (
WKE
) which gives existence of solutions in Schwartz space, and Appendix
D
contains three tables collecting some of the important notations used in this paper.

2.5.2.
Relation with previous works
[
19
,
20
,
22
]

As pointed out at the end of Section
2.4
, the long-time proof in the current paper is not an iteration of the short-time proof in our previous works
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
; in fact the inductive step in this paper is fundamentally different from the short time analysis in
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
. On the other hand, the current work does rely on many technical ingredients developed in
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, which we briefly describe below.

First, our proof adopts the same Feynman diagram expansion paradigm from
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, where iterates of (
NLS
) are expanded in trees, two-point correlations of these iterates are expanded in
couples
of trees
[
19
,
22
]
, and multi-point correlations (and cumulants) are expanded in
gardens
of trees
[
20
]
. We still give self-contained definitions of all those objects and the quantities associated to them in Section
3
.

Next, at a number of instances, especially concerning the common combinatorial objects that appear in both
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
and the current paper, we are directly citing the lemmas and propositions that are proved there. These mainly include (i) Propositions
4.4
–
4.6
and
6.6
–
6.8
, concerning structural properties of regular couples and exact asymptotics of certain expressions associated with them, (ii) Parts of Section
7
concerning structural properties of vines and their relation to couples and gardens, and (iii) Proposition
11.7
concerning counting estimates for certain molecules, which is proved in Proposition 9.6 of
[
22
]
and used as a black box here.

At each place where we directly cite from
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, we make sure that the relevant proof in the current paper is either exactly the same as in the cited papers, or requires at most minimal and essentially trivial adaptations. For any result whose proof requires even minor modifications from
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
, we have included a full self-contained proof in the current paper.

3.
Preparations for the proof

3.1.
First reductions

Let
\delta
be a small parameter to be fixed below, such that
\tau_{*}=\mathfrak{D}\delta,\,\mathfrak{D}\in\mathbb{Z}
(see Section
3.3.2
). We perform the first reduction for the equation (
NLS
) as follows. Let
u
be a solution to (
NLS
), and recall
\alpha=L^{-\gamma}
. Let
M=\fint|u|^{2}
be the conserved mass of
u
(where
\fint
takes the average on
\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d}
), and define
v:=e^{-2iL^{-\gamma}Mt}\cdot u
, then
v
satisfies the Wick ordered equation

(i\partial_{t}-\Delta)v+L^{-\gamma}\bigg(|v|^{2}v-2\fint|v|^{2}\cdot v\bigg)=0.

(3.1)

By switching to Fourier space, rescaling in time and reverting the linear Schrödinger flow, we define

a_{k}(t)=e^{-\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}|k|^{2}t}\cdot\widehat{v}(\delta T_{\mathrm{kin}}\cdot t,k)

(3.2)

with
\widehat{v}
as in (
1.1
). Then
\textit{{a}}:=a_{k}(t)
, where
t\in[0,\mathfrak{D}]
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, will satisfy the equation

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}a_{k}&=\mathcal{C}_{+}(\textit{{a}},\overline{\textit{{a}}},\textit{{a}})_{k}(t),\\
a_{k}(0)&=(a_{k})_{\mathrm{in}}=\sqrt{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k)}\cdot\mathfrak{g}_{k},\end{aligned}\right.

(3.3)

with the nonlinearity

\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}(\textit{{f}},\textit{{g}},\textit{{h}})_{k}(t):=\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\cdot(i\zeta)\sum_{k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}=k}\epsilon_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}e^{\zeta\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k)t}f_{k_{1}}(t)g_{k_{2}}(t)h_{k_{3}}(t)

(3.4)

for
\zeta\in\{\pm\}
. Here in (
3.4
) and below, the summation is taken over
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d})^{3}
, and

\epsilon_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}=\left\{\begin{aligned} +&1,&&\mathrm{if\ }k_{2}\not\in\{k_{1},k_{3}\};\\
-&1,&&\mathrm{if\ }k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3};\\
&0,&&\mathrm{otherwise},\end{aligned}\right.

(3.5)

and the resonance factor (assuming
k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}=k
) is

\Omega=\Omega(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k):=|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2}=2\langle k_{1}-k,k-k_{3}\rangle.

(3.6)

Note that
\epsilon_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}
is always supported in the set

\mathfrak{G}:=\big\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}):\mathrm{\ either\ }k_{2}\not\in\{k_{1},k_{3}\},\mathrm{\ or\ }k_{1}=k_{2}=k_{3}\big\}.

(3.7)

The rest of this paper is focused on the system (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) for
a
, with the relevant terms defined in (
3.5
)–(
3.6
), in the time interval
t\in[0,\mathfrak{D}]
.

3.2.
Moments and cumulants

Let
a_{k}(t)
be the (random) solution to (
3.3
)–(
3.4
). By gauge (
u\mapsto e^{i\theta}u
) and space translation (
u(x)\mapsto u(x-x_{0})
) invariance it is easy to see that, for any
k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
,
t_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}
we have

\mathbb{E}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{r}a_{k_{j}}(t_{j})^{\zeta_{j}}\bigg)=0

(3.8)

unless
\zeta_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{r}=0
(in particular
r
is even) and
\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{r}k_{r}=0
(in particular
k_{1}=k_{2}=k
when
r=2
). Note that any event
\mathfrak{F}
can be interpreted as a subset of initial data
u_{\mathrm{in}}
as in (
DAT
). We say an event
\mathfrak{F}
has gauge or space translation symmetry, if the corresponding subset is gauge or space translation invariant. Now we recall the standard notion of cumulants (see
[
55
]
):

Definition 3.1

(Cumulants
[
55
]
)
.

For any complex random variables
X_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
, define the
cumulant

\mathbb{K}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{r}):=\sum_{\mathcal{P}}(-1)^{|\mathcal{P}|-1}(|\mathcal{P}|-1)!\prod_{A\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\prod_{j\in A}X_{j}\bigg),

(3.9)

where the sum is taken over all partitions
\mathcal{P}
of
\{1,\cdots,r\}
. We then have the inversion formula (see for example
[
55
]
)

\mathbb{E}(X_{1}\cdots X_{r}):=\sum_{\mathcal{P}}\prod_{A\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{K}\big((X_{j})_{j\in A}\big).

(3.10)

Note that, due to (
3.8
), if
X_{j}=a_{k_{j}}(t_{j})^{\zeta_{j}}
, then (
3.9
) also vanishes unless
\zeta_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{r}=0
and
\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{r}k_{r}=0
, and in both (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) we can always assume that
(\zeta_{j}:j\in A)
contains half
+
and half
-
for each
A\in\mathcal{P}
. In addition, if all the moments and cumulants are taken assuming a particular event
\mathfrak{F}
(i.e. taken with an additional factor
\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{F}}
) with the same gauge and space translation symmetries, then all the above conclusions remain true.

3.3.
Norms, parameters, and notations

We next define the necessary norms, fix the parameters and introduce the relevant notations.

3.3.1.
Norms

In the rest of this paper, we use
\widehat{\cdot}

only
for the time Fourier transform, defined as

\widehat{u}(\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(t)e^{-2\pi i\lambda t}\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad u(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{u}(\lambda)e^{2\pi i\lambda t}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,

and similarly for higher dimensional versions.

For
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, integers
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\geq 0
and a function
F=F(t_{1},\cdots,t_{r},k)
on
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
t_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
are the time variables and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the vector variable, define the
\mathfrak{X}^{\theta,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}
norm

\|F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\theta,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}}=\max_{|\rho|\leq\Lambda_{1}}\int(\max(\langle\lambda_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle\lambda_{r}\rangle))^{\theta}\cdot\|\langle k\rangle^{\Lambda_{2}}\partial_{k}^{\rho}\widehat{F}(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{r},k)\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}.

(3.11)

In special cases where
F=F(t_{1},\cdots,t_{r})
or
F=F(k)
, define accordingly the norms

\|F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\theta}}=\int(\max(\langle\lambda_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle\lambda_{r}\rangle))^{\theta}\cdot|\widehat{F}(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{r})|\,\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\lambda_{r}

(3.12)

\|F\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}}=\max_{|\rho|\leq\Lambda_{1}}\|\langle k\rangle^{\Lambda_{2}}\partial_{k}^{\rho}F(k)\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}}.

(3.13)

Suppose
F=F(t_{1},\cdots,t_{r},k)
is a function on
\mathcal{B}\times\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
(or any subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
), where
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{R}^{r}
is a set of time variables
(t_{j})
, then we may define the
\mathfrak{X}^{\theta,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}(\mathcal{B})
norm of
F
by

\|F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\theta,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}(\mathcal{B})}=\inf\big\{\|G\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\theta,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}}:G|_{\mathcal{B}\times\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}=F\big\}.

(3.14)

The same applies to norms
\mathfrak{X}^{\theta}
(so we can define the
\mathfrak{X}^{\theta}(\mathcal{B})
norm) and
\mathfrak{S}^{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}}
(so it can be defined for functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
). In addition, if
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{2}=0
, we can define the
\mathfrak{X}^{\theta,0,0}
and
\mathfrak{X}^{\theta,0,0}(\mathcal{B})
norms for functions
F
depending on multiple variables
k_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q)
with the same formula.

Finally, for any interval
\mathcal{B}
, integer
\Lambda\geq 0
and any function
\textit{{a}}=a_{k}(t)
where
t\in\mathcal{B}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, define the norm

\|\textit{{a}}\|_{Z^{\Lambda}(\mathcal{B})}=\sup_{t\in\mathcal{B}}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\langle k\rangle^{\Lambda}|a_{k}(t)|.

(3.15)

3.3.2.
Parameters

First recall the local well-posedness result proved in
[
36
]
. For our purpose, we state it below in the form of an (equivalent) blowup criterion.

Proposition 3.2
.

Given any Schwartz initial data
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}
, there exists a maximal time
\tau_{\max}>0
(may be
\infty
) such that (
WKE
) has a unique solution
\varphi=\varphi(\tau,k)
in Schwartz class, for
\tau\in[0,\tau_{\max})
. Moreover, if
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}<\infty
then we have the blowup criterion

\lim_{\tau\to\tau_{\max}}\|\langle k\rangle^{s}\varphi(\tau,k)\|_{L_{k}^{2}}=\infty,\quad\forall s>d/2-1.

(3.16)

Proof.

See Appendix
C
.
∎

Throughout this paper, we will fix
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}
as in Proposition
3.2
, and fix
\tau_{*}\in(0,\tau_{\mathrm{max}})
as in Theorem
1.1
. We also fix the solution
\varphi(\tau,k)
to (
WKE
) as in Theorem
1.1
and Proposition
3.2
. We will use the notation
C_{0}
to denote arbitrary large constant that depends only on
(d,\gamma)
, and fix
\eta
that is small enough depending only on
C_{0}
. Define

\mathfrak{C}:=\sup_{\tau\in[0,\tau_{*}]}\|\varphi(\tau)\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}}<\infty,

(3.17)

with
\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}
defined as above, which depends only on
\tau_{*}
and the initial data
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}
. We use
