C_{1}
to denote arbitrary large constant that depends only on
(\tau_{*},\eta)
and
\mathfrak{C}
. Fix also
\nu
that is small enough depending on
C_{1}
, and
\delta
that is small enough depending on
\nu
, such that

\mathfrak{D}:=\frac{\tau_{*}}{\delta}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.18)

We will use
C_{2}
to denote arbitrary large constant that depends only on
\mathfrak{D}
and
\delta
(and also
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}
), and assume
L
is large enough depending on
C_{2}
. For integer
0\leq p\leq\mathfrak{D}
, define

\theta_{p}:=\eta^{20}(1+2^{-p}),\quad\Lambda_{p}:=(40d)^{\mathfrak{D}-p+1},

(3.19)

note that
\theta_{p+1}<\frac{4\theta_{p+1}+\theta_{p}}{5}<\frac{3\theta_{p+1}+2\theta_{p}}{5}<\frac{2\theta_{p+1}+3\theta_{p}}{5}<\frac{\theta_{p+1}+4\theta_{p}}{5}<\theta_{p}
. We choose the
\theta
in Theorem
1.1
as
\theta=\eta^{20}
, which is smaller than all the
\theta_{p}
in (
3.19
). Define also
N_{p}
and
R_{p}
such that
8
8

8

This choice is just for convenience; the same proof allows for any choice of
R_{\mathfrak{D}}
between
(\log L)^{40d}
and
L^{\nu^{2}(40d)^{-2\mathfrak{D}}}
.

R_{\mathfrak{D}}:=\lfloor(\log L)^{40d}\rfloor;\quad N_{p}=R_{p}^{40d}\,(0\leq p\leq\mathfrak{D}),\quad R_{p-1}=N_{p}^{40d}\,(1\leq p\leq\mathfrak{D}).

(3.20)

Finally, define

\gamma_{0}:=\min(\gamma,1-\gamma),\quad\gamma_{1}:=\min(2\gamma,1,2(d-1)(1-\gamma)).

(3.21)

3.3.3.
Notations

We will use
X=\mathcal{O}_{j}(Y)
,
X\lesssim_{j}Y
and
X\ll_{j}Y
etc., where
j\in\{0,1,2\}
, to indicate that the implicit constants depend only on
C_{j}
(where
C_{j}
are defined in Section
3.3.2
). Note that by definition we have
\nu\ll_{1}1
and
\delta^{\nu}\ll_{1}1
. For multi-index
\rho=(\rho_{1},\cdots,\rho_{m})
, denote
|\rho|=\rho_{1}+\cdots+\rho_{m}
and
\rho!=(\rho_{1})!\cdots(\rho_{m})!
, etc. For an index set
A
, we use the vector notation
\alpha[A]=(\alpha_{j})_{j\in A}
and
\mathrm{d}\alpha[A]=\prod_{j\in A}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{j}
, etc. The sign
\zeta\in\{\pm\}
is understood as
\pm 1
in algebraic manipulations. Denote
x^{j}
as the coordinates of vector
(x^{1},\cdots,x^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
z^{+}=z
for a complex number
z
, and
z^{-}=\overline{z}
.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
fix the cutoff function

\chi_{0}=\chi_{0}(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0,&&\mathrm{for\ }|x|\geq 1;\\
&1,&&\mathrm{for\ }|x|\leq 1/2;\\
&\mathrm{smooth\ in\ }[0,1],&&\mathrm{for\ }1/2\leq|x|\leq 1.\end{aligned}\right.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
define
\chi_{0}(x)=\chi_{0}(x^{1})\cdots\chi_{0}(x^{d})
, and
\chi_{\infty}=1-\chi_{0}
. In general, we may use
\chi
to denote other cutoff functions with slightly different supports. These functions, as well as the other cutoff functions, will be in Gevrey class
2
(i.e. the
m
-th order derivatives are bounded by
(2m)!
).

3.4.
Combinatorial structures

We define the combinatorial structures that will be used throughout this paper, including trees, gardens, couples and their decorations. For illustrations of structures defined in
[
19
,
20
,
22
]
the reader may refer to those papers; here we only provide illustration for the new structures introduced in this paper.

3.4.1.
Trees, gardens and couples

We start with the notion of trees in
[
19
]
, which will be restricted to ternary trees throughout this paper.

Definition 3.3

(Trees
[
19
]
)
.

A
ternary tree

\mathcal{T}
(we will simply say a
tree
below) is a rooted tree where each non-leaf (or
branching
) node has exactly three children nodes, which we shall distinguish as the
left
,
mid
and
right
ones. A node
\mathfrak{m}
is a
descendant
of a node
\mathfrak{n}
, or
\mathfrak{n}
is an
ancestor
of
\mathfrak{m}
, if
\mathfrak{m}
belongs to the subtree rooted at
\mathfrak{n}
(we allow
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}
). We say
\mathcal{T}
is
trivial
(and write
\mathcal{T}=\bullet
) if it has only the root, in which case this root is also viewed as a leaf.

We denote generic nodes by
\mathfrak{n}
, generic leaves by
\mathfrak{l}
, the root by
\mathfrak{r}
, the set of leaves by
\mathcal{L}
and the set of branching nodes by
\mathcal{N}
. The
order
of a tree
\mathcal{T}
is defined by
n(\mathcal{T})=|\mathcal{N}|
, so if
n(\mathcal{T})=n
then
|\mathcal{L}|=2n+1
and
|\mathcal{T}|=3n+1
. Denote the parent node of a non-root node
\mathfrak{n}
by
\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}
.

A tree
\mathcal{T}
may have sign
+
or
-
. If its sign is fixed then we decide the signs of its nodes as follows: the root
\mathfrak{r}
has the same sign as
\mathcal{T}
, and for any branching node
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
, the signs of the three children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}
from left to right are
(\zeta,-\zeta,\zeta)
if
\mathfrak{n}
has sign
\zeta\in\{\pm\}
. Once the sign of
\mathcal{T}
is fixed, we will denote the sign of
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{T}
by
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Define
\zeta(\mathcal{T})=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}(i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}})
. We also define the conjugate
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
of a tree
\mathcal{T}
to be the same tree but with opposite sign.

Next we define gardens (and the special case of couples), which are collections of trees with their leaves paired. The definition of couples first appeared in
[
19
]
, and general gardens in
[
20
]
.

Definition 3.4

(Gardens and couples
[
19
,
20
]
)
.

Given a sequence
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
, where
\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}
and exactly half of them are
+
, we define a
garden

\mathcal{G}
of
signature

(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
, to be an ordered collection of trees
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{2R})
, such that
\mathcal{T}_{j}
has sign
\zeta_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq 2R
, together with a partition
\mathscr{P}
of the set of leaves in all
\mathcal{T}_{j}
into two-element subsets (called
pairings
) such that the two paired leaves have opposite signs. The
width
of the garden is defined to be
2R
, which is always an even number. The
order

n(\mathcal{G})
of a garden
\mathcal{G}
is the sum of orders of all
\mathcal{T}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
. We denote
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{L}_{2R}
to be the set of leaves and
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{N}_{2R}
to be the set of branching nodes, where
\mathcal{L}_{j}
and
\mathcal{N}_{j}
are the sets of leaves and branching nodes of
\mathcal{T}_{j}
, and define
\zeta(\mathcal{G})=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}(i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}})
.

A garden of width
2
(i.e.
R=1
) is called a
couple
, and denoted by
\mathcal{Q}
. A non-couple garden (i.e.
R\geq 2
) is called
proper
. For a couple we will always assume
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=(+,-)
and denote
(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})
by
(\mathcal{T}^{+},\mathcal{T}^{-})
. We also define a
paired tree
to be a tree
\mathcal{T}
whose leaves are all paired to each other according to the same pairing rule for gardens and couples, except for
one unpaired leaf
which is called the
lone leaf
. Note that if
\mathcal{T}
is a paired tree, then it automatically forms a couple with the trivial tree
\bullet
. Define the conjugate of a couple
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{T}^{+},\mathcal{T}^{-})
as
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}=(\overline{\mathcal{T}^{-}},\overline{\mathcal{T}^{+}})
; for a paired tree
\mathcal{T}
we also define its conjugate as
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
with the same pairings, where
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
is as in Definition
3.3
.

We say a garden
\mathcal{G}
is
irreducible
if there exists no proper subset
A\subset\{1,\cdots,2R\}
(i.e.
A\neq\varnothing
and
A\neq\{1,\cdots,2R\}
) such that the leaves of
\mathcal{T}_{j}\,(j\in A)
are all paired with each other. Clearly any garden
\mathcal{G}
can be uniquely decomposed into irreducible gardens corresponding to a partition
(A_{i})
of
\{1,\cdots,2R\}
, where
|A_{i}|
is even and
(\zeta_{j}:j\in A_{i})
contains half
+
and half
-
for each
i
. These are called the
components
of
\mathcal{G}
. We say
\mathcal{G}
is a
multi-couple
if all its components are couples (i.e. have width
2
), and
trivial
if each tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is trivial. Note that a trivial garden must be a multi-couple formed by
R
trivial couples.

Next we define decorations of couples and gardens (this is defined in
[
19
]
for couples, and
[
20
]
for general gardens).

Definition 3.5

(Decorations
[
19
,
20
]
)
.

For any tree
\mathcal{T}
, a
decoration

\mathscr{D}
of
\mathcal{T}
is a mapping
k[\mathcal{T}]:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
consisting of
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
for each node
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathcal{T}
, such that

k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}+k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}}=k_{\mathfrak{n}},\quad\mathrm{equivalently}\quad\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}+\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}k_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}+\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}}k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}}=\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}k_{\mathfrak{n}}

for any branching node
\mathfrak{n}
and its three children nodes
(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{3})
from left to right (recall
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}
defined in Definition
3.3
). Clearly a decoration
\mathscr{D}
is uniquely determined by the values of
(k_{\mathfrak{l}})_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, we say
\mathscr{D}
is a
k
-decoration if
k_{\mathfrak{r}}=k
for the root
\mathfrak{r}
.

For any garden
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{T}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{2R})
, a
decoration

\mathscr{I}
of
\mathcal{G}
is a mapping
k[\mathcal{G}]:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
consisting of
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
for each node
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathcal{G}
, such that the restriction to each
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is a decoration of
\mathcal{T}_{j}
, and
k_{\mathfrak{l}}=k_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
for each leaf pair
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{G}
. For any
k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
, we say
\mathscr{I}
is a
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration if
k_{\mathfrak{r}_{j}}=k_{j}
where
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
is the root of the tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
. Note that in this case we necessarily have
\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{2R}k_{2R}=0
, where
\zeta_{j}
are as in Definition
3.4
; in particular for couples we must have
k_{1}=k_{2}:=k
, thus for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
we may also talk about
k
-decorations for couples. We can define decorations of paired trees in the same way (again requiring that
k_{\mathfrak{l}}=k_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
for each leaf pair
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})
); in this case we must have
k_{\mathfrak{r}}=k_{\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{lo}}}:=k
for the root
\mathfrak{r}
and lone leaf
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{lo}}
, so we may also talk about
k
-decorations of paired trees. Moreover,
for couples and paired trees
, we shall define
k
-decorations for
general vectors

k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by requiring
k_{\mathfrak{n}}-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
instead of
k_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, and keeping all other requirements.

For any decoration
\mathscr{D}
or
\mathscr{I}
defined above, we define the coefficient

\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}\,(\mathrm{or\ }\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}})=\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}\epsilon_{k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}k_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}}}

(3.22)

where
(\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2},\mathfrak{n}_{3})
is as above and
\epsilon_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}
is as in (
3.5
); in the support of
\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}
(or
\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}}
) we have
(k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}},k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}})\in\mathfrak{G}
as in (
3.7
) for any
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
. Define the resonance factor
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
for each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
by

\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}=\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}(k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}},k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}},k_{\mathfrak{n}})=|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}|^{2}-|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{2}}|^{2}+|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}}|^{2}-|k_{\mathfrak{n}}|^{2}.

(3.23)

Note that, for a
k
-decoration of couples and paired trees, if we translate all the
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
, including
k
, by the same constant vector
\overline{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
(which may not be in
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
), then the decoration conditions, as well as the values of
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
, do not change.

3.4.2.
Layerings

Now we introduce the key new structure of
layerings
imposed on gardens (and couples and paired trees), which corresponds to iterating the solution at different time slices.

Definition 3.6

(Layered gardens)
.

A
layering
of a garden
\mathcal{G}
is a mapping
\mathfrak{L}[\mathcal{G}]:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{Z}
consisting of
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}
for each node
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathcal{G}
, such that
0\leq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\leq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}
whenever
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is a child of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
whenever
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})
are two paired leaves, see Figure
4
. We call
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}
the
layer
of
\mathfrak{n}
. A garden
\mathcal{G}
with a layering fixed is called a
layered
garden. We also define layerings of paired trees in the same way. Moreover, define a
pre-layering
(of a garden or a paired tree) to be the same as layering except we define
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}
only for
branching nodes

\mathfrak{n}
, and according to the same rules as above.

Figure 4.
A layered garden, as in Definition
3.6
. The signs of trees are
(+,-,+,-)
from left to right.

We will consider an important class of layered gardens, namely the
canonical layered gardens
, which includes the ones that occur in the actual inductive scheme (see Proposition
3.9
).

Definition 3.7

(Canonical layered gardens)
.

Let
p\geq 0
. We inductively define a collection
\mathscr{G}_{p}
of irreducible proper layered gardens (see Definition
3.4
); gardens in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
will be called
canonical layered gardens of depth
p
. Let
\mathscr{G}_{0}=\varnothing
. Suppose
\mathscr{G}_{p}
is defined for some
p\geq 0
, to define
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
we consider the following process.

First choose any
2R\,(R\geq 2)
trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
with signs
\zeta_{j}
as in Definition
3.4
(half
+
and half
-
), and put all their branching nodes in layer
p
. Next, divide the set of leaves of all these trees into subsets
B_{i}
such that the signs of leaves in each subset are half
+
and half
-
; we also require that there is no proper subset
A\subset\{1,\cdots,2R\}
such that the set of leaves of
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}
for all
j\in A
equals the union of
some of the sets

B_{i}
. Then, we turn any
two-element
subset
B_{i}
into a pairing, putting the involved leaves again in layer
p
. For any subset
B_{i}
of
at least four elements
, we replace its leaves by an irreducible proper layered garden in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
, such that the roots of the trees in this garden (including the signs) are just the elements of
B_{i}
. This results in a big proper layered garden of width
2R
, which is easily shown to be irreducible, and we define
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
to consist of all the irreducible proper layered gardens that can be obtained in this way, see Figure
5
.

We also define, for
p\geq 0
, a collection
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
of layered
couples
, by choosing
R=1
and following the same procedure in the above construction (i.e. choosing two trees of signs
+
and
-
, dividing the set of leaves into pairings and subsets of at least four elements, and replacing the latters by layered gardens in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
). Couples in
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
are called
canonical layered couples
.

Figure 5.
An illustration showing that the layered garden in Figure
4
is canonical, following the inductive construction in Definition
3.7
.

Finally, we define an important quantity
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
for gardens
\mathcal{G}
(as well as
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
for couples
\mathcal{Q}
and paired trees
\mathcal{T}
), which is similar to the ones in
[
19
,
20
]
, but also takes into account the layers. This quantity is the main subject of study in the proof of the current paper.

Definition 3.8

(Definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
)
.

Fix an integer
p\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{G}
be a layered garden consisting of trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
with sign
\zeta_{j}
, such that all nodes of
\mathcal{G}
are in layer at most
p
. Fix also the input functions
F_{q}=F_{q}(k)
for
0\leq q\leq p
, then for any
k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
(satisfying
\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{2R}k_{2R}=0
, where
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
is the signature of
\mathcal{G}
) and
t>p
, we may define

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}(t,k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n}\zeta(\mathcal{G})\sum_{\mathscr{I}}\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}}\int_{\mathcal{I}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}e^{\pi i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}^{(+)}F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}}).

(3.24)

Here in (
3.24
)
n
is the order of
\mathcal{G}
,
\zeta(\mathcal{G})
is as in Definition
3.4
and
\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}}
as in Definition
3.5
. The sum is taken over all
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decorations
\mathscr{I}
of
\mathcal{G}
,
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
is as in (
3.23
), and the last product is taken over all
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}
with sign
+
. The time domain
\mathcal{I}
is defined by

\mathcal{I}=\left\{t[\mathcal{N}]:\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}}<\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}+1\quad\mathrm{and}\quad 0<t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}}<t,\mathrm{\ whenever\ \mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\ is\ a\ child\ of\ \mathfrak{n}}\right\},

(3.25)

where
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}
is the layer of
\mathfrak{n}
as in Definition
3.6
. Note that the definition of
\mathcal{I}
depends only on the
pre-layering
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Now we can define
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
for couples
\mathcal{Q}
via (
3.24
), in which case we can allow
k_{1}=k_{2}:=k
to be
arbitrary vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, provided that the input functions
F_{r}=F_{r}(k)
for
0\leq r\leq p
are defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and that the sum in (
3.24
) is still taken over all
k
-decorations
\mathscr{I}
of
\mathcal{Q}
as in Definition
3.5
.
For later use, we will extend this definition to
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
, and also define similar expression
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
for paired trees
\mathcal{T}
, as follows.

First, let
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{T}^{+},\mathcal{T}^{-})
be a layered couple such that the two roots are in layer at most
q
and
q^{\prime}
respectively where
q,q^{\prime}\leq p
, and fix the input functions
F_{r}=F_{r}(k)
for
0\leq r\leq p
(which are defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as stated above). Then for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t>q
,
s>q^{\prime}
, we may define
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s,k)
, in the same way as (
3.24
), except that the time domain is now

\mathcal{I}^{*}=\left\{t[\mathcal{N}]:\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}}<\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}+1\quad\mathrm{and}\quad 0<t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathrm{\ whenever\ \mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\ is\ a\ child\ of\ \mathfrak{n}},\right.\\
\left.\mathrm{and}\quad t_{\mathfrak{n}}<t\ \mathrm{(or\ }s),\mathrm{\ whenever\ \mathfrak{n}\ is\ a\ node\ of\ \mathcal{T}^{+}\ (or\ \mathcal{T}^{-})}\right\}.

(3.26)

This gives an extension of the expression
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
in (
3.24
) when
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}
is a couple and
k_{1}=k_{2}=k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that the latter corresponds to the special case
t=s>p
.

Similarly, let
\mathcal{T}
be a layered paired tree such that the root is in layer at most
q
and the lone leaf is in layer
q^{\prime}
where
q^{\prime}\leq q\leq p
, and fix the input functions
F_{r}=F_{r}(k)
for
0\leq r\leq p
which are defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then
for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
(t,s)
satisfying
t>q
,
s<q^{\prime}+1
and
t>s
, we may define
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s,k)
in the same way as (
3.24
), except that (i) the sum is taken over all
k
-decorations
\mathscr{D}
of
\mathcal{T}
where again
k
may not be in
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, (ii) the last product is taken over all leaves
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}
with sign
+

other than the lone leaf
\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{lo}}
, and (iii) the time domain is now

\mathcal{D}^{*}=\big\{t[\mathcal{N}]:\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}}<\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}+1\quad\mathrm{and}\quad 0<t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}}<t,\mathrm{\ whenever\ \mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\ is\ a\ child\ of\ \mathfrak{n}},\\
\mathrm{and}\quad t_{\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\mathrm{pr}}}>s,\mathrm{\ where\ \mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\mathrm{pr}}\ is\ the\ parent\ node\ of\ the\ lone\ leaf\ \mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{lo}}}\big\}.

(3.27)

3.5.
The inductive scheme

With all the above preparations, we can now describe the inductive scheme which is the central component in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Recall that we are considering the system (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) for
t\in[0,\mathfrak{D}]
. Moreover, as the distinction between different
C_{j}
in Section
3.3.2
will be important, we also recall it here:

C_{0}\ll\eta^{-1}\ll C_{1}\ll\delta^{-1}\ll C_{2}.

Here
C_{0}
is any “absolute” constant depending only on
(d,\gamma)
, which can be absorbed by
\eta
;
C_{1}
is much larger than
\eta^{-1}
and also depends on the uniform bound
\mathfrak{C}
of the solution
\varphi
to (
WKE
) defined in (
3.17
), but can still be absorbed by the small parameter
\delta
indicating the length of each short interval;
C_{2}
is the largest and may depend on the number
\mathfrak{D}
of intervals which may be much larger than
\delta^{-1}
, but can still be absorbed by any quantity that increases with
L
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Recall the parameters defined in (
3.20
). For
0\leq p\leq\mathfrak{D}
and
2\leq R\leq R_{p}
, we can define an event
\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, functions
\mathscr{R}_{p}=\mathscr{R}_{p}(k)
and
\mathscr{E}_{p}=\mathscr{E}_{p}(k)
, and
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}=\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R},k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
, such that
\mathscr{R}_{p}(k)
is real valued, and the followings hold for each
0\leq p\leq\mathfrak{D}
:

(1)

Assuming
\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, the system (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) has a unique solution
\textit{{a}}=a_{k}(t)
on
[0,p]
. We also have
\mathfrak{F}_{p}\supset\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p})\geq 1-e^{-N_{p}}
. Moreover each
\mathfrak{F}_{p}
has gauge and space translation symmetry; the expectations
\mathbb{E}
and cumulants
\mathbb{K}
below are taken assuming
\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, and they come with subscript
p
.

(2)

Let the cumulant
\mathbb{K}
be defined as in (
3.9
), and
\mathbb{K}_{p}
as in (1). For any
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
we have

\mathbb{K}_{p}(a_{k}(p),\overline{a_{\ell}(p)})=\mathbb{E}_{p}(a_{k}(p)\overline{a_{\ell}(p)})=\mathbf{1}_{k=\ell}\cdot\big(\varphi(\delta p,k)+\mathscr{R}_{p}(k)+\mathscr{E}_{p}(k)\big),

(3.28)

where
\varphi
is the solution to (
WKE
), the sub-leading term
\mathscr{R}_{p}(k)
and remainder
\mathscr{E}_{p}(k)
satisfy

\|\mathscr{R}_{p}(k)\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{\Lambda_{p},\Lambda_{p}}}\leq L^{-\theta_{p}},\quad|\mathscr{E}_{p}(k)|\leq\langle k\rangle^{-\Lambda_{p}}\cdot e^{-N_{p}}.

(3.29)

(3)

For any
2\leq R\leq R_{p}
, signs
\zeta_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
as in Definition
3.4
, and
k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
, we have

\mathbb{K}_{p}\big(a_{k_{1}}(p)^{\zeta_{1}},\cdots,a_{k_{2R}}(p)^{\zeta_{2R}}\big)=\sum_{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}(p,k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})+\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}.

(3.30)

Here the sum is taken over all
canonical layered gardens

\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p}
of signature
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
and
order at most
N_{p}
(in particular all nodes of
\mathcal{G}
are in layer at most
p-1
), and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
is as in (
3.24
) in Definition
3.8
with input functions
F_{q}(k)=\varphi(\delta q,k)+\mathscr{R}_{q}(k)
for
0\leq q\leq p-1
. The remainder
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}
is supported in the set
\zeta_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{2R}=0
and
\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{2R}k_{2R}=0
, and satisfies that

|\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}|\leq\big(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle\big)^{-\Lambda_{p}}\cdot e^{-N_{p}}.

(3.31)

(4)

In addition, for any
n\leq N_{p}
, consider the expression on the right hand side of (
3.30
), but with the summation taken over all canonical layered gardens
\mathcal{G}

of order
n
and without the remainder
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}
. Then this expression, denoted by
\mathscr{K}_{p,n}
, satisfies that

|\mathscr{K}_{p,n}|\leq\big(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle\big)^{-\Lambda_{p}}\cdot L^{-(R-1)/6}\cdot\delta^{\nu n}.

(3.32)

3.6.
The Duhamel expansion

The proof of Proposition
3.9
will occupy most of the remaining parts of this paper. Since the statements (1)–(4) are trivially true for
p=0
(with
\mathscr{R}_{0}=\mathscr{E}_{0}=\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{0}}=0
and
\mathfrak{F}_{0}
the trivial true event) due to the independence and Gaussian assumption (
DAT
), we only need to justify the inductive step. Thus, for the rest of this paper (except Section
3.7
), we will fix
0\leq p\leq\mathfrak{D}-1
and assume (1)–(4) of Proposition
3.9
hold for any
0\leq q\leq p
, and we just need to prove the same for
p+1
.

To achieve this, we need to expand the solution
(a_{k}(p+1))
in terms of the previous data
(a_{k}(p))
. Assuming the event
\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, for any tree
\mathcal{T}
and any
t>p
, define the expression

(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t)=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n}\zeta(\mathcal{T})\sum_{\mathscr{D}}\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}e^{\pi i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}a_{k_{\mathfrak{l}}}(p).

(3.33)

Here in (
3.33
),
n
is the order of
\mathcal{T}
,
\zeta(\mathcal{T})
is as in Definition
3.3
and
\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}
as in Definition
3.5
. The sum is taken over all
k
-decorations
\mathscr{D}
of
\mathcal{T}
, and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
is as in (
3.23
). The integral domain
\mathcal{D}
is defined by

\mathcal{D}=\left\{t[\mathcal{N}]:p<t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}<t_{\mathfrak{n}}<t,\mathrm{\ whenever\ \mathfrak{n}^{\prime}\ is\ a\ child\ of\ \mathfrak{n}}\right\}.

(3.34)

Now, define

(\mathcal{J}_{n})_{k}(t)=\sum_{n(\mathcal{T}^{+})=n}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}^{+}})_{k}(t)

(3.35)

where the sum is taken over all trees
\mathcal{T}^{+}
of order
n
and sign
+
. Define the function
\textit{{b}}=b_{k}(t)
, where
t\in[p,p+1]
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, by

a_{k}(t)=a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)+b_{k}(t),\quad a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t):=\sum_{n=0}^{N_{p+1}}(\mathcal{J}_{n})_{k}(t).

(3.36)

It is then easy to see that the system (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) for
\textit{{a}}=a_{k}(t)
on
[p,p+1]
is equivalent to the following equation for
b
:

\textit{{b}}=\mathscr{L}_{0}+\mathscr{L}_{1}\textit{{b}}+\mathscr{L}_{2}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}})+\mathscr{L}_{3}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}}),

(3.37)

or equivalently

\textit{{b}}=(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})^{-1}(\mathscr{L}_{0}+\mathscr{L}_{2}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}})+\mathscr{L}_{3}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}})),

(3.38)

where

(\mathscr{L}_{j}(\textit{{b}},\cdots,\textit{{b}}))_{k}(t)=\sum_{(j)}\int_{p}^{t}\mathcal{C}_{+}(\textit{{u}},\textit{{v}},\textit{{w}})_{k}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.39)

The sums in (
3.39
) are taken over
(\textit{{u}},\textit{{v}},\textit{{w}})
, where each of them equals either
b
or
\mathcal{J}_{n}
for some
0\leq n\leq N_{p+1}
. Assume in the sum
\sum_{(j)}
that exactly
j
inputs in
(\textit{{u}},\textit{{v}},\textit{{w}})
equal
b
, and in the sum
\sum_{(0)}
we further require that
(\textit{{u}},\textit{{v}},\textit{{w}})=(\mathcal{J}_{n_{1}},\mathcal{J}_{n_{2}},\mathcal{J}_{n_{3}})
with
n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}\geq N_{p+1}
. Note that each
\mathscr{L}_{j}
is a
j
-multilinear form over
\mathbb{R}
.

To calculate the cumulants

\mathbb{K}_{p+1}\big(a_{k_{1}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{1}},\cdots,a_{k_{2R}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{2R}}\big)

(3.40)

in Proposition
3.9
, we first calculate the contribution of truncated tree terms, namely

\mathbb{K}_{p}\big(a_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{1}},\cdots,a_{k_{2R}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{2R}}\big).

(3.41)

Now, by Lemma
A.1
, Definition
3.7
, gauge and space translation symmetry of
\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, and the induction hypothesis, we can write the cumulant (
3.41
) as the sum of one explicit term, plus remainder terms that contain one of
\mathscr{E}_{p}
or
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}
. For
R\geq 2
this explicit term equals

\sum_{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}(p+1,k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R}),

where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
is as in (
3.24
) in Definition
3.8
with input functions
F_{q}(k)=\varphi(\delta q,k)+\mathscr{R}_{q}(k)
for
0\leq q\leq p
, and
\mathcal{G}
runs over a specific collection
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
of canonical layered gardens in
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
; for
R=1
we should replace
\mathcal{G}
by a couple
\mathcal{Q}
which runs over a specific collection
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
of canonical layered couples in
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
. Here
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
are defined as follows:

Definition 3.10
.

For any canonical layered garden
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(or couple
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
), we define
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
(or
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
) if and only if, following the construction process in Definition
3.7
, we have that (i) each initial tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}
in Definition
3.7
has order at most
N_{p+1}
, and (ii) each layered garden in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
that replaces the leaves in some subset
B_{i}
has order at most
N_{p}
.

We illustrate the above expansion by a concrete example, which corresponds to the canonical layered garden in Figure
5
. Consider (
3.41
) with
p=2
and
R=2
, and
\zeta_{j}=(-1)^{j-1}
. Replace the four
a^{\mathrm{tr}}
factors in (
3.41
), from left to right, by
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}_{j}^{2}}
for the four trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{2}
in Figure
5
(where
\mathcal{T}_{2}^{2}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}^{2}
have sign
-
, corresponding to
\zeta_{2}=\zeta_{4}=-
). Note that the root of
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{2}
is decorated by
k_{j}
, and we denote the decoration of the nodes in
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{2}
by
k_{ji}\,(i\geq 1)
with numbering from top to bottom and left to right; similarly we denote the time variables associated with
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{2}
by
t_{j}^{\prime}<t_{j}
etc. This then leads to the expression

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}=k_{11}-k_{12}+k_{13}\\
k_{12}=k_{14}-k_{15}+k_{16}\end{subarray}}\sum_{k_{2}=k_{21}-k_{22}+k_{23}}\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{3}(-i)\cdot\epsilon_{k_{11}k_{12}k_{13}}\epsilon_{k_{14}k_{15}k_{16}}\epsilon_{k_{21}k_{22}k_{23}}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\int_{2<t_{1}^{\prime}<t_{1}<3}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{12}|^{2}-|k_{14}|^{2}+|k_{15}|^{2}-|k_{16}|^{2})t_{1}^{\prime}}\cdot e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{11}|^{2}-|k_{12}|^{2}+|k_{13}|^{2}-|k_{1}|^{2})t_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\int_{2<t_{2}<3}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{2}|^{2}-|k_{21}|^{2}+|k_{22}|^{2}-|k_{23}|^{2})t_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\mathbb{K}_{2}\big(a_{k_{11}}(2)\overline{a_{k_{14}}(2)}a_{k_{15}}(2)\overline{a_{k_{16}}(2)}a_{k_{13}}(2),\overline{a_{k_{21}}(2)}a_{k_{22}}(2)\overline{a_{k_{23}}(2)},a_{k_{3}}(2),\overline{a_{k_{4}}(2)}\big).

(3.42)

Now we apply Lemma
A.1
to the cumulant expression in (
3.42
); corresponding to the pairings between
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{2}
in Figure
5
, we select the term corresponding to

\mathbb{K}_{2}(a_{k_{11}}(2),\overline{a_{k_{16}}(2)})\cdot\mathbb{K}_{2}(a_{k_{15}}(2),\overline{a_{k_{23}}(2)})\cdot\mathbb{K}_{2}\big(\overline{a_{k_{14}}(2)},a_{k_{13}}(2),\overline{a_{k_{21}}(2)},a_{k_{22}}(2),a_{k_{3}}(2),\overline{a_{k_{4}}(2)}\big),

which also determines the sets
B_{i}
in Definition
3.7
. By induction hypothesis, the first two cumulants above can be replaced by
\mathbf{1}_{k_{11}=k_{16}}\cdot F_{2}(k_{11})
etc. (ignoring
\mathscr{E}_{p}
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}
, same below); the last cumulant is the sum of

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(2,k_{14},k_{13},k_{21},k_{22},k_{3},k_{4})

over all
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{2}
(with signature
(-,+,-,+,+,-)
and an upper bound for
n(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
). If we select the garden
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{2}
in Figure
5
, we get the factor

\displaystyle\sum_{k_{21}=k_{24}-k_{25}+k_{26}}\sum_{k_{3}=k_{31}-k_{32}+k_{33}}\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{2}(+1)\cdot\epsilon_{k_{24}k_{25}k_{26}}\epsilon_{k_{31}k_{32}k_{33}}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\int_{1<t_{2}^{\prime}<2}\int_{0<t_{3}<1}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{21}|^{2}-|k_{24}|^{2}+|k_{25}|^{2}-|k_{26}|^{2})t_{2}^{\prime}}\cdot e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{31}|^{2}-|k_{32}|^{2}+|k_{33}|^{2}-|k_{3}|^{2})t_{3}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{2}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t_{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{14}=k_{33}}F_{0}(k_{14})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{13}=k_{26}}F_{1}(k_{13})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{24}=k_{31}}F_{0}(k_{24})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{25}=k_{4}}F_{1}(k_{25})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{22}=k_{32}}F_{0}(k_{22}).

Plugging this into (
3.42
), we get the total expression

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}=k_{11}-k_{12}+k_{13}\\
k_{12}=k_{14}-k_{15}+k_{16}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{2}=k_{21}-k_{22}+k_{23}\\
k_{21}=k_{24}-k_{25}+k_{26}\end{subarray}}\sum_{k_{3}=k_{31}-k_{32}+k_{33}}\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{5}(-i)\cdot\epsilon_{k_{11}k_{12}k_{13}}\epsilon_{k_{14}k_{15}k_{16}}\epsilon_{k_{21}k_{22}k_{23}}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\int_{2<t_{1}^{\prime}<t_{1}<3}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{12}|^{2}-|k_{14}|^{2}+|k_{15}|^{2}-|k_{16}|^{2})t_{1}^{\prime}}\cdot e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{11}|^{2}-|k_{12}|^{2}+|k_{13}|^{2}-|k_{1}|^{2})t_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\int_{1<t_{2}^{\prime}<2}\int_{2<t_{2}<3}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{2}|^{2}-|k_{21}|^{2}+|k_{22}|^{2}-|k_{23}|^{2})t_{2}}\cdot e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{21}|^{2}-|k_{24}|^{2}+|k_{25}|^{2}-|k_{26}|^{2})t_{2}^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{2}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\epsilon_{k_{24}k_{25}k_{26}}\epsilon_{k_{31}k_{32}k_{33}}\int_{0<t_{3}<1}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(|k_{31}|^{2}-|k_{32}|^{2}+|k_{33}|^{2}-|k_{3}|^{2})t_{3}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{3}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{11}=k_{16}}F_{2}(k_{11})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{15}=k_{23}}F_{2}(K_{15})

\displaystyle\qquad\,\,\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{14}=k_{33}}F_{0}(k_{14})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{13}=k_{26}}F_{1}(k_{13})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{24}=k_{31}}F_{0}(k_{24})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{25}=k_{4}}F_{1}(k_{25})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{k_{22}=k_{32}}F_{0}(k_{22}),

which is exactly
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}(3,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})
for the canonical layered garden
\mathcal{G}
illustrated in Figure
5
.

Now we recall that, due to the smallness of
\delta
relative to
\mathfrak{C}
defined in (
3.17
), the solution
\varphi((p+1)\delta,k)
to the wave kinetic equation (
WKE
) admits a power series expansion in terms of the data
\varphi(p\delta,k)
, namely, for
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\varphi((p+1)\delta,k)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{n}(p+1,k),

(3.43)

where
\mathcal{U}_{n}(t,k)
is defined for
t\in[p,p+1]
such that

\mathcal{U}_{0}(t,k)=\varphi(p\delta,k),\quad\mathcal{U}_{n}(t,k)=\delta\sum_{n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3}=n-1}\int_{p}^{t}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{U}_{n_{1}}(t^{\prime}),\mathcal{U}_{n_{2}}(t^{\prime}),\mathcal{U}_{n_{3}}(t^{\prime}))(k)\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime},

(3.44)

where
\mathcal{K}
is defined as in (
COL
). It is easy to see that

\sup_{t\in[p,p+1]}\|\mathcal{U}_{n}(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{4\Lambda_{p+1},4\Lambda_{p+1}}}\leq C_{2}\cdot(C_{1}\delta)^{n}

(3.45)

for any
n\geq 0
.

We can now state the main estimates needed in the inductive step. They are listed in the following propositions:

Proposition 3.11
.

Given any
1\leq R\leq 10R_{p+1}
and
n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
, consider the expression

\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R},k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R}):=\sum_{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}(p+1,k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R}).

(3.46)

Here, for
R\geq 2
,
\mathcal{G}
runs over all canonical layered gardens in
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
of signature
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
and order
n
; for
R=1
we replace
\mathcal{G}
by a couple
\mathcal{Q}
which runs over all canonical layered couples in
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
of order
n
, where
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
are defined in Definition
3.10
. Note that when
n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
, the requirement (ii) in Definition
3.10
, namely each layered garden in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
that replaces the leaves in some subset
B_{i}
has order at most
N_{p}
, is redundant. Then we have that:

(1)

If
R\geq 2
, then for any choices of
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
, we have

|\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}|\leq\big(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle\big)^{-4\Lambda_{p+1}}\cdot L^{-(R-1)/4}\cdot\delta^{2\nu n}.

(3.47)

(2)

If
R=1
, the expression
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}(+,-,k_{1},k_{2})
is nonzero only when
k_{1}=k_{2}:=k
. In this case, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{p+1,2n}^{\mathrm{tr}}(+,-,k,k)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}_{n}(p+1,k)+\mathscr{R}_{p+1,2n}(k),

(3.48)

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{p+1,2n+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}(+,-,k,k)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{R}_{p+1,2n+1}(k),

where
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is defined in (
3.44
); moreover for any
n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
we have

\|\mathscr{R}_{p+1,n}(k)\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{4\Lambda_{p+1},4\Lambda_{p+1}}}\leq L^{-(4\theta_{p+1}+\theta_{p})/5}\cdot\delta^{2\nu n}.

(3.49)

Proposition 3.12
.

There exists an event
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, which has gauge and space translation symmetry, such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p+1})\leq e^{-4N_{p+1}}
. Moreover, assuming
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}
, we have the following estimates:

(1)

For the term
a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)
, and each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
t\in[p,p+1]
, we have

|(a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}})(t)|\leq\langle k\rangle^{-(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}\cdot e^{3N_{p+1}}.

(3.50)

(2)

For the term
\mathscr{L}_{0}
defined in (
3.39
), and each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
t\in[p,p+1]
, we have

|(\mathscr{L}_{0})_{k}(t)|\leq\langle k\rangle^{-(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}\cdot e^{-20N_{p+1}}.

(3.51)

(3)

The
\mathbb{R}
-linear operator
\mathscr{L}_{1}
defined in (
3.39
) satisfies that
1-\mathscr{L}_{1}
is invertible in the space
Z:=Z^{(5/4)\Lambda_{p+1}}([p,p+1])
(see (
3.15
) for definition), and that

\|(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})^{-1}\|_{Z\to Z}\leq e^{3N_{p+1}}.

(3.52)

Finally, assuming (
3.50
)–(
3.52
), the mapping

\textit{{b}}\mapsto(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})^{-1}(\mathscr{L}_{0}+\mathscr{L}_{2}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}})+\mathscr{L}_{3}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}}))

(3.53)

is a contraction mapping from the metric space
\{\textit{{b}}\in Z:\|\textit{{b}}\|_{Z}\leq e^{-10N_{p+1}}\}
to itself.

3.7.
Proof of Proposition
3.9
and Theorem
1.1

Assuming Propositions
3.11
–
3.12
, we can now prove Proposition
3.9
and Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.9
.

As pointed out in Section
3.6
, we only need to prove the statements (1)–(4) in Proposition
3.9
for
p+1
. Let the event
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}
be defined as in Proposition
3.12
. Then, assuming
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}
, by the contraction mapping property of (
3.53
), the equation (
3.37
) will have a unique solution
\textit{{b}}=b_{k}(t)
, which means that
\textit{{a}}=a_{k}(t)
as in (
3.36
) solves (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) on
[p,p+1]
. As
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, we get a unique solution
\textit{{a}}=a_{k}(t)
to (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) on
[0,p+1]
, and

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p+1})=\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p})-\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p+1})\geq 1-e^{-N_{p}}-e^{-4N_{p+1}}\geq 1-e^{-N_{p+1}},

which proves statement (1).

Next, for any
1\leq R\leq R_{p+1}
and any
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R},k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
, consider the cumulant (
3.40
). By gauge and space translation symmetry, we know it satisfies the support conditions required in (2) and (3) of Proposition
3.9
. As
a_{k}(t)=a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)+b_{k}(t)
, we shall first consider the contribution (
3.41
). Following the discussions in Section
3.6
, we expand each
a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}
into tree terms of order at most
N_{p+1}
(see (
3.36
)), apply Lemma
A.1
and replace each resulting cumulant term using the induction hypothesis; note that these cumulants have degree at most
2R^{\prime}\leq 10R_{p+1}N_{p+1}\leq R_{p}/10
(recall
R_{p}=N_{p+1}^{40d}
and
N_{p+1}=R_{p+1}^{40d}
). Using Definition
3.7
and the induction hypothesis, we get

(\ref{cmattr})=\sum_{\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}(p+1,k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})+\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(1)},

(3.54)

where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
is as in (
3.24
) in Definition
3.8
with input functions
F_{q}(k)=\varphi(\delta q,k)+\mathscr{R}_{q}(k)
for
0\leq q\leq p
, and
\mathcal{G}
runs over
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
as defined in Definition
3.10
; for
R=1
we should replace
\mathcal{G}
by a couple
\mathcal{Q}
which runs over
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
.

The error term
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(1)}
in (
3.54
) contains those contributions where at least one of the resulting cumulant terms is replaced by
\mathscr{E}_{p}
or
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}_{p}}
. By (
3.29
) and (
3.31
) for
p
, these terms decay like
e^{-N_{p}}
, where recall that
N_{p}=R_{p}^{40d}
; the other cumulant terms can be estimated using (
3.32
) (by summing over all
n\leq N_{p}
), and they are simply bounded by
L^{-(R-1)/6}\leq 1
. Since the whole expression involves the summation of at most
10R_{p+1}N_{p+1}
vector variables
k_{\mathfrak{l}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, and the negative powers
\langle k_{j}\rangle^{-\Lambda_{p}}
in (
3.29
), (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) allows us to restrict each
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
to a fixed unit ball using summability, we conclude that

|\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(1)}|\leq\big(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle\big)^{-\Lambda_{p}/4}\cdot L^{20R_{p+1}N_{p+1}}\cdot e^{-N_{p}}

pointwise, where the negative power of
\max(\langle k_{1}\rangle,\cdots,\langle k_{2R}\rangle)
also comes from the
\langle k_{\mathfrak{l}}\rangle^{-\Lambda_{p}}
powers above. This is clearly sufficient for the bounds (
3.29
) and (
3.31
) for
p+1
.

Next consider the sum of the
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
terms in (
3.54
), and assume
n(\mathcal{G})=n
. If
n>N_{p+1}^{4}
, then in the process of replacing each cumulant term using the induction hypothesis as described above, one of these cumulants must be replaced by a
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
term as in (
3.30
) for some
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
with
n(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\geq N_{p+1}^{2}
(because the number of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
involved at most
10R_{p+1}N_{p+1}
, while the sum of all these
n(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
is
n\geq N_{p+1}^{4}
). Since we are also summing in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
with the value of
n(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
fixed, using (
3.32
) for
p
, we can estimate the sum of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
by
e^{-N_{p+1}^{2}}
and the product of other cumulants simply by
1
as above, with the same decay factors
\langle k_{\mathfrak{l}}\rangle^{-\Lambda_{p}}
. This contribution, denoted by
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(2)}
, thensatisfies that

|\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(2)}|\leq\big(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle\big)^{-\Lambda_{p}/4}\cdot L^{20R_{p+1}N_{p+1}}\cdot e^{-N_{p+1}^{2}}

pointwise, which is also sufficient for (
3.29
) and (
3.31
) for
p+1
.

Moreover, if
N_{p+1}<n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
, denote this contribution by
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(3)}
, then we can use (
3.47
) and (
3.49
) from Proposition
3.11
, to bound it by

|\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(3)}|\leq\big(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle\big)^{-(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}\cdot e^{-10N_{p+1}},

which is again sufficient for (
3.29
) and (
3.31
) for
p+1
. This finally allows us to restrict to
n\leq N_{p+1}
, in which case both requirements (i) and (ii) in the definition of
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
in Definition
3.10
become redundant. Therefore, in (
3.54
) we can just sum over all
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(or
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
) that has order at most
N_{p+1}
, which matches the description of Proposition
3.9
for
p+1
. Apart from the difference between (
3.40
) and (
3.41
), this already proves statement (3) of Proposition
3.9
; moreover statement (2) follows from (
3.48
)–(
3.49
) of Proposition
3.11
together with (
3.43
) and (
3.45
) (to get real valued
\mathscr{R}_{p+1}
we simply take real part), and statement (4) follows from (
3.47
) of Proposition
3.11
.

Now it remains to consider the difference between (
3.40
) and (
3.41
), and prove that it belongs to an error term. Recall the definition of
\mathbb{K}_{p+1}
and
\mathbb{K}_{p}
in Proposition
3.9
and that
a_{k}(t)=a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)+b_{k}(t)
, we know that this difference consists of two terms, denoted by
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(4)}
and
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(5)}
. First
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(4)}
contains terms of

\mathbb{K}_{p+1}\big(c_{k_{1}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{1}},\cdots,c_{k_{2R}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{2R}}\big)

where each
c_{k}(t)
is either
a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)
or
b_{k}(t)
but with at least one factor being
b_{k}(t)
; note that, assuming the event
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}
, we always have
\|b_{k}(t)\|_{Z}\leq e^{-10N_{p+1}}
by Proposition
3.12
, where
Z:=Z^{(5/4)\Lambda_{p+1}([p,p+1])}
. Now there are at most
2^{2R}
choice for
c_{j}
which can be easily absorbed, and using (
3.9
) we can reduce the cumulants to the corresponding moments with a loss of at most
(C_{0}R_{p+1})!
. Then, by using Cauchy-Schwartz for each moment, we can control
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(4)}
by terms of form

|\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(4)}|\leq(C_{0}R_{p+1})!\cdot e^{-10N_{p+1}}\prod_{j\not\in A}\langle k_{j}\rangle^{-(5/4)\Lambda_{p+1}}\cdot\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{F}_{p}}\prod_{j\in A}|a_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)|^{2}\bigg)\bigg]^{1/2}

for some
A\subset\{1,\cdots,2R\}
. The last moment expression can again be rewritten, using (
3.10
), in terms of cumulants of
\{a_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)\}
and
\{\overline{a_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)}\}
at another loss of at most
(C_{0}R_{p+1})!
, while each of these cumulants has the form (
3.40
) (with
R
replaced by
2R
, which does not matter). By repeating all the above arguments, we can bound

\mathbb{E}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{F}_{p}}\prod_{j\in A}|a_{k_{j}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)|^{2}\bigg)\leq(C_{0}R_{p+1})!\cdot\prod_{j\in A}\langle k_{j}\rangle^{-3\Lambda_{p+1}},

(3.55)

and putting together we get the bound for
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(4)}
that is sufficient for (
3.29
) and (
3.31
) for
p+1
, noticing that
N_{p+1}=R_{p+1}^{40d}
. Finally, the term
\mathscr{E}_{p+1}^{(5)}
equals the difference

(\mathbb{K}_{p+1}-\mathbb{K}_{p})\big(a_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{1}},\cdots,a_{k_{2R}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p+1)^{\zeta_{2R}}\big).

By (
3.9
), we can write this as a sum of products of moments, with at least one moment taken assuming the event
\mathfrak{F}_{p}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}
. By using another Cauchy-Schwartz, we can bound each such moment by (
3.55
), with at least one extra factor
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p+1})^{1/2}
. This is again sufficient for (
3.29
) and (
3.31
) for
p+1
, since
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p+1})\leq e^{-4N_{p+1}}
by (
3.12
). This proves Proposition
3.9
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Let
t=T_{\mathrm{kin}}\cdot\tau
where
0<\tau\leq\tau_{*}
. Since
\tau_{*}=\mathfrak{D}\cdot\delta
, we may assume
\tau=t^{\prime}\delta
with
t^{\prime}\in[p,p+1]
for some
0\leq p<\mathfrak{D}
. First assume
t^{\prime}=p
, then Proposition
3.9
already implies

\mathbb{E}\big(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{F}_{p}}|\widehat{u}(t,k)|^{2}\big)=\varphi(\tau,k)+\mathcal{O}_{0}(L^{-\theta_{p}})

uniformly in
p
and
k
, so we only need to consider the expectation with
\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{F}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p}}
, where
\mathfrak{F}\supset\mathfrak{F}_{p}
is the event that (
NLS
) has a smooth solution on
[0,T]
(so
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F})\geq\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p})\geq\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{\mathfrak{D}})\geq 1-e^{-(\log L)^{20d}}
). By mass conservation for (
NLS
), we have

\mathbb{E}\big(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{F}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p}}|\widehat{u}(t,k)|^{2}\big)\leq\sum_{k}\mathbb{E}\big(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{F}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p}}|\widehat{u_{\mathrm{in}}}(k)|^{2}\big)\leq 2\sum_{k}\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}\backslash\mathfrak{F}_{p})^{1/2}\leq C_{0}L^{d}\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{F}_{p}^{c})\to 0

uniformly in
p
and
k
, which then proves (
1.2
).

In general, suppose
t^{\prime}\in[p,p+1]
. We use Proposition
3.9
for
p
, then, assuming
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}
, we repeat the arguments applied in the inductive step (which is used to prove Proposition
3.9
for
p+1
), but apply them to rescaled time
t^{\prime}
instead of
p+1
. It is clear from the proof that none of the ingredients is affected by this change, and the result follows in the same way. This proves Theorem
1.1
.
∎

The rest of this paper is devoted to the proof of Propositions
3.11
and
3.12
. Recall that we have fixed
p\in[0,\mathfrak{D}-1]
as in Section
3.6
; this value will be kept the same throughout the proof.

4.
Layered regular objects I: Combinatorics

4.1.
Regular objects and structure theorem

First recall the definition and basic properties of
regular couples
and
regular trees
in
[
19
]
, which are of fundamental importance in both
[
19
]
and the current paper.

Definition 4.1

(Regular couples and regular trees
[
19
]
)
.

Define a
(1,1)
-mini-couple
to be a couple of order
2
formed by two trees of order
1
with no siblings paired. It has two possibilities indicated by codes
00
and
01
, see Figure
6
. Define a
mini tree
to be a paired tree of order
2
, again with no siblings paired. It has six possibilities indicated by codes
10,11,20,21,30,31
, see Figure
7
.

For any couple
\mathcal{Q}
we can define two operations: operation A where a leaf pair is replaced by a
(1,1)
-mini-couple, and operation B where a node is replaced by a mini tree, see Figure
8
. Then, we define a couple
\mathcal{Q}
to be
regular
if it can be formed, starting from the trivial couple, by operations A and B. We also define a paired tree
\mathcal{T}
to be a
regular tree
, if
\mathcal{T}
forms a regular couple with the trivial tree
\bullet
. Clearly the order of any regular couple and regular tree must be even.

Figure 6.
A
(1,1)
-mini-couple as in Definition
4.1
; the codes
00
and
01
indicate the two different possibilities.

Figure 7.
A mini-tree as in Definition
4.1
; the codes
10,11,20,21,30,31
indicate the six different possibilities.

Figure 8.
Operations
A
and
B
as in Definition
4.1
; there are different possibilities depending on the structure of the
(1,1)
-mini-couple and mini-tree, see Figures
6
and
7
. The ends
X
and
Y
here represent the rest of the couple, which is unaffected by the operation.

We also need the notion of
sub-couples
and
sub-paired-trees
, which are intuitively clear, and are made precise in the following definition.

Definition 4.2

(Embedded objects)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a garden, for nodes
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, if the leaves in the two trees rooted at
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are all paired to each other, then these two trees form a couple
\mathcal{Q}
, called a
sub-couple
of
\mathcal{G}
; we also say
\mathcal{Q}
is
embedded in

\mathcal{G}
. Similarly, if
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{n}
, and all the leaves that are descendants of
\mathfrak{n}
but not descendants of
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are paired to each other, then the tree rooted at
\mathfrak{n}
becomes a paired tree
\mathcal{T}
after replacing the subtree rooted at
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
by a single (lone) leaf. In this case we also call
\mathcal{T}
a
sub-paired-tree
and say
\mathcal{T}
is
embedded in

\mathcal{G}
. Also, starting from a garden
\mathcal{G}
, when we say we “replace” a leaf pair by a couple, or a node by a paired tree (such as in Definition
4.1
), then this couple or paired tree will be embedded in the resulting new garden
\mathcal{G}_{1}
. Conversely, we may “collapse” an embedded couple or paired tree into a leaf pair or a node.

Before introducing the notion of layers, we first recall the definition of regular chains in
[
19
]
, as well as two structure theorems: first one for regular couples and regular trees, and second one for general gardens. These results are proved in
[
19
]
and
[
20
]
.

Definition 4.3

(Regular chains
[
19
]
)
.

Given
m\geq 0
, consider a partition
\mathcal{P}
of
\{1,\cdots,2m\}
into
m
pairwise disjoint two-element subsets (or pairs). We say this partition
\mathcal{P}
is
legal
if there do not exist
a<b<c<d
such that
\{a,c\}\in\mathcal{P}
and
\{b,d\}\in\mathcal{P}
; below we will always assume
a<b
and order these pairs
\{a,b\}
in the increasing order of
a
. Now, for any legal partition
\mathcal{P}
, we define a
regular chain
to be a paired tree of
2m
branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 2m)
, such that
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
is a child of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq 2m-1
, and some specified child of
\mathfrak{n}_{2m}
is the lone leaf. Moreover, for any
\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{P}
, the two remaining children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}_{a}
are paired with the two remaining children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}_{b}
with no siblings paired. Define also a
dominant chain
to be a regular chain where
\mathcal{P}=\{\{1,2\},\cdots,\{2m-1,2m\}\}
.

Proposition 4.4

(Structure theorem for regular objects)
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a nontrivial regular couple. Then exactly one of the two cases happen:

(1)

There exist unique regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
such that
\mathcal{Q}
is obtained from the
(1,1)
-mini-couple
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
(see Figure
6
), by replacing the red, green and blue leaf pair with
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{3}
respectively. In this case we say
\mathcal{Q}
is a
type 1
regular couple.

(2)

There exists a unique nontrivial regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
which is formed by pairing the two lone leaves of two regular chains
\mathcal{X}^{\epsilon}
of sign
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
and order
2m_{\epsilon}
, and unique regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
where
1\leq j\leq m_{\epsilon}
and
\iota\in\{1,2\}
, and a unique type 1 or trivial regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
, such that
\mathcal{Q}
is obtained from
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
by the following operations. First we replace the lone leaf pair with
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
. Next, let the branching nodes of
\mathcal{X}^{\epsilon}
be
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}\,(1\leq a\leq 2m_{\epsilon})
with legal partition
\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
as in Definition
4.3
; for
1\leq j\leq m_{\epsilon}
, let the
j
-th pair in
\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
(in the ordering of Definition
4.3
) be
\{a,b\}
, then the nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{\epsilon}
and all their children can be rearranged into a mini-tree as in Figure
7
(by setting
\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{\epsilon}
as the child of
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}
in this new mini-tree). Then, we replace the red leaf pair in this mini-tree with
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,1}
, and replace the green leaf pair in this mini-tree with
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,2}
. In this case we say
\mathcal{Q}
is a
type 2
regular couple. See Figure
9
.

Similarly, let
\mathcal{T}
be a nontrivial regular tree, then there exists a unique nontrivial regular chain
\mathcal{X}_{0}
of order
2m_{0}
, and unique regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota}
where
1\leq j\leq m_{0}
and
\iota\in\{1,2\}
, such that
\mathcal{T}
is obtained from
\mathcal{X}_{0}
by the following operations. Let the branching nodes of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
be
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{0}\,(1\leq a\leq 2m_{0})
with legal partition
\mathcal{P}^{0}
; for
1\leq j\leq m_{0}
, let the
j
-th pair in
\mathcal{P}^{0}
be
\{a,b\}
, then the nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{0}
,
\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{0}
and all their children can be rearranged into a mini-tree as in Figure
7
. Then, we replace the red leaf pair in this mini-tree with
\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,1}
, and replace the green leaf pair in this mini-tree with
\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,2}
.

We may also inductively define the notion of
dominant couples
, which is a subclass of regular couples, as follows: a regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
is dominant, if and only if it is trivial, or it has type 1 and the corresponding regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
are all dominant, or it has type 2, and the regular chain
\mathcal{X}^{\epsilon}
is dominant for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
, and the regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
are all dominant. Similarly, we define a regular tree
\mathcal{T}
is dominant, if and only if the regular chain
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is dominant, and the corresponding regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota}
are all dominant.

Finally, given any integer
m\geq 0
, the number of regular couples of order
2m
is at most
C_{0}^{m}
, and the same holds for regular trees (see notations for
C_{j}
in Section
3.5
).

Figure 9
.
A type 2 regular (in fact dominant) couple formed by two regular chains as in Proposition
4.4
, with the corresponding notations
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
. The regular tree case is similar.

Proof.

See Propositions 4.5, 4.7, 4.8 and Corollary 4.9 of
[
19
]
.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Define a garden
\mathcal{G}
to be
prime
if it does not contain any embedded
(1,1)
-mini-couple or mini-tree. Then, for any garden
\mathcal{G}
there is a unique prime garden
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, called the
skeleton
of
\mathcal{G}
, such that
\mathcal{G}
is obtained from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
by replacing each leaf pair with a regular couple and each branching node with a regular tree. The choices of these regular couples and regular trees are also unique. Moreover, given any
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, the number of gardens
\mathcal{G}
that has order m, width 2R and skeleton
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
is
\leq C_{0}^{m+R}
.

Proof.

See Propositions 4.4, 4.5 and 4.6 of
[
20
]
.
∎

Finally, recall from
[
19
]
that branching nodes in any regular couple and regular tree are also naturally divided into pairs, which we refer to as
links
for distinction:

Proposition 4.6

(Links of branching nodes)
.

For any regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
and regular tree
\mathcal{T}
, there is a unique way to divide all the
branching nodes
into two-element subsets called
links
. This is defined inductively as follows:

For any regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
(or any regular tree
\mathcal{T}
), let the relevant notions be defined as in Proposition
4.4
. Then, we link the branching nodes of the couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
, or
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
, or
\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota}
depending on different situations, by the induction hypothesis. Moreover, if
\mathcal{Q}
has type 1, then we link the two roots of the two trees of
\mathcal{Q}
; if
\mathcal{Q}
has type 2, then we link
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}
with
\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{\epsilon}
for any pair
\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
, and for regular tree
\mathcal{T}
we link
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{0}
with
\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{0}
for any pair
\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{P}^{0}
. For later use, we may also fix a set
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}\subset\mathcal{N}
by inductively selecting one branching node from each link: in each inductive step, for type 1 regular couples we select the root with sign
+
into
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
, and for type 2 regular couples or regular trees we select
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}
or
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{0}
into
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
for each pair
\{a,b\}
with
a<b
.

Then, for any decoration
\mathscr{I}
of
\mathcal{Q}
(or
\mathscr{D}
of
\mathcal{T}
) as in Definition
3.5
, we must have
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}=-\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
for any linked branching nodes
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
, with
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}
as in Definition
3.3
and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
as in (
3.23
). Moreover, let
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
be the children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
as in Definition
3.5
, and define
(x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}})=(k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}},k_{\mathfrak{n}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}})
and similarly for
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, then we have
\{x_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}},y_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\}=\{\zeta_{1}x_{\mathfrak{n}},\zeta_{2}y_{\mathfrak{n}}\}
for some choices of
\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}
.

Proof.

See Proposition 4.3 of
[
19
]
. By Propositions 4.7 and 4.8 of
[
19
]
, it is easy to see that the definition of links stated in the proof of Proposition 4.3 of
[
19
]
coincides with the definition here.
∎

4.2.
Layered regular objects

Now we study the structures of regular couples and regular trees with layering. Note that the definition of sub-couples and sub-paired-trees in Definition
4.2
can obviously be extended to the corresponding layered objects, so we can talk about a layered couple or layered paired tree being embedded in some layered garden.

In fact, throughout the proof we will mainly be looking at canonical layered gardens as in Definition
3.7
; therefore the regular couples and regular trees studied will also appear as sub-couples and sub-paired-trees of canonical gardens. We now introduce the following definition, which is of key importance in the study of layered regular objects:

Definition 4.7

(Coherent regular objects)
.

We say a layered regular couple or regular tree is
coherent
, if for any link of branching nodes
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
as in Definition
4.6
we have
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
. In general we define the
incoherency index
to be the number of links of branching nodes
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
.

Our goal is to classify all coherent layered regular couples and regular trees, as well as those with fixed incoherency index,
that are embedded in canonical layered gardens
. For this, we first need to state a convenient equivalent condition for a layered garden to be canonical.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an irreducible proper layered garden. For any
p\geq 0
, we have
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
if and only if
\mathcal{G}
satisfies the following two conditions: (i) all nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}
satisfy
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\leq p
; (ii) if two nodes
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are such that all leaves of the trees rooted at
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are completely paired, then we must have
\max(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\geq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}},\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})
, where
\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}
and
(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}
are the parent nodes of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, as in Definition
3.3
; if
\mathfrak{n}
or
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is a root then the corresponding
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
or
\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}}
is replaced by
p
. Similarly, we have that a couple
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
if and only if it satisfies both (i) and (ii) above.

Proof.

Consider first the garden case. Note that (i) is obvious by definition (as in Definition
3.7
we put the branching nodes and paired leaves of the trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}
in layer
p
, and all other nodes are in layer
<p
), we first prove (ii) by induction. If
p=0
then
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}=0
for all nodes
\mathfrak{n}
, so (ii) is trivial. Suppose (ii) holds for
p-1
, now let us consider
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
and two nodes
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
as in (ii). Consider the trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}
in the recursive construction process in Definition
3.7
(see for example Figure
5
); all their branching nodes and leaf pairs are in layer
p
, while all their unpaired leaves (which may be leaves or branching nodes in the garden
\mathcal{G}
) are in layer
\leq p-1
. Moreover, these nodes are divided into groups of at least four, such that the layered trees rooted at all nodes in each individual group form a canonical layered garden in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
. We may assume
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}<p
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}<p
or otherwise (ii) already holds, so in particular both
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
belong to one of these
\mathscr{G}_{p}
gardens; by the complete pairing assumption for
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, we know they must belong to the same
\mathscr{G}_{p}
garden, say
\mathcal{G}_{1}
.

Next, note that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=p
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}
is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
(same for
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
). Consider three cases: if
\max(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}},\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})<p
, then both
\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}
and
(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}
are nodes in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
, so the result in (ii) follows from induction hypothesis. If
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=p
and
\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}}<p
, then
\mathfrak{n}
is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}
is a node in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
, so the induction hypothesis gives that

\max(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\geq\min(p-1,\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})=\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}}=\min(p,\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})=\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}},\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}}),

thus (ii) is still true. If
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}}=p
, then
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are the roots of two trees in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
, which contradicts the assumption that
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is an irreducible proper garden. Note also that if
\mathfrak{n}
(or
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
) is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}
, then the proof goes in the same way as in the case
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=p
(or
\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}}=p
). This completes the proof of (ii) in the garden case; the couple case is completely analogous.

Finally, we prove that (i) and (ii) imply
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(again the couple case follows from the same proof). If
p=0
the result is true, because any irreducible proper garden with all nodes in layer
0
belongs to
\mathscr{G}_{1}
. Suppose the result holds for
p-1
, then consider any irreducible proper layered garden
\mathcal{G}
satisfying (i) and (ii) for
p
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be the set of nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}<p
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=p
(including when
\mathfrak{n}
is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}
), then none of these
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}
can be ancestor or descendant of each other, and the trees roots at these nodes
\mathfrak{n}
form a garden (leaves in these garden are precisely those leaves in layer
\leq p-1
, which are paired to each other). Moreover all nodes in this garden are in layer
\leq p-1
, and by (ii) and induction hypothesis, we see that this garden can be decomposed into irreducible proper layered gardens (which cannot be couples) that belong to
\mathscr{G}_{p}
. Then, we consider the trees formed by replacing the tree rooted at each node
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}
with a single leaf node
\mathfrak{n}
, and use them as the trees
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
in Definition
3.7
. The branching nodes and leaf pairs in these
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}
trees then are all in layer
p
; moreover, to form
\mathcal{G}
, the unpaired leaves in these trees are divided into groups of at least four, and each group is replaced by a canonical garden in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
. In addition, the requirement in Definition
3.7
that no union of any of such leaf pairs and leaf groups equals the set of leaves of
\mathcal{T}_{j}^{p}\,(j\in A)
for any proper subset
A\subset\{1,\cdots,2R\}
, follows from the irreducibility of
\mathcal{G}
. Therefore, we have verified all conditions in Definition
3.7
and hence
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
. This completes the proof.
∎

We can now state the two main results of this section, namely the classification of coherent and near-coherent layered regular couples in canonical layered gardens.

Proposition 4.9

(Structure theorem for coherent regular objects)
.

Suppose a regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
is embedded in a
canonical
layered garden
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
, with roots located at nodes
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{-}
of
\mathcal{G}
. Assume that (i) this layering makes
\mathcal{Q}
a
coherent
layered regular couple, and (ii) some
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
are fixed such that
\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{+})^{\mathrm{pr}}}=q
and
\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{-})^{\mathrm{pr}}}=q^{\prime}
; if
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
or
\mathfrak{n}^{-}
is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}
then the corresponding
q
or
q^{\prime}
is replaced by
p
. Assume
q\geq q^{\prime}
(the case
q<q^{\prime}
follows by symmetry) and define the relative notions as in Proposition
4.4
, then the followings hold. If
\mathcal{Q}
has type 1 or trivial, then all nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
must be in layer
q^{\prime}
. If
\mathcal{Q}
has type 2, then all nodes in the couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,-,\iota}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{-})
and the couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
, as well as the nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{-}\,(1\leq a\leq 2m_{-})
, must be in layer
q^{\prime}
. We also have
q\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{+}}\geq\cdots\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{2m_{+}}^{+}}\geq q^{\prime}
; for the
j
-th pair
\{a,b\}
in
\mathcal{P}^{+}
(where
1\leq j\leq m_{+}
), we have
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{+}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{+}}
and all nodes in the couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,+,\iota}
must be in this layer. See Figure
10
.

Similarly, suppose a regular tree
\mathcal{T}
is embedded in a
canonical
layered garden
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
, with the roots located at node
\mathfrak{n}
and lone leaf located at node
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{G}
. Assume that (i) this layering makes
\mathcal{T}
a
coherent
layered regular tree, and (ii) some
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
are fixed such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=q\geq q^{\prime}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
; if
\mathfrak{n}
is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}
then
q
is replaced by
p
. Define the relative notions as in Proposition
4.4
, then the followings hold. We have
q\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{0}}\geq\cdots\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{2m_{0}}^{0}}\geq q^{\prime}
; for the
j
-th pair
\{a,b\}
in
\mathcal{P}^{0}
(where
1\leq j\leq m_{0}
), we have
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{0}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{0}}
and all the nodes in the couple
\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota}
must be in this layer.

In addition, if we fix
(q,q^{\prime})
and the structure and layering of rest of the garden, but replace the regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
or regular tree
\mathcal{T}
by any
other
regular couple or regular tree with layering satisfying all the above assumptions, then the resulting garden also belong to
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
.
9
9

9

As a result, when we talk about coherent regular objects in the context of a larger garden, they actually only rely on the values
(q,q^{\prime})
and not on the rest structure of the garden.
Finally, all the above results hold if the garden
\mathcal{G}
is replaced by a couple, and
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
is replaced by
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
.

Figure 10
.
A layering of the regular couple in Figure
9
that makes it coherent, as in Proposition
4.9
. Here
r_{j}^{+}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{+}}
and
q\geq r_{1}^{+}\geq r_{2}^{+}\geq q^{\prime}
. The regular tree case is similar.

Proof.

(1) We prove by induction. If
\mathcal{Q}
is the trivial couple, which is a leaf pair where the two leaves are in the same layer by Definition
3.6
, then these two leaves must be in layer
q^{\prime}
by Definition
3.6
and Proposition
4.8
. Suppose the result holds for any smaller couple
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
, then in particular, when
q=q^{\prime}
, all nodes in
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
must be in layer
q
. Now consider any
\mathcal{Q}
, and let the the relative notions be defined as in Proposition
4.4
.

If
\mathcal{Q}
has type 1, then by Proposition
4.6
and Definition
4.7
, the two roots of the two trees of
\mathcal{Q}
must be in the same layer, and this layer again must be
q^{\prime}
due to Proposition
4.8
. Then, we apply the induction hypothesis to the regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
, to see that all nodes of each
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
must be in layer
q^{\prime}
. Therefore we obtain that all nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
are in layer
q^{\prime}
.

Now Suppose
\mathcal{Q}
has type 2. By Proposition
4.6
and Definition
4.7
, we know that for
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{\epsilon}}
for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
and each pair
\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
. Applying the induction hypothesis to
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
where
1\leq j\leq m_{\epsilon}
is such that
\{a,b\}
is the
j
-th pair in
\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
, we know that all the nodes in
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
are in layer
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}}
.

Next, consider the lone leaves of
\mathcal{X}^{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
, which we denote by
\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\epsilon}
. They are also the roots of trees of the type 1 or trivial couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
, so they must be in the same layer (say
q_{*}
) due to Proposition
4.6
and Definition
4.7
; again by induction hypothesis, all nodes in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
are also in layer
q_{*}
. Clearly
q_{*}\leq q^{\prime}
; if
q_{*}<q^{\prime}
(and by assumption
q^{\prime}\leq q
), then for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
, we may choose the smallest
a=a(\epsilon)\in\{1,\cdots,2m_{\epsilon}+1\}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a(\epsilon)}^{\epsilon}}=q_{*}
(we understand that
\mathfrak{n}_{2m_{\epsilon}+1}^{\epsilon}:=\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\epsilon}
). Using that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{\epsilon}}
for each pair
\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
, it is easy to see that the branching nodes in the set
\{\mathfrak{n}_{a^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}:a(\epsilon)\leq a\leq 2m_{\epsilon}\}
are linked to each other, which means that the leaves of the two trees rooted at
\mathfrak{n}_{a(\epsilon)}^{\epsilon}\,(\epsilon\in\{\pm\})
are completely paired, but this contradicts Proposition
4.8
.

Now we know that
q_{*}=q^{\prime}
, so in particular all nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{-}\,(1\leq a\leq 2m_{-})
and all nodes in the couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,-,\iota}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{-},\iota\in\{1,2\})
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
are in layer
q_{*}
. The rest of the claims regarding
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{+}}
and layers of nodes in
\mathcal{Q}_{j,+,\iota}
is already obtained above, and clearly we have
q\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{+}}\geq\cdots\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{2m_{+}}^{+}}\geq q^{\prime}
because
\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{+})^{\mathrm{pr}}}=q
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{+}}=q_{*}=q^{\prime}
.

The case of regular tree
\mathcal{T}
is proved similarly. With the regular chain
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and relevant notions fixed as in Proposition
4.4
, we have that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{0}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{0}}
and this equals the layer of all nodes in the couple
\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota}
, in the same way as above. Clearly also
q\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}^{0}}\geq\cdots\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{2m_{0}}^{0}}\geq q^{\prime}
because
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=q
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}=q^{\prime}
. The whole proof works for couples in
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
instead of gardens in
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
without any change, and if
\mathfrak{n}^{+}
or
\mathfrak{n}
is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}
then we simply replace
\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{n}^{+})^{\mathrm{pr}}}
or
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
by
p
.

(2) To prove the last statement about replacing
\mathcal{Q}
or
\mathcal{T}
by any other regular couple or regular tree of the same structure, we first show that attaching a layered regular couple to a leaf pair or attaching a layered regular tree to a node
does not change
the canonicity of a layered garden, provided that all nodes in the regular couple or regular tree (other than the lone leaf) are in
the same
layer.

More precisely, let
\mathcal{G}
be a layered garden (the case of layered couple being exactly the same), and pick a leaf pair
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}=q
, then we may form a new layered garden
\mathcal{G}_{1}
by replacing this leaf pair with a layered couple
\mathcal{Q}
(with roots of the two trees at
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
) such that all nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
are in layer
q
. Similarly, picking any node
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathcal{G}
(which can be leaf or branching node) such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}=q^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\mathrm{pr}}}=q\geq q^{\prime}
(with
q
replaced by
p
if
\mathfrak{n}
is the root of a tree in
\mathcal{G}
), we may form a new layered garden
\mathcal{G}_{2}
by replacing
\mathfrak{n}
with a paired tree
\mathcal{T}
, such that the lone leaf of
\mathcal{T}
is still in layer
q^{\prime}
, while all other nodes in
\mathcal{T}
are in layer
r
for some
q\geq r\geq q^{\prime}
. Then, we have that

\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{1}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{2}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}.

This is intuitively clear, and can be verified using the equivalent condition for canonicity introduced in Proposition
4.8
. Consider for example
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
(the case of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is similar and easier); by Proposition
4.8
, to check if they are canonical, we only need to check for instances where

\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}},\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})>\max(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}),

(4.1)

while the leaves of the two trees rooted at
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
are completely paired. By our choice, in any such instance, neither
\mathfrak{m}
nor
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
can be any node in
\mathcal{T}
other than the root (denoted by
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{rt}}
here) and the lone leaf (denoted by
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{lo}}
here); if neither
\mathfrak{m}
nor
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
equals
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{rt}}
or
\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{lo}}
then the form of (
4.1
) does not change from
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathcal{G}_{2}
, so equivalence holds in this case. Now consider the cases where (say)
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{rt}}
or
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{lo}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
, and
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathcal{G}
, then we can verify the elementary fact that

\min(q,\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})>\max(q^{\prime},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}})\Leftrightarrow[\min(q,\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})>\max(r,\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}})]\vee[\min(r,\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})>\max(q^{\prime},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}})],

therefore an instance (
4.1
) happens in
\mathcal{G}
if and only if an instance (
4.1
) happens in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
.

Now let
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
be the garden obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by replacing the regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
with one leaf pair in layer
q^{\prime}
