, or by replacing the regular tree
\mathcal{T}
with one single node in layer
q^{\prime}
. By the structure of the layered regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
or layered regular tree
\mathcal{T}
described above, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{G}
can be formed from
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
by repeatedly applying the
\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{2}
transformations described above, so we have that

\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}\Leftrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}.

Now if
\mathcal{Q}
or
\mathcal{T}
is replaced by any other regular couple or regular tree with layering satisfying all the above assumptions, to form a new garden
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, then we still have

\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}\Leftrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Consider the same setting as in Proposition
4.9
, and with
(q,q^{\prime})
fixed, but instead of assuming
\mathcal{Q}
(or
\mathcal{T}
) is coherent, we assume it has
incoherency index

g
with
0\leq g\leq m
, where
2m
is the order of
\mathcal{Q}
(or
\mathcal{T}
). Then, for all possible choices of layerings given by
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(or
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
), the number of possible layerings it induces on
\mathcal{Q}
(or
\mathcal{T}
) does not exceed
C_{2}^{g}C_{0}^{m+|q-q^{\prime}|}
.

Proof.

We will consider the case of regular couples
\mathcal{Q}
, while the case of regular trees
\mathcal{T}
follows the same way. By Proposition
4.4
, we may fix the structure (without layering) of
\mathcal{Q}
at a cost of
C_{0}^{m}
; then we may also fix the exact positions of the
g
links of branching nodes
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
, at a cost of
\binom{m}{g}\leq 2^{m}
. Therefore, we will assume below that
\mathcal{Q}
is fixed as a regular couple, and the places where
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
happens are also fixed.

We proceed by induction. If
\mathcal{Q}
is trivial then
m=g=0
, and both leaves have to be in layer
q^{\prime}
by Proposition
4.8
, so the number of choices of layerings is
1
. Suppose this number of choices is bounded by
C_{2}^{g}C_{0}^{m+|q-q^{\prime}|}
for any smaller couple
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
, then consider a couple
\mathcal{Q}
. If
\mathcal{Q}
has type 1, then it is formed by two roots
\mathfrak{n}_{+},\mathfrak{n}_{-}
and three couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
. Let the
m
and
g
values for
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
be
m_{j}
and
g_{j}
, then we have
m=m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}+1
and
g=g_{1}+g_{2}+g_{3}+\xi
, where
\xi
equals
0
or
1
depending on whether
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{+}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{-}}
or not. Now, if
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{+}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{-}}
, then this value must be
q^{\prime}
due to Proposition
4.8
, and
\xi=0
; by induction hypothesis, the number of choices of layerings for
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
is at most
C_{2}^{g_{j}}C_{0}^{m_{j}}
, noticing also
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{+}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{-}}
. Putting together we get that the number of choices of layerings for
\mathcal{Q}
is at most

C_{2}^{g_{1}+g_{2}+g_{3}}\cdot C_{0}^{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}}\leq C_{2}^{g}C_{0}^{m}.

If instead
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{+}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{-}}
, then the number of choices for them is at most
(\mathfrak{D}+1)^{2}
since we always have
0\leq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\leq p\leq\mathfrak{D}
, and moreover
\xi=1
. By induction hypothesis for each
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
, we get that the number of choices of layerings for
\mathcal{Q}
is at most

(\mathfrak{D}+1)^{2}\cdot C_{2}^{g_{1}+g_{2}+g_{3}}C_{0}^{m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}}\cdot C_{0}^{3\mathfrak{D}}\leq C_{2}^{g}C_{0}^{m}

using that
g=g_{1}+g_{2}+g_{3}+1
, provided that
C_{2}\geq(\mathfrak{D}+1)^{2}C_{0}^{3\mathfrak{D}}
.

Now assume
\mathcal{Q}
has type 2, let the relevant notions be defined as in Proposition
4.4
. Let the
m
and
g
values for
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
be
m_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
and
g_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
etc., and similarly for
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
is either trivial or has type 1; if it has type 1, then let
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp},j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
be defined from
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
as in Proposition
4.4
(a), let
m_{\mathrm{lp},j}
and
g_{\mathrm{lp},j}
be corresponding
m
and
g
values. We then have

m=\sum_{j,\epsilon,\iota}m_{j,\epsilon,\iota}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}m_{\mathrm{lp},j}+m_{+}+m_{-}+\mathbf{1}_{m_{\mathrm{lp}}>0};\quad g=\sum_{j,\epsilon,\iota}g_{j,\epsilon,\iota}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}g_{\mathrm{lp},j}+\xi_{+}+\xi_{-}+\eta,

where for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
,
\xi_{\epsilon}
is defined to be the number of pairs
\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{\epsilon}}
, and
\eta
equals
0
or
1
depending on whether
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{+}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{-}}
or not, where
\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\epsilon}
are the lone leaves (i.e. roots of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
). When
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
is trivial we understand that the
m_{\mathrm{lp},j}
and
g_{\mathrm{lp},j}
terms are absent (same in the proof below), in which case we also have
\eta=0
. Next we classify the value of
g_{0}:=\xi_{+}+\xi_{-}+\eta
.

If
g_{0}=0
, then we must have
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{+}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{-}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{b}^{\epsilon}}
for each pair
\{a,b\}\in\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
. This puts us in the same situation as in part (1) of the proof of Proposition
4.9
, so the same proof there leads to the conclusion that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{+}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{-}}=q^{\prime}
(assuming say
q\geq q^{\prime}
). Therefore
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{-}}=q^{\prime}
for all
1\leq a\leq 2m_{-}
just as in Proposition
4.9
, and
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{+}}:1\leq a\leq 2m_{+})
forms a decreasing sequence in
[q^{\prime},q]
, while an elementary combinatorial argument shows that the number of such sequences is at most
2^{q-q^{\prime}+2m_{+}}
. Now as long as
C_{0}\geq 4
, by using the induction hypothesis for each of the couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp},j}
, we get that the number of choices of layerings for
\mathcal{Q}
is at most

\prod_{j,\epsilon,\iota}C_{2}^{g_{j,\epsilon,\iota}}C_{0}^{m_{j,\epsilon,\iota}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{3}C_{2}^{g_{\mathrm{lp},j}}C_{0}^{m_{\mathrm{lp},j}}\cdot 2^{q-q^{\prime}+2m_{+}}\leq C_{2}^{g}C_{0}^{m+|q-q^{\prime}|}.

Now assume
g_{0}\geq 1
. In this case
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\epsilon}}
may not be
q^{\prime}
, but
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{\epsilon}}:1\leq a\leq 2m_{\epsilon})
for
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
are still two decreasing sequences in
[0,\mathfrak{D}]
, so they have at most
2^{2\mathfrak{D}+2m_{+}+2m_{-}}
choices. The
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{+}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{-}})
has at most
(\mathfrak{D}+1)^{2}
choices, and using again the the induction hypothesis for each of the couples
\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp},j}
, we get that the number of choices of layerings for
\mathcal{Q}
is at most

\prod_{j,\epsilon,\iota}C_{2}^{g_{j,\epsilon,\iota}}C_{0}^{m_{j,\epsilon,\iota}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{3}C_{2}^{g_{\mathrm{lp},j}}C_{0}^{m_{\mathrm{lp},j}}\cdot 2^{2\mathfrak{D}+2m_{+}+2m_{-}}\cdot(\mathfrak{D}+1)^{2}C_{0}^{2g_{0}\mathfrak{D}+3\mathfrak{D}}\leq C_{2}^{g}C_{0}^{m},

as long as
C_{0}\geq 4
, and
C_{2}\geq(4C_{0})^{5\mathfrak{D}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.
Layered regular objects II: Decay for non-coherent objects

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
for layered regular couples
\mathcal{Q}
and regular trees
\mathcal{T}
in Definition
3.8
. The goal of this section and Section
6
is to analyze these expressions; in this section we consider the non-coherent case, and in Section
6
we consider the coherent case.

Below and in Section
6
, we fix a layered regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
and a layered regular tree
\mathcal{T}
of order
2n
and incoherency index
0\leq g\leq n
(except in Proposition
6.3
where the sum of the two orders is
2n
), where
n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
; if
g=0
we shall restrict to those coherent objects described in Proposition
4.9
. Consider the expression
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
as in Definition
3.8
, with input functions
F_{r}(k)=\varphi(\delta r,k)+\mathscr{R}_{r}(k)
, as in Proposition
3.9
and Section
3.6
. Assume
\mathscr{R}_{r}(k)
is
defined for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
real valued
, and satisfies the estimate (
3.29
) for
0\leq r\leq p
, but with
\Lambda_{p}
replaced by
40d
. Moreover, let
q
and
q^{\prime}
be as in Definition
3.8
(and in Proposition
4.9
if
g=0
), and let
\mathcal{B}=[q,q+1]\times[q^{\prime},q^{\prime}+1]
for
\mathcal{Q}
, and let
\mathcal{B}=([q,q+1]\times[q^{\prime},q^{\prime}+1])\cap\{t>s\}
for
\mathcal{T}
.

5.1.
The non-coherent estimates

We start by proving the main estimates for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
for non-coherent objects
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
.

Proposition 5.1
.

If
g\geq 1
, then we have

\|\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,30d}(\mathcal{B})}+\|\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,0}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\delta)^{n}\cdot L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})\cdot g}.

(5.1)

The proof of Proposition
5.1
follows the strategy of Section 7 of
[
22
]
, which is much simpler than the arguments in
[
19
]
. We need the following two lemmas from
[
22
]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

We use the notation
e(z)=e^{2\pi iz}
(same for Lemma
5.3
below), and recall the cutoff function
\chi_{0}
defined in Section
3.3.3
. Then for any
|s|\leq L
, and uniformly in
(a,b,\xi,\xi^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4}
, we have

\sum_{(g,h)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2d}}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\chi_{0}(x-a)\chi_{0}(y-b)\cdot e[\langle Lg+\xi,x\rangle+\langle Lh+\xi^{\prime},y\rangle+s\langle x,y\rangle]\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\bigg|\lesssim_{0}\langle s\rangle^{-d}.

(5.2)

Proof.

The proof is an easier version of Lemma 6.5 of
[
22
]
(with
\Phi
replaced by
1
in Lemma 6.5 of
[
22
]
). For fixed
(a,b,\xi,\xi^{\prime})
, consider the two cases where (i)
|Lg+\xi+sb|\geq 10dL
or
|Lh+\xi^{\prime}+sa|\geq 10dL
, and (ii)
|Lg+\xi+sb|\leq 10dL
and
|Lh+\xi^{\prime}+sa|\leq 10dL
. In case (i), define

u:=x-a,\quad v:=y-b;\quad a^{\prime}:=a+\frac{Lh+\xi^{\prime}}{s},\quad b^{\prime}:=b+\frac{Lg+\xi}{s},

where we may assume
s\neq 0
, then up to a unimodular factor, the integral in
(x,y)
reduces to

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\chi_{0}(u)\chi_{0}(v)\cdot e(s\langle u+a^{\prime},v+b^{\prime}\rangle)\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

(5.3)

Note that
|s|\leq L
, and
|u|,|v|\leq d
due to the cutoff, and
\max(|sa^{\prime}|,|sb^{\prime}|)\geq 10dL
due to assumption (i). Thus we can integrate by parts in
u
(if
|sb^{\prime}|\geq 10dL
) or
v
(if
|sa^{\prime}|\geq 10dL
) a total of
10d
times to bound the
(x,y)
integral by

L^{-3d}(|sa+Lh+\xi^{\prime}|^{-3d}+|sb+Lg+\xi|^{-3d}),

which satisfies (
5.2
) upon summation in
(g,h)
, using also
|s|\leq L
.

We are left with case (ii). With fixed
(a,b,\xi,\xi^{\prime})
and
s
, this leaves at most
(20d+1)^{2d}\leq C_{0}
choices for
(g,h)
, while for fixed
(g,h)
, the standard stationary phase argument implies that the integral in
(x,y)
is bounded by
C_{0}\langle s\rangle^{-d}
, so (
5.2
) is also true in this case.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Uniformly in
(a,b,\xi,\xi^{\prime})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{4}
, we have

\int_{L\leq|s|\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma}}\bigg|\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2d}}\chi_{0}(x-a)\chi_{0}(y-b)\cdot e(\langle x,\xi\rangle+\langle y,\xi^{\prime}\rangle+s\langle x,y\rangle)\bigg|\,\mathrm{d}s\\
\lesssim_{2}L^{2d-2(d-1)(1-\gamma)+\eta}.

(5.4)

Proof.

See Lemma 6.6 of
[
22
]
(with
D=\lambda=1
and
\Phi\equiv 1
in Lemma 6.6 of
[
22
]
). The
\lesssim_{2}
inequality is due to the upper bound
|s|\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma}
where
\mathfrak{D}
is controlled by
C_{2}
.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
5.1
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

We start with
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
; the discussion of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
is similar (with some additional twists) and will be left to the last step. We use the notation
e(z)=e^{2\pi iz}
throughout this proof.

(1)
Preparations.
Recall the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
as in Definition
3.8
, with the expression similar to (
3.24
) and time domain as in (
3.26
). For regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
we can link its branching nodes and select the subset
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
as in Proposition
4.6
, then
|\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}|=n
is half the order of
\mathcal{Q}
. For each
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
define
x_{\mathfrak{n}}=k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
y_{\mathfrak{n}}=k_{\mathfrak{n}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}_{3}}
as in Definition
4.6
, where
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{j}^{\prime}
are the children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
as in Definition
3.5
; note that
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}=2\langle x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle
and
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}=-\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
by Proposition
4.6
.

Now consider the
2n+1
vectors
k_{\mathfrak{l}}-k
for all leaves
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}
of sign
+
; by examining the
k
-decoration of the tree with sign
+
, we see that they satisfy one linear equation of form

\sum_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}^{(+)}\sigma(\mathfrak{l})(k_{\mathfrak{l}}-k)=0,

(5.5)

where
\sigma(\mathfrak{l})\in\{-1,0,1\}
with the sum of all the
\sigma(\mathfrak{l})
being also
\pm 1
; moreover each
k_{\mathfrak{l}}-k
is a fixed integer linear combination of
(x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}})
and vice versa, which forms an integer coefficient linear bijection between
(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d})^{2n}
and the subspace of
(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d})^{2n+1}
defined by (
5.5
). Therefore, the summation over all
k
-decorations
\mathscr{I}
of
\mathcal{Q}
, appearing in (
3.24
), can be replaced by the summation over all
(x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])\in(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d})^{2n}
(using also the last statement in Proposition
4.6
), so we can reduce (
3.24
) to

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s,k)=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{2n}\zeta(\mathcal{Q})\int_{\mathcal{I}^{*}}\sum_{(x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])}\epsilon\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}e^{2\pi i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\langle x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle}\\
\times W(k,x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])\,\mathrm{d}t[\mathcal{N}].

(5.6)

Here
\mathcal{I}^{*}
is the same as (
3.26
) and
(x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])\in(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d})^{2n}
, and
\epsilon=\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}}
is as in Definition
3.5
(in particular it only depends on
(x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}})
). Moreover
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is the branching node linked with
\mathfrak{n}
, and
W
is the function defined by

W(k,x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])=\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}^{(+)}F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k+z_{\mathfrak{l}}),

(5.7)

where
z_{\mathfrak{l}}=k_{\mathfrak{l}}-k
is a fixed integer coefficient linear combination of
(x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])
.

Note that the expression (
5.6
) depends on
k
only via
W
and
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}
in (
5.7
), so we if take any derivative in
k
, the derivative will fall on one of the
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}
factors. Since we are taking
30d
derivatives in (
5.1
) and each
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
is bounded in
\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}
by assumption, we may absorb these derivatives at the price of weakening the
\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}
norms by
\mathfrak{S}^{10d,10d}
. Similarly, we can also absorb the weight
\langle k\rangle^{30d}
occurring in (
5.1
), because
k
is a linear combination of all the
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
by (
5.5
), which implies

\langle k\rangle^{30d}\lesssim_{0}n^{30d}\cdot\max_{\mathfrak{l}}\langle k_{\mathfrak{l}}\rangle^{30d},

where the
n^{30d}
factor can be absorbed by
C_{1}^{n}
. Therefore, below we will only bound the
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,0,0}(\mathcal{B})
norm of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
, assuming only that each (
\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
and)
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
is bounded in
\mathfrak{S}^{10d,10d}
. Here note that, strictly speaking, the norm
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,30d}(\mathcal{B})
involves an extension of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
to all values of
(t,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
; however such extension will not depend on the derivatives taken, so this point does not affect the proof. In fact, this extension will be canonically fixed using some fixed smooth cutoff functions (see for example (
5.23
)), after which we only need to deal with the
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,0,0}
norm.

(2)
Localization of
x_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
y_{\mathfrak{n}}
.
Now start with (
5.6
). Note that the
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,0,0}
norm, defined in (
3.11
), takes
L_{k}^{\infty}
first before
L_{\lambda_{j}}^{1}
, which is less convenient here; however, using the extra decay in
k
and Sobolev we have

\|G(k)\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}}\lesssim_{0}\max_{|\rho|\leq 2d}\|\partial_{k}^{\rho}G(k)\|_{L_{k}^{1}}\lesssim_{0}\max_{|\rho|\leq 2d}\|\langle k\rangle^{2d}\partial_{k}^{\rho}G(k)\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}}

for any function
G(k)
, which then implies that

\|F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,0,0}}\lesssim_{0}\max_{|\rho|\leq 2d}\sup_{k}\langle k\rangle^{2d}\cdot\|\partial_{k}^{\rho}F(\cdot,k)\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta}},

(5.8)

where in the last norm we fix
k
and measure the
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta}
norm in the time variables. The
2d
derivatives and weight
\langle k\rangle^{2d}
can again be absorbed at the price of weakening the
\mathfrak{S}^{10d,10d}
norm of
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
to
\mathfrak{S}^{8d,8d}
, which then allows us to fix
k
and only deal with the
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta}
norm (where, again, the extension does not depend on the extra derivatives nor on
k
).

With fixed
k
, we may fix some value
k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for each
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}
of sign
+
, and restrict to the region
|k_{\mathfrak{l}}-k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0}|\leq 1
for each such
\mathfrak{l}
, where
k_{\mathfrak{l}}=k+z_{\mathfrak{l}}
as above; such term carries a quickly decaying and summable coefficient in
(k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0})
, due to the decay in
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
provided by the
\mathfrak{S}^{8d,8d}
norm bounds of each
\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
and
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
. Then, with fixed
(k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0})
, we may apply Lemma
A.4
, which allows to decompose each single term with fixed
(k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0})
into at most
C_{0}^{n}
sub-terms, such that for each sub-term there exist constant vectors
(a_{\mathfrak{n}},b_{\mathfrak{n}})
for
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
such that

|x_{\mathfrak{n}}-a_{\mathfrak{n}}|\leq 1/2\mathrm{\ and\ }\quad|y_{\mathfrak{n}}-b_{\mathfrak{n}}|\leq 1/2\mathrm{\ for\ all\ }\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}.

Now we will focus on one of the sub-terms (the factor
C_{0}^{n}
can be absorbed) and fix
(a_{\mathfrak{n}},b_{\mathfrak{n}})
; in particular, using a partition of unity we can insert the cutoff functions
\chi_{0}(x_{\mathfrak{n}}-a_{\mathfrak{n}})\chi_{0}(y_{\mathfrak{n}}-b_{\mathfrak{n}})
. Next, notice that the two sets of vectors
(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
and
(k,x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}})
, where
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}
has sign
+
and
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
, are related by a (volume preserving) integer coefficient linear transform, all of whose coefficients are at most
1
in absolute value. Now the function

W=\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}^{(+)}F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})=\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}^{(+)}(\varphi(\delta\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}},k+z_{\mathfrak{l}})+\mathscr{R}_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k+z_{\mathfrak{l}})),

(5.9)

viewed in the variables
(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, has Fourier
L^{1}
norm
\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n}
due to the factorization structure and
\mathfrak{S}^{8d,8d}
bound assumptions; with the change of variables, we can bound the Fourier
L^{1}
norm in the
(k,x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}})
variables, namely

W(k,x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2n+1}}\widehat{W}(\lambda,\xi[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],\xi^{\prime}[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])\cdot e(\langle\lambda,k\rangle)\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}e(\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}},x_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle)\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\lambda,

(5.10)

where

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2n+1}}|\widehat{W}(\lambda,\xi[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],\xi^{\prime}[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])|\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\mathrm{d}\xi[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}]\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}]\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n}.

(5.11)

As
C_{1}^{n}
can be absorbed by the right hand side of (
5.1
), we can replace the function
W
in (
5.6
) by products of
\chi_{0}
and phase-shift factors, and we only need to estimate

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s):=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{2n}\zeta(\mathcal{Q})\int_{\mathcal{I}^{*}}\sum_{(x[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}],y[\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}])}\epsilon\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}\bigg(\chi_{0}(x_{\mathfrak{n}}-a_{\mathfrak{n}})\chi_{0}(y_{\mathfrak{n}}-b_{\mathfrak{n}})\\
\times e\big[\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\langle x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}},x_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle\big]\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}t[\mathcal{N}]

(5.12)

uniformly in all choices of
(\xi_{\mathfrak{n}},\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime})
.

(3)
Estimating the summation.
Now consider
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s)
, with fixed
k
, defined in (
5.12
). With fixed
(a_{\mathfrak{n}},b_{\mathfrak{n}},\xi_{\mathfrak{n}},\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime})
, we can write (where
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
is the branching node linked with
\mathfrak{n}
)

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s)=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{2n}\zeta(\mathcal{Q})\int_{\mathcal{I}^{*}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}(t_{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}t[\mathcal{N}],

(5.13)

where

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}(t_{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})=\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2d}}\epsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\chi_{0}(x_{\mathfrak{n}}-a_{\mathfrak{n}})\chi_{0}(y_{\mathfrak{n}}-b_{\mathfrak{n}})\cdot e\big[\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\langle x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}},x_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle\big],

(5.14)

and
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}
equals
1
,
0
or
-1
if
0
,
1
or
2
vectors in
\{x,y\}
equal
0
. Note also that, by (
3.26
), we have

\mathcal{I}^{*}\subset\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{n}},\quad\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{n}}=[\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}+1]\times[\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}+1];

(5.15)

in particular
for each
\mathfrak{n}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
,
there exists a fixed integer
q
such that
t_{\mathfrak{n}}\leq q\leq t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
or
t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\leq q\leq t_{\mathfrak{n}}
.

We now analyze
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. A trivial bound is
|\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}|\lesssim_{1}L^{2d}
. Next, let
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}):=s_{\mathfrak{n}}
, so that
|s_{\mathfrak{n}}|\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}
depends only on
s_{\mathfrak{n}}
, then upon replacing
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{n}}
with
1
, we get that

\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}=\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{2d}}\chi_{0}(x_{\mathfrak{n}}-a_{\mathfrak{n}})\chi_{0}(y_{\mathfrak{n}}-b_{\mathfrak{n}})\cdot e\big[s_{\mathfrak{n}}\langle x_{\mathfrak{n}},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}},x_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime},y_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle\big]+\mathcal{O}_{0}(L^{d}).

(5.16)

If
|s_{\mathfrak{n}}|\geq L
, then Lemma
5.3
implies that

\|\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{|s_{\mathfrak{n}}|\geq L}\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{n}})}\lesssim_{2}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}\cdot L^{-2(d-1)(1-\gamma)+\eta};

(5.17)

if
|s_{\mathfrak{n}}|\leq L
, we can apply Poisson summation to (
5.16
) to bound it by an expression similar to the left hand side of (
5.2
), then Lemma
5.2
implies that
|\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}|\lesssim_{0}L^{2d}\langle s\rangle^{-d}
, and hence

\|\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{|s_{\mathfrak{n}}|\leq L}\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{n}})}\lesssim_{0}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}.

(5.18)

Moreover,
if
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
, then there exists a fixed integer
q
such that
|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-q|\leq|t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|=\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma}|s_{\mathfrak{n}}|
, hence

\|\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{|s_{\mathfrak{n}}|\leq L}\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{n}})}\lesssim_{2}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}\cdot L^{-2\gamma}

(5.19)

as
\delta^{-1}\leq C_{2}
. Putting together, and noticing that there are exactly
1\leq g\leq n
nodes
\mathfrak{n}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}\neq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}
, we get that

\int_{\mathcal{I}^{*}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}|\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}(t_{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})|\,\mathrm{d}t[\mathcal{N}]\leq C_{0}^{n}C_{2}^{g}\cdot\delta^{-n}L^{2(d-\gamma)n}\cdot L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\eta)g}.

(5.20)

Finally, we turn to the study of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s)
. By (
5.13
) and the definition of
\mathcal{I}^{*}
, we can write

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s)=\int_{q}^{t}\int_{q^{\prime}}^{s}\mathcal{S}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}

(5.21)

for some function
\mathcal{S}
derived from (
5.13
), where
t^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
are the
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
variables with
\mathfrak{n}
being the roots of two trees of
\mathcal{Q}
. Note that, in view of the possibilities of layerings, one or both of the integrations in (
5.21
) may be taken on a unit interval independent of
t
(or
s
) such as
[r,r+1]
for some
r<q
(or
r<q^{\prime}
). In such cases, including the case when one of the trees of
\mathcal{Q}
is trivial, the function
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s)
will not depend on
t
(or
s
) and the proof becomes much easier. Moreover, in (
5.21
) we have

\|\mathcal{S}\|_{L^{1}}\leq(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-2\eta)g},\quad\|\mathcal{S}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-2\eta)g}\cdot L^{2d},

(5.22)

where the first inequality follows from (
5.20
), and the second inequality follows from (
5.17
)–(
5.19
) and the pointwise bound for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}
. Now by translation we may replace
q
and
q^{\prime}
by
0
and assume
t,s\in[0,1]
, then from (
5.22
), Lemma
A.2
and interpolation, we conclude that a canonical extension

(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})_{\mathrm{ext}}(t,s)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\chi(t)\chi(t^{\prime})\chi(s)\chi(s^{\prime})\cdot\mathcal{S}(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

(5.23)

for some suitable cutoff functions
\chi
, satisfies that

\|(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})_{\mathrm{ext}}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta}}\lesssim_{1}(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-100d\eta)g}.

(5.24)

By the discussion at the end of (1), this proves (
5.1
) for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
.

(4)
The
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
case.
The proof of (
5.1
) for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
is mostly similar to the
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
case but with one important difference, namely that, due to the absence of the factor
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
for the lone leaf
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{l}_{\mathrm{lo}}
, the function
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s,k)
will not have any decay in
k
. This first leads to the choice of the
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,0}
norm without weight in (
5.1
) (while the derivatives are treated in the same way as
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
); moreover the manipulation in (
5.8
) is not possible, so we still need to use the original
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,0}
norm that takes
L_{k}^{\infty}
before
L_{\lambda_{j}}^{1}
, but the first inequality in (
A.6
) is not true for such norms because the Hausdorff-Young inequality is not available for Banach space valued functions. Therefore new arguments are needed to treat this norm.

By the same arguments as in the
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
case, we can reduce the estimate of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
to that of

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s,k)=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{2n}\zeta(\mathcal{T})\int_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}(t_{\mathfrak{n}},t_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}})\,\mathrm{d}t[\mathcal{N}]

(5.25)

similar to (
5.13
), where
\mathcal{D}^{*}
is as in (
3.27
),
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{n}}
is as in (
5.14
) and satisfies (
5.17
)–(
5.19
). This expression depends on the parameters
(a_{\mathfrak{n}},b_{\mathfrak{n}},\xi_{\mathfrak{n}},\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime})
as defined above, all of which are allowed to depend on
k
.

Consider the nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\{\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{u}\}
and the corresponding linked nodes
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, where
\mathfrak{r}
is the root of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathfrak{u}:=\mathfrak{n}_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\mathrm{pr}}
is the parent node of the lone leaf. By Propositions
4.4
and
4.6
there are only two cases: when
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{u}
, or when
(\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}^{\prime},\mathfrak{u}^{\prime},\mathfrak{u})
are listed in this order with each being a descendant of the previous one. In the first case we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s,k)=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{S}_{0}(t_{1},s_{1},k)\mathcal{S}(t_{1},s_{1},k)\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{1},

(5.26)

and in the second case we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}(t,s,k)=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{t_{1}}\int_{s}^{t_{2}}\int_{s}^{s_{2}}\mathcal{S}_{1}(t_{1},t_{2},k)\mathcal{S}_{2}(s_{2},s_{1},k)\mathcal{S}(t_{2},s_{2},k)\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}

(5.27)

for some functions
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}_{j}
derived from (
5.25
), where in (
5.26
) we have
t_{1}=t_{\mathfrak{r}}
and
s_{1}=t_{\mathfrak{u}}
, and in (
5.27
) we have
(t_{1},t_{2},s_{2},s_{1})=(t_{\mathfrak{r}},t_{\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}},t_{\mathfrak{u}})
. Note that, in view of the possibilities of layerings, the upper and lower bounds
t_{j}
or
s_{j}
in some of the integrals in (
5.26
) and (
5.27
) may be replaced by some constant integer
r
with
q\leq r\leq q^{\prime}
, but the proof in these cases can be done similarly.

Now, with the formulas (
5.26
) and (
5.27
), we can define the canonical extension
(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})_{\mathrm{ext}}
by inserting all the cutoff functions
\chi(t)\chi(s)
and
\chi(t_{j})\chi(s_{j})
(after suitable translations) for all
j
as in (
5.23
), and we only need to prove that

\|(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})_{\mathrm{ext}}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,0,0}}\lesssim_{1}(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-100d\eta)g}

(5.28)

under the assumption that all input functions
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
are bounded in
\mathfrak{S}^{10d,10d}
. We may assume that all the
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}_{j}
functions are supported in some fixed unit boxes; they satisfy the bound

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{0}(t_{1},s_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\cdot\|\mathcal{S}(t_{1},s_{1})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-2\eta)g}\qquad\mathrm{for\ }(\ref{incohproof22}),

(5.29)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}_{1}(t_{1},t_{2})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\cdot\|\mathcal{S}_{2}(s_{2},s_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\cdot\|\mathcal{S}(t_{2},s_{2})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-2\eta)g}\qquad\mathrm{for\ }(\ref{incohproof23})

uniformly in
k
(due to (
5.17
)–(
5.19
)), so Lemma
A.2
already implies

\|(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})_{\mathrm{ext}}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{-\eta,0,0}}\lesssim_{1}(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-2d\eta)g}.

(5.30)

Next, by expanding out
\mathcal{S}_{j}
using (
5.14
) and allowing to lose a power of at most
L^{4d}
, we can replace
\mathcal{S}_{0}
in (
5.26
) by
e^{i\Gamma_{1}(t_{1}-s_{1})}
, and replace
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
in (
5.27
) by
e^{i\Gamma_{1}(t_{1}-t_{2})}
and
e^{i\Gamma_{2}(s_{2}-s_{1})}
in (
5.27
), where
\Gamma_{j}
are quantities depending on
k
. We also have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t_{1}}\partial_{s_{1}}\mathcal{S}(t_{1},s_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-100d\eta)g}\cdot L^{4d}\qquad\mathrm{for\ }(\ref{incohproof22}),

(5.31)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t_{2}}\partial_{s_{2}}\mathcal{S}(t_{2},s_{2})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-100d\eta)g}\cdot L^{4d}\qquad\mathrm{for\ }(\ref{incohproof23})

again uniformly in
k
, because any exponential factor depending on
(t_{1},s_{1})
(or
(t_{2},s_{2})
for (
5.27
)) are already included in the
\mathcal{S}_{0}
and
(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2})
factors. Then, by Lemma
A.3
, we get

\|(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}})_{\mathrm{ext}}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1/4,0,0}}\lesssim_{1}(C_{0}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-100d\eta)g}\cdot L^{4d};

(5.32)

finally by interpolating with (
5.30
) we get (
5.28
).
∎

6.
Layered regular objects III: Asymptotics for coherent objects

In this section we consider the coherent case, as described in Proposition
4.9
. Let the objects and notations (including
(q,q^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{B}
etc.) be fixed as in the beginning of Section
5
, where we now assume
g=0
. Note that the expressions
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*},\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})
and all our analysis below depend only on the values
(q,q^{\prime})
, which are defined using the bigger garden
\mathcal{G}
in Proposition
4.9
, but
not
on the rest of the garden
\mathcal{G}
.

6.1.
The coherent estimates

The main estimates for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
for coherent objects
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
, including the asymptotic information, are stated as follows.

Proposition 6.1
.

If
g=0
, then we can decompose

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}=(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}+\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*},\quad\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}=(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}+\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*},

(6.1)

where
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
and
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
are nonzero only for
dominant
couples
\mathcal{Q}
and trees
\mathcal{T}
(see Proposition
4.4
for definition), and the remainders satisfy that

\|\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,30d}(\mathcal{B})}+\|\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,0}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\delta)^{n}\cdot L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})}.

(6.2)

Moreover,
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
can be written as the sum of at most
2^{n}
terms of form

(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t,s,k)=\sum\delta^{n}\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(t,s)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(k),

(6.3)

where each term satisfies

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1-\eta}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n},\quad\|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}}\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{30d,30d}}\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n}.

(6.4)

Similarly,
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
can be written as the sum of at most
2^{n}
terms of form (
6.3
) but with
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{T}}}
instead of
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}}
, where each term satisfies

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1-\eta}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n},\quad\|\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{T}}}\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{30d,0}}\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n}.

(6.5)

Proposition 6.2
.

For
t\in[p,p+1]
and
0\leq n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
, we have

\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t,t,k)=\mathcal{U}_{n}(t,k)+\widetilde{\mathscr{R}_{n}}(t,k),

(6.6)

where
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is defined as in (
3.44
), and the sum is taken over all regular couples
\mathcal{Q}
of order
2n
such that all its nodes are in layer
p
. The remainder satisfies, for all
t\in[p,p+1]
and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, that

|\widetilde{\mathscr{R}_{n}}(t,k)|\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\delta)^{n}\langle k\rangle^{-30d}\cdot L^{-(\theta_{p+1}+4\theta_{p})/5}.

(6.7)

Proposition 6.3
.

Let the conjugate of couple
\mathcal{Q}
and paired tree
\mathcal{T}
be
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{T}}
as in Definitions
3.3
and
3.4
(which are obviously extended to include layerings), then we have

\mathcal{K}_{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}}^{*}(t,s,k)=\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(s,t,k)},\quad\mathcal{K}_{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}^{*}(t,s,k)=\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s,k)}

(6.8)

(the same holds for the
(\cdots)_{\mathrm{app}}
approximations in Proposition
6.1
). Moreover, fix any
0\leq q^{\prime}\leq q\leq p
and
0\leq n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
. Then for any
(t,s)\in\mathcal{B}
(with
t>s
) and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\sum_{(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t,s,k)\cdot\overline{(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t,s,k)}\in\mathbb{R},

(6.9)

where the sum is taken over all the coherent regular couples
\mathcal{Q}
and regular trees
\mathcal{T}
as described in Proposition
4.9
corresponding to this fixed
(q,q^{\prime})
, such that
\mathcal{T}
has sign
+
, and
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
has
total order

n(\mathcal{Q})+n(\mathcal{T})=2n
. In addition, if in (
6.9
) we make the further restriction that the total number of layer
p
branching nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
equals a given constant, then (
6.9
) remains true.

6.2.
Preparations for the proof

The proof of Propositions
6.1
–
6.3
mainly rely on combinatorial arguments, plus the analysis of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
, where
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
are regular couples and regular trees such that all their nodes (except the lone leaf) are in the same layer. Such analysis is proved in earlier works
[
19
,
22
]
, and will be used as black box.

Definition 6.4

(Enhanced dominant couples and equivalence
[
19
,
22
]
)
.

Recall the notion of type 1 and 2 regular couples, and dominant couples (and paired trees) in Proposition
4.4
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a layered dominant couple, and assume that all nodes of
\mathcal{Q}
are in the same layer. We define an
enhanced dominant couple
to be a pair
\mathscr{Q}=(\mathcal{Q},Z)
, such that
Z\subset\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
, and for each
\mathfrak{n}\in Z
the branching node
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
linked with
\mathfrak{n}
is a child node of
\mathfrak{n}
(we call such
Z\subset\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
a
special
subset).

Using Proposition
4.4
, we can define an equivalence relation
\sim
between enhanced dominant couples
\mathscr{Q}=(\mathcal{Q},Z)
, as follows. First the enhanced trivial couple (with no branching node so
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}=Z=\varnothing
) is only equivalent to itself, and two enhanced dominant couples where
\mathcal{Q}
have different types or different layers are never equivalent. Next, if
\mathscr{Q}=(\mathcal{Q},Z)
and
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
have type 1 and the same layer, then we have
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}=\mathcal{N}_{1}^{\mathrm{ch}}\cup\mathcal{N}_{2}^{\mathrm{ch}}\cup\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathrm{ch}}\cup\{\mathfrak{r}\}
as in Proposition
4.6
, where
\mathcal{N}_{j}^{\mathrm{ch}}
is the
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
set for
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{r}
is the root with
+
sign, see Proposition
4.4
. In this case
Z
is special if and only if
Z=Z_{1}\cup Z_{2}\cup Z_{3}
(i.e.
\mathfrak{r}
is
not
in
Z
) where
Z_{j}\subset\mathcal{N}_{j}^{\mathrm{ch}}
is special, and similarly for
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
. Let
\mathscr{Q}_{j}=(\mathcal{Q}_{j},Z_{j})
, we then define
\mathscr{Q}\sim\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathscr{Q}_{j}\sim\mathscr{Q}_{j}^{\prime}
for
1\leq j\leq 3
.

Now let
\mathscr{Q}
and
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
be as before, but suppose
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
have type
2
and the same layer. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\epsilon}
be associated with
\mathcal{Q}
as in Proposition
4.4
, and similarly for
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
(same for the other objects appearing below). We use the notation in Proposition
4.4
for
\mathcal{Q}
, and correspondingly for
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
; note that
\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{\pm}
is paired with
\mathfrak{n}_{2j}^{\pm}
for
1\leq j\leq m_{\pm}
by definition of dominant couples. Recall also that

\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}=\bigg(\bigcup_{j,\epsilon,\iota}\mathcal{N}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}^{\mathrm{ch}}\bigg)\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{lp}}^{\mathrm{\mathrm{ch}}}\cup\big\{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{+}:1\leq j\leq m_{+}\big\}\cup\big\{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{-}:1\leq j\leq m_{-}\big\}

(6.10)

as in Proposition
4.6
; then
Z
is special if and only if

Z=\bigg(\bigcup_{j,\epsilon,\iota}Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota}\bigg)\cup Z_{\mathrm{lp}}\cup\big\{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{+}:j\in Z^{+}\big\}\cup\big\{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{-}:j\in Z^{-}\big\}

(6.11)

for some special subsets
Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota}\subset\mathcal{N}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}^{\mathrm{ch}}
and
Z_{\mathrm{lp}}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{lp}}^{\mathrm{ch}}
, and some subsets
Z^{\epsilon}\subset\{1,\cdots,m_{\epsilon}\}
. Similar representations are defined for
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
. For
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
and each
1\leq j\leq m_{\epsilon}
, consider the tuple
(\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon},\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,2})
; here
\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon}=1
if
j\in Z^{\epsilon}
and
\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon}=0
otherwise,
\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon}\in\{1,2,3\}
is the
first digit of
the code of the mini tree associated with the pair
\{2j-1,2j\}\in\mathcal{P}^{\epsilon}
(see Definition
4.1
), and
\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
is the equivalence class of the enhanced dominant couple
\mathscr{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}=(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota},Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota})
for
\iota\in\{1,2\}
. Define also
\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}
to be the equivalence class of the enhanced dominant couple
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}=(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}},Z_{\mathrm{lp}})
.

We now define
\mathscr{Q}\sim\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
, if and only if (i)
m_{+}+m_{-}=m_{+}^{\prime}+m_{-}^{\prime}
, and (ii) the tuples coming from
\mathcal{Q}
(there are total
m_{+}+m_{-}
of them) form
a permutation of
the corresponding tuples coming from
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
(there are total
m_{+}^{\prime}+m_{-}^{\prime}
of them), and (iii)
\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}=\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}^{\prime}
. Finally, note that if
\mathscr{Q}=(\mathcal{Q},Z)
and
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
are equivalent then
n(\mathcal{Q})=n(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})
and
|Z|=|Z^{\prime}|
.

Definition 6.5

(Enhanced dominant trees and equivalence
[
22
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a layered dominant tree, and assume that all its nodes (except the lone leaf) are in the same layer. We define an
enhanced dominant tree
to be a pair
\mathscr{T}=(\mathcal{T},Z)
where
Z\subset\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
is a
special
subset in the sense that for each
\mathfrak{n}\in Z
the branching node
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
linked with
\mathfrak{n}
is a child node of
\mathfrak{n}
.

We then define the notion of equivalence between enhanced dominant trees in the same way as in Definition
6.4
: instead of (
6.10
)–(
6.11
) we have

\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}=\bigg(\bigcup_{j,\epsilon,\iota}\mathcal{N}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}^{\mathrm{ch}}\bigg)\cup\big\{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{0}:1\leq j\leq m_{0}\big\},\quad Z=\bigg(\bigcup_{j,\epsilon,\iota}Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota}\bigg)\cup\big\{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{0}:j\in Z^{0}\big\}

(6.12)

in the notation of Proposition
4.4
. Consider the tuples
(\mathtt{I}_{j,0},\mathtt{c}_{j,0},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,2})
; here
\mathtt{I}_{j,0}=1
if
j\in Z^{0}
and
\mathtt{I}_{j,0}=0
otherwise,
\mathtt{c}_{j,0}\in\{1,2,3\}
is the first digit of the code of the mini tree associated with the pair
\{2j-1,2j\}\in\mathcal{P}^{0}
(see Definition
4.1
), and
\mathscr{X}_{j,0,\iota}
is the equivalence class of the enhanced dominant couple
\mathscr{Q}_{j,0,\iota}=(\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota},Z_{j,0,\iota})
for
\iota\in\{1,2\}
. Then, we define
\mathscr{T}=(\mathcal{T},Z)
and
\mathscr{T}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T}^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
to be equivalent if they have the same layer, and the tuples coming from
\mathcal{T}
form a permutation of the corresponding tuples coming from
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

We now state the results for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
proved in
[
19
,
22
]
. In Propositions
6.6
–
6.8
below, we assume
\mathcal{Q}
is a regular couple and
\mathcal{T}
is a regular tree of order
2n
, such that all the nodes (except the lone leaf of
\mathcal{T}
) are in the same layer
r
with
0\leq r\leq p
.

Proposition 6.6
.

If
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
are
not dominant
, then we have

\|\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,30d}(\mathcal{B})}+\|\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta,30d,0}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\delta)^{n}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})}.

(6.13)

If
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
are dominant, then we have

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}=(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}+\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*},\quad\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}=(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}+\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}

(6.14)

with
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
satisfying the same bound (
6.13
).

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t,s,k)

\displaystyle=2^{-2n}\delta^{n}\zeta(\mathcal{Q})\sum_{Z}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in Z}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s)\cdot\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Q}}^{*}(k)[F_{r}],

(6.15)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t,s,k)

\displaystyle=2^{-2n}\delta^{n}\zeta(\mathcal{T})\sum_{Z}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in Z}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s)\cdot\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{T}}^{*}(k)[F_{r}],

where the sum is taken over all special subsets
Z\subset\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ch}}
, and
\mathscr{Q}=(\mathcal{Q},Z)
and
\mathscr{T}=(\mathcal{T},Z)
are the corresponding enhanced dominant couple and dominant tree. The functions
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s)
and
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s)
depend only on
\mathcal{Q}
(and
\mathcal{T}
) and does not depend on
Z
. The functions
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Q}}^{*}(k)
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{T}}^{*}(k)
are constructed from the input functions
F_{r}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})=\varphi(\delta{r},k_{\mathfrak{l}})+\mathscr{R}_{r}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, and also depends on the enhanced object
\mathscr{Q}
(and
\mathscr{T}
). All these functions are real valued, and for any
\mathscr{Q}
and
\mathscr{T}
, they satisfy the bounds

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1-\eta}(\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Q}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{30d,30d}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n},

(6.16)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1-\eta}(\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{T}}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{30d,0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{n}.

Finally, if
{r}=p
and the input functions
F_{p}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})=\varphi(\delta p,k_{\mathfrak{l}})+\mathscr{R}_{p}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
are replaced by
\varphi(\delta p,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, then we have

\sum_{n(\mathcal{Q})=2n}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,t,k)=\mathcal{U}_{n}(t,k),

(6.17)

where the sum is taken over all dominant couples
\mathcal{Q}
of order
2n
, and
\mathcal{U}_{n}(t,k)
is defined as in (
3.44
).

Proof.

When the scaling law
\gamma
is close to
1
, this is proved in Propositions 6.7, 6.10, 7.5 and 7.11 of
[
19
]
(the case of regular tree
\mathcal{T}
is not included there, but follows in the same way as it forms a regular couple with the trivial tree). For general
\gamma
, the general decomposition leading to (
6.13
)–(
6.14
) is proved in Proposition 6.1 of
[
22
]
, while the exact expressions are independent of
\gamma
, so (
6.15
)–(
6.17
) are the same as in
[
19
]
.

Note that the current setting has two differences compared to
[
19
,
22
]
: first, all the nodes are in layer
{r}
instead of layer
0
, but this does not matter because
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
are clearly invariant after translating all the time variables by the same value
{r}
. Second, the input functions here are
F_{r}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})=\varphi(\delta{r},k_{\mathfrak{l}})+\mathscr{R}_{r}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
instead of
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})}
in
[
19
,
22
]
, but by our assumptions on
\mathscr{R}_{r}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, these inputs are also real valued, and are bounded in the same regularity space that is more than enough for the proofs in
[
19
,
22
]
to work. Thus the same proof in
[
19
,
22
]
also applies here.
∎

Proposition 6.7
.

Let the functions
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s)
and
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s)
be defined as in Proposition
6.6
. Then for for trivial couple
\mathcal{Q}
we have
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s)=1
. For type 1 dominant couple
\mathcal{Q}
, let the notations be as in Proposition
4.4
, then for any
t,s\in[{r},{r}+1]
we have

\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s)=\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(\min(t,s))=2\int_{{r}}^{\min(t,s)}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j}}^{*}(\tau,\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(6.18)

If
\mathcal{Q}
is a dominant couple of type 2, let the notations be as in Proposition
4.4
, then for any
t,s\in[{r},{r}+1]
, we have (understanding
t_{0}=t
and
s_{0}=s
) that

\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s)=\int_{t>t_{1}>\cdots>t_{m_{+}}>{r}}\int_{s>s_{1}>\cdots>s_{m-}>{r}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{+}}\prod_{\iota=1}^{2}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,+,\iota}}^{*}(t_{j},t_{j})\\
\times\prod_{j=1}^{m_{-}}\prod_{\iota=1}^{2}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,-,\iota}}^{*}(s_{j},s_{j})\cdot\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}}^{*}(\min(t_{m_{+}},s_{m_{-}}))\prod_{j=1}^{m_{+}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{-}}\mathrm{d}s_{j}.

(6.19)

If
\mathcal{T}
is a dominant tree, let the notations be as in Proposition
4.4
, then for any
t,s\in[{r},{r}+1]
and
t>s
, we have

\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s)=\int_{t>t_{1}>\cdots>t_{m_{0}}>s}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\prod_{\iota=1}^{2}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota}}^{*}(t_{j},t_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}.

(6.20)

Moreover, for any equivalence class
\mathscr{X}
of enhanced dominant couples
\mathscr{Q}=(\mathcal{Q},Z)

with
Z\neq\varnothing
, we have

\sum_{\mathscr{Q}=(\mathcal{Q},Z)\in\mathscr{X}}\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in Z}\frac{1}{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i}\bigg)\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,t)=0.

(6.21)

Proof.

See Proposition 7.5 and 7.8 of
[
19
]
(the case of dominant tree
\mathcal{T}
is not included there, but follows in the same way as it forms a dominant couple with the trivial tree). These results are independent of the scaling law
\gamma
, so they also apply to the current case.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

Let the functions
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Q}}^{*}(k)
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{T}}^{*}(k)
be defined as in Proposition
6.6
, then they only depend on the
equivalence class

\mathscr{X}
(and
\mathscr{Z}
) of the enhanced dominant couples
\mathscr{Q}
(and enhanced dominant trees
\mathscr{T}
), so we may denote
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Q}}^{*}(k)=\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}}^{*}(k)
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{T}}^{*}(k)=\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Z}}^{*}(k)
. If
\mathcal{Q}
is trivial then we have
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Q}}^{*}(k)=F_{r}(k)
. If
\mathcal{Q}
is a type 1 dominant couple, let the notations be fixed as in Proposition
4.4
and let
\mathscr{X}_{j}
be the equivalence class of
\mathscr{Q}_{j}
in Definition
6.4
, then

\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}}^{*}(k)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\bm{\delta}(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k)\bm{\delta}(|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2})\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{j}}^{*}(k_{j})\,\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}.

(6.22)

If
\mathcal{Q}
is a type 2 dominant couple, let the notations be fixed as in Proposition
4.4
, and also recall the tuples
(\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon},\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,2})
and
\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}
, as in Definition
6.4
. Then we have

\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}}^{*}(k)=\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}}^{*}(k)\cdot\prod_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{N}_{(j,\epsilon)}^{*}(k).

(6.23)

If
\mathcal{T}
is a dominant tree, let the notations be fixed as in Proposition
4.4
, and also recall the tuples
(\mathtt{I}_{j,0},\mathtt{c}_{j,0},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,2})
as in Definition
6.5
. Then we have

\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{Z}}^{*}(k)=\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathcal{N}_{(j,0)}^{*}(k).

(6.24)

Here in (
6.23
) and (
6.24
) we have

\mathcal{N}_{(j,\epsilon)}^{*}(k)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\bm{\delta}(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k)\cdot(\mathrm{Factor\ }1)\cdot(\mathrm{Factor\ }2)\,\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3},

(6.25)

and the same with
\epsilon
replaced by
0
, where

(\mathrm{Factor\ }1)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\bm{\delta}(|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2}),&\mathrm{if\ }\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon}&=0;\\
&\mathrm{p.v.}\,\frac{1}{|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2}},&\mathrm{if\ }\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon}&=1,\end{aligned}\right.

(6.26)

and

(\mathrm{Factor\ }2)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,1}}^{*}(k_{2})\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,2}}^{*}(k_{3}),&\mathrm{if\ }\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon}&=1;\\
&\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,1}}^{*}(k_{1})\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,2}}^{*}(k_{3}),&\mathrm{if\ }\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon}&=2;\\
&\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,1}}^{*}(k_{1})\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,2}}^{*}(k_{2}),&\mathrm{if\ }\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon}&=3.\end{aligned}\right.

(6.27)

Proof.

See Proposition 7.7 of
[
19
]
. Again the result is independent of the scaling law
\gamma
, so it also applies to the current case.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Propositions
6.1
–
6.3

We now prove Propositions
6.1
–
6.3
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We will assume
q>q^{\prime}
, since when
q=q^{\prime}
all the nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
must be in the same layer and the result follows from Proposition
6.6
. Recall the structure of
\mathcal{Q}
described in Proposition
4.9
. For each
q^{\prime}\leq r\leq q
, consider all the nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{+}
where
1\leq a\leq 2m_{+}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{+}}=r
. All these branching nodes are linked to each other, and they are precisely all the branching nodes of some regular tree
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
. Denote the root of
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
by
\mathfrak{r}_{[r]}
, then the lone leaf of
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
is just
\mathfrak{r}_{[r-1]}
or the root of a tree in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
, and all the nodes in
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
except the lone leaf are in layer
r
(note that some of the
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
may be trivial, in which case some of the
\mathfrak{r}_{[r]}
may coincide).

Next, note that the tree rooted at
\mathfrak{r}_{[q^{\prime}]}
(which contains one of the trees in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
) forms a regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}
with the other tree
\mathcal{T}^{-}
of
\mathcal{Q}
, and all nodes in
\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}
are in layer
q^{\prime}
. Moreover, in the
k
-decoration we must have
k_{\mathfrak{r}_{[r]}}=k
for each
q^{\prime}\leq r\leq q
. For each
q^{\prime}\leq r<q
, in the domain
\mathcal{I}^{*}
defined in (
3.26
), the range for
t_{\mathfrak{r}_{[r]}}
is
[r,r+1]
; for
r=q
(so
\mathfrak{r}_{[q]}
is just the root
\mathfrak{r}^{+}
of the tree
\mathcal{T}^{+}
of
\mathcal{Q}
) the range should be replaced by
t_{\mathfrak{r}^{+}}\in[q,t]
, and similarly the range of
t_{\mathfrak{r}^{-}}
is
t_{\mathfrak{r}^{-}}\in[q^{\prime},s]
(if
\mathfrak{r}_{[q^{\prime}]}
or
\mathfrak{r}^{-}
is a leaf then these should be suitably modified but this will not affect (
6.28
) below).

Therefore, by definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
, we obtain that

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,s,k)=\prod_{q^{\prime}<r<q}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{[r]}}^{*}(r+1,r,k)\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{[q]}}^{*}(t,q,k)\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}}^{*}(q^{\prime}+1,s,k).

(6.28)

Since all the nodes of each individual
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
(except the lone leaf) and
\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}
are in the same layer, we may decompose each
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{[r]}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}}^{*}
as in (
6.14
) and (
6.15
), and putting together gives the decomposition (
6.1
) and (
6.3
) for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
(where any term with at least one
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{T}_{[r]}}^{*}
or
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}}^{*}
goes into
\mathscr{R}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
). The same argument applies for
\mathcal{T}
, where we have
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
for
q^{\prime}\leq r\leq q
but no
\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}
, and similarly

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}(t,s,k)=\prod_{q^{\prime}<r<q}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{[r]}}^{*}(r+1,r,k)\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{[q]}}^{*}(t,q,k)\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{[q^{\prime}]}}^{*}(q^{\prime}+1,s,k).

(6.29)

Finally, if
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
are not dominant, the at least one of the
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
or
\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}
will not be dominant, so the corresponding
(\cdots)_{\mathrm{app}}
quantities will vanish, thus
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
and
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
will also vanish.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2
.

This basically follows from (
6.17
). The input functions here are
F_{p}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})=\varphi(\delta p,k_{\mathfrak{l}})+\mathscr{R}_{p}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, if we replace them by
\varphi(\delta p,k_{\mathfrak{l}})
, the resulting
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
will be exactly
\mathcal{U}_{n}(t,k)
by (
6.17
); the difference, on the other hand, contains those terms with at least one input being
\mathscr{R}_{p}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
. The number of these terms is at most
2^{n}
which can be absorbed by
C_{1}^{n}
, while each individual term includes an additional decay factor
L^{-\theta_{p}}
due to the bound (
3.29
) for
\mathscr{R}_{p}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
. This proves (
6.7
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

First (
6.8
) directly follows from the definitions of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
in Definition
3.8
and definition of conjugate in Definition
3.4
(the same holds for the
(\cdots)_{\mathrm{app}}
approximations).

Now we focus on the proof of (
6.9
). Let
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
be fixed such that
n(\mathcal{Q})+n(\mathcal{T})=2n
. We may also assume
q>q^{\prime}
, since otherwise all the nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
must be in the same layer, and the result follows from Proposition 6.3 of
[
22
]
. In fact, the proof here can be viewed as an extended version of the proof of Proposition 6.3 of
[
22
]
, taking into account of the layers.

Next, by Proposition
6.1
we may restrict
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
to be dominant. Then, each
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{[q^{\prime}]}
is dominant and has all its nodes (except the lone leaf for
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
) in the same layer, so the corresponding
(\cdots)_{\mathrm{app}}
quantities are given by (
6.15
). Moreover each of these objects has its own inner structure (for example each
\mathcal{T}_{[r]}
has the form in Proposition
4.4
with branching nodes of the regular chain being those
\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{+}
where
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{a}^{+}}=r
) that allows us to apply Propositions
6.7
and
6.8
. Now recall the notations in Proposition
4.9
. Let
Z
be the union of a special subset of
\mathcal{Q}
and a special subset of
\mathcal{T}
, then
Z
is uniquely determined by the sets
Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
and
Z_{j,0,\iota}
(where
\epsilon\in\{\pm\}
,
1\leq j\leq m_{\epsilon}
or
1\leq j\leq m_{0}
, and
\iota\in\{1,2\}
),
Z_{\mathrm{lp}}
, and the sets
(Z^{\epsilon},Z^{0})
, as in Definitions
6.4
and
6.5
. Define also
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}
and
\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}
, and the tuples
(\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon},\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,2})
and
(\mathtt{I}_{j,0},\mathtt{c}_{j,0},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,2})
etc., as in Definitions
6.4
and
6.5
. Define
r_{j}^{+}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{+}}
for
1\leq j\leq m_{+}
and
r_{j}^{0}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}_{2j-1}^{0}}
for
1\leq j\leq m_{0}
, and
r_{j}^{-}=q^{\prime}
.

Starting with the formulas (
6.28
) and (
6.29
), by applying (
6.15
), (
6.19
)–(
6.20
) and (
6.23
)–(
6.24
), we get the formula

\displaystyle(\ref{layerregest10})

\displaystyle=2^{-2n}\delta^{n}\sum_{m}\sum_{m_{+}+m_{-}+m_{0}=m}\sum_{(\mathrm{tuples})}\sum_{(\mathscr{Q})}\zeta(\mathcal{Q})\zeta(\mathcal{T})\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in Z}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}}\pi i}\int_{t>t_{1}>\cdots>t_{m_{+}}>q^{\prime};\,r_{j}^{+}<t_{j}<r_{j}^{+}+1}

\displaystyle\times\int_{s>s_{1}>\cdots>s_{m_{-}}>q^{\prime}}\int_{t>u_{1}>\cdots>u_{m_{0}}>s;\,r_{j}^{0}<u_{j}<r_{j}^{0}+1}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{+}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{-}}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathrm{d}u_{j}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j,\iota}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,+,\iota}}^{*}(t_{j},t_{j})\prod_{j,\iota}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,-,\iota}}^{*}(s_{j},s_{j})\prod_{j,\iota}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,0,\iota}}^{*}(u_{j},u_{j})\cdot\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{lp}}}^{*}(s_{\mathrm{min}})

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}}^{*}(k)\cdot\prod_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{N}_{(j,\epsilon)}^{*}(k)\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathcal{N}_{(j,0)}^{*}(k).

(6.30)

Here in (
6.30
) we have
s_{\mathrm{min}}=\min(t_{m_{+}},s_{m_{-}},s)
,
Z
is the union of all the special sets
(Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota},Z_{j,0,\iota})
and
Z_{\mathrm{lp}}
and
(Z^{\epsilon},Z^{0})
, and
\mathcal{N}_{(j,\epsilon)}^{*}
and
\mathcal{N}_{(j,0)}^{*}
are defined in (
6.25
). The symbol
\widetilde{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}}
equals
-\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}
if
\mathfrak{n}
belongs to
Z_{j,0,\iota}
or
Z^{0}
, and equals
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}
otherwise. Finally, the sum
\sum_{(\mathrm{tuples})}
is taken over all the
the extended tuples

\big\{(r_{j}^{\epsilon},\mathtt{I}_{j,\epsilon},\mathtt{c}_{j,\epsilon},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,2}):\epsilon\in\{\pm\},1\leq j\leq m_{\epsilon}\big\}\cup\big\{(r_{j}^{0},\mathtt{I}_{j,0},\mathtt{c}_{j,0},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,1},\mathscr{X}_{j,0,2}):1\leq j\leq m_{0}\big\},

(6.31)

and the sum
\sum_{(\mathscr{Q})}
is taken over all the enhanced regular couples
\mathscr{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}=(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota},Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota})
(and similarly for
(j,0,\iota)
and the lone pair) that belong to the equivalence class
\mathscr{X}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
. In these summations we assume
n(\mathcal{Q})+n(\mathcal{T})=2n
; for the last statement of Proposition
6.3
we make the further restriction that the total number
n_{p}
of layer
p
branching nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
equals a given constant.

Now continue with (
6.30
). If
Z=\varnothing
, then the whole expression is clearly real valued. If any of the sets
Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
(or
Z_{j,0,\iota}
or
Z_{\mathrm{lp}}
) is not empty, then the sum of
\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in Z_{j,\epsilon,\iota}}(\widetilde{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}}\pi i)^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}}^{*}(\cdots)
over all
\mathscr{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
, which is a part of the summation
\sum_{(\mathscr{Q})}
, will give
0
due to (
6.21
); here note that all the other ingredients of (
6.30
) are independent of
\mathscr{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
(in particular we can check that varying
\mathscr{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota}
within a fixed equivalence class will not change the value of
\zeta(\mathcal{Q}_{j,\epsilon,\iota})
). Therefore, below we may assume
Z=Z^{+}\cup Z^{-}\cup Z^{0}\neq\varnothing
(and in particular
m=m_{+}+m_{-}+m_{0}>0
).

In the sum
\sum_{(\mathrm{tuples})}
in (
6.30
), we shall first fix
\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}
and the
unordered collection
of all the extended tuples in (
6.31
), and replace
\sum_{(\mathrm{tuples})}
by the summation over all choices of individual tuples, which are permutations of this fixed unordered collection; see Figure
11
for an illustration. We can do this because the value of
n(\mathcal{Q})+n(\mathcal{T})
is determined by the unordered collection. In the same way, if in (
6.9
) we make the further restriction that
n_{p}
equals a given constant, then we can again first fix the unordered collection of extended tuples, because the value of
n_{p}
is also determined by the unordered collection. Now it will suffice to prove that the result is
0
for each fixed unordered collection. For convenience, we shall denote the extended tuples in (
6.31
) by
\mathscr{U}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{+})
and
\mathscr{V}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{-})
and
\mathscr{W}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{0})
. Let the fixed unordered collection be
(\mathscr{Y}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{Y}_{m})
where
m=m_{+}+m_{-}+m_{0}
is also fixed.

We proceed by simplifying the expression (
6.30
). First
\mathcal{M}_{\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}}^{*}(k)
is fixed in the summation, moreover, using the definition in (
6.25
), we can write the product

\prod_{\epsilon\in\{\pm\}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{\epsilon}}\mathcal{N}_{(j,\epsilon)}^{*}(k)\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathcal{N}_{(j,0)}^{*}(k)=\prod_{j=1}^{m_{+}}\mathfrak{M}[\mathscr{U}_{j}]\prod_{j=1}^{m_{-}}\mathfrak{M}[\mathscr{V}_{j}]\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathfrak{M}[\mathscr{W}_{j}]=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathfrak{M}[\mathscr{Y}_{j}],

where
\mathfrak{M}[\mathscr{Y}]
is an expression that depends only on the extended tuple
\mathscr{Y}
. In particular this product does not change when we permute all the
\mathscr{Y}_{j}
, so it can be extracted as a common factor. We can also verify that the value of
\zeta(\mathcal{Q})\zeta(\mathcal{T})
does not vary, so it can also be extracted.

Next, for any equivalence class
\mathscr{X}
and extended tuple
\mathscr{Y}=(r,\mathtt{I},\mathtt{c},\mathscr{X}_{1},\mathscr{X}_{2})
, define

\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{X}](t):=\sum_{\mathscr{Q}\in\mathscr{X}}\mathcal{J}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}(t,t),\quad\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{Y}](t):=\mathbf{1}_{r<t<r+1}\cdot\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{X}_{1}](t)\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{X}_{2}](t).

(6.32)

Note also that the value of
\widetilde{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}}
equals
1
for
\mathfrak{n}\in Z^{+}
, and equals
-1
for
\mathfrak{n}\in Z^{-}\cup Z^{0}
, and that the choices of the
second digits
of the codes of mini trees gives another factor
2^{m}
(which is another common factor). Then, after extracting the common factors, the remaining summation in (
6.30
) then reads

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{+}+m_{-}+m_{0}=m}\sum_{(\mathscr{U}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{U}_{m_{+}},\mathscr{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{V}_{m_{-}},\mathscr{W}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{W}_{m_{0}})}\int_{t>t_{1}>\cdots>t_{m_{+}}>q^{\prime}}\int_{s>s_{1}>\cdots>s_{m_{-}}>q^{\prime}}\int_{t>u_{1}>\cdots>u_{m_{0}}>s}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=1}^{m_{-}}(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j,-}^{\prime}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j,0}^{\prime}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{+}}\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{U}_{j}](t_{j})\,\mathrm{d}t_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{-}}\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{V}_{j}](s_{j})\,\mathrm{d}s_{j}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{W}_{j}](u_{j})\,\mathrm{d}u_{j}\cdot\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}](s_{\mathrm{min}}).

(6.33)

Here
\mathtt{I}_{j,-}^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{I}_{j,0}^{\prime}
represent the “
\mathtt{I}
” component of
\mathscr{V}_{j}
and
\mathscr{W}_{j}
respectively, and the sequence
(\mathscr{U}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{U}_{m_{+}},\mathscr{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{V}_{m_{-}},\mathscr{W}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{W}_{m_{0}})
runs over
all permutations
of the sequence of extended tuples
(\mathscr{Y}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{Y}_{m})
. Strictly speaking we should also require that the “
r
” components of
\mathscr{U}_{j}
must be decreasing in
j
(same for
\mathscr{V}_{j}
and
\mathscr{W}_{j}
), but this restriction is redundant, since otherwise the product of the
\mathfrak{B}
factors will be zero due to the
\mathbf{1}_{r<t<r+1}
factor in
\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{Y}]
as in (
6.32
).

Now we rearrange the time variables
t_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{+})
,
s_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{-})
and
u_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m_{0})
into
t>v_{1}>\cdots>v_{m}>q^{\prime}
, and let
A_{+}
be the set of
j
such that
v_{j}
equals some
t_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq m_{+})
, similarly define
A_{-}
and
A_{0}
, then these subsets completely determine the rule of correspondence between the variables
(v_{j})
and
(t_{i},s_{i},u_{i})
. Note that
s_{\mathrm{min}}=\min(v_{m},s)
, and

\sum_{(\mathscr{U}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{U}_{m_{+}},\mathscr{V}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{V}_{m_{-}},\mathscr{W}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{W}_{m_{0}})}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{+}}\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{U}_{j}](t_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{m_{-}}\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{V}_{j}](s_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{m_{0}}\mathfrak{B}[\mathscr{W}_{j}](u_{j})=\sum_{(\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{1}},\cdots,\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{m}})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathfrak{B}[\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{j}}](v_{j}),

where the latter summation is taken over all permutations
(\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{1}},\cdots,\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{m}})
of
(\mathscr{Y}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{Y}_{m})
. Note also that any
j
for which
v_{j}<s
can only be put in
A_{+}
or
A_{-}
, and any
j
for which
v_{j}>s
can only be put in
A_{+}
or
A_{0}
. Then we can reduce (
6.33
) to

\int_{t>v_{1}>\cdots>v_{m}>q^{\prime}}\sum_{(\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{1}},\cdots,\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{m}})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathfrak{B}(\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{j}})(v_{j})\cdot\mathfrak{B}_{\mathscr{X}_{\mathrm{lp}}}\left(\min(v_{m},s)\right)\\
\times\sum_{A_{+}\cup A_{-}\cup A_{0}=\{1,\cdots,m\}}\prod_{j\in A_{-}}(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{v_{j}<s}\prod_{j\in A_{0}}(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{v_{j}>s}\,\mathrm{d}v_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}v_{m},

(6.34)

where the first sum is taken over all permutations
(\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{1}},\cdots,\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{m}})
of
(\mathscr{Y}_{1},\cdots,\mathscr{Y}_{m})
, the second sum is taken over all partitions of
\{1,\cdots,m\}
into
(A_{+},A_{-},A_{0})
, and
\mathtt{I}_{j}
is the “
\mathtt{I}
” component of
\widetilde{\mathscr{Y}_{j}}
. Finally, since

\sum_{(A_{+},A_{-},A_{0})}\prod_{j\in A_{-}}(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{v_{j}<s}\prod_{j\in A_{0}}(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{v_{j}>s}=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(1+(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{v_{j}<s}+(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{v_{j}>s}\right)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(1+(-1)^{\mathtt{I}_{j}}\right),

we know that this expression must be
0
as long as
Z\neq\varnothing
, since this implies that at least one
\mathtt{I}_{j}=1
. This completes the proof.

Figure 11.
An example of two terms, associated with
(\mathcal{Q}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{1})
and
(\mathcal{Q}_{2},\mathcal{T}_{2})
, that occur in the summation with the same unordered collection of extended tuples. Here
r_{A}
etc. are layers,
\mathcal{Q}_{A,1}
etc. (with
Z_{A,1}=\varnothing
etc.) are equivalent to
\mathcal{Q}_{A,1}^{\prime}
etc., and
[Z]
indicates that the corresponding branching node belongs to
Z
.

∎

7.
Molecules, vines and twists

7.1.
Molecules

We recall the notion of molecules introduced in
[
19
,
20
]
.

Definition 7.1

(Molecules
[
19
,
20
]
)
.

A
molecule

\mathbb{M}
is a directed graph, formed by vertices (called
atoms
) and edges (called
bonds
), where multiple and self-connecting bonds are allowed. We will write
v\in\mathbb{M}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{M}
for atoms
v
and bonds
\ell
in
\mathbb{M}
, and write
\ell\leftrightarrow v
if
v
is one of the two endpoints of
\ell
. The degree of an atom
v
(including bonds of both directions) is denoted by
d(v)
. We further require that (i) each atom has at most
2
outgoing bonds and at most
2
incoming bonds (a self-connecting bond counts as outgoing once and incoming once), and that (ii) there is no saturated (connected) component, where connectedness is always understood in terms of undirected graphs, and a component is saturated if it contains only degree
4
atoms. For a molecule
\mathbb{M}
we define
V
to be the number of atoms,
E
the number of bonds and
F
the number of components. Define
\chi:=E-V+F
to be its
circuit rank
. Finally, by an
atomic group
we mean any subset
\mathbb{G}
of atoms of
\mathbb{M}
, together with all the bonds between atoms in
\mathbb{G}
.

Definition 7.2

(Molecules from gardens
[
19
,
20
]
)
.

Given a garden
\mathcal{G}
, define the molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
associated with
\mathcal{G}
, as follows. The atoms of
\mathbb{M}
are the branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}
of
\mathcal{G}
. For any two atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
, we connect them by a bond if either (i)
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
is the parent of
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
, or (ii) a child of
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
is paired to a child of
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
as leaves. We fix the direction of each bond as follows: in case (i) the bond should go from
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
(or
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
) if
\mathfrak{n}_{2}
has sign
-
(or sign
+
); in case (ii) the bond should go from the
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
whose paired child has sign
-
to the one whose paired child has sign
+
.

For any atom
v\in\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
, let
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}(v)
be the corresponding branching node in
\mathcal{G}
. We also introduce a labeling system to bonds in
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})

in addition to its molecule structure
: for any bond between
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, if
\mathfrak{n}(v_{1})
is the parent of
\mathfrak{n}(v_{2})
then we label this bond by PC, place a label P at
v_{1}
, and place a label C at
v_{2}
; otherwise we label this bond by LP. Note that one atom
v
may receive multiple P and C labels coming from different bonds
\ell\leftrightarrow v
. Finally, for any bond
\ell\leftrightarrow v
, define also
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}(v,\ell)
such that (i) if
\ell
is PC with
v
labeled C, then
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}(v)
; (ii) if
\ell
is PC with
v
labeled P, then
\mathfrak{m}
is the branching node corresponding to the other endpoint of
\ell
(which is a child of
\mathfrak{n}(v)
); (iii) if
\ell
is LP then
\mathfrak{m}
is the leaf in the leaf pair defining
\ell
that is a child of
\mathfrak{n}(v)
.

Note that, if
\mathcal{G}
is a pre-layered garden, then this naturally leads to a layering of the corresponding molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
, where each atom
v
is in layer
\mathfrak{L}_{v}:=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}(v)}
, which is the layer of the branching node
\mathfrak{n}(v)
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

If
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}
is a nontrivial couple of order
n
, then
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
has either two atoms of degree
3
or one atom of degree
2
, and the other atoms all have degree
4
; in particular it has
n
atoms and
2n-1
bonds, and circular rank
\chi(\mathbb{M})=n
. If
\mathcal{G}
is an irreducible garden of width
2R
and order
n
, then
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
has
n
atoms and
2n-R
bonds, and circular rank
\chi(\mathbb{M})=n-R+1
, in particular
n\geq R-1
. In both cases
\mathbb{M}
is connected. Finally, the number of gardens
\mathcal{G}
with fixed order
n
, fixed width
2R
, and fixed molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
(as a directed graph), is at most
(C_{0}R)!C_{0}^{n}
.

Proof.

The count for atoms and bonds follows from Definition
7.2
(some discussion is needed when some trees of
\mathcal{G}
is trivial), which also implies the statement about degrees when
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{Q}
is a couple. Connectivity follows because all atoms (branching nodes) in the same tree are connected by PC bonds, and those from different trees are connected by LP bonds due to irreducibility of
\mathcal{G}
. Finally, the bound on the number of choices for
\mathcal{G}
is proved in Proposition 6.4 of
[
20
]
.
∎

Definition 7.4

(Decorations of molecules
[
19
,
20
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule, suppose we also fix the vectors
c_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
for each
v\in\mathbb{M}
such that
c_{v}=0
when
v
has degree
4
. We then define a
(c_{v})
-
decoration
(or just a decoration) of
\mathbb{M}
to be a set of vectors
(k_{\ell})
for all bonds
\ell\in\mathbb{M}
, such that
k_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and

\sum_{\ell\leftrightarrow v}\zeta_{v,\ell}k_{\ell}=c_{v}

(7.1)

for each atom
v\in\mathbb{M}
. Here the sum is taken over all bonds
\ell\leftrightarrow v
, and
\zeta_{v,\ell}
equals
1
if
\ell
is outgoing from
v
, and equals
-1
otherwise. For each such decoration and each atom
v
, define also that

\Gamma_{v}=\sum_{\ell\leftrightarrow v}\zeta_{v,\ell}|k_{\ell}|^{2}.

(7.2)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
comes from a garden
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{T}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{2R})
with signature
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
. Let
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
be the root of
\mathcal{T}_{j}
. By Definition
7.2
, we have
d(v)<4
for an atom
v
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}(v)=\mathfrak{r}_{i}
for some
i
or a child of
\mathfrak{n}(v)
is paired with
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
as leaves for some
j
(or both). Denote these two possibilities by
\mathfrak{R}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{P}_{j}
. Now, for
k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\,(1\leq j\leq 2R)
satisfying
\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{2R}k_{2R}=0
, we define a
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}
to be a
(c_{v})
-decoration with
(c_{v})
given by

c_{v}=-\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}}\cdot\zeta_{i}k_{i}+\sum_{j}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}}\cdot\zeta_{j}k_{j}\bigg),

(7.3)

where the summation is taking over all
j
such that
\mathfrak{P}_{j}
holds. Note also that for
couples
\mathcal{Q}
and
k
-decorations of
\mathcal{Q}
, the vectors involved (including
k
itself) may be general
\mathbb{R}^{d}
vectors as in Definition
3.5
. Thus we shall define
k
-decorations of
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
for any
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the same way as above, but allow
k_{\ell}
and
c_{v}
to be general
\mathbb{R}^{d}
vectors such that
k_{\ell}-k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
.

Given any
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration of
\mathcal{G}
in the sense of Definition
3.5
, define a
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
such that
k_{\ell}=k_{\mathfrak{m}(v,\ell)}
for an endpoint
v
of
\ell
(Definition
7.2
). We can verify that this
k_{\ell}
is well-defined (i.e. does not depend on the choice of
v
), and gives a bijection between
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decorations of
\mathcal{G}
and
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
