. For such decorations we have

\Gamma_{v}=-\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}(v)}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}(v)}-\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{R}_{i}}\cdot\zeta_{i}|k_{i}|^{2}+\sum_{j}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{P}_{j}}\cdot\zeta_{j}|k_{j}|^{2}\bigg).

(7.4)

All the above definitions also work in the case of
k
-decorations of couples and molecules, which allows for general vectors
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Finally, given
\beta_{v}\in\mathbb{R}
for each
v\in\mathbb{M}
and
k_{\ell}^{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
for each
\ell\in\mathbb{M}
, we define a decoration
(k_{\ell})
to be
restricted by

(\beta_{v})
and/or
(k_{\ell}^{0})
, if
|\Gamma_{v}-\beta_{v}|\leq\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma}
for each
v
and/or
|k_{\ell}-k_{\ell}^{0}|\leq 1
for each
\ell
.

7.2.
Blocks, vines and ladders

On the molecular level, the combinatorial structures of
blocks
with the special case of
vines
, as well as
ladders
, were introduced in
[
22
]
and also play a fundamental role in the current paper.

7.2.1.
Blocks

We start by recalling the definition of blocks.

Definition 7.5

(Blocks
[
22
]
)
.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
, an atomic group
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{M}
is called a
block
, if all atoms in
\mathbb{B}
have degree
4
within
\mathbb{B}
, except for exactly two atoms
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
(called
joints
of the block, the other atoms called
interior atoms
), each of which having out-degree
1
and in-degree
1
(hence total degree
2
) within
\mathbb{B}
, see Figure
12
. Define
\sigma(\mathbb{B})
as the number of bonds between
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Note that
\sigma(\mathbb{B})\in\{0,1,2\}
, and
\sigma(\mathbb{B})=2
if and only if
\mathbb{B}
is a double bond. Moreover, we define a
hyper-block

\mathbb{H}
to be the atomic group formed by adding one bond between the two joints
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
of a block
\mathbb{B}
(we call
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{B}
the
adjoint
of each other), and define
\sigma(\mathbb{H})=\sigma(\mathbb{B})+1
.

If two blocks share one common joint and no other common atom, we define their
concatenation
to be their union, which is either a block or a hyper-block (depending on whether the two other joints of the two blocks are connected by a bond), see Figure
12
. Note that a hyper-block cannot be concatenated with another block or hyper-block in this way. In general any finitely many (at least two) blocks can be concatenated to form a new block
\mathbb{B}
, or a new hyper-block
\mathbb{H}
, in which case we must have
\sigma(\mathbb{B})=0
and
\sigma(\mathbb{H})=1
.

Figure 12.
An illustration of a block, and two concatenated blocks sharing a joint, as in Definition
7.5
. Here the two joint atoms at the end are colored blue, the one common joint atom colored red, and all other atoms have degree
4
. Longer chains can be constructed similarly.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule. Suppose
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{M}
, each of them is a block or a hyper-block, and
\mathbb{A}\not\subset\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{B}\not\subset\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{B}\neq\varnothing
.

Let
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
be the joints of
\mathbb{A}
, and
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
be the joints of
\mathbb{B}
. Suppose further that (i)
\mathbb{B}\backslash\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
is connected, and (ii) for any
v\in\mathbb{B}\backslash\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
, the subset
\mathbb{B}\backslash\{v\}
is either connected, or has two connected components containing
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
respectively, and (iii) the same holds for
\mathbb{A}
.

Then
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
are both blocks, and exactly one of the three following scenarios happens: (a)
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
share two common joints and no other common atom, and
\sigma(\mathbb{A})=\sigma(\mathbb{B})=1
, (b)
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
share one common joint and no other common atom, and can be concatenated like in Definition
7.5
; (c)
\mathbb{A}
is formed by concatenating two blocks
\mathbb{C}_{0}
and
\mathbb{C}_{1}
, and
\mathbb{B}
is formed by concatenating
\mathbb{C}_{1}
with another block
\mathbb{C}_{2}
(where
\mathbb{C}_{0}\cap\mathbb{C}_{2}=\varnothing
).

Proof.

See Lemma 4.11 of
[
22
]
.∎

Next, we recall a result regarding the relative position of a block
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
in a garden
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition 7.7

(Structure of blocks in gardens)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a garden of width
2R
and
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
be a block with two joints
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, and let
\mathfrak{u}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}(v_{j})
with the notations in Definition
7.2
.

(1)

Then, up to symmetry, exactly one of the following two scenarios happens.

(a)

There is a child
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
and two children
\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
, such that (i)
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
has the same sign as
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
has sign
+
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
has sign
-
, (ii)
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a descendant
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
but not of
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
, and (iii) all the leaves in the set
\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
are completely paired, where
\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
denotes all nodes that are descendants of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
but not of
\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21}
or
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
(in particular
\mathfrak{u}_{1}\in\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
and
\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22}\not\in\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
). Here we say
\mathbb{B}
is a
(CL) block
and also denote
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
as the child node of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
other than
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
, see Figure
13
.

(b)

There is a child
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
, such that (i)
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
has the same sign as
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
has the same sign as
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
, (ii)
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is either a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
or not a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
(similar for
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
), and (iii) all the leaves in the set
\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
are completely paired, where
\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
denotes all the nodes that are descendants of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
but not of
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
, and all the nodes that are descendants of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
but not of
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
(in particular
\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{2}\in\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
and
\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21}\not\in\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
). Here we say
\mathbb{B}
is a
(CN) block
, see Figure
14
.

(2)

For (CL) blocks
\mathbb{B}
we can define a new garden
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{sp}}
by removing all nodes
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
, and turning
\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
into the three new children of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
with corresponding subtrees attached; here the position of
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
as a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
remains the same as in
\mathcal{G}
, and the positions of
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
as children of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
are determined by their signs. Then, the molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\mathrm{sp}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{sp}})
is formed from
\mathbb{M}
by merging all the atoms in
\mathbb{B}
(including two joints) into one single atom. We call this operation going from
\mathcal{G}
to
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{sp}}

splicing
.

Figure 13.
A (CL) block (as in Proposition
7.7
) viewed in the garden, together with the result of splicing. Here
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
is the right (or left) child of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
(assume both have sign
+
)
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
have signs
+
and
-
respectively, and all the leaves in the red circle (including
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
) are completely paired.

Figure 14.
A (CN) block, as in Proposition
7.7
, viewed in the garden. Here
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
is the right (or left) child of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
is the right (or left) child of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
, and all the leaves in the two red circles are completely paired.

Proof.

For couples
\mathcal{Q}
, this is proved in Proposition 4.12 of
[
22
]
. The general gardens
\mathcal{G}
make no difference.
∎

Proposition 7.8
.

Given a garden
\mathcal{G}
of width
2R
. Consider any (CL) block in
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
, we call it a
root block
if the total degree of its two joints is at most
6
. Then, if we remove
any set of disjoint (CN) and root (CL) blocks
, where by removing a block
\mathbb{B}
we mean removing all non-joint atoms and all bonds
\ell\in\mathbb{B}
, then the resulting molecule
has at most
2R
connected components. If there are exactly
2R
components, then one of them must be an isolated atom.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.3
(decomposing
\mathcal{G}
into irreducible gardens if necessary) and counting degrees, we see that there are at most
R
root (CL) blocks. For each root (CL) block, either (i) one of its joints has degree
2
, or both of its joints have degree
3
. In the latter case, due to a simple discussion using Definition
7.2
, we see that there are only two possibilities, namely that (ii)
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
is the root of one tree
\mathcal{T}_{1}
in
\mathcal{G}
, and a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
(say
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
) is paired with the root of another tree
\mathcal{T}_{2}
as leaves, or (iii)
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
and a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
(say
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
) are paired with the roots of two trees in
\mathcal{G}
as leaves. Moreover each root (CL) block in case (iii) corresponds to two trivial trees in
\mathcal{G}
, so there are at most
R-1
of them (and there is none when
R=1
).

We may choose a root (CL) block
\mathbb{B}_{0}
that is either in case (i) or case (ii) (if there are at most
R-1
root (CL) blocks then we do not need to choose
\mathbb{B}_{0}
and the proof works the same way). If
\mathbb{B}_{0}
is case (i), then we first remove all the (CN) blocks. As in the proof of Proposition 4.12 in
[
22
]
, we may define the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
for generalized gardens
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
formed by
2R
trees whose branching nodes have
one or three
children nodes (plus that we only keep the pairing structure but ignore the signs of nodes and directions of bonds), similar to Definition
7.2
. Then, the resulting molecule after removing all the (CN) blocks will be
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
, where
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a generalized garden such that for each removed (CN) block, only nodes
\{\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{2},\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21}\}
from
\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
remain in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and that
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
is the only child of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
is the only child of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
.

It is now easy to see that
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
has at most
R
connected components, because each tree still has an odd number of leaves that cannot all be paired with each other. We next remove
\mathbb{B}_{0}
which is in case (i), and generates one new connected component which is an isolated atom; subsequently, removing the remaining
R-1
root (CL) blocks generates at most
R-1
new connected components, so the result is true. Finally, if
\mathbb{B}_{0}
is in case (ii), then we first remove
\mathbb{B}_{0}
, the resulting molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
will be
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
plus two separate extra single bonds, where
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the new garden with the two trees
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
in
\mathcal{G}
replaced by the two trees rooted at
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
and

\mathfrak{u}_{22}
. Since all the (CN) blocks in
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
are are also (CN) blocks in
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
, we can repeat the above argument and still get at most
2R-1
connected components. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

The set
\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
, as defined in Proposition
7.7
, will be called the
realization
of the block
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
in
\mathcal{G}
. Denote also
\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{B}]
and
\mathcal{L}[\mathbb{B}]
to be the set of branching nodes and leaves in
\mathcal{G}[\mathbb{B}]
.

Corollary 7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a garden and
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
be a block or hyper-block that is concatenated by at least two blocks
\mathbb{B}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq m)
as in Definition
7.5
, where
m\geq 2
. Then at most one
\mathbb{B}_{j}
can be a (CN) block. If
\mathbb{B}
is a block and all
\mathbb{B}_{j}
are (CL) blocks, then
\mathbb{B}
is a (CL) block. If
\mathbb{B}
is a block and there is one (CN) block
\mathbb{B}_{j}
, then after doing splicing at all other (CL) blocks, this
\mathbb{B}
becomes a single (CN) block
\mathbb{B}_{j}
.

Proof.

See Corollary 4.13 of
[
22
]
.
∎

7.2.2.
Vines and ladders

Next we recall the definition of vines and ladders in
[
22
]
.

Definition 7.11

(Vines and ladders
[
22
]
)
.

Vines
are defined as the blocks (I)–(VIII) illustrated in Figure
15
. We also define the notion of
ladders
as illustrated in Figure
15
. We require each ladder to have
m+1\geq 2
double bonds, and that each pair of two parallel single bonds must have opposite directions. Define the
length
of a ladder to be
m\geq 1
. We refer to vines (I)–(II) as
bad vines
, and vines (III)–(VIII) as
normal vines
. Note that
\sigma(\mathbb{V})=0
for all vines
\mathbb{V}
except vines (V) and vines (I) (see Definition
7.5
), for which
\sigma(\mathbb{V})=1
and
\sigma(\mathbb{V})=2
respectively.

Define
hyper-vines
(or HV for short) to be the hyper-blocks that are adjoints of vines, as in Definition
7.5
. We also define
vine-chains
(or VC’s), resp.
hyper-vine-chains
(or HVC’s), to be the blocks, resp. hyper-blocks, that are formed by concatenating finitely many vines as in Definition
7.5
(these vines are called
ingredients
). Note that a single vine is viewed as a VC, but an HV is not viewed as an HVC. It is easy to verify that assumptions (i) and (ii) in Lemma
7.6
hold for any HV, VC or HVC. For simplicity, we will refer to any HV, VC or HVC as
vine-like objects
.

Note that, if the molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
comes from a garden, then any vine could be a (CL) or (CN) vine depending on whether it is a (CL) or (CN) block.

Figure 15.
Vines (I)–(VIII). Here some bonds are drawn with directions, indicating that certain pairs of bonds must have opposite directions (see Definition
7.5
). Moreover, a ladder may be inserted between each pair of parallel bonds that are drawn as dashed lines (distinguished by different colors).

The next proposition discussed the relative position of (CL) vines (I), and the part of (CL) vines (II) near one of its joints, in a garden.

Proposition 7.12

(Structure of vines in gardens)
.

Consider a (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
with joints
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Let
\mathfrak{u}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}(v_{j})
, by Proposition
7.7
we may assume
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
, and also specify two children
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
that have signs
+
and
-
respectively; let
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
be the other child of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
(as in Proposition
7.7
), note that
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
has the same sign as
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
.

(1)

If
\mathbb{V}
is Vine (I), then exactly one of the following two scenarios happens. See Figure
16
.

(a)

Vine (I-a):
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is the left or right child of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
, and
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
is paired to the middle child
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
as leaves.

(b)

Vine (I-b):
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is the middle child of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
, and
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
is paired to the left or right child
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
as leaves.

(2)

If
\mathbb{V}
is Vine (II), then
v_{2}
is connected to two atoms
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
by single bonds, while
v_{3}
and
v_{4}
are connected by a double bond. Let
\mathfrak{u}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}(v_{j})
, then (up to symmetry) exactly one of the following five scenarios happens. See Figure

.

(a)

Vine (II-a):
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
, and
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
is paired with one child
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
as leaves, and the other two children of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
are paired with the other two children of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
as leaves. Here neither
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
nor
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
is a descendant of the other, but they have a common ancestor, namely
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
.

(b)

Vine (II-b):
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
is paired with one child
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves, and the other two children of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
are paired with the other two children of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves. Here neither
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
nor
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is a descendant of the other, but they have a common ancestor, namely
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
.

(c)

Vine (II-c):
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
. One of the the other two children of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is paired with one of the other children of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves, and the remaining child
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is paired with
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
as leaves. Here
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
.

(d)

Vine (II-d):
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
are two children of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
is paired with one child
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves, and the remaining child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is paired with another child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves. Here
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
.

(e)

Vine (II-e):
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
, and
\mathfrak{u}_{4}=\mathfrak{u}_{23}
. The other two children of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
are paired with two of the children of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves. Here
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
.

Figure 16
.
Vines (II-a)–(II-e) and (I-a)–(I-b) in gardens (the relevant positions of nodes may vary). Here the labels of the bonds in the molecule are indicated as in Definition

. The nodes in white color are called

, with a tree

For simplicity, below we will call a (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}

core
if it is bad and not Vine (II-e), and
non-core
if it is normal or Vine (II-e).

Proof.

]
.

Remark 7.13
.

Note that the classification of vines (I)–(VIII) only involves the structure of the molecule
\mathbb{M}
(as a directed graph), but the distinction between (CL) and (CN) vines, as well as classification of vines (II-a)–(II-e), is intrinsic to the structure of the
garden

\mathcal{G}
; for instance, it does not make sense to talk about (CL) vines or vines (II-e) if
\mathbb{M}
does not have the form
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
.

7.3.
Twists

By exploiting the structure of bad (CL) vines in a garden, as described in Proposition
7.12
, we can define the operation of
twisting
, which captures the cancellation between such vines.

Definition 7.14

(Twists
[
22
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a given garden with the corresponding molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
, and let
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
be a core (CL) vine as in Proposition
7.12
. Let
v_{j}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq 4
be as in Proposition
7.12
, we shall define a new garden
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, which we call a
unit twist
of
\mathcal{G}
, as follows.

First, in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, let any possible parent-child relation, as well as any possible children pairings, between
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
, be exactly the same as in
\mathcal{G}
. Next, let the structure of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}

excluding the subtrees rooted at
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
, or the subtree rooted at
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
for Vine (I)
, be exactly the same as
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover, consider the
free children
in Figure
16
, i.e. the nodes in white color; we require that the positions of the two free children
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
(as children of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
), as well as the positions of the two subtrees (namely
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
) rooted at them, be
switched
10
10

10

They are switched because the sign of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is changed (see Remark
7.16
); if we locate
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
as the child of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
other than
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
that has sign
+
(and same for
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
but with sign
-
), then this remains the same for both gardens.
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
compared to
\mathcal{G}
. For the other free child (if it exists), we require that its position (as a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
or
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
) and the subtree (namely
\mathcal{T}_{3}
) rooted at it, be exactly the same in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as in
\mathcal{G}
. Then, it is easy to see that there are exactly two options to insert
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
, one as a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
, and the other as a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
(for Vine (I), the two options are children of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
that has the same or opposite sign with
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
). One of these two choices leads to
\mathcal{G}
, and we define the garden given by the other choice as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Clearly
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is prime iff
\mathcal{G}
is, where we recall the notion of prime gardens in Proposition
4.5
.

In general, suppose we start with a collection of (CL) vines
\mathbb{V}_{j}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})\,(0\leq j\leq q-1)
, such that any two are either disjoint or only share one common joint and no other common atom (i.e. the union of all
\mathbb{V}_{j}
equals the disjoint union of finitely many VC’s and HVC’s). Then, we call any garden
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
a
twist
of
\mathcal{G}
, if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by performing the unit twist operation at a subset of these blocks, which only contains core vines. In particular, for any given
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathbb{V}_{j}
, the number of possible twists is a power of two, and at most
2^{q}
.

Since the notion of twisting is of vital importance in our proof (especially in Section
9
), we will make several remarks below explaining Definition
7.14
in more detail.

Remark 7.15
.

We discuss an example of the (unit) twist operation in Definition
7.14
. Suppose
\mathbb{V}
is Vine (II-c) or (II-d) in Figure
16
. Then we have that:

•

The node
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
is the left child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
, and the middle child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
is paired with the right child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves.

•

The left child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
is a free child with subtree
\mathcal{T}_{3}
. The left and middle children of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
are the two free children
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
(or
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
), with the subtrees rooted at
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
being
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
respectively.

•

\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
(or
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
), and the right child
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is paired to a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
(or
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
) as leaves.

Now by Definition
7.14
, all these properties must hold in both
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
; also the structure of
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, excluding the subtree rooted at
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
, must be the same. This leaves only two possibilities: either
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is
the middle child
of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
is paired to
the right child
of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
as leaves, or
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is
the right child
of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
is paired to
the middle child
of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
as leaves. These are exactly vines (II-c) and (II-d) in in Proposition
7.12
. Note that for vines (II-c),
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
is the left child of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
is the middle child, while for vines (II-d)
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
is the left child and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
is the middle child, which is consistent with the description in Definition
7.14
.

In the same way, we can see that performing one unit twist operation
exactly switches vines (I-a), (II-a), (II-c) vines with vines (I-b), (II-b), (II-d) vines
, respectively.

Remark 7.16
.

Throughout the proof below, for any fixed (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}
, we always adopt the notations
(\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{2},\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22})
as in Proposition
7.7
; for bad (CL) vines we also adopt the notations
(\mathfrak{u}_{3},\mathfrak{u}_{4},\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})
as in Proposition
7.12
, whenever applicable. The following useful facts are easily verified from Definition
7.14
. They are stated for unit twists but can be extended to general twists.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be unit twists of each other at a bad (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
, then
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
are the same as directed graphs. They also have the same labelings of bonds, except at the atom
v_{2}
, where the labels of the two bonds connecting
v_{2}
to atoms in
\mathbb{V}
are switched (one label is PC with
v_{2}
labeled C and the other label is LP), see Figure
16
.

(2)

The values of
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{j}}
for any branching node
\mathfrak{u}_{j}\,(j\neq 2)
are the same for
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, while the values of
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}
are the opposite for
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(3)

If we do splicing (as defined in Proposition
7.7
) for
\mathcal{G}
and the (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}
, or for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and the same (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}
(as shown in (a) above), then the two resulting
gardens
, defined as
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{sp}}
and
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{sp}}
, are the same.

(4)

The
k
-decorations of
\mathcal{G}
are in bijection with
k
-decorations of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, where the values of
k_{\mathfrak{m}}
for any branching node or leaf
\mathfrak{m}
are the same in both cases, but we
switch
the values of
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}
and
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}}
, see Figure
17
.

(5)

Moreover, for any
k
-decoration of
\mathcal{G}
and the corresponding
k
-decoration of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as in (d), the decorations of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{sp}}=(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{sp}}

inherited
from the are the same; here
inheriting
means that the value of
k_{\mathfrak{m}}
is kept the same for any
\mathfrak{m}
, whether it is viewed as a node of
\mathcal{G}
or
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{sp}}
(this notion will also be used in other similar settings).

Figure 17.
Decorations for two gardens with Vine (II-c) and Vine (II-d) which are unit twists of each other, see Remark
7.16
. Note that, in the context of Figure
16
, the values of
k_{\mathfrak{m}}
are the same for each
\mathfrak{m}
, except that the values of
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}
and
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}}
are switched.

7.4.
Layered full twists

Note that the definition and discussion of twists in Section
7.3
involves only the garden structure of
\mathcal{G}
(and the molecule structure of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
), but not the
layering structure
. Since we are dealing with layered gardens in Proposition
3.11
, in the proof below we need to apply twisting operations that take into account the layerings and the regular trees and regular couples in Proposition
4.5
. These are called
layered full twists
or
LF twists
, which we will now discuss.

Before proceeding, we first make one observation: let
\mathcal{G}
be a garden obtained from another garden
\mathcal{G}_{1}
(which may be
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
) by replacing each leaf pair with a regular couple and each branching node with a regular tree, in the sense of Proposition
4.5
. Then, any layering of
\mathcal{G}
naturally induces a
pre-layering
of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
, where for each branching node
\mathfrak{m}
of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
, let the corresponding regular tree in
\mathcal{G}
be
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
, then we assign the layer of
\mathfrak{m}
in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
to be the layer of the
lone leaf
of
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 7.17
.

In general, a layering of
\mathcal{G}
only induces a pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
; however, by Proposition
4.9
, if
\mathcal{G}
is canonical, and if all the regular couples and regular trees involved in the process of obtaining
\mathcal{G}
from
\mathcal{G}_{1}
are
coherent
, then the pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{1}
can be uniquely extended to a canonical layering. In fact, the layer of each leaf pair
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})
is just
\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},\mathfrak{L}_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})
, where
\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}
and
(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}
are the parents of
\mathfrak{l}
and
\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.18

(Layered full twists)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(or
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
) be a canonical layered object, and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
be its skeleton as in Proposition
4.5
. We then have a pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
as described above (and also a layering of atoms of the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
following Definition
7.2
). For any branching node
\mathfrak{m}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
and any leaf pair
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, let the corresponding regular tree and regular couple be
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
.

Now let
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
be a core (CL) vine as in Definition
7.14
, and let the notations

(\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{2},\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22},\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0},\mathfrak{u}_{3},\mathfrak{u}_{4})

be as in Propositions
7.7
and
7.12
(note
(\mathfrak{u}_{3},\mathfrak{u}_{4})
is absent for vines (I-a) and (I-b)). Assume further that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}\leq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}})
for the pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, and that the regular tree
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}
and the regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}
in
\mathcal{G}
are both
coherent
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
be the unit twist of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
at
\mathbb{V}
as in Definition
7.14
, then the branching nodes and leaf pairs of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
are in one-to-one correspondence with those of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
: the branching node
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
and the leaf pair
(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})
appear in both gardens, and the other branching nodes and leaf pairs are exactly the same. We then define a
unit layered full twist
(or
unit LF twist
)
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{G}
, as follows.

Let the pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
be given such that the layer
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}}
of each branching node
\mathfrak{m}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
equals the layer of
\mathfrak{m}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
. To form
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, starting from either
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
or
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
, we replace each branching node
\mathfrak{m}\neq\mathfrak{u}_{2}
by the
same
regular tree
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
as in
\mathcal{G}
, and replace each leaf pair
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})\neq(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})
by the
same
regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
, with the same layerings. Then we replace the coherent regular tree
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}
and regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}
by regular tree
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}^{\,\prime}
and regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}^{\,\prime}
respectively, which satisfy the followings:

•

The layer of the lone leaf of
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}^{\,\prime}
equals the layer
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}
of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
.

•

The objects
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}^{\,\prime}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}^{\,\prime}
are coherent, and have the exact structure described in Proposition
4.9
, where
(q,q^{\prime})
in Proposition
4.9
are given by the pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
.

•

We have
n(\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}^{\,\prime})+n(\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}^{\,\prime})=n(\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})})+n(\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})})
, and the total number of
layer
p
branching nodes in
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}^{\,\prime}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}^{\,\prime}
equals that of
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}
.

In general, suppose we start with a collection of (CL) vines
\mathbb{V}_{j}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})\,(0\leq j\leq q-1)
, such that any two are either disjoint or only share one common joint and no other common atom. Then, we call any garden
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
a
layered full twist
(or
LF twist
) of
\mathcal{G}
, if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by performing some unit LF twist operation at each
\mathbb{V}_{j}
that is core and satisfies the above assumptions (i.e.
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}\leq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}})
and that
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}
are both coherent).

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
and let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be an LF twist of
\mathcal{G}
as in Definition
7.18
, then we have
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
and
n(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=n(\mathcal{G})
. Recall also the definition of
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
in Definition
3.10
. If
n(\mathcal{G})\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
, then we have
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
if and only if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
. The same holds for
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
and
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
.

Proof.

We only need to consider
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
and unit LF twists. The fact that
n(\mathcal{G})=n(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
is obvious from definition. Moreover, if
n(\mathcal{G})\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
, then the requirement (ii) in the definition of
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
and
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
in Definition
3.10
is redundant; also the requirement (i) is just that the number of
layer
p
branching nodes in each of the trees in
\mathcal{G}
is at most
N_{p+1}
. But for each tree, this number is the same for
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
due to the requirements about layer
p
branching nodes in Definition
7.18
, so we only need to prove
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(same for
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
), then it will follow that
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
.

Now we prove
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
. We may assume that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is constructed starting from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
(the unit twist of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
), since the case when
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is constructed from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
is easier. Next, in
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we shall replace both coherent regular trees
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}^{\,\prime}
by the single node
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
, and both coherent regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})}^{\,\prime}
by the leaf pair
(\mathfrak{u}_{23},\mathfrak{u}_{0})
. By Proposition
4.9
and Remark
7.17
, we know that these operations do not change the canonicity of
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Suppose the layered gardens
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
become
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
after these operations, then
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is a unit LF twist of
\mathcal{H}
as in Definition
7.14
with each node layered in the same way as the corresponding node in
\mathcal{H}
. we only need to prove that if
\mathcal{H}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
then
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
.

To show
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
, we only need to verify that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}\leq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{n}}
for any branching node
\mathfrak{n}
and its child node
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
, and also verify the statement of Proposition
4.8
for any
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
with all their descendant leaves completely paired. The former is true because the layering of
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is the same as that of
\mathcal{H}
, and all the parent-child relations in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{H}
are also the same (except that
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
in vines (II-a) and (II-c), while it is a child of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
in vines (II-b) and (II-d), but this does not matter because
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}\leq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}})
in any case). The latter can be verified by examining the different cases in Figure
16
; for example, consider when
\mathcal{H}
is Vine (II-a) and
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is Vine (II-b). Let
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
be arbitrary nodes in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
as in Proposition
4.8
. If neither of
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
is an ancestor node of
\mathfrak{u}_{23}
or
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
, then
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
is not affected by the twisting, hence the result for
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
follows from that of
\mathcal{H}
. If one of
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})
is an ancestor of
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
(or
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
), then either
\mathfrak{n}
or
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
must also be an ancestor of
\mathfrak{u}_{4}
(or
\mathfrak{u}_{3}
) due to complete pairing of leaves, in which case all the descendant leaves of
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
are also completely paired in
\mathcal{H}
, so the result again follows. The remaining cases are when
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{u}_{0},\mathfrak{u}_{23})
or
(\mathfrak{n},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{u}_{0},\mathfrak{u}_{2})
, which are again easily checked. The other cases in Figure
16
follow from similar discussions.
∎

8.
Layered vines and ladders: Combinatorics

In this section we first introduce the notion of
coherent
vines and ladders, which plays a similar role as that of coherent regular couples and regular trees in Definition
4.7
, but works on the molecular level.

Definition 8.1

(Coherent vines and ladders)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
be the molecule coming from a garden
\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{M}
is a vine as illustrated in Figure
15
. We may split the set of non-joint atoms of
\mathbb{V}
into groups of
2
or
3
elements, as follows:

•

For Vine (I) there is no non-joint atom.

•

For Vine (II) we put each pair of atoms connected by a double bond into one group.

•

For vines (III) and (V)–(VIII) there is a unique triangle that contains no joint, and for Vine (IV) there is a unique three-atom subset with two double bonds between them; we put these three atoms into one group.

•

For vines (III)–(VIII), after fixing this three-atom group, we put each pair of remaining atoms connected by a double bond into one group.

•

For Vine (VII) there are two atoms left after the above, and we put them in the same group.

Now, suppose
\mathcal{G}
is
pre-layered
, so each atom
v\in\mathbb{M}
has a layer
\mathfrak{L}_{v}
as in Definition
7.2
. Then, we define the vine
\mathbb{V}
to be
coherent
if the atoms in each group are in the same layer. Similarly, we define a ladder to be coherent, if any two atoms connected by a double bond are in the same layer. In both cases, for non-coherent objects, we define the
incoherency index
to be the number
g
of groups such not all atoms in this group are in the same layer.

8.1.
Number of coherent layerings

We now establish an upper bound for the number of coherent layerings of a given vine or ladder, in the same spirit as Proposition
4.10
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\mathbb{L}
be a ladder of length
m
in any molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G})
coming from a garden
\mathcal{G}
. Consider a pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}
which leads to a layering of atoms of
\mathbb{M}
; assume that
\mathbb{L}
has fixed incoherency index
g
under this layering, where
0\leq g\leq m+1
. Then we have

\sum_{(\mathfrak{L}_{v})}\prod_{\ell}C_{0}^{|\mathfrak{L}_{v}-\mathfrak{L}_{v^{\prime}}|}\leq C_{0}^{m}C_{2}^{g+1},

(8.1)

with possibly different values of
C_{0}
(see the convention in Section
3.3.2
), where the summation is taken over all possible layerings of atoms in
\mathbb{L}
with fixed
g
, and the product is taken over all bonds
\ell
connecting atoms
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}
.

Proof.

Given
g
, there are at most
\binom{m+1}{g}\leq 2^{m+1}
choices for the locations of the double bonds where incoherency happens. With these locations fixed, we may consider the ladder between any two consecutive locations which is then coherent, so we only need to prove the result when
g=0
. By disregarding the atoms at both ends of
\mathbb{L}
, we may also assume that all atoms in the ladder have degree
4
. Let the atoms in
\mathbb{L}
be
u_{j}
and
v_{j}
(which are connected by a double bond) where
0\leq j\leq m
, and define
\mathfrak{L}_{u_{j}}=\mathfrak{L}_{v_{j}}:=q_{j}
.

We claim that, for any
1\leq j\leq m-1
, either
q_{j}\leq q_{j-1}\leq\cdots\leq q_{0}
, or
q_{j}\leq q_{j+1}\leq\cdots\leq q_{m}
. In fact, suppose not, then there exist
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq j\leq j^{\prime\prime}\leq m-1
, such that

q_{j}\leq q_{j-1}\leq\cdots\leq q_{j^{\prime}},\,\,q_{j^{\prime}}>q_{j^{\prime}-1};\quad q_{j}\leq q_{j+1}\leq\cdots\leq q_{j^{\prime\prime}},\,\,q_{j^{\prime\prime}}>q_{j^{\prime\prime}+1}.

By symmetry, we may also assume
q_{j^{\prime}}\geq q_{j^{\prime\prime}}
. Starting from the atom
w_{1}:=u_{j^{\prime}}
, we successively choose
w_{i}
such that
\mathfrak{n}(w_{i})
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{n}(w_{i-1})
in the notation of Definition
7.2
, then
w_{i}
is connected to
w_{i-1}
by a bond, so if
w_{i}\in\{u_{j_{i}},v_{j_{i}}\}
then
j_{i}\in\{j_{i-1},j_{i-1}+1,j_{i-1}-1\}
. On the other hand
\mathfrak{L}_{w_{i}}
is nondecreasing in
i
, so
j_{i}
cannot equal
j^{\prime}-1
or
j^{\prime\prime}+1
, thus we have
j^{\prime}\leq j_{i}\leq j^{\prime\prime}
for each
i
, which means the process continues indefinitely instead of reaching either end of the ladder. This is clearly impossible as all the
w_{i}
must be different, which proves the claim.

Now, by the above claim, we know that the whole sequence
(q_{0},\cdots,q_{m})
can be divided into two monotonic subsequences
q_{0}\geq\cdots\geq q_{j}
and
q_{j+1}\leq\cdots\leq q_{m}
(just look at the place where the first inequality in the claim changes to the second inequality when
j
changes to
j+1
). It is well known that there are at most
2^{m+\mathfrak{D}+1}
ways to choose a nondecreasing sequence of
m+1
terms in
\{0,\cdots,\mathfrak{D}\}
, so the number of choices for
(q_{0},\cdots,q_{m})
is at most
2^{2(m+\mathfrak{D}+1)}=C_{0}^{m}C_{2}
. In addition, due to the monotonicity of the subsequences of
(q_{j})
, the product in (
8.1
) is also bounded by

C_{0}^{2(q_{0}-q_{j})}\cdot C_{0}^{2(q_{m}-q_{j+1})}\leq C_{0}^{2\mathfrak{D}}\leq C_{2}.

Putting together, this proves (
8.1
).
∎

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(or
\mathscr{C}_{p+1}
) be a canonical layered object, and let the skeleton
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, the molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
and the regular objects
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
be as in Definition
7.18
. Let
\mathbb{V}\subset\mathbb{M}
be any (CL) vine, note that the layering of
\mathcal{G}
induces a layering of atoms of
\mathbb{V}
by Section
7.4
and Definition
7.2
.

Assume
\mathbb{V}
contains
2m+2
(for bad vine) or
2m+5
(for normal vine) atoms, and has incoherency index
0\leq g\leq m
(for bad vine) or
0\leq g\leq m+1
(for normal vine). Let the two joints of
\mathbb{V}
be
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, such that
\mathfrak{u}_{1}=\mathfrak{n}(v_{1})
is an ancestor of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}=\mathfrak{n}(v_{2})
, and assume that
\mathfrak{L}_{v_{1}}=q
and
\mathfrak{L}_{v_{2}}=q^{\prime}
are given with
q\geq q^{\prime}
.

Then the same bound (
8.1
) holds, where the summation is taken over all possible layerings of atoms in
\mathbb{V}
with fixed
g
, and the product is taken over all bonds
\ell
connecting atoms
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{V}
. Moreover, if
g=0
and the regular objects
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
are
coherent
for all branching nodes
\mathfrak{m}
and leaf pairs
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})

that belong to

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{V}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
, then the right hand side of (
8.1
) can be improved to
C_{0}^{m+|q-q^{\prime}|}
. Here
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{V}]
is the realization of
\mathbb{V}
in the garden
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
as defined in Proposition
7.7
(see Remark
7.9
).

Proof.

First, (
8.1
) follows from Proposition
8.2
, because
\mathbb{V}
consists of at most three ladders plus less than
20
extra atoms, so we can apply Proposition
8.2
to the part of (
8.1
) involving atoms and bonds in one of these ladders, while the part involving the extra atoms contribute at most a factor of
C_{0}^{100\mathfrak{D}}\leq C_{2}
.

Now, we only need to consider the coherent case (
g=0
) and assume all the
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
for
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{V}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
are coherent. Using Remark
7.17
, we may replace each such
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
by a single node and each such
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
by a leaf pair, without affecting the canonicity of
\mathcal{G}
. Therefore, we may replace
\mathcal{G}
by the resulting garden
\mathcal{H}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}
(whose skeleton is also
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
) such that the same (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}
is contained in
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{H})
. Now we look at the possible layerings of
\mathbb{V}
and the product in (
8.1
).

The case of Vine (I) is obvious. For other vines except Vine (V), by the grouping in Definition
8.1
, we can always find groups
\mathbb{G}_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq r)
such that
v_{2}
is connected to
\mathbb{G}_{1}
, each
\mathbb{G}_{i}
is connected to
\mathbb{G}_{i+1}
, and
\mathbb{G}_{r}
is connected to
v_{1}
. Moreover, for each
i
, after removing atoms in
\mathbb{G}_{i}
, then vine becomes disconnected with
v_{2}
and
\mathbb{G}_{j}\,(j<i)
in one component, and
v_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{j}\,(j>i)
in another. These
\mathbb{G}_{i}
exhaust all groups for vines (II)–(IV), while for vines (VI)–(VIII), all the groups other than
\mathbb{G}_{i}
form exactly the shape which is Vine (V) minus two joints. Let the layer of atoms in
\mathbb{G}_{i}
be
\mathfrak{L}_{i}
. If we start from
v_{2}
and each time move from any atom
v
to
v^{+}
where
\mathfrak{n}(v^{+})
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{n}(v)
, just as in the proof of Proposition
8.2
, then eventually we will arrive at
v_{1}
since
\mathbb{V}
is a (CL) vine, due to Proposition
7.7
. In this process we must pass through atoms in
\mathbb{G}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{G}_{r}
in this order, due to the above properties of
\mathbb{G}_{i}
, which implies that
q^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{L}_{1}\leq\cdots\mathfrak{L}_{r}\leq q
. This implies that, both the number of choices for
\mathfrak{L}_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq r)
, and the product in (
8.1
) with the bond
\ell
restricted to those connecting two atoms in
\mathbb{G}_{i}
and
\mathbb{G}_{j}
, are bounded by
C_{0}^{m+|q-q^{\prime}|}
.

This already completes the proof of the improved bound for vines (II)–(IV). As for vines (V)–(VIII), we only need to look at the remaining atoms and bonds not in the groups
\mathbb{G}_{i}
, which form a Vine (V) minus two joints. We shall prove that all these atoms must be in the same layer
\mathfrak{L}
, and that
q\geq\mathfrak{L}\geq q^{\prime}
. This clearly allows to control the number of possible layerings, as well as the product in (
8.1
) involving the remaining bonds, by
C_{0}^{|q-q^{\prime}|}
.

To prove this last claim, let
\mathbb{H}
be the set of all remaining atoms not in the groups
\mathbb{G}_{i}
, then every atom in
\mathbb{H}
has degree
4
, and there are only two bonds connecting atoms in
\mathbb{H}
to atoms not in
\mathbb{H}
, namely two single bonds between atoms
v_{1}\not\in\mathbb{H}
and
v_{2}\in\mathbb{H}
, and between
v_{3}\not\in\mathbb{H}
and
v_{4}\in\mathbb{H}
, see Figure
15
; note also that
v_{2}
and
v_{4}
belong to the same group with the grouping in Definition
8.1
, so
\mathfrak{L}_{v_{2}}=\mathfrak{L}_{v_{4}}
. We only need to prove that
\mathfrak{L}_{v_{2}}
must be between
\mathfrak{L}_{v_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{L}_{v_{3}}
, because then we can remove the atoms
(v_{2},v_{4})
and apply the same argument to the resulting set
\mathbb{H}^{\prime}
, and proceed with induction.

If we start from any atom
v\in\mathbb{H}
and keep going from
v
to
v^{+}
as above, then eventually we will reach an atom not in
\mathbb{H}
, which is either
v_{1}
or
v_{3}
. Therefore, by symmetry we may assume
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{j}=\mathfrak{n}(v_{j})
. If
\mathfrak{m}_{3}
is also the parent node of
\mathfrak{m}_{4}
, then the branching nodes in the trees rooted at
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{4}
must all correspond to atoms in
\mathbb{H}
, and all the leaves in these two trees must be completely paired (otherwise we will have extra bonds between atoms in
\mathbb{H}
and atoms not in
\mathbb{H}
), so by Proposition
4.8
we get
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{2}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{4}}=\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{3}})
, which implies what we need.

Finally, if
\mathfrak{m}_{3}
is not the parent node of
\mathfrak{m}_{4}
, then
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
must be an ancestor of
\mathfrak{m}_{4}
. There are now two cases. The first case is that
\mathfrak{m}_{4}
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{m}_{3}
, in which case we clearly have
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{2}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{4}}\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{3}}
by the ancestor-descendant relations, as desired. The second case is that
\mathfrak{m}_{4}
has a child node
\mathfrak{m}_{6}
paired with a child node
\mathfrak{m}_{5}
of
\mathfrak{m}_{3}
as leaves. In this case, similar arguments as above imply that the branching nodes in the tree rooted at
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
must all correspond to atoms in
\mathbb{H}
, and all the leaves in this tree except
\mathfrak{m}_{6}
must be completely paired. Since also
\mathfrak{m}_{6}
is paired with
\mathfrak{m}_{5}
, by applying Proposition
4.8
to
(\mathfrak{m}_{2},\mathfrak{m}_{5})
and
(\mathfrak{m}_{5},\mathfrak{m}_{6})
we get that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{5}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{6}}=\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{3}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{4}})
and
\max(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{2}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{5}})\geq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{3}})
. Since also
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{2}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{4}}
, we get that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{2}}\geq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{3}})
, which implies what we need because also
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{2}}\leq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}
. This completes the proof.
∎

9.
Analysis of vines: Role of cancellation and incoherency

9.1.
A general estimate for exponential sums

We will state and prove an estimate for general expressions involving exponential sums (and oscillatory integrals), namely Proposition
9.4
; for this we need some number theory lemmas, which are listed below.

9.1.1.
Number theory lemmas

The number theory estimates we need involve summation over
1
or
3
vectors, and are similar to those involving
2
vectors in Lemmas
5.2
and
5.3
.

Lemma 9.1
.

We use the notation
e(z)=e^{2\pi iz}
(same with Lemmas
9.2
–
9.3
below). Fix
0\neq r\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
with
L^{-1}\leq|r|\leq L^{-\gamma+\eta}
, and recall the cutoff function
\chi_{0}
defined in Section
3.3.3
, then uniformly in
(a,\xi)\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\int_{[\tau,\tau+\delta L^{2\gamma}]}\bigg|\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\chi_{0}(x-a)\cdot e(s\langle r,x\rangle+\langle\xi,x\rangle)\bigg|\,\mathrm{d}s\lesssim_{0}\min(\delta L^{d+2\gamma},L^{d}|r|^{-1}).

(9.1)

Proof.

The bound
\delta L^{d+2\gamma}
is trivial as the sum over
x
is bounded by
L^{d}
. For the other bound, we apply Poisson summation to get

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\chi_{0}(x-a)\cdot e(s\langle r,x\rangle+\langle\xi,x\rangle)=L^{d}\sum_{g\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}e(\langle sr+\xi-Lg,a\rangle)\cdot\widehat{\chi_{0}}(sr+\xi-Lg),

so the left hand side if (
9.1
) is bounded by

\int_{[\tau,\tau+\delta L^{2\gamma}]}\sum_{g\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\langle sr+\xi-Lg\rangle^{-10d}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(9.2)

Assume the first coordinate of
r
is
r^{1}
with
|r^{1}|\sim_{0}|r|
; we fix the first coordinate
g^{1}
, then the result of integrating in
s
and summing in
g^{j}\,(j\geq 2)
is bounded by
|r^{1}|^{-1}
, and is moreover bounded by
(|g^{1}|+L)^{-5d}
for all but one choice of
g^{1}
(as
sr^{1}
belongs to a fixed interval of length
\delta L^{2\gamma}|r^{1}|\leq\delta L^{\gamma+\eta}\ll_{0}L
). This allows to bound (
9.2
) by
|r|^{-1}
, which then proves (
9.1
).
∎

Lemma 9.2
.

For any
|s|,|t|\leq L
, and uniformly in
(a,b,c,\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{6}
, we have

\sum_{(g,h,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{3d}}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3d}}\chi_{0}(x-a)\chi_{0}(y-b)\chi_{0}(z-c)\cdot e[\langle Lg+\xi,x\rangle+\langle Lh+\xi^{\prime},y\rangle+\langle Lq+\xi^{\prime\prime},z\rangle]\\
\times e(t\langle x,y\rangle+s\Lambda)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}z\bigg|\,\lesssim_{0}(1+|s|+|t|)^{-d},

(9.3)

where
\Lambda\in\{\langle x,z\rangle,\langle z,x+y-z\rangle\}
.

Proof.

This is similar to the proof of Lemma
5.2
. For fixed
(a,b,c,\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
and
|t|,|s|\leq L
, we have

\nabla_{x,y,z}(t\langle x,y\rangle+s\Lambda)=t\ell_{1}+s\ell_{2}+\mathcal{O}_{0}(L),

where
\ell_{j}=(\ell_{j}^{1},\ell_{j}^{2},\ell_{j}^{3})
is a vector that is a fixed linear combination of
(a,b,c)
. The case where

\max(|Lg+\xi+t\ell_{1}^{1}+s\ell_{2}^{1}|,|Lh+\xi^{\prime}+t\ell_{1}^{2}+s\ell_{2}^{2}|,|Lq+\xi^{\prime\prime}+t\ell_{1}^{3}+s\ell_{2}^{3}|)\geq 10dL

can be treated as in the proof of Lemma
5.2
, by integrating by parts in one of the variables
u:=x-a
,
v:=y-b
or
w:=z-c
. If the above maximum if
\leq 10dL
, then for fixed
(t,s)
there are
\leq C_{0}
choices fo
(g,h,q)
. For fixed
(g,h,q)
, we may fix
z
and apply stationary phase arguments in
(x,y)
, or fix
y
and apply stationary phase arguments in
(x,z)
, to bound the integral in
(x,y,z)
by
\min((1+|s|)^{-d},(1+|t|)^{-d})
.
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
\Lambda\in\{\langle x,z\rangle,\langle z,x+y-z\rangle\}
, then uniformly in
(a,b,c,\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{6}
, we have

\int_{L\leq\max(|t|,|s|)\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma}}\bigg|\sum_{x,y,z\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\chi_{0}(x-a)\chi_{0}(y-b)\chi_{0}(z-c)\\
\times e\big(t\langle x,y\rangle+s\Lambda+\langle\xi,x\rangle+\langle\xi^{\prime},y\rangle+\langle\xi^{\prime\prime},z\rangle\big)\bigg|\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s\lesssim_{2}L^{3d+\gamma-2\gamma_{0}}\log L.

(9.4)

Proof.

First consider
\Lambda=\langle z,x+y-z\rangle
, then we have
4\langle x,y\rangle=|u|^{2}-|v|^{2}
and
4\Lambda=|u|^{2}-|w|^{2}
, where
(u,v,w)=(x+y,x-y,x+y-2z)
, so

t\langle x,y\rangle+s\langle z,x+y-z\rangle=\frac{t+s}{4}|u|^{2}-\frac{t}{4}|v|^{2}-\frac{s}{4}|w|^{2}.

In this case the proof is similar to Lemma 6.6 of
[
22
]
: by changing to the
(u,v,w)
variables and applying standard reductions (decomposing various cutoff functions into Fourier integral and suitably shifting
(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
etc.), we can reduce to

\mathrm{LHS\ of\ }(\ref{lemcub2})\lesssim_{0}L^{4}\int_{L^{-1}\leq\max(|t^{\prime}|,|s^{\prime}|)\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma-2}}|G(t^{\prime})|^{d}|G(s^{\prime})|^{d}|G(t^{\prime}+s^{\prime})|^{d}\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

(9.5)

where
(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})=L^{-2}\cdot(t,s)
and

G(t^{\prime})=\sup_{h\in\mathbb{Z},\rho\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg|\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi_{0}\bigg(\frac{x-h}{L}\bigg)e(t^{\prime}x^{2}+\rho x)\bigg|\lesssim_{0}\frac{L}{\sqrt{q}\left(1+L\big|t^{\prime}-\frac{a}{q}\big|^{1/2}\right)},

(9.6)

where the last inequality is due to the Weyl bound, provided that
|a|<q\leq L
,
\gcd(a,q)=1
and
\big|t^{\prime}-\frac{a}{q}\big|\leq\frac{1}{qL}
. By (
9.5
) and Young’s inequality, we have

\mathrm{LHS\ of\ }(\ref{lemcub2})\lesssim_{0}L^{4}\|G\|_{L^{3d/2}([0,1])}^{2d}\cdot\|G\|_{L^{3d/2}([L^{-1},\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma-2}])}^{d}.

(9.7)

Since
3d/2>4
, it is well-known that
\|G\|_{L^{3d/2}([0,1])}^{3d/2}\lesssim_{0}L^{3d/2-2}
. To bound the localized norm, since
|t^{\prime}|\geq L^{-1}
, we must have
a\neq 0
in the Dirichlet approximation, which implies that
|a|\leq C_{2}L^{2\gamma-2}|q|
and
|q|\geq C_{2}^{-1}L^{2-2\gamma}
since also
|t^{\prime}|\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma-2}
. Let
|q|\sim_{0}Q\leq L
and
\big|t^{\prime}-\frac{a}{q}\big|\sim_{0}R
(or
\big|t^{\prime}-\frac{a}{q}\big|\lesssim_{0}R
for
R=L^{-2}
) with
L^{-2}\leq R\leq(QL)^{-1}
, then using (
9.6
) we can bound

\int_{L^{-1}\leq|t^{\prime}|\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma-2}}|G(t^{\prime})|^{3d/2}\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\lesssim_{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C_{2}^{-1}L^{2-2\gamma}\leq Q\leq L\\
L^{-2}\leq R\leq(QL)^{-1}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|q|\leq Q\\
|a|\leq C_{2}L^{2\gamma-2}Q\end{subarray}}(QR)^{-3d/4}R\lesssim_{2}L^{3d/2-2+(2\gamma-2)};

putting together we get

\mathrm{LHS\ of\ }(\ref{lemcub2})\lesssim_{2}L^{4}L^{2d-8/3}\cdot L^{d-4/3-4\gamma_{0}/3}\lesssim_{2}L^{3d-\gamma_{0}},

(9.8)

which is better than we need.

Now consider
\Lambda=\langle x,z\rangle
. We can apply Poisson summation in
(y,z)
in (
9.4
), to reduce the summation to

L^{2d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}}\sum_{g,h\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\chi_{0}(x-a)\cdot e\big(\langle\xi,x\rangle+\langle tx+\xi^{\prime}-Lg,b\rangle+\langle sx+\xi^{\prime\prime}-Lh,c\rangle\big)\\
\times\widehat{\chi_{0}}(tx+\xi^{\prime}-Lg)\widehat{\chi_{0}}(sx+\xi^{\prime\prime}-Lh).

(9.9)

Let
\{z\}
denote the distance of
z
to the closest integer. For convenience we will replace
\widehat{\chi_{0}}
by a compactly supported function (the proof can be easily adapted), so by (
9.9
) we have

\mathrm{LHS\ of\ }(\ref{lemcub2})\lesssim_{0}L^{2d+4}\int_{L^{-1}\leq\max(|t^{\prime}|,|s^{\prime}|)\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma-2}}H(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})^{d}\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

(9.10)

where
(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})=L^{-2}(t,s)
, and

H(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})=\sup_{r\in\mathbb{Z},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{|x^{\prime}-r|\leq L}\mathbf{1}_{\{t^{\prime}x^{\prime}+\sigma_{1}\}\leq L^{-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\{s^{\prime}x^{\prime}+\sigma_{2}\}\leq L^{-1}},

(9.11)

where
x^{\prime}=Lx^{j}
for some coordinate
x^{j}
, and correspondingly
r=La^{j}
and
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=L^{-1}((\xi^{\prime})^{j},(\xi^{\prime\prime})^{j})
etc. Assume
|t^{\prime}|\geq L^{-1}
, clearly
H(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leq\min(H_{0}(t^{\prime}),H_{0}(s^{\prime}))
where

H_{0}(t^{\prime})=\sup_{r\in\mathbb{Z},\sigma\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{1}_{|x^{\prime}-r|\leq L}\mathbf{1}_{\{t^{\prime}x^{\prime}+\sigma\}\leq L^{-1}},

(9.12)

which implies that

\mathrm{LHS\ of\ }(\ref{lemcub2})\lesssim_{0}L^{2d+4}\sum_{A\leq L}A^{d}\cdot|\{L^{-1}\leq|t^{\prime}|\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma-2}:H_{0}(t^{\prime})\geq A\}|\\
\times|\{|s^{\prime}|\leq\mathfrak{D}\delta L^{2\gamma-2}:H_{0}(s^{\prime})\geq A\}|:=L^{2d+4}\sum_{A\leq L}A^{d}\mu_{1}(A)\mu_{2}(A),

(9.13)

where
A
is dyadic. We may assume
A\geq 1
, as the rest of the integral gives
L^{2d+4}\cdot(C_{2}L^{2\gamma-2})^{2}
which is enough for (
9.4
). By Cauchy-Schwartz we may replace
\mu_{1}(A)\mu_{2}(A)
by
\mu_{1}(A)^{2}
and
\mu_{2}(A)^{2}
and take geometric average.

Consider
\mu_{2}(A)^{2}
; assume
s^{\prime}
has Dirichlet approximation
R\sim_{0}\big|s^{\prime}-\frac{a}{q}\big|\leq\frac{1}{qL}
where
|a|<q\sim_{0}Q\leq L
and
L^{-2}\leq R\leq(QL)^{-1}
(again we assume
\big|s^{\prime}-\frac{a}{q}\big|\lesssim_{0}R
when
R=L^{-2}
). Then for any
\sigma
, the inequality

L^{-1}>\{s^{\prime}x^{\prime}+\sigma\}=\bigg\{\frac{ax^{\prime}}{q}+\sigma+\eta x^{\prime}\bigg\},\qquad(|\eta|\sim_{0}R)

and the fact that
\gcd(a,q)=1
imply that
x^{\prime}
has less then
4
choices modulo
q
, and moreover for each choice,
x^{\prime}
belongs to a fixed interval of length
(LR)^{-1}
. This shows
H_{0}(s^{\prime})\lesssim_{0}\min(LQ^{-1},(LQR)^{-1})
, while the volume for
s^{\prime}
with
(Q,R)
fixed is bounded by
QR(1+C_{2}QL^{2\gamma-2})
upon summing over
(a,q)
and discussing whether
a=0
or not. With fixed
A
such that
H_{0}(s^{\prime})\geq A
, by further summing over
(Q,R)
, we get
\mu_{2}(A)\lesssim_{2}L^{-1}A^{-1}\log L+L^{2\gamma-2}A^{-2}
, so by summing over
A\geq 1
we can bound
(\ref{lemcub5})\lesssim_{2}L^{2d+4}[L^{d-4}\log L+L^{4\gamma-4}\max(1,L^{d-4})]
with
\mu_{2}(A)^{2}
instead of
\mu_{1}(A)\mu_{2}(A)
.

The case of
\mu_{1}(A)^{2}
is similar, but now we have
a\neq 0
due to
|t^{\prime}|\geq L^{-1}
, so the volume for
t^{\prime}
is bounded by
C_{2}Q^{2}RL^{2\gamma-2}
, and we have
(\ref{lemcub5})\lesssim_{2}L^{2d+4}\cdot L^{4\gamma-4}\max(1,L^{d-4})
with
\mu_{1}(A)^{2}
instead of
\mu_{1}(A)\mu_{2}(A)
. Putting together using Cauchy-Schwartz, this then proves (
9.4
).
∎

9.1.2.
Exponential sums

We now state the main exponential sum estimate.

Proposition 9.4
.

Consider an expression
\mathcal{I}
defined as follows. It is a function of the time variables
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
and vector variables
(e,f,g,h)
called
output variables
. Assume each of
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
belongs to a fixed unit interval of form
[q,q+1]
, where
q
may be different for different variables (same below), and
t>\max(t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
; denote this set of
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
by
\mathcal{B}
. Assume each of
(e,f,g,h)
belongs to a fixed unit ball, and
e-f=h-g:=r\neq 0
with
|r|\leq L^{-\gamma+\eta}
.

Assume that
\mathcal{I}
involves
input variables
, including the vector variables
x_{0}^{i},x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and time variables
t_{0}^{i},t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i}
, as well as
parameters
including
\lambda_{0}^{i},\lambda_{j}^{i},\mu_{j}^{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq I
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
. Fix
(\mathrm{Nor},\mathrm{Bad})
as a partition of
\{1,\cdots,I\}
, for
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
, we also include the extra input vector variables
u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i},u_{3}^{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, time input variables
\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i}
and parameters
\sigma_{1}^{i},\sigma_{2}^{i},\sigma_{3}^{i}
. Denote
{\textbf{x}}=(x_{0}^{i},x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i},u_{j}^{i})
to be the collection of all input vector variables, and
M
be the the total number of these variables, which equals the sum of
n_{i}:=2m_{i}+1+3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{Nor}}(i)
.

Suppose there exist
alternative variables

k_{j}^{i},\ell_{j}^{i}
where
1\leq j\leq n_{i}
with
n_{i}
defined as above, and we write
{\textbf{k}}=(k_{j}^{i})
and
\bm{\ell}=(\ell_{j}^{i})
, such that any vector in
(k_{j}^{i},\ell_{j}^{i},e,f,g,h)
belongs to
\underline{w}+\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
for a fixed vector
\underline{w}
. Assume also that (i) we have
{\textbf{k}}=T_{1}{\textbf{x}}+{\textbf{h}}_{1}
and
\bm{\ell}=T_{2}{\textbf{x}}+{\textbf{h}}_{2}
for some constant matrices
T_{j}
and some vectors
{\textbf{h}}_{j}
depending only on
(e,f,g,h)
, such that all coefficients of
T_{1},\,T_{1}^{-1},\,T_{2}
are
0
or
\pm 1
; (ii) for each
(i,j)
there exists
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)
in lexicographic order such that
\ell_{j}^{i}-\ell_{j^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime}}
is an algebraic sum of at most
\Lambda=\Lambda_{j}^{i}
distinct terms in
(k_{\alpha}^{\beta},e,f,g,h)
, and
\prod_{(i,j)}\Lambda_{j}^{i}\leq C_{0}^{M}
; (iii) any input variable (such as
x_{j}^{i}
or
u_{j}^{i}
etc.) is the difference of two variables in
(k_{\alpha}^{\beta},\ell_{\alpha}^{\beta},e,f,g,h)
.

The expression
\mathcal{I}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},e,f,g,h)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\textbf{x}}\prod_{i=1}^{I}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\mathcal{K}_{j}^{i}(k_{j}^{i})\cdot\mathcal{L}_{j}^{i}(\ell_{j}^{i})\cdot\int_{\mathcal{D}}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{I}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-s_{j}^{i})\langle x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}\rangle}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{j=0}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-t)\langle r,\zeta_{j}^{i}\rangle}\prod_{j=0}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\lambda_{j}^{i}t_{j}^{i}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{j}^{i}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\mu_{j}^{i}s_{j}^{i}}\,\mathrm{d}s_{j}^{i}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\prod_{i\in\mathrm{Nor}}\bigg(e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{1}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\langle u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i}\rangle}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{2}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\Lambda_{i}}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{3}^{i}-t)\langle r,\xi_{i}\rangle}\prod_{j=1}^{3}e^{\pi i\sigma_{j}^{i}\tau_{j}^{i}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau_{j}^{i}\bigg)\bigg]

(9.14)

Here in (
9.4
), the sum is taken over all input vector variables in
x
, and
\mathcal{D}
is the domain of all input time variables
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i},\tau_{j}^{i})
defined by the following conditions: (i) each variable belongs to a fixed unit time interval of form
[q,q+1]\,(q\in\mathbb{Z})
, (ii) all variables are
less than
t
, and
t_{0}^{1}>\max(t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
; (iii) any fixed collection of inequalities of form
v<w
where
v
and
w
are variables from
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i},\tau_{j}^{i})
. For each
1\leq i\leq I
and
1\leq j\leq n_{i}
, the functions
\mathcal{K}_{j}^{i}
and
\mathcal{L}_{j}^{i}
satisfy that

\|\mathcal{K}_{j}^{i}\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{30d,30d}}\leq L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})g_{ij}},\quad\|\mathcal{L}_{j}^{i}\|_{\mathfrak{S}^{30d,0}}\leq L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})g_{ij}^{\prime}},

(9.15)

where
g_{ij},g_{ij}^{\prime}\geq 0
are integers. The expressions
\zeta_{j}^{i}=a_{j}^{i}x_{j}^{i}+b_{j}^{i}y_{j}^{i}+c_{j}^{i}r
for some
a_{j}^{i},b_{j}^{i},c_{j}^{i}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
when
j\geq 1
, and
\zeta_{0}^{i}=x_{0}^{i}
when
j=0
; moreover
\Lambda_{i}\in\{\langle u_{1}^{i},u_{3}^{i}\rangle,\langle u_{3}^{i},u_{1}^{i}+u_{2}^{i}-u_{3}^{i}\rangle\}
and
\xi_{i}=a_{i}u_{1}^{i}+b_{i}u_{2}^{i}+c_{i}u_{3}^{i}+d_{i}r
with
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i},d_{i}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
.

In addition, assume for
each

i\in\mathrm{Bad}
that one of the followings holds:

(1)

The conditions in the definition of
\mathcal{D}
include either
s_{1}^{i}<t_{0}^{i}<t_{1}^{i}
or
t_{1}^{i}<t_{0}^{i}<s_{1}^{i}
;

(2)

The summation and integration in (
9.4
) contains an extra factor of
|r|
;

(3)

The summation and integration in (
9.4
) contains an extra factor of
|t_{1}^{i}-s_{1}^{i}|^{1-3\eta}
;

(4)

For at least one
1\leq j\leq n_{i}
we have
\max(g_{ij},g_{ij}^{\prime})\geq 1
.

Moreover, consider the unit interval that each input time variable belongs to; let
M_{0}
be the number of
(i,j)
such that
t_{j}^{i}
and
s_{j}^{i}
are
not
in the same unit interval, plus the number of
i\in A
such that
\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i}
are
not
in the same unit interval. Also let
M_{1}
be the sum of all
g_{ij}
and
g_{ij}^{\prime}
in (
9.15
).

Then, uniformly in all the parameters (including
\underline{w}
), in
\mathcal{B}
and in the choice of the unit balls containing
(e,f,g,h)
described above, we have

\|\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim_{1}\big[\max_{(i,j)}(\langle\lambda_{j}^{i}\rangle,\langle\mu_{j}^{i}\rangle,\langle\sigma_{j}^{i}\rangle)\big]^{\eta^{6}}\cdot(C_{1}\delta^{-1/2})^{M}L^{M(d-\gamma){-\eta^{2}(|\mathrm{Bad}|+M_{1})-\eta^{8}M_{0}+\eta^{7}}},

(9.16)

where the norm
\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}(\mathcal{B})
is defined as in Section
3.3.1
.

Proof.

Step 1: Reductions.
Using the
\langle k_{j}^{i}\rangle^{-30d}
decay of
\mathcal{K}_{j}^{i}(k_{j}^{i})
in (
9.15
), we can localize
k^{i}_{j}
into sets of the form
|k^{i}_{j}-\alpha^{i}_{j}|\leq 1
where
\alpha^{i}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and sum over
\alpha^{i}_{j}
at the end. With all these
\alpha^{i}_{j}
fixed, note that there are
O((\Lambda^{1}_{1})^{d})
choices for the integer parts of the coordinates of
\ell_{1}^{1}
. Similarly, once we fix all those integer parts, there are
O((\Lambda^{1}_{2})^{d})
choices for the integer parts of the coordinates of
\ell_{2}^{1}
, and so on. As a result, at the expense of a multiplicative factor of
(\prod_{(i,j)}\Lambda_{j}^{i})^{d}\leq C_{0}^{M}
, we may fix points
\beta^{i}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
so that
|\ell^{i}_{j}-\beta^{i}_{j}|\leq 1
. Consequently, since each of
(x^{i}_{j},y^{i}_{j},u_{j}^{i})
is the difference of some
k^{i}_{j}
and
\ell^{i}_{j}
, we may fix some
(a^{i}_{j},b^{i}_{j},c_{j}^{i})
(which may depend on the unit balls containing
(e,f,g,h)
) such that
\max(|x^{i}_{j}-a^{i}_{j}|,|y^{i}_{j}-b^{i}_{j}|,|u^{i}_{j}-c^{i}_{j}|)\leq 1
. Finally, note that due to the properties of the transformation
T_{1}
, the change of variables
\bm{x}\leftrightarrow\bm{k}
is volume preserving.

Now set

W(\bm{x}):=\prod_{i=1}^{I}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\mathcal{K}_{j}^{i}(k_{j}^{i})\cdot\mathcal{L}_{j}^{i}(\ell_{j}^{i}).

Under the above support restrictions, we then have
\|\widehat{W}\|_{L^{1}}\leq C_{1}^{M}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})M_{1}}.
In fact, this bound holds if the Fourier transform is taken in the variables
(k_{j}^{i})_{1\leq j\leq n_{i}}
by (
9.15
), thus it also holds for Fourier transform taken in
\bm{x}
, due to the properties of the affine linear transform. Thus, by expanding
W
in terms of its Fourier transform, we can replace this function by the product of modulation factors
\prod_{i}\left(\prod_{j}e^{\pi i\nu^{i}_{j}\cdot x^{i}_{j}}e^{\pi i\rho^{i}_{j}\cdot y^{i}_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{3}e^{\pi i\varpi^{i}_{j}u^{i}_{j}}\right)
, and only need to prove uniform estimates in
(\nu^{i}_{j},\rho^{i}_{j},\varpi_{j}^{i})
. Note that
10\%
of the gain
L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})M_{1}}
already provides the decay in (
9.16
) involving
M_{1}
, and we can save the other
90\%
for the decay involving bad vines case (d).

Step 2: An
L^{\infty}
estimate.
We shall first prove the estimate (
9.16
) for
\|\mathcal{I}\|_{L^{\infty}}
. In this case, we shall move the time integral over
\mathcal{D}
out of the expression in (
9.4
) and take absolute values inside this integral. Denote the integrand (without absolute value) by
\mathcal{K}
. This gets rid of all the exponential factors involving
\lambda_{j}^{i},\mu_{j}^{i},\sigma_{j}^{i}
and allows to pointwise bound
\mathcal{I}
by

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}|

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{I}\int_{[q_{0}^{i},q_{0}^{i}+1]}\mathrm{d}t_{0}^{i}\cdot\bigg|\sum_{x_{0}^{i}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{0}^{i}-t)\langle r,\zeta_{0}^{i}\rangle+\pi i(\nu^{i}_{0}\cdot x^{i}_{0})}\chi_{0}(x_{0}^{i}-a_{0}^{i})\bigg|\cdot\mathcal{N}^{i}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}(t_{0}^{i}),

(9.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{j}^{i}(t_{0}^{i})}\mathrm{d}t_{j}^{i}\,\mathrm{d}s_{j}^{i}\cdot\bigg|\sum_{(x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i})}\chi_{0}(x_{j}^{i}-a_{j}^{i})\chi_{0}(y_{j}^{i}-b_{j}^{i})e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}[(t_{j}^{i}-s_{j}^{i})\langle x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}\rangle+(t_{j}^{i}-t)\langle r,\zeta_{j}^{i}\rangle]+\pi i(\nu^{i}_{j}\cdot x^{i}_{j}+\cdot\rho^{i}_{j}\cdot y^{i}_{j})}\bigg|.

Here
\mathcal{N}^{i}=1
if
i\in\textrm{Bad}
, and if
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
we define

\mathcal{N}^{i}:=\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\,\mathrm{d}\tau_{j}^{i}\cdot\bigg|\sum_{(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})}\prod_{k=1}^{3}e^{\pi i\varpi^{i}_{k}u^{i}_{k}}\chi_{0}(u_{k}^{i}-c_{k}^{i})e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}[(\tau_{1}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\langle u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i}\rangle+(\tau_{2}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\Lambda_{i}+(\tau_{3}^{i}-t)\langle r,\xi_{i}\rangle]}\bigg|.

In the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}
above, we denote by
\mathcal{D}_{j}^{i}(t_{0}^{i})
the integration domain for
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i})
given by the unit interval restrictions of
t_{j}^{i}
and
s_{j}^{i}
, and possibly the conditions in (a) if
j=1
(in particular, we discard all other inequalities involving
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i})
). Similarly, in the definition of
\mathcal{N}^{i}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is the domain of
(\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i})
given by the unit interval restrictions of
(\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i})
.

We start with the estimate for
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}
. We first change variables
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i})
into
(t_{j}^{i},v_{j}^{i})
where
v_{j}^{i}=\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-s_{j}^{i})
, and then split the integration region into
|v_{j}^{i}|\leq L
and
|v_{j}^{i}|>L
. Denoting by
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i,1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i,2}
the contribution of those two respective regions, we can bound using Lemma
5.3
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i,2}

\displaystyle\leq(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-1}\int_{I_{j}^{i}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}^{i}\int_{|v_{j}^{i}|\geq L}\bigg|\sum_{(x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i})}\chi(x_{j}^{i}-a_{j}^{i})\chi(y_{j}^{i}-b_{j}^{i})

\displaystyle\times e^{\pi i\cdot v_{j}^{i}\langle x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}\rangle}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-t)\langle r,\zeta_{j}^{i}\rangle+{\pi i(\nu^{i}_{j}\cdot x^{i}_{j}+\rho^{i}_{j}\cdot y^{i}_{j})}}\bigg|\mathrm{d}v_{j}^{i}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{2}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)-2(d-1)(1-\gamma)+\eta},

where we integrate trivially in
t_{j}^{i}
over its unit interval domain
I_{j}^{i}
.

To bound
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i,1}
we apply Poisson summation in
(x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i})
followed by Lemma
5.2
to bound

\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i,1}\lesssim_{0}(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-1}L^{2d}\int_{I_{j}^{i}}\mathrm{d}t_{j}^{i}\int_{|v_{j}^{i}|\leq L}\langle v_{j}^{i}\rangle^{-d}\mathrm{d}v_{j}^{i}\lesssim_{2}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)},

if we have no restrictions of type (a)–(d) between
t_{j}^{i}
and
v_{j}^{i}
(in the case when
i\in\textrm{Bad}
). If
i\in\textrm{Bad}
and we are in case (a), we have that
|t_{1}^{i}-t_{0}^{i}|\leq|t_{1}^{i}-s_{1}^{i}|=(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-1}|v_{1}^{i}|
, so integrating in
t_{1}^{i}
first would gain a factor of
(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-1}
in the above estimate (since
d\geq 3
). The same argument holds if
t_{j}^{i}
and
s_{j}^{i}
belong to different unit intervals, for which we have

|t_{j}^{i}-q_{j}^{i}|\leq|t_{j}^{i}-s_{j}^{i}|=(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-1}|v_{j}^{i}|

(9.18)

where
q_{j}^{i}
is one of the endpoints of the fixed unit interval containing
t_{j}^{i}
. If we are in case (c) then we have an extra factor of
|t_{1}^{i}-s_{1}^{i}|^{1-3\eta}=(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-(1-3\eta)}|v_{1}^{i}|^{1-3\eta}
which again gains
(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-(1-3\eta)}
. If we are in case (b) or case (d), then we clearly gain a factor of
|r|
or
L^{-0.9(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})}
compared to the above estimate (we only exploit
90\%
of the
L^{-\gamma_{1}+\sqrt{\eta}}
gain). As a consequence of all this, we have

\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}\lesssim_{1}\big(\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}\big)^{m_{i}}\cdot(L^{-1.8\gamma}+L^{-0.9(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})}+|r|)^{\mathbf{1}_{\textrm{Bad}(i)}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}L^{-0.1\gamma\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}(i,j)},

where
\mathfrak{M}_{0}
is the set of
(i,j)
such that
t_{j}^{i}
and
s_{j}^{i}
belong to different unit intervals. The estimate for
\mathcal{N}^{i}
follows in exactly the same way as
\mathcal{M}^{i}_{j}
but with the variables
w_{1}^{i}=(\delta L^{2\gamma})(\tau^{i}_{1}-\tau^{i}_{3})
and
w_{2}^{i}=(\delta L^{2\gamma})(\tau^{i}_{2}-\tau^{i}_{3})
. We split into two regions depending on whether
\max(|w_{1}^{i}|,|w_{2}^{i}|)
is
\leq L
or
\geq L
, use Lemmas
9.2
and
9.3
in place of Lemmas
5.2
and
5.3
, and integrate trivially in
\tau_{3}^{i}
. The contribution of the first region is bounded by
(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-2}L^{3d}
by Lemma
9.2
, whereas that of the second region is bounded by
(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-2}L^{3d+\gamma-2\gamma_{0}}\log L
by Lemma
9.3
. Note that if one of the
\tau_{k}^{i}
belongs to a different unit interval than the others then we get that
|\tau_{3}^{i}-q^{i}|\leq(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-1}\max(|w_{1}^{i}|,|w_{2}^{i}|)
which allows to gain an extra factor of
(\delta L^{2\gamma})^{-0.9}
. As such, we have that

\mathcal{N}^{i}\lesssim_{1}\delta^{-2}L^{3(d-\gamma)}L^{-\gamma_{0}}\cdot L^{-0.1\gamma_{0}1_{\mathfrak{M}_{1}}(i)},

(9.19)

where
\mathfrak{M}_{1}
is the set of
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
such that
\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i}
do not belong to the same unit interval. Finally, using the above two estimates for
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}
and
\mathcal{N}^{i}
(which are uniform in
t_{0}^{i}
), we can go back to (
9.17
), and use Lemma
9.1
(after rescaling
t_{0}^{i}\to\delta L^{2\gamma}t_{0}^{i}
) to obtain the uniform bound

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}|\leq

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in\mathrm{Nor}}C_{1}^{m_{i}}\delta^{-(m_{i}+2)}L^{(2m_{i}+4)(d-\gamma)}L^{-0.1\gamma_{0}M_{0}^{i}}\prod_{i\in\mathrm{Bad}}C_{1}^{m_{i}}\delta^{-(m_{i}+1)}L^{(2m_{i}+1)(d-\gamma)}L^{-0.1\gamma_{0}(1+M_{0}^{i})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}^{M}(\delta^{-1/2})^{M}L^{M(d-\gamma)}L^{-0.1\gamma_{0}(|\mathrm{Bad}|+M_{0})},

(9.20)

where we denote by
M_{0}^{i}
the number of
j
such that
t_{j}^{i}
and
s_{j}^{i}
are not in the same unit interval (plus 1 if
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
and
(\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i})
are not in the same unit interval). Note that in (
9.20
) when
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
, we use the first bound in Lemma
9.1
if
\gamma\leq 1/2
and the second bound if
\gamma>1/2
(when
\gamma>1/2
, the power
L^{-\gamma_{0}}
on the right hand side of (
9.19
) can be replaced by
L^{-\gamma_{0}}\cdot L^{-\min(\gamma-\gamma_{0},0.1\gamma_{0})}
using Lemmas
9.2
and
9.3
). Also the decay factor involving
M_{1}
in (
9.16
) is already taken care of before (same comment applies in Step 3 below).

Step 3: The
\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}
estimate.

Now we prove the needed estimate for
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}
. First define
\mathcal{I}^{*}(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{I}(t,t^{\prime}+t,t^{\prime\prime}+t)
, then we only need to consider
\mathcal{I}^{*}
because the
\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}
norm for both functions are comparable. Now we have

\mathcal{I}^{*}(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})=e^{\pi i\theta t}\int_{{\mathcal{D}^{*}}}\mathcal{K}(\bm{t})\mathrm{d}\bm{t}

for some integrand
\mathcal{K}
, where
\theta
is an algebraic sum of the
(\lambda_{j}^{i},\mu_{j}^{i},\sigma_{j}^{i})
parameters, and we denote by
\bm{t}
the vector of all the
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i},\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i})
for
1\leq i\leq I,1\leq j\leq n_{i}
. The domain of integration
\mathcal{D}^{*}
is the translation by
t
of the domain
\mathcal{D}
. Set
\beta=\eta^{7.5}
. Since
t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
each belongs to a unit interval, we can use a partition of unity to write
\mathcal{I}^{*}
as a sum of
L^{3\beta}
terms where each term is supported on an interval of size
L^{-\beta}
in each of the variables
t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
. As such, up to paying a factor of
L^{3\beta}
, we shall fix
t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
to fixed intervals of size
L^{-\beta}
and only estimate the
\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}
of the resulting function. Since each cutoff factor
\chi_{0}(L^{\beta}t-c)
amplifies the
\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}
norm by at most
L^{\beta}
, by paying another
L^{3\beta}
factor, we can also remove these cutoff functions if necessary.

Note that after the translation, the integrand
\mathcal{K}
has no dependence on
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
. The domain
\mathcal{D}^{*}
does depend on
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
, but we can find a larger domain
{\mathcal{Q}^{*}}
independent of
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
(obtained by enlarging the unit interval restrictions for
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i},\tau_{1}^{i},\tau_{2}^{i},\tau_{3}^{i})
into intervals of size
1+2L^{-\beta}
using the fixed
L^{-\beta}
interval restrictions of
