t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
). We may then assume
\mathcal{K}
is supported in
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
, and we can repeat essentially the same argument as in Step 2 to obtain the weaker bound

\|\mathcal{K}\|_{L_{(e,f,g,h)}^{\infty}L_{\bm{t}}^{1}(\mathcal{Q}^{*})}\leq C_{1}^{M}(\delta^{-1/2})^{M}L^{M(d-\gamma)}L^{-0.1\gamma_{0}|\mathrm{Bad}|-\beta M_{0}+6\beta}.

(9.21)

The only modification needed for the argument in Step 2 is when dealing with the case where
t_{j}^{i}
and
s_{j}^{i}
belong to different unit intervals before translating by
t
, because now the enlarged intervals of length
(1+2L^{-\beta})
in
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
might intersect. However, in this case, we simply replace (
9.18
) with

|t_{j}^{i}-q_{j}^{i}|\leq 2L^{-\beta}+|t_{1}^{i}-s_{1}^{i}|

for some other fixed value
q_{j}^{i}
, which gives a gain of
C_{0}L^{-\beta}
for each such incoherency, thus explaining the bound (
9.21
).

Taking the Fourier transforms of
\mathcal{I}^{*}
in
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
and omitting the time cutoff functions as said above, we obtain that

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{I}^{*}(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime},e,f,g,h)=\int_{\mathcal{Q}^{*}}\mathcal{K}(\bm{t})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},\bm{t})e^{\pi i\left((\xi+\theta)t+\xi^{\prime}t^{\prime}+\xi^{\prime\prime}t^{\prime\prime}\right)}\cdot\psi\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime\prime},

(9.22)

where
\psi=\psi(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
is some other suitable unit support cutoff function. It is clear that, due to the definition of
\mathcal{D}^{*}
, the above integral can be written as the product of three integrals in
t
,
t^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime\prime}
respectively, and each integral is taken on an interval depending on
\bm{t}
. Thus the second integral above is bounded (uniformly in
\bm{t}
) by
\langle\xi+\theta\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime}\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime\prime}\rangle^{-1}
(here we may assume
|\xi+\theta|\geq 1
etc., since the other cases are easily dealt with), which implies that

\|\mathcal{I}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{-\eta^{8},0,0}}\lesssim_{1}\|\mathcal{K}\|_{L_{(e,f,g,h)}^{\infty}L_{\bm{t}}^{1}(\mathcal{Q}^{*})}\lesssim_{1}C_{1}^{M}(\delta^{-1/2})^{M}L^{M(d-\gamma)}L^{-0.1\gamma_{0}|\mathrm{Bad}|-\beta M_{0}+6\beta}.

We shall interpolate the above estimate with a lossy one for the
\mathfrak{X}^{1/4,0,0}
norm to obtain the result. For this, note by definition of
\mathcal{D}^{*}
that, the
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
integral in (
9.22
) can essentially be written as a linear combination (up to unimodular factors) of

\langle\xi+\theta\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime}\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime\prime}\rangle^{-1}e^{\pi i\left((\xi+\theta)t_{1}+\xi^{\prime}t_{2}+\xi^{\prime\prime}t_{3}\right)}

(9.23)

for some variables
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
from the vector
\bm{t}
which may or may not not be distinct; note that the exact choice of
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
may depend on inequality relations between variables in
\bm{t}
, but we may fix such a relation at the cost of another
C_{0}^{M}
factor. First consider the case when the three variables
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
are distinct. In this case, we can write
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{I}^{*}
essentially as a linear combination of

\langle\xi+\theta\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime}\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime\prime}\rangle^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{F}_{(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})}\bigg(\int\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{*}}\mathcal{K}(\cdot,\bm{t^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}\bm{t^{\prime}}\bigg)(\xi+\theta,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})

where
\bm{t^{\prime}}
are the variables in
\bm{t}
other than
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
. In this case, we can estimate
\|\mathcal{I}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1/4,0,0}}
by first bounding
L^{\infty}_{(e,f,g,h)}
by
L_{(e,f,g,h)}^{1}
, then interchanging the
L^{1}
norms in
(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
and
(e,f,g,h)
, and finally bounding
L_{(e,f,g,h)}^{1}
by
L^{\infty}_{(e,f,g,h)}
with a loss of
L^{4d}
(recall that there only
L^{4d}
choices of
(e,f,g,h)
with fixed
\underline{w}
and fixed unit balls). This allows us to fix the values of
(e,f,g,h)
and compute the weighted
L^{1}
norm in
(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
. This reduces to estimating a
scalar
function for which Plancherel is available. Now we apply Cauchy Schwartz in
(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
followed by Plancherel to get that

\|\mathcal{I}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1/4,0,0}}\leq\langle\theta\rangle^{1/4}\cdot C_{0}^{M}L^{4d}\sup_{e,f,g,h}\bigg\|\int\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{*}}\mathcal{K}(\bm{t})\mathrm{d}\bm{t^{\prime}}\bigg\|_{L^{2}_{(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})}}.

(9.24)

The latter can be estimated by fixing
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
and estimating the integral in
\bm{t^{\prime}}
as in Step 2. The effect of fixing
(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
is to replace at most three bounds on
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}
or
\mathcal{N}^{i}
by the trivial bound
L^{2d}
or
L^{3d}
. So up to an extra factor of
L^{14d}
, we have the same bound for (
9.24
) as (
9.20
). Similarly, if in (
9.23
), two of the variables are the same, say
t_{1}\equiv t_{2}
, then
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{I}^{*}
can essentially be bounded by

\langle\xi+\theta\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime}\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi^{\prime\prime}\rangle^{-1}\mathcal{F}_{(t_{1},t_{3})}\bigg(\int\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{*}}\mathcal{K}(\cdot,\bm{t^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}\bm{t^{\prime}}\bigg)(\xi+\theta+\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime}),

where
\bm{t^{\prime}}
are the variables in
\bm{t}
other than
(t_{1},t_{3})
. We argue in the same way as above, except that we replace the use of Cauchy-Schwarz in
(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
above by a combination of Cauchy-Schwarz in
(\xi+\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
and Young’s inequality. The case
t_{1}\equiv t_{2}\equiv t_{3}
is again simialr. As a result, we can estimate
\|\mathcal{I}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1/4,0,0}}
by
L^{14d}\langle\theta\rangle^{1/4}
times the right hand side of (
9.21
), which implies (
9.16
) upon interpolation (recall that
\theta
is an algebraic sum of the
(\lambda_{j}^{i},\mu_{j}^{i},\sigma_{j}^{i})
parameters).
∎

9.2.
Application to vine chains

We now apply Proposition
9.4
to expressions associated with vines in molecules coming from gardens. First we discuss the reduction of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
(Definition
3.8
, (
3.24
)) after collapsing some of the embedded regular couples and regular trees in
\mathcal{G}
.

9.2.1.
Reduction and twisting of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a garden, and suppose it is obtained from its skeleton
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
by replacing each leaf pair
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})
with a regular couple
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
, and each branching node
\mathfrak{m}
with a regular tree
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
, as in Definition
7.18
. Assume we have fixed a canonical layering of
\mathcal{G}
, which induces a layering of each
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
, as well as a pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
. Then, by Definition
3.8
and (
3.24
), it is easy to verify that

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}(p+1,k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{\mathrm{sk}}}\zeta(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})\sum_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}}\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sk}}}e^{\pi i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\\
\times\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sk}}}^{(+)}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sk}}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}}).

(9.25)

Here
n_{\mathrm{sk}}
is the order of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}
is the domain defined in (
3.25
) for
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
with
t
replaced by
p+1
,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}
is a
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, and the other objects are as before but for the garden
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
; the product
\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sk}}}^{(+)}
is taken over all leaf pairs
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})
where
\mathfrak{l}
has sign
+
, and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}
are defined in Definition
3.8
(also, if
\mathfrak{m}
is the root of a tree then we replace
t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
by
p+1
; this will be assumed throughout).

Now suppose
\mathbb{U}
is a
(CL) vine chain
in the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
, and let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be an LF twist of
\mathcal{G}
at
\mathbb{U}
(viewed as a collection of (CL) vines, i.e.
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is formed from
\mathcal{G}
by performing some unit LF twist at each vine in
\mathbb{U}
that is core and satisfies the assumptions in Definition
7.18
). If we write both
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
in the form of (
9.25
), then the differences between these expressions
only occur
in the part of (
9.25
)
involving the realization

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]
of
\mathbb{U}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
(see Remark
7.9
for definition).

More precisely, let the notions
\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{2},\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22}
etc. be associated to
\mathbb{U}
as in Proposition
7.7
. Let
n_{(1)}
be the number of branching nodes in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
, and let
n_{(2)}
be
n_{(1)}
plus the total order of regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and regular trees
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
for
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
. We may then define the expression

\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}=\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})

similar to (
9.25
), but with the following changes:

•

We replace the power
(\delta/(2L^{d-\gamma}))^{n_{\mathrm{sk}}}
by
(\delta/(2L^{d-\gamma}))^{n_{(1)}}
, and the factor
\zeta(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
by the product of
i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}
where
\mathfrak{n}
runs over all branching nodes in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
.

•

In the summation
\sum_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}}(\cdots)
, we only sum over the variables
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
for
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
(including branching nodes and leaves), and treat the other
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
variables as fixed. We also replace
\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}}
by the product of factors on the right hand side of (
3.22
), but only for
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]
.

•

In the integral
\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sk}}}(\cdots)
, we only integrate over the variables
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
for all branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
, and treat the other
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
variables as fixed.

•

In the first product
\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sk}}}(\cdots)
, we only include those factors where
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]
; in the products
\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sk}}}^{(+)}(\cdots)
and
\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sk}}}(\cdots)
, we only include those factors where
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
.

It is easy to check that the function
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}
defined this way indeed only depends on the time variables
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}
and vector variables
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}
.

9.2.2.
(CL) vine chains

We now prove the main result for (CL) vine chains, which exhibits the key cancellation between
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
for LF twists
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition 9.5
.

In the same setting as in Section
9.2.1
including all notations, let
\mathbb{U}
be a (CL) vine chain in the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
. Define

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}}(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}):=e^{-\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}\Gamma}\sum_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}^{\mathbb{U}}(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}),

(9.26)

where the summation is taken over all the
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
which are LF twists of
\mathcal{G}
at
\mathbb{U}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}^{\mathbb{U}}
is defined as in Section
9.2.1
, and
\Gamma
is defined as
\Gamma:=\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}}|k_{u_{11}}|^{2}+\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}}|k_{u_{21}}|^{2}+\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}|k_{u_{22}}|^{2}-\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}|k_{u_{1}}|^{2}
. Same as Proposition
9.4
, we also fix
\underline{w}
such that
k_{\mathfrak{n}}-\underline{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
for each
\mathfrak{n}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},\cdots,\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}
be unit balls, and
\mathcal{B}
be the intersection of the set
\{t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}>\max(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})\}
with the product of three unit intervals, where the unit intervals are
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}\in[\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}+1]
and the same for
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}}
and
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}
, with
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}
being the layer of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
etc. Then, uniformly in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},\cdots,\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}
and
\mathcal{B}
, we have

\big\|\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}}(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}})\cdots\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}}(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}}\big\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}(\mathcal{B})}\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\sqrt{\delta})^{n_{(2)}}L^{-\eta^{8}n_{(3)}-\eta^{2}b+\eta^{7}}.

(9.27)

Here the norm
\mathfrak{X}^{2\eta^{8},0,0}(\mathcal{B})
is defined as in Section
3.3.1
,
n_{(1)}
and
n_{(2)}
are defined in the same way as in Section
9.2.1
,
b
denotes the number of
bad
vines in
\mathbb{U}
, and
n_{(3)}
denotes the sum of all incoherency indices for all the vines in
\mathbb{U}
(see Definition
8.1
) and all the regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and regular trees
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
for
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime},\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
(see Definition
4.7
).

Proof.

The main objective of the proof is to show that (
9.26
) can be reduced to an expression in the form (
9.4
), so that the bound (
9.27
) follows from (
9.16
). This involves a parametrization of the vine chain, which was done for a single vine in
[
22
]
, Proposition 7.5 (for bad (CL) vine) and Proposition 7.6 (for normal (CL) vine). The proof here is similar to
[
22
]
with a few additions associated with vine chains.

Step 1: Notations and reductions.
Start by considering the expression for
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}:=e^{-\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}\Gamma}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}
which is given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{(1)}}\zeta[\mathbb{U}]\sum_{\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]}e^{-\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}\Gamma}\cdot\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{I}[\mathbb{U}]}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]}e^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\\
\times\,{\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathbb{U}]}^{(+)}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}}).

Here
\zeta[\mathbb{U}]
is the product of
i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}
where
\mathfrak{n}
runs over all branching nodes in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
, as in the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}
in Section
9.2.1
above; similarly
\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]
,
\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]}
and
\mathcal{I}[\mathbb{U}]
are also defined as above, and
\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]
and
\mathcal{L}[\mathbb{U}]
are respectively the sets of branching nodes and leaves in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]
. Note that

\delta L^{2\gamma}\cdot\Gamma=\delta L^{2\gamma}\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]}\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}},

since each factor
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}|k_{\mathfrak{n}}|^{2}
with
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}]\setminus\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
appears twice in the above sum with opposite signs. We also define
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}):=(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})
and
(e,f,g,h)=(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})

but possibly with
e
and
f
switched
depending on the signs
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}=\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}}
. Thus, we can rewrite

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{(1)}}\zeta[\mathbb{U}]\sum_{\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]}\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{I}[\mathbb{U}]}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]}e^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}(t_{\mathfrak{n}}-t)}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\\
\times\,{\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathbb{U}]}^{(+)}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}}).

(9.28)

We shall number the (CL) vine ingredients of
\mathbb{U}
as
\mathbb{U}_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq I)
and associate to each
\mathbb{U}^{i}\,(1\leq i\leq I)
the nodes
\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i},\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{i}
etc. as in Proposition
7.7
. We order these
\mathbb{U}^{i}
from bottom to top, i.e. assume that
\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}=\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i+1}
; from this we also have that
\mathfrak{u}_{11}^{i}=\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{i+1}
, and
(\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{11})=(\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{I},\mathfrak{u}_{11}^{I})
and
(\mathfrak{u}_{2},\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22})=(\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{1},\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{1},\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{1})
etc. Also define the two subsets
\mathrm{Bad}
and
\mathrm{Nor}
of
\{1,\cdots I\}
corresponding to bad and normal vines
\mathbb{U}_{i}
. At the level of the molecule, we denote by
v_{1}^{i}
and
v_{2}^{i}
the joints of the vine
\mathbb{U}^{i}
for
1\leq i\leq I
such that
v_{j}^{i}
corresponds to the branching node
\mathfrak{u}_{j}^{i}
, so we have
v_{1}^{i}=v_{2}^{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq I-1
. Define
r:=g-h=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}
, we may also assume
r=e-f=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}}
(the opposite sign case being the same), then for each
1\leq i\leq I
we have that

r=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{i}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{i}}=\pm(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}^{i}})\neq 0.

Step 2: Re-parametrization of decorations.
We now perform re-parametrizations for decorations
\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]
. This will be done for each
\mathbb{U}^{i}
individually, and depends on the type (bad or normal) of
\mathbb{U}^{i}
.

(A) Suppose
i\in\mathrm{Bad}
and
\mathbb{U}^{i}
is Vine (II), then it can be annotated as in Figure
18
. Here we denote by
(v_{1}^{i},v_{2}^{i})
the joints and
(v_{2j+1}^{i},v_{2j+2}^{i})\,(1\leq j\leq m_{i})
the interior atoms such that
v^{i}_{2j+1}
is connected by a double bond to
v^{i}_{2j+2}
. Then
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
contains
n_{i}:=2m_{i}+1
branching nodes (which we denote by
\mathfrak{u}_{j}^{i}\,(2\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+2)
corresponding to
v_{j}^{i}
), and
2m_{i}+1
leaf pairs.

For any decoration, denote the values of
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
for leaves
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
by
(k_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+1)
, and denote the values of
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
for branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
by
(\ell_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+1)
. This decoration leads to decoration of bonds of
\mathbb{U}^{i}
as in Definition
7.4
; for
1\leq j\leq m_{i}-1
we we denote by
(a_{j}^{i},b_{j}^{i})
the decoration of the double bond between
v^{i}_{2j+1}
and
v^{i}_{2j+2}
, and denote by
(c_{j}^{i},d_{j}^{i})
the decoration of the single bonds between
\{v_{2j+1}^{i},v_{2j+2}^{i}\}
and
\{v_{2j+3}^{i},v_{2j+4}^{i}\}
. For
j\in\{0,m_{i}\}
we make obvious modifications as shown in Figure
18
(A). Finally, we also define
\iota^{i}_{j}=+1
if bond decorated by
c^{i}_{j}
is outgoing from
v^{i}_{2j+1}
for
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
or from
v^{i}_{2}
for
j=0
, and
\iota^{i}_{j}=-1
otherwise. Note that specifying
\iota^{i}_{j}
completely determines the directions of all the bonds in
\mathbb{U}^{i}
except for the vertical double bonds in Figure
18
(A).

Figure 18.
(A) is the annotation of the decoration of a Vine (II), whereas (B)–(D) are the annotations of parts of Vine (III), (IV) and (V)–(VIII) respectively as referred to in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
9.5
. The
i
superscripts of
v_{j}^{i}
and
w_{j}^{i}
are omitted in the above diagram to simplify the notation.

From this decoration of the bonds and using Definition
7.4
, we have that

\iota^{i}_{j}(c^{i}_{j}-d^{i}_{j})=\iota^{i}_{j-1}(c^{i}_{j-1}-d^{i}_{j-1})=\pm r,\quad 1\leq j\leq m_{i};

we may assume it is
+r
without loss of generality. Moreover, we have from (
7.4
) that

\iota_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+1}^{i}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+1}^{i}}+\iota_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+2}^{i}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+2}^{i}}=-\Gamma_{v_{2j+1}^{i}}-\Gamma_{v^{i}_{2j+2}}=\pm 2r\cdot(c_{j-1}^{i}-c_{j}^{i}\mathrm{\ or\ }c_{j-1}^{i}-d_{j}^{i}).

(9.29)

As a result of this, we can define new variables
x_{0}^{i},x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}
(
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
) such that (a) each of
(x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i})
is the difference of two vectors among
(a_{j}^{i},b_{j}^{i},c_{j}^{i},c^{i}_{j-1})
for
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
, (b) for
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
we have

\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}^{i}_{2j+2}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}^{i}_{2j+2}}=-\Gamma_{v^{i}_{2j+2}}=2x_{j}^{i}\cdot y_{j}^{i},\qquad\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}^{i}_{2j+1}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}^{i}_{2j+1}}=-2x^{i}_{j}\cdot y^{i}_{j}+2r\cdot\zeta^{i}_{j}

(9.30)

where
\zeta^{i}_{j}=\alpha^{i}_{j}x^{i}_{j}+\beta^{i}_{j}y_{j}+\theta^{i}_{j}r
for some
\alpha^{i}_{j},\beta^{i}_{j},\theta^{i}_{j}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
with
(\alpha^{i}_{j})^{2}+(\beta^{i}_{j})^{2}\neq 0
, and (c) for
j=0
, we choose
x_{0}^{i}
so that

\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}^{i}_{2}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}^{i}_{2}}=2r\cdot x_{0}^{i}.

(9.31)

This choice of
x_{0}^{i}
depends on
c_{0}^{i},d_{0}^{i}
and the decoration of
\mathbb{U}^{i-1}
(if
i=1
, then
x_{0}^{i}
is a linear combination of
(c_{0}^{1},d_{0}^{1},e,f,g,h)
).

We denote
\bm{x}^{i}=(x_{0}^{i},x^{i}_{j},y^{i}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m_{i}}
,
{\bm{k}^{i}}=(k_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+1)
and
\bm{\ell}^{i}=(\ell_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+1)
. By the above discussions, we can use
\bm{x}^{i}
as a substitute or re-parametrization for the variables
k_{\mathfrak{u}}
for
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\setminus\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
(which are just
{\bm{k}^{i}}
and
\bm{\ell}^{i}
). Note that (A-i) we have
{\bm{k}^{i}}=T_{1}^{i}{\bm{x}^{i}}+{\bm{h}}_{1}^{i}
and
\bm{\ell}^{i}=T_{2}^{i}{\bm{x}^{i}}+{\bm{h}}_{2}^{i}
for some matrices
T_{k}^{i}
and some vectors
{\bm{h}}_{k}^{i}
depending only on
(k^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}^{i}_{22}})
, such that all coefficients of
T_{1}^{i},\,(T_{1}^{i})^{-1},\,T_{2}^{i}
are
0
or
\pm 1
; and (A-ii) any variable in
\bm{x}^{i}
is the difference of two variables, such that each of them is either a variable in
{\bm{k}^{i}}
or
\bm{\ell}^{i}
, or in
(k^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k^{i}_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})
. Moreover, we have that (
9.30
) holds for the ladder part and (
9.31
) holds for the joint
v_{2}^{i}
. These follow from simple calculations based on the above parametrization.

(B) Suppose
i\in\mathrm{Bad}
and
\mathbb{U}^{i}
is Vine (I) (which is just a double bond), then this parametrization is much easier. Indeed, it corresponds to the case
m_{i}=0
above, for which we only need to choose
x_{0}^{i}
as above so that (
9.31
) holds. Clearly (A-i) and (A-ii) also hold in this simple case.

(C) Now suppose
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
, so
\mathbb{U}^{i}
is a normal vine. By examining Figure
15
, we see it contains
m_{i}
pairs of atoms connected by double bounds that belong to the ladders represented by colored dashed lines (for Vine (VII) we also include the pair of atoms where all three ladders intersect). Moreover there are in total
n_{i}:=2m_{i}+4
atoms excluding the joint atom
v^{i}_{1}
(hence
2m_{i}+4
branching nodes and
2m_{i}+4
leaf pairs in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
). These atoms can be divided into three groups: (a) the joint
v_{2}^{i}
which corresponds to the branching node
\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}
; (b) the
m_{i}
pairs of atoms in the ladders; and (c) the three remaining atoms. We denote the atoms in (b) by
v_{j}^{i}\,(3\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+2)
and the corresponding branching nodes in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
by
\mathfrak{u}_{j}^{i}
similar to (A), and denote the atoms in (c) by
(w^{i}_{1},w^{i}_{2},w^{i}_{3})
and the corresponding branching nodes by
(\mathfrak{w}_{1}^{i},\mathfrak{w}_{2}^{i},\mathfrak{w}_{3}^{i})
.

For any decoration, denote the values of
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
for leaves
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
by
(k_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+4)
, and denote the values of
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
for branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
by
(\ell_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+4)
. This decoration also leads to decoration of bonds of
\mathbb{U}^{i}
.

Now we want to define new variables
(x^{i}_{0},x^{i}_{j},y^{i}_{j},u^{i}_{1},u^{i}_{2},u^{i}_{3})_{1\leq j\leq m_{i}}
. First, by the same argument for Vine (II) above, we have that
\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}=2r\cdot x^{i}_{0}
where
x_{0}^{i}
can be defined similar to (A). Next, for each pair of atoms
(v_{2j+1}^{i},v_{2j+2}^{i})
connected by a double bond in a ladder and the corresponding branching nodes
\mathfrak{u}_{2j+1}^{i},\mathfrak{u}_{2j+2}^{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
, the same argument for Vine (II) above shows that

\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+1}^{i}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+1}^{i}}+\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+2}^{i}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+2}^{i}}=2\widetilde{r}\cdot\mu.

Here
\mu
is the difference of two
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
vectors corresponding to two of the four single bonds at this pair of atoms, and
\widetilde{r}\in\{0,r\}
is the the same for all pairs of atoms in the same ladder and is equal to
r
for the ladders attached to the joints and zero otherwise
11
11

11

In Figure
15
, the
\widetilde{r}
values for those ladders with cyan color are
0
; we shall call such ladders zero-gap ladders.
. Note that for the pair of atoms where all three ladders intersect in Vine (VII), the argument needs to be slightly adjusted but the result remains the same, with
\widetilde{r}=r
in this case. In any case, we can define
(x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d})^{2}
similar to (A), such that (
9.30
) holds with
r
replaced by
\widetilde{r}
.

It now remains to define the variables
(u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i},u_{3}^{i})
. Here we only discuss Vine (III) in detail below, as arguments in other cases are similar.

(C-i) For Vine (III), using the notation in Figure
18
(B), we may denote
\omega_{j}^{i}=\zeta_{\mathfrak{w}_{j}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{w}_{j}}
where
\mathfrak{w}_{j}^{i}
is the node in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]
corresponding to the atom
w^{i}_{j}
. In Figure
18
(B) and Figure
15
, note that
e_{*}-g_{*}=f_{*}-h_{*}=\pm r
, and
(e_{*},g_{*})
is determined by
x^{i}_{0}
and some of the
(x^{i}_{j},y^{i}_{j})
variables. Now, if the bonds decorated by
c
and
d
have opposite directions (say
c
goes from
w^{i}_{1}
to
w^{i}_{3}
, and
d
goes from
w^{i}_{3}
to
w^{i}_{1}
), we may define
(u^{i}_{1},u^{i}_{2},u^{i}_{3})=(c-d,d-a,d-b)
. If the bonds decorated by
c
and
d
have the same direction (which has to go from
w^{i}_{1}
to
w^{i}_{3}
), then we may define
(u^{i}_{1},u^{i}_{2},u^{i}_{3})=(c-a,a-d,c-b)
. Then we have
\omega^{i}_{1}=2u_{1}^{i}\cdot u_{2}^{i}
, while
\omega_{2}^{i}
equals
2u_{1}^{i}\cdot u_{3}^{i}
in the first case, and equals
2u_{3}^{i}\cdot(u_{1}^{i}+u_{2}^{i}-u_{3}^{i})
in the second case. Moreover we have
\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}=\pm(|e_{*}|^{2}-|f_{*}|^{2}-|g_{*}|^{2}+|h_{*}|^{2})=2r\cdot\xi^{i}
where
\xi^{i}=a^{i}u_{1}^{i}+b^{i}u_{2}^{i}+c^{i}u_{3}^{i}+dr
with
a^{i},b^{i},c^{i},d^{i}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
. In any case, the variables
(u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i},u_{3}^{i})
determine
(a,b,c,d,f_{*},h_{*})
of Figure
18
, and allow us to proceed with parametrizing the next ladder starting from
(f_{*},h_{*})
by the rest of
(x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i})
variables. The argument for Vine (IV) is similar, see Figure
18
(C).

(C-ii) For Vines (V)–(VIII), the argument is again similar, and in fact much easier. In Figure
18
(D) and Figure
15
, note that the two bonds going in and out the triangle are both decorated by
g_{*}
(which is determined by the
(x_{0}^{i},x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i})
variables), which means that the vector
r
for Vine (III) above is replaced by
\widetilde{r}=0
. In particular, we have
\omega_{1}^{i}+\omega_{2}^{i}+\omega_{3}^{i}=0
where
\omega^{i}_{j}=\zeta_{\mathfrak{w}_{j}^{i}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{w}_{j}^{i}}
. Then we argue as above, with
(u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i},u_{3}^{i})=(a-e,b-e,a-c)
if bonds decorated by
a
and
b
have the same direction, and
(u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i},u_{3}^{i})=(e-g_{*},b-g_{*},d-g_{*})
if they have opposite directions, then the same results will hold.

As a result, we can always define the variables
(u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i},u_{3}^{i})
, such that when put together with the other variables defined above, the variables
\bm{x}^{i}=(x_{0}^{i},x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i},u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i},u_{3}^{i})_{1\leq j\leq m_{i}}
can be used as a substitute or re-parametrization for the variables
k_{\mathfrak{u}}
for
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}\}
. These
k_{\mathfrak{u}}
variables are formed by
\bm{k}^{i}=(k_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+4)
and
\bm{\ell}^{i}=(\ell_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq 2m_{i}+4)
; the variables
\bm{x}^{i}
,
\bm{k}^{i}
and
\bm{\ell}^{i}
verify the same properties (A-i) and (A-ii) as stated at the end of (A). Moreover, we have that (
9.30
) holds for each ladder (with
r
replaced by
\widetilde{r}
), and (
9.31
) holds for the joint
v_{2}^{i}
. For the remaining three branching nodes in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}^{i}]
, their corresponding resonance factors are given by
2u_{1}^{i}\cdot u_{2}^{i}
and
\Lambda^{i}\in\{2u_{1}^{i}\cdot u_{3}^{i},2u_{3}^{i}\cdot(u_{1}^{i}+u_{2}^{i}-u_{3}^{i})\}
, and
-2u_{1}^{i}\cdot u_{2}^{i}-\Lambda^{i}+\widetilde{r}\cdot\xi^{i}
where
\xi^{i}=au^{i}_{1}+bu^{i}_{2}+cu^{i}_{3}+dr
with
a,b,c,d\in\{0,\pm 1\}
and
\widetilde{r}\in\{r,0\}
.

We now obtain the new set of variables
\bm{x}=(\bm{x}^{i}:1\leq i\leq I)
, where
\bm{x}^{i}=(x_{0}^{i},x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}:1\leq j\leq m_{i})
for
i\in\mathrm{Bad}
and
\bm{x}^{i}=(x_{0}^{i},x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i},w_{1}^{i},w_{2}^{i},w_{3}^{i}:1\leq j\leq m_{i})
for
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
. Let
\bm{k}=(\bm{k}^{i}:1\leq i\leq I)
and similarly for
\bm{\ell}
, then these variables satisfy the properties (i)–(iii) stated in Proposition
9.4
. In fact (i) and (iii) follow from putting together for each
i
the properties (A-i) and (A-ii) stated at the end of (A) (which are true also for (B) and (C)). Property (ii), after a possible reordering of
\bm{k}
and
\bm{\ell}
variables, is implied by the following statement which is a consequence of Lemma 6.6 of
[
19
]
:

\bullet
For each vector
k_{\mathfrak{u}}
in
\bm{\ell}
(where
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
) there exists
\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}
which is a child of
\mathfrak{u}
such that
k_{\mathfrak{u}}\pm k_{\mathfrak{u}^{\prime}}
is an integer linear combination of
(k_{\mathfrak{l}}:\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathbb{U}])
and
(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})
with absolute value sum of coefficients at most
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{u}}
, and moreover we have
\prod_{\mathfrak{u}}\Lambda_{\mathfrak{u}}\leq C_{0}^{n_{(1)}}
.

In addition, from the above discussion we know that (
9.30
) holds for each ladder (possibly with
r
replaced by
\widetilde{r}
), (
9.31
) holds for each joint
v_{2}^{i}
, and the remaining three resonance factors at each normal vine
\mathbb{U}^{i}
is given by expressions involving
u_{j}^{i}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
as stated above. Also let
n_{i}:=2m_{i}+1+3\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{Nor}}(i)
, so the sum of these
n_{i}
equals
n_{(1)}
which is also the total number of these variables.

We shall rename the time variables as follows: for each
i
let
t_{0}^{i}:=t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}
. For each
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
let
(t_{j}^{i},s_{j}^{i}):=(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+2}^{i}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2j+1}^{i}})
, and for each
i\in\mathrm{Nor}
and
1\leq j\leq 3
let
\tau_{j}^{i}:=t_{\mathfrak{w}_{j}^{i}}
. Then, with all the above preparations, we can write

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}^{\mathbb{U}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{(1)}}\zeta[\mathbb{U}]\sum_{\bm{x}}\epsilon_{\bm{x}}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{I}[\mathbb{U}]}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{I}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-s_{j}^{i})\langle x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}\rangle}\times\prod_{j=0}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-t)\langle r,\zeta_{j}^{i}\rangle}\,\bigg]

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\prod_{i\in\mathrm{Nor}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{1}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\langle u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i}\rangle}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{2}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\Lambda_{i}}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{3}^{i}-t)\langle r,\xi_{i}\rangle}\bigg]

\displaystyle\times{\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathbb{U}]}^{(+)}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}})\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{m}},

(9.32)

where the relationship between
\bm{x}
and
(k_{\mathfrak{m}})
is as described above, and
\epsilon_{\bm{x}}
is just the
\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}[\mathbb{U}]}
in (
9.28
).

Step 3: The case of non-core vines.
We now consider the case when each
\mathbb{U}^{i}
is either non-core or does not satisfy the assumptions in Definition
7.18
(here, if
\mathbb{U}^{i}
is core, these assumptions are that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}\leq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{i}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{i}})
and that both
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{i},\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{i})}
are coherent, where the notations are as in Proposition
7.12
with superscript
i
). In this case, there is no twist and we just need to study the single term
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}
. To this end, we use Propositions
5.1
and
6.1
to replace the factor
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})
on the last line in (
9.32
) by a linear combination of

(\sqrt{\delta})^{n(\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})})}e^{\pi i(\lambda t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}}+\lambda^{\prime}t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}})}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}(k_{\mathfrak{l}}),

with coefficient being in
L_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}^{1}
with
(\langle\lambda\rangle+\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle)^{\eta}
weight. Here each
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
is bounded in
\mathfrak{S}^{30d,30d}
by
L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})g_{\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}}
uniformly in
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
, where
g_{\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
is the incoherency index of
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
. The same can be done for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}
, but with the
\mathfrak{S}^{30d,30d}
norm replaced by
\mathfrak{S}^{30d,0}
, and
g_{\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
replaced by
g_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
which is the incoherency index of
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
. Therefore, with all the
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
variables as above fixed, we can reduce
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}}
to a term of form

(C_{1}\sqrt{\delta})^{n_{(0)}}\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{(1)}}\zeta[\mathbb{U}]\cdot\mathcal{I}(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},e,f,g,h).

Here
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},e,f,g,h)=(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})
as before,
n_{(0)}
is the total order of all
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
, and
\mathcal{I}
is an expression as in (
9.4
). Here note that once we specify
r=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}}\neq 0
, we may replace
\epsilon_{\bm{x}}
in (
9.32
) by
1
, since if any
\epsilon
factor is not
1
, this would imply some restrictions on the relevant variables (such as
x_{j}^{i}=0
or
y_{j}^{i}=0
, or
u_{1}^{i}+u_{2}^{i}-u_{3}^{i}=0
etc.), which allows us to sum over these variables trivially and gain an extra power
L^{-0.1\gamma_{0}}
. Moreover, for any bad vine
\mathbb{U}^{i}
, if it is not core, then it must be Vine (II-e) as in Proposition
7.12
, so we have
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{i}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{i}}
from Figure
16
, which translate to either
t^{i}_{1}<t_{0}^{i}<s_{1}^{i}
or
s^{i}_{1}<t_{0}^{i}<t_{1}^{i}
in the new time variables, hence assumption (a) in Proposition
9.4
holds. If
\mathbb{U}^{i}
is core, then by definition, either
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}>\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{i}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{i}})
or one of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{i},\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{i})}
is not coherent. In the second case assumption (d) in Proposition
9.4
holds; in the first case, since
\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}
is also a child node of either
\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{i}
or
\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{i}
, we know that either
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{i}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{i}}
or
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{i}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{i}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{i}}
, hence assumption (a) in Proposition
9.4
holds.

This justifies that
\mathcal{I}
indeed verifies all assumptions in Proposition
9.4
, thus (
9.27
) follows from (
9.16
), if we notice that in the notation of Proposition
9.4
we have
M=n_{(1)}
and
n_{(2)}=n_{(1)}+n_{(0)}
, and
|\mathrm{Bad}|=b
and
M_{0}+M_{1}=n_{(3)}
. Note also that the
\eta^{6}
power of the
(\lambda_{j}^{i},\mu_{j}^{i},\sigma_{j}^{i})
variables in (
9.16
) can be absorbed by exploiting the
(\langle\lambda\rangle+\langle\lambda^{\prime}\rangle)^{\eta}
weight above, as each
\lambda_{j}^{i}
etc. is an algebraic sum of these
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
.

Step 4: Cancellation at the core vines.
We now consider the general case
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}}=\sum_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}}_{(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}
. We will prove that it can also be written as a linear combination of expressions of form

(\sqrt{\delta})^{n_{(0)}}\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{(1)}}\zeta[\mathbb{U}]\cdot\mathcal{I}(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},e,f,g,h),

(9.33)

with coefficients in a weighted
L^{1}
space, as in Step 3; here
\mathcal{I}
is as in (
9.4
). Once this is done, then (
9.27
) again follows from (
9.16
) by the same arguments as above.

We shall argue by induction on the number of
core vines
\mathbb{U}^{i}
satisfying the assumptions in Definition
7.18
. The base case is when there is none, which is treated in Step 3. Suppose there is at least one such vine, let
\mathbb{U}^{p}
be the one with
p
being largest. Denote by
\mathbb{U}^{<p}
the union of the vines
\mathbb{U}^{1},\cdots,\mathbb{U}^{p-1}
, and define

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}^{<p}}(t_{\mathfrak{u}^{p-1}_{1}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{p-1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}^{p-1}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})

to be the expression in (
9.26
) (where we sum over all LF twists) but with
\mathbb{U}
replaced by
\mathbb{U}^{<p}
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{p-1}=\mathfrak{u}^{p}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{u}^{p-1}_{11}=\mathfrak{u}^{p}_{23}
. Since no LF twisting is needed for
\mathbb{U}^{i}\,(i>p)
, we can use (
9.32
) to get that

\sum_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}}_{(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}=\sum_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{G}_{p}})^{\mathbb{U}^{p}},

where the last sum is taken over all
\mathcal{G}_{p}
which are (unit) LF twists of
\mathcal{G}
at
\mathbb{U}^{p}
only; for each such twist, we have

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{G}_{p}})^{\mathbb{U}^{p}}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{(1)}^{\geq p}}\zeta[\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}]\sum_{\bm{x}_{\geq p}}\int_{\mathcal{I}[\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}]}\bigg[\prod_{i=p}^{I}\bigg(\prod_{j=1}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-s_{j}^{i})\langle x_{j}^{i},y_{j}^{i}\rangle}\times\prod_{j=0}^{m_{i}}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(t_{j}^{i}-t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}})\langle r,\zeta_{j}^{i}\rangle}\,\bigg)

\displaystyle\times\bigg[\prod_{i\geq p+1}e^{\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{1}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\langle u_{1}^{i},u_{2}^{i}\rangle}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{2}^{i}-\tau_{3}^{i})\Lambda_{i}}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(\tau_{3}^{i}-t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}})\langle r,\xi_{i}\rangle}\bigg]

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}[\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}]}^{(+)}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}[\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}\}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}})\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle\times\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}^{<p}}(t_{\mathfrak{u}^{p}_{2}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}).

(9.34)

Here,
\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}
is the vine chain formed by
\mathbb{U}^{i}\,(i\geq p)
, which we identify as the result of splicing/merging all the vines
\mathbb{U}^{i}\,(i<p)
as in Proposition
7.7
, and
n_{(1)}^{\geq p}
is the same as
n_{(1)}
but defined for
\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}
, and the other objects are all associated with
\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}
. We have also replaced
\epsilon_{\bm{x}_{\geq p}}
by
1
since otherwise we would obtain better estimates.

The last factor in (
9.34
) is

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}^{<p}}(t_{p},t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime},e_{p},f_{p},g,h),

where
(t_{p},t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})=(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})
and
(g,h)=(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}})
, and
(e_{p},f_{p})
is either
(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}})
or
(k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}})
similar to Step 1. Note that, if we perform a unit LF twist at
\mathbb{U}^{p}
which corresponds to a unit twist of skeleton, then after this operation, the quantity
\mathcal{K}^{\mathbb{U}^{<p}}
is transformed to another quantity with exactly the role of
e_{p}
and
f_{p}
switched (or equivalently, the roles of
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}
and
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}
switched). However, by Remark
7.16
(d), we know that the roles of
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}
and
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}
in corresponding decorations of
\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}
are switched again due to the unit twist. Thus the quantity
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}^{<p}}
can be extracted as a common factor for all unit LF twists at
\mathbb{U}^{p}
.

Now consider
\mathcal{G}_{p}
which is a unit LF twist of
\mathcal{G}
. By Remark
7.16
and Definition
7.18
, for this
\mathcal{G}_{p}
we have
\zeta[\mathbb{U}^{\geq p}]=\sigma\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}
with
\sigma
independent of the choice of
\mathcal{G}_{p}
. Moreover, the regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and regular trees
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
are also independent of the choice of
\mathcal{G}_{p}
, except for
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p})}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p})}
, which range over all regular couples and trees described at the end of Definition
7.18
(we denote this set by
\mathscr{B}
). Therefore, the sum over all
\mathcal{G}_{p}
equals the sum over
(\mathcal{G}_{p})_{\mathrm{sk}}\in\{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}\}
where
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
is the unit twist of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, together with the sum over all
(\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p})},\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p})})\in\mathscr{B}
(where the set
\mathscr{B}
may also depend on the choice of
(\mathcal{G}_{p})_{\mathrm{sk}}
). Recall from Definition
7.14
that the unit twist exchanges Vine (I-a), (II-a), (II-c) with Vine (I-b), (II-b), (II-d) respectively. Below we will only consider Vines (II-a) and (II-b), as Vines (II-c) and (II-d) are similar, and Vines (I-a) and (I-b) are much simpler
12
12

12

Compared to Vines (II), the case of Vines (I) will always involve an extra factor or
|r|
corresponding to assumption (b) of Proposition
9.4
.
.

Next consider all the input functions
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}
in (
9.34
). For
(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})\neq(\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p})
and
\mathfrak{m}\neq\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}
, we simply expand them as in Step 3; this results in
e^{i\lambda t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}}}
etc. modulation factors and functions of
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
with (
\mathfrak{S}^{30d,30d}
or
\mathfrak{S}^{30d,0}
) bounds of
L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})g}
where
g
is the corresponding incoherency index. As for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p})}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p})}}^{*}
, we know they must be coherent by Definition
7.18
. By Propositions
6.1
and
6.3
, we can replace them by either remainder factors which satisfy the same bound as above with
g\geq 1
(this
g
now is not incoherency index but merely indicates a remainder term), or the approximants
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p})}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
and
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p})}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
which satisfy (
6.3
)–(
6.5
). The remainder term can be treated in the same way as in Step 3, since cancellation is not needed here due to the power gain
L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta}})
, so we will only consider the
(\cdots)_{\mathrm{app}}
terms.

We consider first the case
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}
, in which case the set
\mathscr{B}
does not depend on the choice of
(\mathcal{G}_{p})_{\mathrm{sk}}\in\{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}},\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}\}
. By the induction hypothesis, we can write
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathbb{U}^{<p}}
as a linear combination of expressions of form (
9.33
) but adapted to
\mathbb{U}^{<p}
(where
\mathcal{I}
is as in (
9.4
)). We henceforth perform the above reductions for the
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}
factors, and make the change of vector variables and rename the time variables as in Step 2. Putting all these together, we can write
\sum_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{G}_{p}})^{\mathbb{U}^{p}}
as a linear combination of expressions
\mathcal{J}
that has the same form as (
9.33
) now adapted to
\mathbb{U}
(again with
\mathcal{I}
as in (
9.4
)), but with only one difference. Namely, the factors

e^{\pi i\lambda_{0}^{p}t_{0}^{p}}\cdot e^{\pi i\lambda_{1}^{p}t_{1}^{p}}\cdot e^{\pi i\mu_{1}^{p}s_{1}^{p}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{1}^{p}(k_{1}^{p})\cdot\mathcal{L}_{1}^{p}(\ell_{1}^{p})

(9.35)

that occur in the expression of
\mathcal{I}
in (
9.4
) corresponding to
i=p
, should be replaced by

(C_{1}\sqrt{\delta})^{-(n(\mathcal{Q})+n(\mathcal{T}))}\sum_{(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})\in\mathscr{B}}^{(+)}\bigg\{\mathbf{1}_{t_{0}^{p}<t_{1}^{p}}\cdot(\mathcal{K}_{\overline{\mathcal{Q}}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{0}^{p},s_{1}^{p},y_{*})\cdot(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},x_{*})\\
-\mathbf{1}_{t_{0}^{p}<s_{1}^{p}}\cdot(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},x_{*})\cdot(\mathcal{K}_{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(s_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},y_{*})\bigg\},

(9.36)

where the summation is taken over all
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})\in\mathscr{B}
such that
\mathcal{T}
has sign
+
.

We explain (
9.36
) as follows: the garden
(\mathcal{G}_{p})_{\mathrm{sk}}
has two possibilities which are unit twists of each other, one being Vine (II-a) and the other being Vine (II-b). In either case, by the renaming of time variables above, we always have
(t_{0}^{p},s_{1}^{p},t_{1}^{p})=(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}})
and the unit interval that each of them belongs to does not depend on the twisting; also in relation with (
9.35
), we assume
k_{1}^{p}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p}}
and
\ell_{1}^{p}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}
.

For Vine (II-a), see Figure
16
, we may assume
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}=\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}=+
and
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p}}=-
. We then choose
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p})}=\overline{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p})}=\mathcal{T}
. In (
9.36
) we set
x_{*}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}
and
y_{*}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p}}
. Finally the structure of the couple forces
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}
, which is
t_{0}^{p}<t_{1}^{p}
.

For Vine (II-b), we have
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}=\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}=-
and
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p}}=+
. We then choose
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p},\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p})}=\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p})}=\overline{\mathcal{T}}
. In (
9.36
) we set
x_{*}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{0}^{p}}
and
y_{*}=k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}
(note that the roles of
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}^{p}
and
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{23}^{p}}
in decorations are switched after unit twisting, see Remark
7.16
(d)). Finally the structure of the couple forces
t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}<t_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}
, which is
t_{0}^{p}<s_{1}^{p}
.

In the above discussions, note also that the
x_{*}-y_{*}
is fixed and equals
\pm r
. These arguments then lead to the expression (
9.36
), where the negative sign before the second term is due to the change of
\zeta_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}}
after the unit twisting. Of course these arguments are for Vines (II-a) and (II-b), but the case of Vines (II-c) and (II-d) is similar, and the case of Vines (I) is much easier.

Now it remains to analyze (
9.36
). Recall the identity (
6.8
). We apply (
6.3
)–(
6.5
) to reduce the expression in (
9.36
) to one of the followings:

(1)

Terms containing
\mathbf{1}_{t_{1}^{p}<t_{0}^{p}<s_{1}^{p}}
or
\mathbf{1}_{s_{1}^{p}<t_{0}^{p}<t_{1}^{p}}
. This leads to assumption (a) in Proposition
9.4
.

(2)

Terms containing
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(\cdot,\cdot,y_{*})-(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(\cdot,\cdot,x_{*})
or
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(\cdot,\cdot,x_{*})-(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(\cdot,\cdot,y_{*})
,with the time variables being the same in both functions. Since
x_{*}-y_{*}=\pm r
, this is bounded by
|r|
and leads to assumption (b) in Proposition
9.4
.

(3)

Terms containing
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p})-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(s_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p})
or
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p})-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}(s_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p})
. These come from the decomposition (
6.3
); due to the
\mathfrak{X}^{1-\eta}
bounds in (
6.4
)–(
6.5
), we can extract a factor of
|t_{1}^{p}-s_{1}^{p}|^{1-3\eta}
and still preserve the same weighted Fourier
L^{1}
estimate as above. This leads to assumption (c) in Proposition
9.4
.

(4)

The leading factor, which we can use (
6.8
) to reduce to

\left[\overline{(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},x_{*})(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},x_{*})-(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},x_{*})\overline{(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},x_{*})\right].

Thanks to (
6.9
), this is
exactly zero
after summing over all
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})\in\mathscr{B}
.

Here note that, in cases (a)–(c) above, the number of choices for
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})
is at most
C_{2}\cdot C_{0}^{n(\mathcal{Q})+n(\mathcal{T})}
by Proposition
4.10
, which is acceptable because this only occurs once for
\mathbb{U}^{p}
and can be covered by the
L^{-\eta^{2}}
gain for this bad vine obtained below. Therefore, in the case
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}
, we may fix one choice of
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})
which will satisfy one of the assumptions (a)–(d) Proposition
4.10
.

Finally, consider the case
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}>\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}
(which is true for Vines (II-c) and (II-d), and can be assumed by symmetry for Vines (II-a) and (II-b)). In this case the set
\mathscr{B}
of regular couples and regular trees defined above, is not the same for
(\mathcal{G}_{q})_{\mathrm{sk}}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
and for
(\mathcal{G}_{q})_{\mathrm{sk}}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}^{\prime}
. More precisely,

•

The set
\mathscr{B}
for Vine (II-a) consists of
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})
described at the end of Definition
7.18
, which have the exact structure described in Proposition
4.9
, with
(q,q^{\prime})
in Proposition
4.9
replaced by
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}})
for
\mathcal{Q}
, and by
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}})
for
\mathcal{T}
;

•

The other set
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
for Vine (II-b) consists of the same
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})
, but with
(q,q^{\prime})
in Proposition
4.9
replaced by
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}})
for
\mathcal{Q}
, and by
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}})
for
\mathcal{T}
.

To treat this issue we apply the following argument. First, for any
\mathcal{Q}
we can extend the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(t,s)
(and hence
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t,s,k)
) in Proposition
6.1
to include all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. In fact, currently it is defined for
t\in[q,q+1]

(for notations see Proposition
4.9
and the beginning of Section
5
), where
q\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}
with
\mathfrak{r}
being the positive root of
\mathcal{Q}
. Moreover, by Definition
3.8
, see also the proof of Proposition
6.1
, it is easy to see that (i) if
q>\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}
then
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(t,s)
is constant in
t
for
t\in[q,q+1]
, and (ii) if
q=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}
then
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(t,s)
equals
0
if
t=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}
. Now we can define
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(t,s)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, by keeping the current definition for
t\in[\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}+1]
(when
q=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}
) and extending it continuously as constant (in
t
) functions for
t\in(-\infty,\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}]
and
t\in[\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}+1,+\infty)
. The same extensions can be defined also for
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}
for any
\mathcal{T}
. These extensions are consistent with the current definition for any
q\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}
, and equals
0
for
t\leq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}
; moreover, an elementary argument shows that the norm bounds (
6.4
)–(
6.5
) for
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}
, which hold for
t\in[q,q+1]
, are also preserved (with
\mathfrak{X}^{1-\eta}
weakened by
\mathfrak{X}^{1-2\eta}
) by such continuous constant extensions on any interval of length
O(1)
.

Now we consider the case
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}>\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}
. Recall that
(t_{0}^{p},s_{1}^{p},t_{1}^{p})=(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}})
; if
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}-\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}\geq 2
, then we must have
|t_{1}^{p}-s_{1}^{p}|\geq 1
, so we can trivially insert a factor
|t_{1}^{p}-s_{1}^{p}|^{1-3\eta}
without any loss, which leads to assumption (c) in Proposition
9.4
. Thus we may assume
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}-\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}=1
, in which case
|t_{1}^{p}-s_{1}^{p}|\leq 2
. Here, instead of (
9.36
), we have the difference of two sums of products of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
factors, where the first sum is taken over
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})\in\mathscr{B}
and the second sum is taken over
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
. However, for any fixed
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})
we can use the above-defined extensions (which satisfy (
6.4
)–(
6.5
) on any interval of length at most
4
) to replace any time variable
s_{1}^{p}
in any
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
and
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}
by
t_{1}^{p}
; for the difference created in this process, we can still extract a factor of
|t_{1}^{p}-s_{1}^{p}|^{1-3\eta}
and preserve the same weighted Fourier
L^{1}
estimates. This again leads to assumption (c) in Proposition
9.4
, just as in case (c) described above.

Finally, once all the time variables
s_{1}^{p}
are replaced by
t_{1}^{p}
, we see that the contribution of
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})
(which is basically
(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},\cdot)\cdot(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*})_{\mathrm{app}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p},\cdot)
) will be zero unless
(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{T})\in\mathscr{B}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathscr{B}\cap\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
, which is defined in the same way as
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
above, but with
(q,q^{\prime})
in Proposition
4.9
replaced by
(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}^{p}})
for
both
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{T}
. This is because
t_{1}^{p}\in[\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}+1]
, so for any
\mathcal{Q}
or
\mathcal{T}
with root
\mathfrak{r}
satisfying
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{r}}\geq\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}+1
we must have
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{Q}}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p})=\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}(t_{1}^{p},t_{0}^{p})=0
. Therefore, we can now reduce to a sum of form (
9.36
) but with
\mathscr{B}
replaced by
\mathscr{B}^{\prime\prime}
, and (
6.9
) implies that the leading term as described in case (d) above is again
0
. This completes the case
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}^{p}}>\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}^{p}}
.

With all the above discussions, and by applying again the same arguments in Step 3 exploiting the weighted Fourier
L^{1}
bounds, we can finally reduce the target quantity
\sum_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{G}_{p}})^{\mathbb{U}^{p}}
to a linear combination of expressions of form (
9.33
) (with
\mathcal{I}
as in (
9.4
)) such that at least one of assumptions (a)–(d) in Proposition
9.4
holds for the bad vine
\mathbb{U}^{p}
(and consequently for every bad vine). Here note that the requirements (i)–(ii) in Proposition
9.4
for the relations between the variables
(\bm{x},\bm{k},\bm{\ell})
are verified by using the induction hypothesis, while requirement (iii) follows by again applying Lemma 6.6 of
[
19
]
as in Step 2 above. This completes the proof of (
9.27
).
∎

10.
Stage 1 reduction

We now describe the first reduction step in the proof of Proposition
3.11
. In this step we reduce any garden
\mathcal{G}
first to its skeleton
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
as in Section
9.2.1
, and then to a smaller garden
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
by splicing a suitably chosen set of (CL) vine chains. This set in particular contains all the bad vines where the cancellation argument in
[
22
]
is needed.

10.1.
Vine collections
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{V}_{0}

Recall we are estimating the sum (
3.46
) over all
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
(or
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathscr{C}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
) of fixed signature
(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R})
and order
n
. The starting point is to divide this sum into subset sums, where each subset sum is taken over an
equivalence class
of gardens
\mathcal{G}\in\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
; these equivalence classes are defined via LF twists for a suitable collection of (CL) vines
\mathbb{V}_{j}\subset\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
as in Definition
7.18
. We describe this collection of (CL) vines as follows.

Recall the notions of vines and vine-chains (VC’s), hyper-vines (HV’s) and hyper-vine-chains (HVC’s), and ladders in Definition
7.11
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Define a
double-Vine (V)
, or DV for short, to be the union of two vines (V), see Figure
15
, that share two common joints and no other common atoms (note this is not a vine-like object in Definition
7.11
). Then, for any molecule
\mathbb{M}
, there is a unique collection
\mathscr{C}
of disjoint atomic groups, such that each atomic group in
\mathscr{C}
is an HV, VC, HVC or DV, and any vine-like object in
\mathbb{V}
is a subset of some atomic group in
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

See Lemma 8.1 of
[
22
]
.
∎

Definition 10.2

(The collection
\mathscr{V}

[
22
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a garden with skeleton
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, and let
\mathscr{C}
be defined for the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
by Lemma
10.1
. Define a collection
\mathscr{V}
of (CL) vines as follows: for each
non-root VC
in
\mathscr{C}
(i.e. a VC that is not a root block, see Proposition
7.8
) we add to
\mathscr{V}
all its vine ingredients that are (CL) vines. For each HVC and root VC in
\mathscr{C}
, we add to
\mathscr{V}

all but one
of its vine ingredients, such that (a) if there is a (CN) vine then we only skip this one, and (b) if all vines are (CL) vines then we only skip the “top” vine whose
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
node is the ancestor of all other
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
nodes (as branching nodes of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, in the notation of Proposition
7.7
). We do not add anything that comes from any HV or DV in
\mathscr{C}
.

Note that the vines in
\mathscr{V}
can be arranged into finitely many disjoint VC’s, as is consistent with Definition
7.18
. We then define any couple
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
to be
congruent
to
\mathcal{G}
, if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is an LF twist of
\mathcal{G}
at vines in
\mathscr{V}
(i.e. we perform a unit LF twist at each vine in
\mathscr{V}
that is core and satisfies the assumptions in Definition
7.18
). Clearly, performing a full twist does not affect the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
nor the choice of vines in
\mathscr{V}
, and thus congruence is an equivalence relation.

Definition 10.3

(Large and small gaps
[
22
]
)
.

Given any molecule
\mathbb{M}
and block
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{M}
, let the four bonds in
\mathbb{B}
at the two joints be
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{B}
at one joint, and
\ell_{3},\ell_{4}\in\mathbb{B}
at the other. Then for any decoration
(k_{\ell})
we have
k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}=\pm(k_{\ell_{3}}-k_{\ell_{4}}):=r
; we call this vector the
gap
of
\mathbb{B}
relative to this decoration. Note that once the parameters
(c_{v})
of a decoration are fixed as in Definition
7.4
, then this
r
can be expressed as a function of the vectors
k_{\ell_{j}^{*}}
, where
\ell_{j}^{*}
runs over all bonds connecting a given joint of
\mathbb{B}
to atoms
not
in
\mathbb{B}
. If
\mathbb{B}
is concatenated by blocks
\mathbb{B}_{j}
, then all
\mathbb{B}_{j}
must have the same gap as
\mathbb{B}
. We also define the gap of a hyper-block to be the gap of its adjoint block.

More generally, if
v
is an atom and
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\leftrightarrow v
are two bonds with opposite directions, then we define the
gap
of the triple
(v,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
relative to a given decoration as
r:=k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}
. In particular the gap of any block or hyper-block equals a suitable gap at either of its joints. Next, for any ladder of length
\geq 1
(see Definition
7.11
and Figure
15
), the difference
k_{\ell}-k_{\ell^{\prime}}
for any pair of parallel single bonds
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
must be equal (up to a sign change), which we also define to be the
gap
of the ladder. In particular, if
\mathbb{V}
is a vine (or VC) with gap
r
, then for any ladder contained in
\mathbb{V}
(inserted between parallel dashed bonds of the same color in Figure
15
), the gap of this ladder is either
\pm r
or
0
. Finally, for all the gaps defined above, we say it is
small gap, large gap or zero gap
(writing SG, LG and ZG for short) if
0<|r|\leq L^{-\gamma+\eta}
,
|r|>L^{-\gamma+\eta}
, or
r=0
.

10.2.
Reduction to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
and
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})

By Definition
10.2
and Proposition
7.19
with the assumption
n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
in the statement of Proposition
3.11
, we know that the sum (
3.46
) can be divided into subset sums, where each subset sum is taken over one single congruence class for
\mathcal{G}
.

Let us fix a single subset sum for a certain congruence class. Note that we are summing
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
over
\mathcal{G}
, while each
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
is a sum of form (
9.25
) over decorations of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, which can be identified with decorations of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
via Definition
7.4
; moreover the decorations of congruent gardens are in one-to-one correspondence with each other and preserves the gap of each vine-like object. Thus, let
\mathscr{C}
be as in Lemma
10.1
, then for each vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}
, we can specify whether it has SG, LG or ZG under the decoration (each such specification defines
an extra restriction
on the decoration, which will be summarized in the factor
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
in the proof below). Then, we can further divide the given subset sum into at most
3^{n}
terms, such that each vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}
is specified to be either SG, LG or ZG in each term. Let
\mathscr{V}_{0}
be the collection of VC’s in
\mathscr{V}
that are SG (this collection is not affected by any LF twist at vines in
\mathscr{V}
). Note that
\mathcal{G}
runs over a congruence class, which is defined by LF twists at vines in
\mathscr{V}
; if we strengthen the equivalence relation by allowing only LF twists at vines in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
, then we can further divide the sum into at most
2^{n}
terms, each of which is a sum over a new equivalence class. As such, we only need to consider one of these new terms, which we shall refer to as
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{eqc}}
for below.

Let all the VC’s in the collection
\mathscr{V}_{0}
be
\mathbb{U}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{U}_{q_{\mathrm{sk}}}
. For each
\mathbb{U}_{j}
, denote the corresponding nodes
\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{22}
etc. (as in Proposition
7.7
) by
\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j},\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{j}
etc.; also denote
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}_{j}]\backslash\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}\}:=\mathcal{U}_{j}
. The term
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{eqc}}
is the sum of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
(with the extra SG, LG or ZG restrictions on the decoration defined above) over all gardens
\mathcal{G}
in a new equivalence class, which is defined by LF twists at all the vines in
\mathbb{U}_{j}
. Now for each
1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sk}}
, we consider the expression (
9.25
) of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
(with extra restrictions), and the part of it
involving the realization

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}_{j}]
, which is precisely defined as
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}_{j}}
in Section
9.2.1
. We make three observations, which are easily verified using relevant definitions and structure of the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
:

•

The expression
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}_{j}}
is not affected by LF twists at vines in
\mathbb{U}_{i}\,(i\neq j)
.

•

For any
(i,j)
, the variables

(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{i}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{i}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{i}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}^{i}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{i}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{i}})

which the function
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}[\mathbb{U}_{i}]
depends on, do not overlap with the variables
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
for
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{j}
, nor the variables
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
for branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{j}
.

•

Similarly, the extra SG, LG and ZG restrictions defined above can also be expressed using the variables that do not overlap with any
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
nor
t_{\mathfrak{n}}
for
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{j}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
be the result of splicing all vines in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
as in Proposition
7.7
; note that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is a garden and is not affected by any LF twist for
\mathcal{G}
(see Remark
7.16
(c)). It has a pre-layering inherited from
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
(i.e. when splicing each vine or VC as in Proposition
7.7
, all the nodes in the post-splicing garden, including
(\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22})
in Proposition
7.7
, are in the same layer as in the pre-splicing garden). By the above observations, and note that for each
j
we are also summing
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathbb{U}_{j}}
for
\mathcal{G}
being an LF twist at
\mathbb{U}_{j}
, we can rewrite

\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{eqc}}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\zeta(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})\sum_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sb}}}e^{\pi i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\\
\times\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sb}}}^{(+)}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{l}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{(\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{pr}}},k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}(t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{pr}}},t_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}})\prod_{j=1}^{q_{\mathrm{sk}}}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}_{j}}(t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{j}},t_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{j}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{11}^{j}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{j}},k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{j}}).

(10.1)

Here in (
10.1
), all the notions
n_{\mathrm{sb}}
,
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}
etc. are just like in (
9.25
) but for the garden
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, except that (i) the factor
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
contains the product of factors on the right hand side of (
3.22
)
only for

\mathfrak{n}\not\in\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}:1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sk}}\}
, and (ii) the extra factors
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}_{j}}
are the ones defined in (
9.26
) with
\mathbb{U}
replaced by
\mathbb{U}_{j}
, and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
is the indicator function of all the extra SG, LG and ZG restrictions defined above (this
\mathcal{Z}
depends only on
k[\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}]
). Note that the factor
e^{-\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}t_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}}\Gamma}
in (
9.26
) exactly cancels the factor
e^{\pi i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}
in (
10.1
) for
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}
.

We perform some further reductions to (
10.1
). First, by the decay properties of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}
in Propositions
5.1
and
6.1
, we may decompose (
10.1
) by restricting each
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
to a unit ball
|k_{\mathfrak{l}}-k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0}|\leq 1
; we only need to treat a single term of this form, as summability is provided by the above decay properties. By Lemma
A.4
, with a loss of at most
C_{0}^{n}
, we may also restrict
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
to a unit ball
|k_{\mathfrak{n}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}}^{0}|\leq 1
for each
node

\mathfrak{n}
.

Second, by the weighted Fourier
L^{1}
bounds for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}}^{*}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}}^{*}
(see Propositions
5.1
and
6.1
), and the same bounds for
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}^{\mathbb{U}_{j}}
with each vector variable
k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}}
etc. restricted to a unit ball (see Proposition
9.5
), we can decompose these functions using time Fourier transform, and reduce (
10.1
) to the following expression (with some fixed parameters
\xi
and
\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{K}}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{eqc}}

\displaystyle:=\zeta(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})\cdot e^{\pi i\xi(p+1)}\cdot\big(\max_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\langle\xi\rangle+\langle\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}\rangle)\big)^{-2\eta^{8}}\cdot(C_{1}\sqrt{\delta})^{n-n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot L^{-\eta^{8}g_{\mathrm{sk}}-\eta^{2}b_{\mathrm{sk}}+\eta^{7}q_{\mathrm{sk}}}\cdot\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}},

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}

\displaystyle:=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\sum_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}\mathcal{K}_{(\mathfrak{m})}(k_{\mathfrak{m}})\cdot\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}(k[\mathcal{W}])\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sb}}}e^{\pi i\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\cdot e^{\pi i\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}.

(10.2)

Here
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
is a function of
k_{\mathfrak{m}}
for each node
\mathfrak{m}
(branching node or leaf), which is supported in the unit ball
|k_{\mathfrak{m}}-k_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}|\leq 1
, and has all derivatives up to order
30d
bounded by
1
. The function
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
is the same as above, and
\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}
is a function of
k[\mathcal{W}]
where
\mathcal{W}=\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j},\mathfrak{u}_{11}^{j},\mathfrak{u}_{21}^{j},\mathfrak{u}_{22}^{j}:1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sk}}\}
. This function
\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}
is supported in the unit balls
|k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}}^{0}|\leq 1
etc., and is bounded by
1
(without derivatives). The quantity
g_{\mathrm{sk}}
represents the total incoherency index for all the regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and regular trees
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
for
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sk}}
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, plus the total incoherency index for all the vines in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
. The quantity
b_{\mathrm{sk}}
represents the number of
bad
vines in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
, and
q_{\mathrm{sk}}
is the number of VC’s in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
as above.

10.3.
Analysis of ladders:
L^{1}
bound and extra decay

For simplicity, we denote
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}):=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
below. To estimate
\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
in (
10.2
), we need to deal with the ladders in the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, and for this we need to interpolate between two arguments, which will be presented in Section
11
below. Both arguments involve the process of representing (
10.2
) from the point of view of the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, which we now describe.

Recall that we identify
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decorations
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
with
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decorations
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
via Definition
7.4
. For
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}(v)
the corresponding values of
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
-\Gamma_{v}
differ by a constant
\Gamma_{v}^{0}
which depends only on
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
, see (
7.2
) and (
7.4
). Also by definition, the time domain
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}
as in (
3.25
) can be written as the set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
which contains the tuples
(t_{v})
for atoms
v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
satisfying that (i)
t_{v}\in[\mathfrak{L}_{v},\mathfrak{L}_{v}+1]
where
\mathfrak{L}_{v}
is the layer of
v
as in Definition
7.2
, and (ii)
t_{v_{1}}>t_{v_{2}}
if there is a PC bond between
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
with
v_{2}
labeled C (i.e.
\mathfrak{n}(v_{1})
is the parent of
\mathfrak{n}(v_{2})
, see Definition
7.2
).

Next, The parameters
\xi_{\mathfrak{n}}
are renamed as
\xi_{v}
, the functions
\mathcal{K}_{(\mathfrak{m})}(k_{\mathfrak{m}})
can be written as
\mathcal{K}_{(\ell)}(k_{\ell})
for all bonds
\ell
which satisfy the same assumptions (such as support in
|k_{\ell}-k_{\ell}^{0}|\leq 1
with fixed values of
k_{\ell}^{0}
etc.), and the function
\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}
can be written as a uniformly bounded function of the variables
k[\mathbb{W}]
(which we still denote by
\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}
), where
\mathbb{W}
is the set of bonds
\ell\leftrightarrow v
for all atoms
v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
such that
\mathfrak{n}(v)\in\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}:1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sk}}\}
. We may also define
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
to be equal to
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
in (
10.2
