); in the molecule setting it can be written as a product of
\epsilon_{v}
for all atoms
v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
such that
\mathfrak{n}(v)\not\in\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}:1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sk}}\}
, where each
\epsilon_{v}
is a suitable function of
(k_{\ell}:\ell\leftrightarrow v)
valued in
\{-1,0,1\}
. Similarly we define
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
to be equal to
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
in (
10.2
), which can be written as a product of
\mathcal{Z}_{v}
for some of the atoms
v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
. Here each
\mathcal{Z}_{v}
is a suitable function of
(k_{\ell_{1}},k_{\ell_{2}})
for some opposite-direction bonds
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\leftrightarrow v
, which has value in
\{0,1\}
depending on the SG, LG and ZG properties of
v
.

With all the above interpretations, we can rewrite (
10.2
) as

\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}:=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\sum_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\prod_{\ell}\mathcal{K}_{(\ell)}(k_{\ell})\cdot\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}(k[\mathbb{W}])\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\prod_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}}e^{\pi i\delta L^{2\gamma}(-\Gamma_{v}+\Gamma_{v}^{0})t_{v}}e^{\pi i\xi_{v}t_{v}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{v}.

(10.3)

Our first argument depends on an estimate which is in the same spirit as Proposition
9.5
(but technically much easier as it does not involve Fourier
L^{1}
norms).

Proposition 10.4
.

Let the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
be as in Section
10.2
. Let
\mathbb{L}
be a ladder in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, which has length
m
and incoherency index
g
, such that
\mathfrak{n}(v)\not\in\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}:1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sk}}\}
for any atom
v\in\mathbb{L}
. Let
v_{j}
be the (at most
4
) atoms that are not in
\mathbb{L}
but connected to atoms in
\mathbb{L}
by a bond, and let
\ell_{j}
be the (at most
4
) bonds that connect atoms in
\mathbb{L}
to atoms not in
\mathbb{L}
. Define the expression

\mathscr{K}^{\mathbb{L}}=\mathscr{K}^{\mathbb{L}}(t_{v_{1}},t_{v_{2}},t_{v_{3}},t_{v_{4}},k_{\ell_{1}},k_{\ell_{2}},k_{\ell_{3}},k_{\ell_{4}})

similar to (
10.3
), but with the following changes:

•

We replace the power
(\delta/(2L^{d-\gamma}))^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}
by
(\delta/(2L^{d-\gamma}))^{2m+1}
and remove the
\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}
factor.

•

In the summation
\sum_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\cdots)
, we only sum over the variables
k_{\ell}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{L}
(i.e.
\ell
connecting two atoms in
\mathbb{L}
), and treat the other
k_{\ell}
variables as fixed. We also replace
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
by the product of
\epsilon_{v}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{v}
(defined above) over all
v\in\mathbb{L}
.

•

In the integration
\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\cdots)
, we only integrate over the variables
t_{v}
for
v\in\mathbb{L}
, and treat the other
t_{v}
variables as fixed.

•

In the product
\prod_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\cdots)
, we only include those factors where
v\in\mathbb{L}
; in the product
\prod_{\ell}
, we only include those factors where
\ell\in\mathbb{L}
.

It is easy to check that the function
\mathscr{K}^{\mathbb{L}}
defined this way indeed only depends on the time variables
t_{v_{1}},\cdots,t_{v_{4}}
and vector variables
k_{\ell_{1}},\cdots,k_{\ell_{4}}
. Then, uniformly in all
t_{v_{j}}
and
k_{\ell_{j}}
variables, we have

|\mathscr{K}^{\mathbb{L}}|\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\delta)^{m}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})g+20d}.

(10.4)

Proof.

We shall only sketch the proof here, as the argument is a combination of the simpler parts of the proofs of Propositions
9.4
and
9.5
. First, note that the gap associated with the ladder is fixed by the difference
r:=k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}
. Next, we argue as in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
9.5
to re-parametrize the sum over the decoration as we did for the ladder component of Vine (II), see Figure
18
(A). This leads to the new set of variables
(x_{j},y_{j})_{1\leq j\leq m}
, where each
(x_{j},y_{j})
corresponds to a double bond in the ladder connecting two atoms
v
and
w
, such that
\Gamma_{v}=2x_{j}\cdot y_{j}
and
\Gamma_{w}=-2x_{j}\cdot y_{j}+2r\cdot\zeta_{j}
. Here
\zeta_{j}=\alpha_{j}x_{j}+\beta_{j}y_{j}+\theta_{j}r
for some
\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\theta_{j}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
with
\alpha_{j}^{2}+\beta_{j}^{2}\neq 0
.

Recall that in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
9.4
we estimated each quantity
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{i}
pointwise, leading to the final
L^{\infty}
bound of
\mathcal{I}
. Here we can do exactly the same thing, and estimate the sum in
(x_{j},y_{j})
by either
\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}
(if the corresponding atoms
v
and
w
are in the same layer) or
\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\eta})}
(if
v
and
w
are in different layers). In the end we are left with the last remaining sum, which is taken over the decoration of the last double bond in the ladder, and can be bounded trivially by
L^{2d}
. Putting together, this then implies (
10.4
).
∎

Our second argument depends on the following
L^{1}
estimate for time integral, which is basically the same as in
[
19
,
22
]
but with some differences due to layerings.

Proposition 10.5
.

Let the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
be as in Section
10.2
. Consider all the maximal ladders in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, which we denote by
\mathbb{L}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sb}})
with length
z_{j}
. Let
\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}:=q_{\mathrm{sb}}+m_{\mathrm{sb}}
, where
m_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the number of atoms not in any of these maximal ladders. Note the decoration
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
in
\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
in (
10.3
) satisfies
|k_{\ell}-k_{\ell}^{0}|\leq 1
and
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\neq 0
; we fix a dyadic number
P_{j}\in[L^{-1},1]\cup\{0\}
for each
1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sb}}
, and further restrict
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
such that the gap
r_{j}
of the ladder
\mathbb{L}_{j}
satisfies
|r_{j}|\sim_{0}P_{j}
(or
|r_{j}|\gtrsim_{0}1
if
P_{j}=1
).

Consider the integral
\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\cdots)
in (
10.3
), which is a function of
\alpha_{v}:=\delta L^{2\gamma}(-\Gamma_{v}+\Gamma_{v}^{0})+\xi_{v}
where
v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, and denote it by
\mathbb{B}(\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}])
. Let
\lfloor\alpha_{v}\rfloor:=\sigma_{v}
, and we restrict
\Gamma_{v}
(and hence
\alpha_{v}
and
\sigma_{v}
) to those that
may occur
for
some
decoration
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
satisfying all the above restrictions, including the further restrictions on gaps for
\mathbb{L}_{j}
. Then, under these assumptions, we have

\sum_{\sigma[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]}\sup_{\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]:|\alpha_{v}-\sigma_{v}|\leq 1}|\mathcal{B}(\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}])|\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\delta^{-1/2-5\nu})^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot L^{\delta^{5\nu}\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}}\prod_{j=1}^{q_{\mathrm{sb}}}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{z_{j}},

(10.5)

where
\mathfrak{X}_{j}:=\min((\log L)^{2},1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P_{j})
.

Proof.

The proof is divided into several steps.

Step 1: Reduction and localization.
First recall the following elementary inequality (see also (10.5) of
[
19
]
): for any unit box
[0,1]^{n}
, any function
F:[0,1]^{\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\to\mathbb{R}
and any
\mathfrak{L}_{v}\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\sup_{\alpha\in[0,1]^{\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}}}|F(\alpha)|\leq\sum_{A\subset\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\int_{[0,1]^{\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}}}\bigg|\prod_{v\in A}(\partial_{\alpha_{v}}-\pi i\mathfrak{L}_{v})F(\alpha)\bigg|\,\mathrm{d}\alpha

(10.6)

For the proof, simply get rid of
\mathfrak{L}_{v}
by multiplying by the
e^{-\pi i\mathfrak{L}_{v}\alpha_{v}}
factor, and then proceed by induction in
n
.

Using (
10.6
), with an acceptable loss of
C_{0}^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}
, we can reduce the left hand side of (
10.5
) to the integral of
|\mathcal{B}(\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}])|
on the union of the unit boxes
\lfloor\alpha_{v}\rfloor=\sigma_{v}
for all possible
(\sigma_{v})
. The derivatives
\partial_{\alpha_{v}}-\pi i\mathfrak{L}_{v}
turn into factors
\pi i(t_{v}-\mathfrak{L}_{v})
in the
\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\cdots)
integral in (
10.3
), which are harmless due to the restriction
t_{v}\in[\mathfrak{L}_{v},\mathfrak{L}_{v}+1]
, so we will ignore them below. Denote by
\mathcal{W}
the above union of unit boxes, next we will specify some localization properties of
\mathcal{W}
.

Suppose that
\alpha=(\alpha_{v})
is defined from some decoration
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
satisfying all the restrictions stated in the assumptions, and fix one ladder
\mathbb{L}_{j}
satisfying
\mathfrak{X}_{j}=1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P_{j}\leq(\log L)^{2}
, and any atoms
v
and
w
connected by a double bond, we have
\Gamma_{v}+\Gamma_{w}=2r_{j}\cdot y_{w}
where
r_{j}
is the gap of
\mathbb{L}_{j}
and
y_{w}
is the difference of the two vectors
k_{\ell}
for two different single bonds at
v
and
w
. Using that
|k_{\ell}-k_{\ell}^{0}|\leq 1
, we see that
\Gamma_{v}+\Gamma_{w}=2r_{j}\cdot z_{w}^{0}+\mathcal{O}_{0}(P_{j})
where
z_{w}^{0}
is a fixed vector (note however that
r_{j}
is not a fixed vector), and thus
\alpha_{v}+\alpha_{w}=r_{j}\cdot y_{w}^{0}+c_{w}+\mathcal{O}_{0}(\mathfrak{X}_{j})
for some fixed vector
y_{w}^{0}
and constant
c_{w}
that are independent of the decoration. The integer part of this
\mathcal{O}_{0}(\mathfrak{X}_{j})
has at most
\mathcal{O}_{0}(\mathfrak{X}_{j})
choices, so we may fix one choice for each
j
and each
(v,w)
, at an additional cost of

C_{0}^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\prod_{j:\,\mathfrak{X}_{j}=1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P_{j}}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{z_{j}+1};

(10.7)

this can be viewed as dividing
\mathcal{W}
into subsets, where the total number of subsets is given by (
10.7
). Once each integer part is fixed, we can then absorb it into
c_{w}
and write
\alpha_{v}+\alpha_{w}=r_{j}\cdot y_{w}^{0}+c_{w}+\mathcal{O}_{0}(1)
. Note also that the
z_{j}+1
in (
10.7
) may be replaced by
z_{j}
, as the loss caused by this is at most
(\log L)^{2q_{\mathrm{sb}}}
which is acceptable.

Now, by Lemma
A.8
, for each
j
we can find
q=q_{j}\leq d
and atoms
w_{1}^{j},\cdots,w_{q}^{j}\in\mathbb{L}_{j}
, such that for any
w\in\mathbb{L}_{j}
we can write

y_{w}^{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\lambda_{i}y_{w_{i}^{j}}^{0},\quad|\lambda_{i}|\leq C_{0}.

This implies that for each pair of atoms
(v,w)
in
\mathbb{L}_{j}
as above, we have

\alpha_{v}+\alpha_{w}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\lambda_{i}\big(\alpha_{v_{i}^{j}}+\alpha_{w_{i}^{j}}\big)+d_{w}+\mathcal{O}_{0}(1),

(10.8)

where
d_{w}
is another constant that is independent of the decoration. Clearly (
10.8
) does not change if each
\alpha_{v}
is shifted by
\mathcal{O}_{0}(1)
, so it is satisfied by any point in
\mathcal{W}
. In addition, the difference of any two vectors in the decoration
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is a vector that belongs to
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and a fixed unit ball, so for each
v
the quantity
\Gamma_{v}
may take at most
L^{20d}
values for all possible decorations. This means that if
\alpha=(\alpha_{v})\in\mathcal{W}
, then for each
v
we must have
\alpha_{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{v}
where
\mathcal{S}_{v}
is the union of at most
L^{20d}
unit intervals that depends only on
v
.

Step 2: Monotonicity.
Fix a ladder
\mathbb{L}_{j}
with
\mathfrak{X}_{j}=1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P_{j}
, and let
w_{i}^{j}\,(1\leq i\leq q=q_{j})
be as above. Consider the sequence
\{\max(t_{v},t_{w})\}
where
(v,w)
runs over all pairs of atoms in
\mathbb{L}_{j}
connected by a double bond. We claim that this sequence can be divided into two subsequences, each of which is
monotnonic
when viewed from left to right as in Figure
15
.

In fact, fix any pair
(v,w)
, and assume (say)
t_{v}>t_{w}
. Note that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
; define
v^{+}
to be the unique atom such that
\mathfrak{n}(v^{+})
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{n}(v)
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
. Then
v^{+}\neq w
and is adjacent to
v
, so it must be the atom to the left or right of
v
in Figure
15
. Let
w^{+}
be the atom connected to
v^{+}
by a double bond, then
\max(t_{v^{+}},t_{w^{+}})\geq t_{v^{+}}>t_{v}=\max(t_{v},t_{w})
. This means that the sequence
\{\max(t_{v},t_{w})\}
has
no local maximum
, thus it is formed by at most two monotonic subsequences when viewed from let to right as in Figure
15
.

Now, for each pair
(v,w)
in
\mathbb{L}_{j}
such that
w\not\in\{w_{i}^{j}:1\leq i\leq q=q_{j}\}
, we may specify whether
t_{v}>t_{w}
or
t_{v}<t_{w}
in addition to the existing inequality relations between
(t_{v})
forced by parent-child relations. With an acceptable loss of
C_{0}^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}
, we may fix such a set of inequalities; then for each such
(v,w)
, we choose the atom corresponding to the bigger time variable. Define the collection of these atoms by
\mathbb{X}
, and let
\mathbb{Y}=\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}\backslash\mathbb{X}
. Note that
\mathbb{Y}
consists of (i) the atoms in
(v,w)
corresponding to the smaller time variable, (ii) the atoms
w_{i}^{j}
and corresponding
v_{i}^{j}
for
1\leq i\leq q
, (iii) all atoms in ladders
\mathbb{L}_{j}
where
\mathfrak{X}_{j}=(\log L)^{2}
, and (iv) all atoms that are not in any ladder
\mathbb{L}_{j}
.

Step 3: Partial time integration.
Now we analyze the target expression, which is

\int_{\mathcal{W}}\bigg|\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\prod_{v\in\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{Y}}e^{\pi i\alpha_{v}t_{v}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{v}\bigg|\,\mathrm{d}\alpha[\mathbb{X}]\mathrm{d}\alpha[\mathbb{Y}]

(10.9)

thanks to the reduction in Step 1. Here
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the set
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
but with the extra inequalities in Step 2 added. In this integral, we will first integrate in the
t[\mathbb{Y}]
variables; clearly we may only integrate the product for
v\in\mathbb{Y}
, and the result may depend on
\alpha[\mathbb{Y}]
and
t[\mathbb{X}]
, so we denote it by

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(t[\mathbb{X}],\alpha[\mathbb{Y}])=\int_{(t[\mathbb{X}],t[\mathbb{Y}])\in\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\prod_{v\in\mathbb{Y}}e^{\pi i\alpha_{v}t_{v}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{v}.

(10.10)

Note that
\alpha_{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{v}
for any
\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]\in\mathcal{W}
as shown above, moreover we have

\int_{\alpha_{v}\in\mathcal{S}_{v}\,\!(\forall v\in\mathbb{Y})}|\mathcal{H}(t[\mathbb{X}],\alpha[\mathbb{Y}])|\,\mathrm{d}\alpha[\mathbb{Y}]\leq(C_{0}\log L)^{|\mathbb{Y}|}

(10.11)

uniformly in
t[\mathbb{X}]
. This can be proved by integrating in the
t[\mathbb{Y}]
variables in a certain order in (
10.10
) and proceeding by induction, as in Lemma 10.2 of
[
19
]
or Proposition 2.3 of
[
18
]
. The only caution is that each time we should integrate in the
t_{v}
variable where
v
is a
minimal element
under the ancestor-descendant partial ordering (i.e.
v_{1}\succeq v_{2}
if and only if
\mathfrak{n}(v_{1})
is an ancestor of
\mathfrak{n}(v_{2})
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
). Note that the lower bound of this integration in
t_{v}
is always a constant, and the upper bound is either a constant or another variable
t_{v^{+}}
where
\mathfrak{n}(v^{+})
is the parent of
\mathfrak{n}(v)
. After integrating in
t_{v}
, we obtain a factor that is bounded by
\langle\alpha_{v}+\beta\rangle^{-1}
, where
\beta
is either
0
or some quantity determined by the
\alpha_{v_{*}}
variables where
t_{v_{*}}
has been integrated out in previous steps (this bound is true even when
|\alpha_{v}+\beta|\leq 1
because the interval of integration in
t_{v}
has length at most
1
). This factor can be bounded upon integrating in
\alpha_{v}
, using the elementary inequality

\sup_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{v}}\frac{1}{\langle\alpha_{v}+\beta\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{v}\leq C_{0}\log L.

We are then left with an expression that is the sum of at most four integrals with similar forms (possibly depending on the size of
t_{v^{+}}
) but one fewer integrated variable; next we simply integrate in
t_{v^{\prime}}
where
v^{\prime}
is a minimal element in the same partial ordering after removing
v
, and so on.

Step 4: Completing the proof.
With (
10.11
) we can now complete the proof of (
10.5
). First we perform the reduction in Step 1 and reduce to estimating (
10.9
) with a loss of
\prod_{j=1}^{q_{\mathrm{sb}}}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{z_{j}}
. Then we apply (
10.11
) and also apply the fact from Step 2 that, all the variables
t[\mathbb{X}\cap\mathbb{L}_{j}]
belong to
[0,\mathfrak{D}]
and can be arranged into
at most two monotonic sequences
. Note also that, by definition of
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
, once
\alpha[\mathbb{Y}]
is fixed, each
\alpha_{v}\,(v\in\mathbb{X})
must belong to an interval of length
\mathcal{O}_{0}(1)
thanks to (
10.8
). Putting together, this leads to the bound

(\ref{targettimeint})\leq\int_{(\mathrm{monotonicity})}\,\mathrm{d}t[\mathbb{X}]\int_{\mathcal{W}}|\mathcal{H}(t[\mathbb{X}],\alpha[\mathbb{Y}])|\,\mathrm{d}\alpha[\mathbb{X}]\mathrm{d}\alpha[\mathbb{Y}]\leq C_{0}^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\log L)^{|\mathbb{Y}|}\prod_{j:\,\mathfrak{X}_{j}=1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P_{j}}\frac{\mathfrak{D}^{z_{j}}}{(z_{j}-d)!},

where the integral in
t[\mathbb{X}]
is taken under the above monotonicity conditions. Note that

(\log L)^{|\mathbb{Y}|}\leq\prod_{j:\,\mathfrak{X}_{j}=(\log L)^{2}}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{z_{j}}\cdot\prod_{j:\,\mathfrak{X}_{j}=1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P_{j}}(\log L)^{z_{j}}\cdot(\log L)^{10d\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}}

due to the definition of
\mathbb{Y}
; note also that in view of the
(\delta^{-1/2-5\nu)^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}}
factor in (
10.5
), we have

\delta^{(1/2+5\nu)n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\leq\prod_{j:\,\mathfrak{X}_{j}=1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P_{j}}\delta^{(1+10\nu)z_{j}}.

Therefore, the desired bound (
10.5
) follows from the elementary inequality

\frac{(\log L)^{z_{j}}\mathfrak{D}^{z_{j}}\delta^{(1+10\nu)z_{j}}}{(z_{j}-d)!}\leq(\log L)^{2d}\exp\big(\mathfrak{D}\delta^{1+10\nu}\log L\big)\leq L^{\delta^{8\nu}}

for each
j
, where we recall that
\mathfrak{D}\cdot\delta=\tau_{*}\ll\delta^{-\nu}
from (
3.18
).
∎

10.4.
Properties of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}

We state some properties of the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
, which are essentially the same as in
[
22
]
.

Proposition 10.6
.

Consider the garden
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
and the corresponding molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
. Consider also a
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration
\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
such that
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\neq 0
, which we identify with a
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
satisfying
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\neq 0
via Definition
7.4
(this can be viewed as inherited from certain decoration of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
or
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
). Then we have the followings:

(1)

The molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is connected, has
n_{\mathrm{sb}}
atoms and
2n_{\mathrm{sb}}-R
bonds.

(2)

\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
contains a collection
\mathscr{C}_{1}
of disjoint SG vine-like objects, such that each of them is either an HV, or a (CN) vine or a root (CL) vine. We can also specify a total of
q_{\mathrm{sk}}
atoms which we call
hinge atoms
; any triple bond must have an endpoint that is a hinge atom.

(3)

We say an atom
v
is
degenerate
if there are two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
at
v
of opposite directions and
k_{\ell_{1}}=k_{\ell_{2}}
(note this is equivalent to being ZG as in Definition
10.3
), and that it is
tame
if the values of
k_{\ell}
for all
\ell\leftrightarrow v
are equal, and equals a fixed value in
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
if
d(v)<4
. Then any degenerate/ZG atom that is not a hinge atom must be tame.

(4)

Any SG vine-like object which is not a subset of an SG vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, and also
does not contain
a hinge atom, must be a subset of a DV or an LG or ZG vine-like object
13
13

13

Note that a ZG vine-like object means that the joints are degenerate, hence tame by (3).
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, which is disjoint with the vine-like objects in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
and does not contain any hinge atom. Note that here we must be in one of the cases defined in Lemma
A.5
.

(5)

For any hinge atom
v
, we can find two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
at
v
, such that the triple
(v,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
always has SG. If
v
is also a joint of an SG vine (or HV) in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, then
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is chosen to belong to this vine (or the adjoint vine of this HV).

(6)

Any SG vine-like object
\mathbb{U}
which is not a subset of an SG vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, and also
contains
a hinge atom,
must
contain a hinge atom
v
, such that either
v
is an interior atom of
\mathbb{U}
, or
v
is a joint of
\mathbb{U}
and exactly one bond in
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
belongs to
\mathbb{U}
, where
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is defined in (5) above.

Proof.

Recall the reduction process from
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
by merging all VC’s in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
. Now each VC in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
is reduced to an atom, and we define them to be the hinge atoms (in fact they are just those atoms
v
where
\mathfrak{n}(v)\in\{\mathfrak{u}_{1}^{j}:1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sk}}\}
). Moreover, by Corollary
7.10
, we know that each SGVC, SGHV and SGHVC in
\mathscr{C}
is reduced to either a single atom, or an SGHV, or an SG vine that is also a (CN) vine, or an SG vine that is also a root (CL) vine; we then define
\mathscr{C}_{1}
to be the collection of all such vine-like objects (other than single atoms). For each hinge atom
v
, it is merged from an SGVC in
\mathscr{C}
(say
\mathbb{U}
), then we choose
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
in (5) such that in the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
they are the two bonds at one joint of
\mathbb{U}
that do not belong to
\mathbb{U}
(this must exist because
\mathbb{U}
is not a root VC). Note that if
d(v)=4
then the other two bonds
(\ell_{3},\ell_{4})
also have the same properties. If
v
is also a joint of an SG vine (or HV) in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, then we specify
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
to belong to this vine (or the joint vine of this HV); if not, we can choose freely between
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
and
(\ell_{3},\ell_{4})
.

The above definitions are the same as in Proposition 9.1 of
[
22
]
(with the trivial difference that the DV’s are not included in the collection
\mathscr{C}_{1}
defined in (2), so the case of an SG vine-like object that is a subset of a DV in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is now included in (4)). This means that conclusions (2)–(6) also follow directly from the proof of Proposition 9.1 of
[
22
]
. Note that here we are dealing with gardens, but the proof in
[
22
]
does not distinguish between gardens and couples. As for conclusion (1), it simply follows from Proposition
7.3
and the fact that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
(just as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
) is an irreducible garden.
∎

11.
Stage 2 reduction and proof of Proposition
3.11

11.1.
Reduction to counting estimates

To prove Proposition
3.11
, we need to consider the expression
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
defined in (
3.46
), and the difference term
\mathscr{R}_{p+1,n}
defined in (
3.48
). These involve four types of terms:

(1)

The term

\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\zeta_{1},\cdots,\zeta_{2R},k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})

(11.1)

as in (
3.46
) for
R\geq 2
;

(2)

The term

\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr,nr}}(+,-,k,k)

(11.2)

as in (
3.46
) for
R=1
, where we only sum over
non-regular
couples;

(3)

The term

\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr,nc}}(+,-,k,k)

(11.3)

as in (
3.46
) for
R=1
, where we only sum over
regular, non-coherent
layered couples;

(4)

The term

\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr,co}}(+,-,k,k)-\mathcal{U}_{n/2}(p+1,k)

(11.4)

as in (
3.46
) for
R=1
, where we only sum over the
regular, coherent
layered couples, and
n
is even,
\mathcal{U}_{n/2}
is as in (
3.48
).

The terms (
11.3
) and (
11.4
) will be estimated using Propositions
4.9
,
5.1
,
6.1
and
6.2
. The terms (
11.1
) and (
11.2
) will be estimated in this section. In both cases, note that the estimates (
3.47
) and (
3.49
) involves the weights
(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle)^{4\Lambda_{p+1}}
for both gardens and couples, and the derivatives
\partial_{k}^{\rho}\,(|\rho|\leq 4\Lambda_{p+1})
for couples, which we now discuss.

First, for each
1\leq j\leq 2R
we have

\langle k_{j}\rangle^{4\Lambda_{p+1}}\leq(3n_{j})^{4\Lambda_{p+1}}\cdot\langle k_{\mathfrak{l}_{j}}\rangle^{4\Lambda_{p+1}}

for some leaf
\mathfrak{l}_{j}\in\mathcal{T}_{j}
, where
n_{j}
is the order of the tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
, since
k_{j}
is an algebraic sum of all such
k_{\mathfrak{l}_{j}}
. Since
4\Lambda_{p+1}\leq C_{2}
, we have
(3n_{j})^{4\Lambda_{p+1}}\leq 2^{n_{j}}\cdot C_{2}
, and putting together all
1\leq j\leq 2R
yields the factors
2^{n}
and
C_{2}^{R}
. Moreover, if we include the extra factor
\langle k_{\mathfrak{l}_{j}}\rangle^{4\Lambda_{p+1}}
to the input function
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}_{j}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}_{j}})
in (
3.24
), then this function is still bounded in
\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}
because of
F_{q}(k)=\varphi(\delta q,k)+\mathscr{R}_{q}(k)
, the bound (
3.29
) and
\Lambda_{p}=40\Lambda_{p+1}
, but with norm being
C_{2}
instead of
C_{1}
for the
\varphi(\delta q,k)
term. However this happens for at most
R
input factors, so again the resulting loss is at most
C_{2}^{R}
. These losses can be covered by proving slightly stronger bounds with
L^{-(R-1)/4}
replaced by
L^{-(R-1)(1/4+1/100)}
in (
3.47
), and
L^{-(4\theta_{p+1}+\theta_{p})/5}
replaced by
L^{-(3\theta_{p+1}+2\theta_{p})/5}
in (
3.49
), and
\delta^{2\nu n}
replaced by
\delta^{3\nu n}
in both, but these stronger bounds will follow from same arguments below.

Similarly, note that for couples (
R=1
), the function
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
is defined for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
if we extend the input functions
F_{q}(k)=\varphi(\delta q,k)+\mathscr{R}_{q}(k)
for
q\leq p
by using (
3.29
). If we translate
k
and all
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
by the same vector the whole expression is invariant, so taking any
\partial_{k}
derivative is equivalent to taking exactly one
\partial_{k_{\mathfrak{l}}}
derivative for some leaf
\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{G}
. If we take at most
4\Lambda_{p+1}\leq C_{2}
derivatives, then we get at most
n^{C_{2}}
terms, where at most
C_{2}
input factors are affected for each term, and each affected factor is still bounded in
\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}
but by
C_{2}
instead of
C_{1}
, for the same reason as above. This again leads to loss of at most
C_{2}n^{C_{2}}\leq C_{2}2^{n}
, which is acceptable provided we prove the slightly stronger bounds above.

Therefore, from now on we will focus on the proof of (
3.47
) and (
3.49
) with
4\Lambda_{p+1}
replaced by
0
, i.e. the supremum norm in
k
(or
k_{j}
). In the proof below we will fix the values of
k
(or
k_{j}
), and all our estimates will be uniform in them; the input factors
F_{q}(k)=\varphi(\delta q,k)+\mathscr{R}_{q}(k)
are then bounded in
\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}
by
C_{1}
.

With the above reductions, we now start with the term
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
in (
11.1
) or
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{tr,nr}}
in (
11.2
), which is a sum of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{G}}
as in (
3.46
). We then perform the stage 1 reduction in Section
10
and consider the sum over a single congruence class as defined in Definition
10.2
. Note that we still need to count the number of congruence classes, which will be left to Section
11.3
below; for now we will fix this single sum and reduce it to (
10.3
) as done in Section
10
.

11.1.1.
Ladders and
L^{1}
bounds

Now start from the expression
\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
in (
10.3
). Recall the various parameters defined in Proposition
10.5
:
n_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the order of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}
,
q_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the number of maximal ladders in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
,
z_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sb}})
are the lengths of these ladders,
m_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the number of atoms not in these ladders, and
\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}=q_{\mathrm{sb}}+m_{\mathrm{sb}}
. Also recall the parameters coming from the stage 1 reduction process in Sections
10.1
–
10.2
:
g_{\mathrm{sk}}
is the total incoherency index for all the regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and regular trees
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
for
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sk}}
and
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, plus the total incoherency index for all the vines in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
,
b_{\mathrm{sk}}
represents the number of bad vines in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
, and
q_{\mathrm{sk}}
is the number of VC’s in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
. Note that
q_{\mathrm{sk}}
also equals the number of hinge atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
(Proposition
10.6
(2)). In addition, we also define
g_{\mathrm{sb}}
to be the total incoherency index for all the maximal ladders in Proposition
10.5
.

The goal until the end of Section
11.3
is to prove the following two estimates:

|\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}|\lesssim_{2}(C_{1}\sqrt{\delta})^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}L^{-\eta\cdot g_{\mathrm{sb}}+80d\cdot(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+q_{\mathrm{sk}})},

(11.5)

|\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}|\lesssim_{1}(C_{1}\delta^{-10\nu})^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}L^{-\eta^{6}\cdot(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+q_{\mathrm{sk}})}\cdot L^{-(R-1)(1/4+1/50)},

(11.6)

which we then interpolate to prove Proposition
3.11
. The proof of (
11.5
) is very easy with Proposition
10.4
, which we describe below.

Starting with (
10.3
), we choose all the maximal ladders in Proposition
10.5
; if any of them contains hinge atoms, then we replace it by the shorter ladders separated by these hinge atoms. This leads to a new set of
q^{\prime}\leq q_{\mathrm{sb}}+q_{\mathrm{sk}}
maximal ladders not containing hinge atom, such that the total incoherency index equals
g^{\prime}\geq g_{\mathrm{sb}}-2q_{\mathrm{sk}}
. For each new ladder
\mathbb{L}
, we apply Proposition
10.4
, noting that the function
\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}(k[\mathbb{W}])
does not depend on any of the
k_{\ell}
variables for
\ell\in\mathbb{L}
; the resulting functions
\mathscr{K}^{\mathbb{L}}
is then estimated using (
10.4
). The rest of (
10.3
), namely summation and integration over the
k_{\ell}
and
t_{v}
variables for
\ell
and
v
not in any ladder, is estimated trivially, using the fact that each
k_{\ell}
belongs to a fixed unit ball and that each
t_{v}
belongs to a fixed unit interval. Note that the number of atoms not in these new ladders is
m^{\prime}\leq m_{\mathrm{sb}}+2q_{\mathrm{sk}}
, so we obtain that

|\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}|\leq(C_{1}\sqrt{\delta})^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}-m^{\prime}}\delta^{m^{\prime}}L^{-(\gamma_{1}-\sqrt{\delta})g^{\prime}+20dq^{\prime}}\cdot L^{20dm^{\prime}},

(11.7)

which easily implies (
11.5
) due to the upper bounds for
(q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
and lower bound for
g^{\prime}
.

From now on we will focus on the proof of (
11.6
). Starting with (
10.3
), which we may rewrite as

\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\sum_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\prod_{\ell}\mathcal{K}_{(\ell)}(k_{\ell})\cdot\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}(k[\mathbb{W}])\cdot\mathcal{B}(\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}])

(11.8)

using the definition in Proposition
10.5
, with the notions
\alpha_{v}:=\delta L^{2\gamma}(-\Gamma_{v}+\Gamma_{v}^{0})+\xi_{v}
etc. Then we may fix the values of
P_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q_{\mathrm{sb}})
as in Proposition
10.5
; this loses a factor
(\log L)^{q_{\mathrm{sb}}}
, which can be covered in view of the gain in (
11.6
) (say if we prove the stronger bound with
\eta^{6}
replaced by
2\eta^{6}
, which follows from same arguments).

Next, we shall fix the values of
\sigma_{v}=\lfloor\alpha_{v}\rfloor
, and bound (
11.8
) by

|\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}|\leq\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\bigg)^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\sum_{\sigma[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]}\sup_{\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]:|\alpha_{v}-\sigma_{v}|\leq 1}|\mathcal{B}(\alpha[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}])|\\
\times\sup_{\sigma[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]}\sum_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}:|\alpha_{v}-\sigma_{v}|\leq 1}\big|\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\prod_{\ell}\mathcal{K}_{(\ell)}(k_{\ell})\cdot\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}(k[\mathbb{W}])\big|.

(11.9)

Note that the
\sum_{\sigma[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]}(\cdots)
factor in (
11.9
) is bounded by (
10.5
), so to prove (
11.6
) we only need to bound the
\sup_{\sigma[\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}]}(\cdots)
factor uniformly in
(\sigma_{v})
. This corresponds to solving a counting problem for all the
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decorations
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
of the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
. Moreover, the decoration
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
must satisfy the following requirements:

(1)

It is restricted by some fixed
(k_{\ell}^{0})
and
(\beta_{v})
in the sense of Definition
7.4
, where
(k_{\ell}^{0})
and
(\beta_{v})
are fixed parameters (the
(\beta_{v})
is determined by
(\sigma_{v},\Gamma_{v}^{0},\xi_{v})
etc.).

(2)

It is in the support of
\widetilde{\epsilon}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
, in particular it must satisfy all the requirements in Proposition
10.6
.

(3)

The gap
r_{j}
or the ladder
\mathbb{L}_{j}
is such that
|r_{j}|\sim_{0}P_{j}
.

For later use, we shall impose some further requirements on this decoration
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, which we describe below. Consider all the SG vine-like objects in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
that are subsets of DV or LG or ZG vine-like objects (Proposition
10.6
(4), note that here we must be in one of the cases defined in Lemma
A.5
), and select the ones that are maximal (i.e. those that are not subsets of larger SG vine-like objects with same properties). Here for any DV, if both vines (V) forming this DV are SG, then we select only one of them, plus all the maximal SG vine-like proper subsets of the other. Define by
\mathscr{C}_{2}
the collection of SG vine-like objects formed in this way. Then:

(4)

We fix each atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
as either SG, LG or ZG; here we say an atom
v
is SG if there is at least one choice of
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
such that
(v,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is SG. Note that an atom may be SG in more than one way, in which case we will further specify it below.

(5)

For each SG atom
v
, we specify the two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
at
v
such that
(v,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is SG. Here we require that (i) for any hinge atom
v
we must specify
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
as in Proposition
10.6
(5), and (ii) for any joint
v
of any SG vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
or
\mathscr{C}_{2}
, we must specify
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
to belong to this vine-like object
14
14

14

This is consistent with (i) due to Proposition
10.6
(5). Also, if this vine-like object is an HV or HVC, then
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is chosen to belong to the corresponding adjoint VC.
; for any other atom
v
, we can specify
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
arbitrarily.

(6)

Finally, for each SG atom
v
which is not an interior atom of an SG vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
or
\mathscr{C}_{2}
, we fix its gap as
|r|\sim_{0}Q_{v}
, where
Q_{v}\in[L^{-1},L^{-\gamma+\eta}]
is a dyadic number.

Note that putting all the extra requirements in (d)–(f) leads to a loss of at most
C_{0}^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}(\log L)^{p_{\mathrm{sb}}}
, where
p_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the total number of SG atoms satisfying the condition in (f).

Now, define
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathrm{sb}}
to be the number of
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decorations
\mathscr{O}_{\mathrm{sb}}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
that satisfy all the requirements (a)–(f) defined above, and define

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{sb}}:=\mathfrak{E}_{\mathrm{sb}}\cdot L^{-(d-\gamma)\chi(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}})}\cdot(C_{1}^{-1}\delta^{1/2})^{\chi(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}})}\prod_{j=1}^{q_{\mathrm{sb}}}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{z_{j}},

(11.10)

where we recall the circular rank
\chi
defined in Proposition
7.3
. Then, using (
11.9
) and (
10.5
), and that
\chi(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}})=n_{\mathrm{sb}}-R+1
, we know that (
11.6
) would follow if we can prove that

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{sb}}\leq L^{-4\eta^{6}(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+p_{\mathrm{sb}})}\cdot L^{(R-1)(d-\gamma-1/4-1/40)}.

(11.11)

Here note that each hinge atom is SG by Proposition
10.6
(5), and cannot be an interior atom of any SG vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
or
\mathscr{C}_{2}
, so we always have
q_{\mathrm{sk}}\leq p_{\mathrm{sb}}
.

11.2.
Reduction to large gap molecules

Our goal now is to prove (
11.11
). To bound the number of decorations
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathrm{sb}}
in the counting problem, we will reduce
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
by performing suitable
cutting
operations introduced in
[
22
]
, at all the SG vine-like objects and SG atoms. The proof is essentially the same as in Sections 9–10 of
[
22
]
, with only minor differences due to the fact that here we are dealing with general gardens instead of couples. We start by recalling the definition of cuts.

Definition 11.1

(The cutting operation
[
22
]
)
.

Given a molecule
\mathbb{M}
and an atom
v
. Suppose
v
has two bonds
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
of opposite directions, then we may
cut
the atom
v
along the bonds
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
, by replacing
v
with two atoms
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, such that the endpoint
v
for the bonds
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
is moved to
v_{1}
, and the endpoint
v
for the other bond(s) is moved to
v_{2}
, see Figure
19
. We say this cut is an
\alpha
- (resp.
\beta
-) cut, if it does not (resp. does) generate a new connected component, and accordingly we say the resulting atoms
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are
\alpha
- or
\beta
- atoms. If we are also given a decoration
(k_{\ell})
of
\mathbb{M}
, then we may define the gap of this cut to be
r:=k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}
.

Figure 19.
A cutting operation executed at a degree
4
atom
v
, see Definition
11.1
.

Remark 11.2
.

Compared to
[
22
]
, here we also allow to perform cutting at a degree
2
atom
v
. This case is in fact trivial: after the cut
v_{2}
becomes an isolated atom (without any bond) which forms a single connected component by itself.

With Definition
11.1
, we shall now reduce
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
by performing various cutting operations, and possibly removing some connected components created in this process, until reaching a final molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
. At each step, let the molecule before and after the operation be
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}}
, then any decoration
(k_{\ell})
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
can be naturally inherited to obtain a decoration of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}}
.

For any molecule
\mathbb{M}
in this process (which could be
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
or
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}}
), consider the possible
(c_{v})
-decorations of
\mathbb{M}
, also restricted by some
(\beta_{v})
and
(k_{\ell}^{0})
; we also assume that this decoration is inherited from a
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
that satisfies all assumptions (a)–(f) in Section
11.1.1
. Then we consider the number of such restricted decorations, take supremum over the parameters
(c_{v},\beta_{v},k_{\ell}^{0})
, and define it to be
\mathfrak{E}
. Similar to (
11.10
) we also define

\mathfrak{A}:=\mathfrak{E}\cdot L^{-(d-\gamma)\chi(\mathbb{M})}(C_{1}^{-1}\delta^{1/2})^{\chi(\mathbb{M})}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{z_{j}},

(11.12)

where
\chi(\mathbb{M})
is the circular rank of
\mathbb{M}
, and
z_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q)
are the lengths of the maximal ladders
\mathbb{L}_{j}
in
\mathbb{M}
, and
\mathfrak{X}_{j}
are defined from
P_{j}
as in Proposition
10.5
, while
P_{j}
are fixed such that the gap
r_{j}
of
\mathbb{L}_{j}
is such that
|r_{j}|\sim_{0}P_{j}
. Define also
m
to be the number of atoms not in the maximal ladders, and
\rho=q+m
. The notations
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{E})
and
(\rho,q,m)
will apply to the molecules appearing below, with possible subscripts matching those of
\mathbb{M}
(so
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}
is defined for the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
etc.)

We will prove, for each operation, an inequality of form

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{I}\cdot\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}

(11.13)

for some quantity
\mathfrak{I}
, which we define to be the
deviation
of this operation. Clearly, if we can bound the deviation in each operation step, as well as
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}
for the final molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
, then we can deduce from these an upper bound for
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{sb}}
using (
11.13
).

We will define the needed cutting operations, and prove (
11.13
) for each operation, in Section
11.2.1
; then in Section
11.3
we obtain an upper bound for
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}
and complete the proof of (
11.11
).

11.2.1.
Cutting operations and estimates

Recall the notion of
V,E,F
and
\chi
as in Definition
7.1
; define also
V_{\alpha}
and
V_{\beta}
to be the number of
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms (see Definition
11.1
, and also the description of operations below), and use
\Delta
to denote increments (such as
\Delta F=F_{\mathrm{pos}}-F_{\mathrm{pre}}
).

Operation 1: Removing SG vine-like objects.
In Operation 1, we collect all the SG vine-like objects in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
and
\mathscr{C}_{2}
, as defined in Section
11.1.1
; note that these also include any possible triple bonds in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
(see Proposition
10.6
(2)). For each of these objects
\mathbb{U}
, we cut the molecule at each of its joints
v
, along the two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
fixed as in requirement (e) defined in Section
11.1.1
(i.e. the two bonds that belong to
\mathbb{U}
). This disconnects a VC, denoted by
\mathbb{U}^{\prime}
, from the rest of the molecule, and then we remove
\mathbb{U}^{\prime}
. The two joints then become two atoms of degree at most
2
(it is possible that they have degree
0
, i.e. isolated atoms); we define them as
\alpha
-atoms if no new connected component is created, and
\beta
-atoms otherwise. If they are
\alpha
-atoms, we also label each of them by the dyadic number
Q\in[L^{-1},L^{-\gamma+\eta}]
such that
|r|\sim_{0}Q
for the gap
r
of
\mathbb{U}^{\prime}
(note
r\neq 0
).

Operation 2: Removing triangles.
In Operation 2, we consider the possible triangles
v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}
in the molecule, such that there are bonds
\ell_{j}
connecting
v_{j+1}
and
v_{j+2}
(where
v_{4}=v_{1}
and
\ell_{4}=\ell_{1}
etc.), and
(v_{j},\ell_{j+1},\ell_{j+2})
is an SG triple for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Let
|k_{\ell_{j+1}}-k_{\ell_{j+2}}|\sim_{0}Q_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
with
Q_{j}\in[L^{-1},L^{-\gamma+\eta}]
. We then cut the molecule at each
v_{j}
along the bonds
(\ell_{j+1},\ell_{j+2})
, which disconnects the triangle formed by
v_{j}
and
\ell_{j}
from the rest of the molecule, and remove the triangle. This leaves
3
atoms
v_{j}
of degree at most
2
. We define
v_{3}
as a
\beta
-atom, and define
v_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 2)
as an
\alpha
- (resp.
\beta
-) atom if it belongs to the same (resp. different) component with
v_{3}
, except when
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are in the same component different from
v_{3}
, in which case we define
v_{1}
as an
\alpha
-atom and define
v_{2}
as a
\beta
-atom. For any
\alpha
-atom
v_{j}
we label it by the corresponding
Q_{j}
.

Operation 3: Remaining SG cuts.
In Operation 3, we select each of the remaining SG atoms
v
, and cut them along the designated bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
as in requirement (e) in Section
11.1.1
. Note that each cut may be
\alpha
- or
\beta
-cut; we define the resulting atoms as
\alpha
- or
\beta
-atoms, and label any
\alpha
-atom by the dyadic number
Q\in[L^{-1},L^{-\gamma+\eta}]
, as in Definition
11.1
.

Operation 4: Remaining
\beta
-cuts.
In Operation 4, we look for all the possible atoms where a
\beta
-cut is possible, and perform the corresponding
\beta
-cut (defining the resulting atoms as
\beta
-atoms), until this can no longer be done.

Order of these operations.
We first perform Operation 1 for all SG vine-like objects in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
(first for the (CN) vines and root (CL) vines, and then for the HV’s), then perform Operation 1 for all SG vine-like objects in
\mathscr{C}_{2}
. Next we perform Operation 2 for all eligible triangles, and then perform Operation 3 for each of the remaining SG atoms. Finally we perform Operation 4 for all remaining atoms where a
\beta
-cut is possible.

Remark 11.3
.

After all the cutting operations, the resulting graph will contain some
\alpha
-atoms. For each
\alpha
-atom
v
and a given decoration we define an auxiliary number
\iota_{v}\in\{0,1\}
, such that
\iota_{v}=1
if a cutting operation happens before the cutting at the atom
v
, such that the gaps
r,r^{\prime}
of these cuttings satisfy
0<|r\pm r^{\prime}|\leq L^{-50\eta}Q_{v}
; if no such cutting operation exists then define
\iota_{v}=0
. Note that the number of choices for all
(\iota_{v})
is at most
2^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}
which can be safely ignored; therefore we may assume a choice of
(\iota_{v})
is fixed in the proof below.

Remark 11.4
.

Note that the joints of the SG vine-like object in Operation 1, the atoms
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in Operation 2 and the atom
v
in Operation 3 are all SG with fixed
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
. We will also assume that no atom
v_{3}
in Operation 2 or atom
v
in Operation 4 is ZG; in fact, if any such atom
v
is ZG, then it must be tame by Proposition
10.6
(3), and thus can be treated by the arguments in Section 10.5 of
[
22
]
. Namely, we consider the connected components after removing the atom
v
. If (i)
d(v)<4
, or if some component contains exactly one atom adjacent to
v
, then the values of
k_{\ell}
for
\ell\leftrightarrow v
must all be fixed in the given decoration, so we always have enough gain whether the resulting
\alpha
- or
\beta
-atoms have zero gap. We then cut at
v
and proceed normally. If (ii)
d(v)=4
and no component contains exactly one atom adjacent to
v
, then removing
v
will yield
\Delta\chi\leq-2
by direct calculation, which leads to a power gain
\mathfrak{I}\leq L^{-(d-2\gamma-\eta)}
, as
(k_{\ell})
for
\ell\leftrightarrow v
have at most
L^{d}
choices. We then remove
v
without assigning any new
\alpha
- or
\beta
-atoms and proceed with the rest of the molecule. The big gain
L^{-(d-2\gamma-\eta)}
is enough to cover any loss that may occur, see Remark
11.8
.

Proposition 11.5
.

The followings hold regarding Operations 1–4, and the process of applying them as described above:

(1)

After each Operation 1–4,
except for an Operation 1 that creates a new connected component
, we have that

\mathfrak{I}\leq L^{-\eta^{3}}\cdot\prod_{v}^{(\alpha)}L^{(\gamma+3\eta)/2-\kappa_{v}\eta}Q_{v}\cdot L^{(2\gamma_{0}+5\eta^{2})\Delta F-(\gamma_{0}+2\eta^{2})\Delta V_{\beta}}

(11.14)

for any
Q_{v}\in[L^{-1},L^{-\gamma+\eta}]
, where the product is taken over all the newly created
\alpha
-atoms
v
, and
Q_{v}
denotes the label of
v
. The parameter
\kappa_{v}
equals
0
if
\iota_{v}=0
, and equals
50
if
\iota_{v}\neq 0
.

(2)

If an Operation 1 creates a new connected component, then we have that

\mathfrak{I}\leq L^{\gamma_{0}-\eta^{3}}\cdot\prod_{v}^{(\alpha)}L^{(\gamma+3\eta)/2-\kappa_{v}\eta}Q_{v}\cdot L^{(2\gamma_{0}+5\eta^{2})\Delta F-(\gamma_{0}+2\eta^{2})\Delta V_{\beta}},

(11.15)

where the notations are the same as in (
11.14
).

(3)

In the whole process, the number of Operations 1 that indeed create new connected components, is at most
2R-1
. If it is exactly
2R-1
, then one of the new connected components created must be an isolated
\beta
-atom.

Proof.

(1) This is exactly Proposition 9.4 of
[
22
]
, and follows from exactly the same proof. Note that in the couple setting in Section 9 of
[
22
]
(i.e.
R=1
), no Operation 1 can create a new component, and the proof of Proposition 9.4 of
[
22
]
relies on this fact.

(2) Note that all the SG vine-like objects in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
and
\mathscr{C}_{2}
are disjoint (and the DV’s and LG and ZG vine-like objects containing those objects in
\mathscr{C}_{2}
are also disjoint with the objects in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
), so removing any of them will not affect the others. By Lemma
A.5
, any Operations 1 concerning
\mathscr{C}_{2}
will
not
create a new connected component, because the corresponding DV or LG or ZG vine-like object will still remain connected.

Therefore, we only need to consider Operations 1 concerning
\mathscr{C}_{1}
, which is always removing a single vine
\mathbb{U}
. By definition we have
\Delta F=1
and
\Delta V_{\beta}=2
, and no new
\alpha
-atom is created. If
\mathbb{U}
contains
m
atoms (including joints). Consider the collection
(k_{\ell})
for all bonds
\ell\in\mathbb{U}
in the decoration of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
, and let the number of choices for them be
\mathfrak{F}
, then we have

\mathfrak{I}\lesssim_{1}\mathfrak{F}\cdot L^{-(m-1)(d-\gamma)}\cdot(C_{1}^{-1}\delta^{1/2})^{m-1}\prod_{j:\mathbb{L}_{j}\subset\mathbb{U}}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{z_{j}}

(11.16)

where the product is taken over all
j
such that the maximal ladder
\mathbb{L}_{j}
(Proposition
10.5
) is a subset of
\mathbb{U}
(so there are at most three such
j
, see Figure
15
), and
z_{j}
is the length of
\mathbb{L}_{j}
.

Now we calculate
\mathfrak{F}
. Consider the two bonds
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
at one joint of
\mathbb{V}\mathbb{C}
that belong to
\mathbb{U}
. Note that removing
\mathbb{U}
will create a new connected component; thus in the
(c_{v})
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
, we must have that
k_{\ell_{1}}\pm k_{\ell_{2}}
equals a fixed constant and that
|k_{\ell_{1}}|^{2}\pm|k_{\ell_{2}}|^{2}
belongs to a fixed interval of length at most
n_{\mathrm{sb}}\cdot\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma}
, both depending only on the parameters
(c_{v})
and
(\beta_{v})
. By losing another factor
n_{\mathrm{sb}}\leq(\log L)^{C_{2}}
which can be ignored in view of the
L^{-\eta^{3}}
factor in (
11.15
), we may assume that
|k_{\ell_{1}}|^{2}\pm|k_{\ell_{2}}|^{2}
belongs to a fixed interval of length
\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma}
. By (
A.24
) in Lemma
A.10
, the number of choices for
(k_{\ell_{1}},k_{\ell_{2}})
is at most
L^{d-\gamma+\gamma_{0}+\eta^{3}}
.

Now, once
(k_{\ell_{1}},k_{\ell_{2}})
is fixed, we can invoke the re-parametrization introduced in the proof of Proposition
9.5
(note that we are dealing with a single vine now), and define the new variables
(x_{j},y_{j})\,(1\leq j\leq\widetilde{m})
if
\mathbb{U}
is bad vine, or
(x_{j},y_{j})\,(1\leq j\leq\widetilde{m})
and
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
if
\mathbb{U}
is normal vine, where
m=2\widetilde{m}+2
for bad vine and
m=2\widetilde{m}+5
for normal vine. In either case, since each
\Gamma_{v}
belongs to a fixed interval of length
\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma}
, we know that each
(x_{j},y_{j})
satisfies a system of form (
A.21
), and
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
satisfies a system of form (
A.22
). Moreover for each
j
, if the atoms associated with
(x_{j},y_{j})
(see the proof of Proposition
9.5
) belongs to a certain ladder
\mathbb{L}
, then the value of
r
in (
A.21
) must equal the gap of
\mathbb{L}
. Therefore, by Lemma
A.9
, we get in either case that

\mathfrak{F}\lesssim_{1}L^{d-\gamma+\gamma_{0}+\eta^{3}}L^{(m-2)(d-\gamma)}\cdot L^{\eta^{3}}(C_{1}\delta^{-1/2})^{m-1}\prod_{j:\mathbb{L}_{j}\subset\mathbb{U}}\mathfrak{X}_{j}^{-z_{j}},

hence
\mathfrak{I}\lesssim_{1}L^{\gamma_{0}+2\eta^{3}}
, so (
11.15
) is true.

(3) By definition, we are doing Operations 1 concerning
\mathscr{C}_{1}
before those concerning
\mathscr{C}_{2}
. Moreover, those Operations 1 concerning
\mathscr{C}_{2}
and HV’s in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
will not create any new connected component, so we only need to consider the Operations 1 concerning (CN) and root (CL) vines in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
, which are done before all the others. By Proposition
7.8
, after removing all these (CN) and root (CL) vines, the number of connected components increases to at most
2R
, so in this process the number of Operations 1 that create new connected components is at most
2R-1
; if equality holds, then one of the new connected components created must be an isolated atom, which also has to be
\beta
-atom by definition.
∎

11.3.
The rigidity theorem

Starting from
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, we perform all the Operations 1–4 as described in Section
11.2.1
, and obtain a final molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
. This molecule may not be connected, and let
\mathbb{M}
be any of its connected components. Consider also a decoration of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
(and
\mathbb{M}
) that is inherited from a decoration of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
as in Section
11.2
. Then, they satisfy certain crucial properties, which we summarize in the following proposition.

Proposition 11.6
.

In any (connected) component
\mathbb{M}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
, each
\alpha
-atom
v
is labeled by a dyadic number
Q_{v}\in[L^{-1},L^{-\gamma+\eta}]
, such that if
v
has two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
then
|k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}|\sim_{0}Q_{v}
in the decoration; recall also
\iota_{v}
and
\kappa_{v}
introduced in Remark
11.3
and Proposition
11.5
. Define any atom that is not an
\alpha
- nor
\beta
-atom to be an
\varepsilon
-atom
. Then, any
\varepsilon
-atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}

must
be LG or ZG in the decoration, and must be tame if it is ZG. Moreover
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
contains no triple bond.

We say a component is
perfect
if all
\alpha
- and
\beta
- atoms have degree
2
, and all
\varepsilon
-atoms have degree
4
. Otherwise we say
\mathbb{M}
is
imperfect
. Then, there are at most
R
imperfect components. Moreover, any perfect component has at least one
\beta
-atom. If a perfect component
\mathbb{M}
is a cycle (i.e. all atoms have degree
2
), then it is either a double bond (which is also Vine (I)), or a cycle of length at least
4
, or a triangle with at most one
\alpha
-atom. If
\mathbb{M}
is not a cycle, then all its
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms form several disjoint chains, such that the two ends of each chain are connected to two distinct
\varepsilon
-atoms. Finally, no
\alpha
- or
\beta
-atom is ZG with the assumptions in Remark
11.4
, and if any (perfect or imperfect) component
\mathbb{M}
is a
vine
with two joints having degree
2
, then it
must
be LG in the decoration.

Proof.

We only need to prove that there are at most
R
imperfect components. All the other statements follow from exactly the same proof as in Proposition 9.5 of
[
22
]
(note also that any ZG
\varepsilon
-atom cannot be a hinge atom, thus must be tame by Proposition
10.6
(3)).

The molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
has
n=n_{\mathrm{sb}}
atoms and
2n-R
bonds, as in Proposition
10.6
(1). It is easy to check that in each Operation 1–4, the value of
4V-2E-2(V_{\alpha}+V_{\beta})
is preserved. This value is
2R
for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, and has to be the same for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
. But this value also equals the sum of
4-d(v)
over all
\varepsilon
-atoms
v
, plus the sum of
2-d(v)
over all
\alpha
- and
\beta
- atoms
v
. If we add up these quantities over each component, then by definition, we get
0
for a perfect component, and at least
2
for an imperfect component. This implies that the number of imperfect components is at most
R
(in fact, it is exactly
R
if
R=1
).
∎

With Proposition
11.6
, we can reduce the proof of (
11.11
) to the estimates for quantities
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{E}
associated with the counting problems for each component
\mathbb{M}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
. These estimates are stated as a
rigidity theorem
in Proposition 9.6 of
[
22
]
; its proof is a major technical component of
[
22
]
. Fortunately, here we can use it (almost) as a black box.

Proposition 11.7
.

For each component
\mathbb{M}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
, define
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{E})
and
(\rho,q,m)
as in Section
11.2
and (
11.12
), but associated with
\mathbb{M}
. Let also each
\alpha
-atom
v
be labeled by the dyadic number
Q_{v}
, then we have

\mathfrak{A}\leq\prod_{v}^{(\alpha)}L^{-(\gamma+3\eta)/2+\kappa_{v}\eta}Q_{v}^{-1}\cdot L^{(\gamma_{0}+2\eta^{2})V_{\beta}-(2\gamma_{0}+5\eta^{2})G}\cdot L^{-\eta^{5}\rho+\eta Z},

(11.17)

where the product is taken over all
\alpha
-atoms
v
, and
V_{\beta}
is the number of
\beta
-atoms in
\mathbb{M}
, and
G
is
0
or
1
depending on whether
\mathbb{M}
is imperfect or perfect component. Finally
Z
equals
0
if
R=1
; if
R\geq 2
then
Z
equals the number of
\varepsilon
-atoms
not
of degree
4
in
\mathbb{M}
.

Proof.

For perfect components, or imperfect components when
R=1
(which are called
odd
components in
[
22
]
), this is exactly the same as Proposition 9.6 of
[
22
]
; see also Section 10.5 of
[
22
]
that addresses tame atoms. Now consider an imperfect component when
R\geq 2
. By repeating the same proof in Proposition 9.6 of
[
22
]
we can obtain that
\mathfrak{A}\lesssim_{1}1
(the proof in
[
22
]
trivially extends to general imperfect components). On the other hand, we may also apply Lemma 10.2 of
[
22
]
to get

\mathfrak{A}\lesssim_{1}L^{-\eta^{2}\rho+C_{0}(V_{\alpha}+V_{\beta}+Z)},

noticing that
V_{\alpha}+V_{\beta}+Z\geq 1
. Since for any
Q_{v}\leq L^{-\gamma+\eta}
we have

L^{-(\gamma+3\eta)/2+\kappa_{v}\eta}Q_{v}^{-1}\geq L^{(\gamma-5\eta)/2}\geq L^{\gamma/4},

and also
G=0
, by interpolation we obtain that

\mathfrak{A}\lesssim_{1}L^{-\eta^{4}\rho+C_{0}\eta^{2}(V_{\alpha}+V_{\beta}+Z)}\ll_{1}\mathrm{RHS\ of\ }(\ref{finalcount}).\qed

With Proposition
11.7
, we are finally ready to prove (
11.11
).

Proof of the bound (
11.11
).

By iterating (
11.13
) for each of the Operations 1–4 in reducing
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
to
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
, and using the fact that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
is formed by all the components
\mathbb{M}
, we have

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{sb}}\leq\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}\cdot\prod_{\mathrm{(all\ operations)}}\mathfrak{I},\qquad\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{fin}}\leq\prod_{\mathrm{(all\ components)}}\mathfrak{A},

(11.18)

where the products are taken over all Operations 1–4 and all components
\mathbb{M}
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
. Putting together (
11.14
), (
11.15
) and (
11.17
), we get that

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{sb}}\leq L^{(2\gamma_{0}+5\eta^{2})(F_{\mathrm{im}}-1)}\cdot L^{\gamma_{0}\cdot p_{\mathrm{op}}^{+}}\cdot L^{\eta Z_{\mathrm{tot}}}\cdot L^{-\eta^{5}\rho_{\mathrm{fin}}-\eta^{3}p_{\mathrm{op}}}.

(11.19)

Here in (
11.19
):

•

\rho_{\mathrm{fin}}
is the
\rho
value for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
defined as in Section
11.2
, and
F_{\mathrm{im}}
is the number of imperfect components in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}
, so
F_{\mathrm{im}}\leq R
by Proposition
11.6
.

•

p_{\mathrm{op}}
is the total number of Operations 1–4, and
p_{\mathrm{op}}^{+}
is the total number of Operations 1 that create new components, so
p_{\mathrm{op}}^{+}\leq 2R-1
by Proposition
11.5
(3). If equality holds, then one of the components must be an isolated
\beta
-atom, so we gain an extra power
L^{-(\gamma_{0}+\eta^{2})}
in (
11.17
) (where
V_{\beta}=\rho=1
and
G=Z=0
); thus in practice we can assume
p_{\mathrm{op}}^{+}\leq 2R-2
.

•

Z_{\mathrm{tot}}
is the sum of
Z
, defined in Proposition
11.7
, over all components
\mathbb{M}
, so
Z_{\mathrm{tot}}=0
if
R=1
; if
R\geq 2
then
Z_{\mathrm{tot}}
is the total number of
\varepsilon
-atoms not of degree
4
, so by Proposition
10.6
(1) we have
Z_{\mathrm{tot}}\leq 2R
. In any case we have
Z_{\mathrm{tot}}\leq 4(R-1)
.

Note also that if an SG atom
v
satisfies requirement (f) in Section
11.1.1
(i.e. is not an interior atom of an SG vine-like object in
\mathscr{C}_{1}
or
\mathscr{C}_{2}
), then we must be cutting at
v
in one of the Operations 1–4, so we have
p_{\mathrm{sb}}\leq p_{\mathrm{op}}
. Moreover each of Operations 1–4 changes the value of
\rho
by at most
C_{0}
, since each vine contains at most
3
ladders and no more than
20
atoms apart from these ladders, thus
\rho_{\mathrm{fin}}\geq\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}-C_{0}\cdot p_{\mathrm{op}}
. Putting together we get that

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{sb}}\leq L^{(R-1)(4\gamma_{0}+4\eta+5\eta^{2})}\cdot L^{-\eta^{5}(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}-C_{0}\cdot p_{\mathrm{op}})-\eta^{3}p_{\mathrm{op}}}\leq L^{-\eta^{5}(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+p_{\mathrm{sb}})}\cdot L^{(R-1)(4\gamma_{0}+1/10)},

(11.20)

which implies (
11.11
) because
d-\gamma-4\gamma_{0}\geq 1/2
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 11.8
.

Recall the discussion of ZG/tame atoms in Remark
11.4
. In case (i) the proof is modified trivially. In case (ii), by the arguments in Remark
11.4
, we know that this operation satisfies (
11.14
) with an extra power gain
L^{-(d-2\gamma-2\eta)-(2\gamma_{0}+5\eta^{2})}
(assuming
\Delta F=1
, otherwise
\Delta\chi=-3
and the estimate is much better). On the other hand, this increases the number of imperfect components by
2
, leading to a loss
L^{2(2\gamma_{0}+5\eta^{2})}
in (
11.19
), but this is acceptable as
d-2\gamma-2\gamma_{0}\geq 1
.

11.3.1.
Proof of Proposition
3.11

By interpolating (
11.5
) with (
11.6
), which follows from (
11.11
), we get that

|\mathscr{K}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}}}|\lesssim_{2}(C_{1}\delta^{10\nu})^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot L^{-40\nu\eta\cdot g_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot L^{-\eta^{6}(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+q_{\mathrm{sk}})/2}\cdot L^{-(R-1)(1/4+1/60)}.

(11.21)

Combining with (
10.2
) and arguments in Section
10.2
, we get that

\displaystyle|\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{eqc}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{2}(C_{1}\sqrt{\delta})^{n-n_{\mathrm{sb}}}(C_{1}\delta^{10\nu})^{n_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot L^{-\eta^{8}g_{\mathrm{sk}}-\eta^{2}b_{\mathrm{sk}}+\eta^{7}q_{\mathrm{sk}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\times L^{-40\nu\eta\cdot g_{\mathrm{sb}}}\cdot L^{-\eta^{6}(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+q_{\mathrm{sk}})/2}\cdot L^{-(R-1)(1/4+1/60)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{2}(C_{1}\delta^{10\nu})^{n}\cdot L^{-\nu\eta(g_{\mathrm{sk}}+g_{\mathrm{sb}})}\cdot L^{-\eta^{6}(\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+q_{\mathrm{sk}})/4}\cdot L^{-(R-1)(1/4+1/60)},

(11.22)

where
g_{\mathrm{sk}}
and
b_{\mathrm{sk}}
are as in Section
10.2
.

With (
11.22
) we can now finish the proof of Proposition
3.11
.

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

We only need to consider the terms (a)–(d) in (
11.1
)–(
11.4
). For each term, we can treat the
(\langle k_{1}\rangle\cdots\langle k_{2R}\rangle)^{4\Lambda_{p+1}}
weights and
\partial_{k}^{\rho}
derivative for couples as described in Section
11
, so we just need to bound it pointwise for each fixed
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{2R})
. By translation invariance, for (
11.2
)–(
11.4
) we will assume
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
(the general
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
case is the same).

First consider (
11.3
) and (
11.4
). If in (
11.3
) we restrict to couples of incoherency index
g\geq 1
, then the contribution of each individual couple is bounded by
(C_{1}\delta)^{n}\cdot L^{-\gamma_{1}g/2}
by (
5.1
). For fixed
g
, the number of choices for the regular couple (without layering) is at most
C_{0}^{n}
by Proposition
4.5
, and the number of possible layerings is at most
C_{0}^{n}C_{2}^{g}
by Proposition
4.10
. This implies that

|(\ref{finaltermc})|\lesssim_{2}\sum_{g=1}^{n}(C_{1}\delta)^{n}\cdot L^{-\gamma_{1}g/2}\cdot C_{0}^{n}C_{2}^{g}\leq L^{-\gamma_{1}/4}\cdot\delta^{n/2}.

(11.23)

As for (
11.4
), let the (canonical and) regular, coherent layered couple be
\mathcal{Q}
. By Proposition
4.9
, since both
q
and
q^{\prime}
are now replaced by
p
, we know that all nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
must be in layer
p
, in particular the number of choices for
\mathcal{Q}
is at most
C_{0}^{n}
. Then
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is simply a restriction of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
defined in Definition
3.8
, so by (
6.1
), (
6.2
), (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) we get

|(\ref{finaltermd})|\leq L^{-(2\theta_{p+1}+3\theta_{p})/5}\cdot\delta^{n/2}.

(11.24)

Now we consider (
11.1
) and (
11.2
). By reductions in Sections
9
and
10
, each of them can be reduced to a summation of terms of form
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{eqc}}
which satisfy (
11.22
). Let
\bar{g}:=g_{\mathrm{sk}}+g_{\mathrm{sb}}
and
\bar{\rho}:=\rho_{\mathrm{sb}}+q_{\mathrm{sk}}
with the relevant parameters defined as before; clearly these values are not changed under congruence relation defined in Definition
10.2
. Also when
R=1
and for any non regular couple
\mathcal{Q}
, the skeleton
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sk}}
is nontrivial and hence
\bar{\rho}\geq 1
.

With
\bar{g}
and
\bar{\rho}
fixed, we consider the number of choices for the canonical layered garden
\mathcal{G}
. First consider the molecule after removing all maximal ladders from
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
, which is a molecule of at most
C_{0}\bar{\rho}
atoms and thus has at most
(C_{0}\bar{\rho})!
choices; then, by adding back at most
C_{0}\bar{\rho}
disjoint maximal ladders of total length at most
n
, we get at most
C_{0}^{n}(C_{0}\bar{\rho})!
choices for
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
. Similarly by adding back at most
C_{0}\bar{\rho}
disjoint VC’s in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
in Section
10.1
, we get at most
C_{0}^{n}(C_{0}\bar{\rho})!
choices for
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
. Then by Proposition
7.3
we get at most
C_{0}^{n}(C_{0}\bar{\rho})!(C_{0}R)!
choices for the garden
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
, and by Proposition
4.5
we get at most
C_{0}^{n}(C_{0}\bar{\rho})!(C_{0}R)!
choices for the garden
\mathcal{G}
if we do not consider layering.

Now we consider the possible canonical layerings of
\mathcal{G}
. By losing another factor
2^{n}
, we may fix the exact positions of all the
\bar{g}
incoherencies. If we fix the pre-layering of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
(which is equivalent to the layering of atoms of the molecule
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
), then by Propositions
4.9
and
4.10
, we know that the number of layerings for all the regular couples
\mathcal{Q}_{(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{l}^{\prime})}
and
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{m})}
attached to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
is bounded by

C_{0}^{n}C_{2}^{\bar{g}}\cdot\prod_{\ell}C_{0}^{|\mathfrak{L}_{v}-\mathfrak{L}_{v^{\prime}}|},

(11.25)

where the product is taken over all bonds
\ell
of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
connecting atoms
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
similar to (
8.1
). Therefore, we only need to bound the summation

\sum_{(\mathfrak{L}_{v})}\prod_{\ell}C_{0}^{|\mathfrak{L}_{v}-\mathfrak{L}_{v^{\prime}}|},

(11.26)

where the summation is taken over all possible layerings of atoms of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
.

Next, consider all the VC’s in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
defined in Definition
10.2
which are merged in reducing
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
to
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
in Sections
10.1
–
10.2
. Since they are formed by (CL) vines only, by Proposition
7.7
we know that for each of the VC in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
, the layers of the joint atoms of its vine ingredients form a monotonic sequence. Thus, by Proposition
8.3
, we know that the number of possible layerings of atoms in these VC’s, multiplied by the product in (
11.26
) involving bonds in these VC’s, is bounded by
C_{0}^{n}C_{2}^{\bar{g}}\cdot C_{2}^{q_{\mathrm{sk}}}
(where
q_{\mathrm{sk}}
is the number of all these VC’s).

Finally, consider all the maximal ladders in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sb}})
. By repeating the above arguments but now using Proposition
8.2
, we can bound the the number of possible layerings of atoms in these ladders, multiplied by the product in (
11.26
) involving bonds in these ladders, by
C_{0}^{n}C_{2}^{\bar{g}}\cdot C_{2}^{q_{\mathrm{sb}}}
(where
q_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the number of all these maximal ladders). The number of atoms not in these maximal ladders is
m_{\mathrm{sb}}
, and the number of layerings for them, multiplied by the product in (
11.26
) involving bonds at these atoms, is trivially bounded by
C_{2}^{m_{\mathrm{sb}}}
.

Putting altogether, we know that with fixed values of
n
,
R
,
\bar{g}
and
\bar{\rho}
, the number of possible canonical layered gardens
\mathcal{G}
is bounded by

C_{0}^{n}(C_{0}\bar{\rho})!(C_{0}R)!C_{2}^{\bar{g}+\bar{\rho}}.

The number of congruence classes, which is the number of terms of form
\mathscr{K}_{p+1,n}^{\mathrm{eqc}}
contained in (
11.1
) and (
11.2
), is also bounded by this number. Combining with (
11.22
), we get that

|(\ref{finalterma})|\mathrm{\ or\ }|(\ref{finaltermb})|\lesssim_{2}(C_{1}\delta^{10\nu})^{n}L^{-\eta^{7}}\cdot L^{-\nu\eta(\bar{\rho}+\bar{g})}C_{2}^{\bar{g}+\bar{\rho}}\cdot L^{-(R-1)(1/4+1/60)}(C_{0}R)!\cdot(C_{0}\bar{\rho})!.

(11.27)

Since
\max(R,\bar{\rho},\bar{g})\leq n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}\leq(\log L)^{C_{2}}
by the assumption in Proposition
3.11
, from (
11.27
) we easily deduce that

|(\ref{finalterma})|\mathrm{\ or\ }|(\ref{finaltermb})|\lesssim_{2}\delta^{8\nu n}L^{-\eta^{7}}\cdot L^{-(R-1)(1/4+1/70)}.

(11.28)

This completes the proof of Proposition
3.11
.
∎

12.
Linearization and proof of Proposition
3.12

12.1.
Flower trees and construction of parametrix

We turn to the proof of Proposition
3.12
. Like in
[
19
,
22
]
, the key point is to construct a suitable parametrix of
1-\mathscr{L}_{1}
for the
\mathbb{R}
-linear operator
\mathscr{L}_{1}
defined in (
3.39
), which involves the notions of of flower trees and flower gardens.

Definition 12.1

(Flower trees and flower gardens
[
19
,
22
]
)
.

We define a
flower tree
to be a ternary tree
\mathcal{T}
with one particular leaf
\mathfrak{f}
fixed. This leaf
\mathfrak{f}
is called the
flower
of
\mathcal{T}
. There is a unique path from the root
\mathfrak{r}
to the flower
\mathfrak{f}
, which we call the
stem
of
\mathcal{T}
. Similarly we can define a
flower garden

\mathcal{G}
of width
2R
to be a collection of
2R
flower trees whose roots have signs
(+,-,\cdots,+,-)
, such that all the
non-flower
leaves are completely paired
15
15

15

We may also pair the flowers (for example pair the flower of the first tree with the second, the third with the fourth, etc.) to make
\mathcal{G}
a genuine garden, as was done in
[
22
]
, but this is not necessary in the proof below.
as in Definition
3.4
. For any
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
we define a
(k,k^{\prime})
-decoration of
\mathcal{T}
or
\mathcal{G}
to be a decoration in the sense of Definition
3.5
such that
k_{\mathfrak{r}}=k
and
k_{\mathfrak{f}}=k^{\prime}
for the root
\mathfrak{r}
and flower
\mathfrak{f}
of each tree.

We define the layering of a flower garden as in Definition
3.6
, but with the additional requirement that all the nodes on the stem of each tree (from the root to the flower) are in layer
p
. We also define the collection
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}
of
canonical layered flower gardens

\mathcal{G}
in the same way as in Definition
3.7
(but without any assumption about irreducibility of
\mathcal{G}
), where we start from
2R
flower trees with all branching nodes, flowers and paired leaves in layer
p
, then divide the set of remaining (non-flower) leaves into subsets of size
\geq 4
and replace each subset by an irreducible proper layered garden in
\mathscr{G}_{p}
. Note that Proposition
4.8
also holds for
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}
with essentially the same proof.

For any flower tree
\mathcal{T}
and layered flower garden
\mathcal{G}
of order
n
, and
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
p+1>t>s>p
, define the quantities

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-1}}\bigg)^{n}\zeta(\mathcal{T})\sum_{\mathscr{D}}\epsilon_{\mathscr{D}}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}e^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}-s)\prod_{\mathfrak{f}\neq\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}a_{k_{\mathfrak{l}}}(p),

(12.1)

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{G}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})=\bigg(\frac{\delta}{2L^{d-1}}\bigg)^{n}\zeta(\mathcal{G})\sum_{\mathscr{I}}\epsilon_{\mathscr{I}}\int_{\mathcal{I}}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{N}}e^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{n}}\prod_{\mathfrak{f}}\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}-s){\prod_{\mathfrak{f}\neq\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}^{(+)}F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})},

(12.2)

which are slight modifications of (
3.33
) and (
3.24
). Here in (
12.1
),
\mathscr{D}
is a
(k,k^{\prime})
-decoration of
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{D}
is defined as in (
3.34
), and the other objects are associated with the tree
\mathcal{T}
. In (
12.2
),
\mathscr{I}
is a
(k,k^{\prime})
-decoration of
\mathcal{G}
, the other objects are associated with the garden
\mathcal{G}
, and the set
\mathcal{I}
is defined as in (
3.25
); the second product is taken over all the flowers
\mathfrak{f}
in
\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{f}
, and in the last product we assume
\mathfrak{l}
has sign
+
and is not any flower
\mathfrak{f}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Now we define the parametrix of the operator
1-\mathscr{L}_{1}
. Define the operator

(\mathscr{X}\textit{{b}})_{k}(t)=\sum_{\zeta\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{k^{\prime}}\int_{p}^{t}\mathscr{X}_{kk^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)b_{k^{\prime}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s;\quad\textit{{b}}=b_{k^{\prime}}(s),

(12.3)

with the kernel given by

\mathscr{X}_{kk^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime}),

(12.4)

where the summation is taken over all flower trees
\mathcal{T}
of order
\leq N_{p+1}
such that
\zeta_{\mathfrak{r}}=+
and
\zeta_{\mathfrak{f}}=\zeta
for the root
\mathfrak{r}
and flower
\mathfrak{f}
.

Let
\mathscr{Y}=(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})\mathscr{X}-1
and
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{X}(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})-1
. Note that the operator
\mathscr{L}_{1}
defined in (
3.39
) formally corresponds to attaching two trees to a single node as siblings, as in the proof of Proposition 11.2 of
[
19
]
, we then see that
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
both have the same expression as in (
12.3
) and (
12.4
), but with the sum in (
12.4
) taken over different sets of
\mathcal{T}
(cf. Section 11.1.1 of
[
22
]
). Namely, for
\mathscr{Y}
we require that (Y-1) the order
n
of each tree
\mathcal{T}
in the sum satisfy
n>N_{p+1}
, and (Y-2) the subtree rooted at each child node of
\mathfrak{r}
has order
\leq N_{p+1}
; for
\mathscr{W}
we require that (W-1) the order
n>N_{p+1}
, and (W-2) the subtree rooted at each sibling node of
\mathfrak{f}
has order
\leq N_{p+1}
, and (W-3) the flower tree obtained by replacing the parent
\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
of
\mathfrak{f}
with a new flower has also order
\leq N_{p+1}
. See Figure
20
for illustration. Note that the above requirement imposes that the order of
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
n\in[N_{p+1}+1,3N_{p+1}+1]
for both
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
.

Figure 20.
A flower tree
\mathcal{T}
with root
\mathfrak{r}
and flower
\mathfrak{f}
. Let the order of subtrees
\mathcal{T}_{j}
be
n_{j}
etc., and the order of
\mathcal{T}

\mathscr{Y}_{m}

N_{p+1}<m\leq N_{p+1}+n_{1}^{\prime}+n_{2}^{\prime}+1
