\mathscr{W}_{m}
is that
N_{p+1}<m\leq N_{p+1}+n_{1}+n_{2}+1
and
n_{j}\leq N_{p+1}
.

Proposition 12.2
.

Let
P
be an integer that is large enough depending on
C_{1}
, assume also that
\delta
is small enough depending on
P
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{p}
be as in Proposition
3.9
. Then for any
(t,s,k,k^{\prime})
we have

\mathbb{E}_{p}|\mathscr{X}_{kk^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)|^{2P}\leq\langle k-\zeta k^{\prime}\rangle^{-4\Lambda_{p+1}P}L^{(100d)^{3}P}.

(12.5)

The same estimate holds for
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
, but with an extra factor
e^{-100N_{p+1}}
on the right hand side.

Next, let
M_{1},M_{2}\geq L
be two fixed dyadic numbers. Consider the definition (
12.1
) of
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}
that occurs in (
12.4
). Suppose we restrict to

\max\{|k_{\mathfrak{l}}|:\mathfrak{f}\neq\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}\}\sim_{0}M_{1}

(or with
\sim_{0}
replaced by
\lesssim_{0}
if
M_{1}=L
) by a smooth cutoff function for the tree
\mathcal{T}
, where
\mathfrak{f}
is the flower of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{L}
the set of leaves, then (
12.5
) holds with an extra factor
M_{1}^{-80dP}
on the right hand side; the same is true for
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
.

Moreover, suppose
|k|\geq M_{1}^{10}
, and in (
12.1
) we further restrict to

\max_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{S}}|\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}|\sim_{0}M_{2}

(or with
\sim_{0}
replaced by
\lesssim_{0}
if
M_{2}=L
) by a smooth cutoff function for the tree
\mathcal{T}
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the set of all branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}
in the stem of
\mathcal{T}

other than
the parent node
\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
of
\mathfrak{f}
, then (
12.5
) holds with an extra factor
M_{2}^{-P/9}
on the right hand side. The same is true for
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
.

Proof.

We follow the arguments in Section
3.6
used to expand (
3.41
) based on (
3.33
), and now apply similar arguments to expand the left hand side of (
12.5
) based on (
12.1
); note that now we are dealing with moments instead of cumulants of
(a_{k_{\mathfrak{l}}}(p))
, so we will make no irreducibility assumption about flower gardens. This gives, upon application of (
3.10
), that

\mathbb{E}_{p}|\mathscr{X}_{kk^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)|^{2P}=\sum_{\mathcal{G}}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{G}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})+\mathscr{E}^{(1)},

(12.6)

where the sum is taken over all flower gardens
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{T}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{T}_{2R})\in\mathscr{H}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
of width
2P
, and
\mathscr{E}^{(1)}
is a remainder term containing one of
\mathscr{E}_{p}
or
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}_{p}
in (
3.28
) and (
3.30
). Here
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
is a subset of
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}
defined similar to
\mathscr{G}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
in Definition
3.10
, where we require in Definition
12.1
that each of the initial flower trees has order
\leq N_{p+1}
, and that each of the layered gardens replacing leaf subsets has order
\leq N_{p}
. For
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
we have the same formulas, but we also require the initial trees to satisfy (Y-1)–(Y-2) or (W-1)–(W-3) above.

We may treat
\mathscr{E}^{(1)}
just as in the proof of Proposition
3.9
in Section
3.7
; moreover, if the order of
\mathcal{G}
is
n(\mathcal{G})>N_{p+1}^{4}
, then the resulting term
\mathscr{E}^{(2)}
can also be treated just as in the proof of Proposition
3.9
. Thus we may restrict to
n(\mathcal{G})\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
in (
12.6
), so the definition of
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
is equivalent to each tree of
\mathcal{G}
having
\leq N_{p+1}
layer
p
branching nodes. Similarly, the extra requirements for
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
are also just (Y-1)–(Y-2) or (W-1)–(W-3), but with the notion of order in these requirements being replaced by the number of layer
p
branching nodes.

Now, to estimate (
12.6
), we simply repeat the arguments in Sections
4
–
11
devoted to the proof of Proposition
3.11
. Below we only discuss the necessary (minor) adjustments needed in the flower garden case here, as most of the proof is completely analogous.

(1)

Regarding the decay factor
\langle k-\zeta k^{\prime}\rangle^{-4\Lambda_{p+1}P}
: this is obtained by the same arguments as in Section
11.1
, using the decay of
F_{\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{l}}}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
in (
12.2
); note that an algebraic sum of
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
for all non-flower leaves in each tree equals
k-\zeta k^{\prime}
. The extra factor
M_{1}^{-80dP}
when assuming
\max\{|k_{\mathfrak{l}}|:\mathfrak{f}_{j}\neq\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{j}\}\sim_{0}M_{1}
comes in the same way (note that if we insert some cutoff to the tree
\mathcal{T}
associated to
\mathscr{X}
, then the same cutoff will appear in each tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
occurring in the garden
\mathcal{G}
associated with the expansion (
12.6
)).

(2)

Regarding the extra power
L^{(100d)^{3}P}
: compared to (
3.24
), the modification in (
12.2
) in the flower garden case only involves two nodes (the flower
\mathfrak{f}
and its parent
\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
) for each tree, and each such modification may cause a loss of
\leq L^{80d}
. For example, this loss may come from taking the
\mathfrak{S}^{40d,40d}
norm of the input function
\mathbf{1}_{k_{\mathfrak{f}}=k^{\prime}}
which occurs due to the definition of
(k,k^{\prime})
-decorations, or from the atoms corresponding to these one or two nodes in the molecule. They may also come from the
\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}-s)
factor which removes the integration in the
t_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
variable, but
\Omega_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
has at most
L^{10d}
possible values once we fix each
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
to a unit ball, so we may fix the value of
\Omega_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
and safely get rid of the factor
e^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}
for
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
at a loss of at most
L^{10d}
. In any case the total loss is
\leq L^{200dP}
for the total of
2P
trees.

(3)

Regarding the arbitrariness of
k
and
k^{\prime}
: all the proofs in Section
4
through Section
11
are translation invariant
16
16

16

Except those leading to the
\langle k_{j}\rangle^{-4\Lambda_{p+1}}
factors in Proposition
3.11
; but these factors are replaced by the
\langle k-\zeta k^{\prime}\rangle^{-4\Lambda_{p+1}P}
factor, which is treated in (1).
, so this is not a problem once
k
and
k^{\prime}
are fixed.

(4)

Regarding LF twisting and cancellation: we only need to consider those LF twists for
\mathcal{G}
that may affect the property of belonging to
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
. Note that the definition of
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
, including the modified versions for
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
, only involves the number of layer
p
branching nodes in (i) each tree
\mathcal{T}_{j}
of
\mathcal{G}
, (ii) each of the trees rooted at the children nodes of each root
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
, and (iii) each of the trees rooted at the sibling nodes of each flower
\mathfrak{f}_{j}
, see Figure
20
. Therefore, if the realization of a vine
\mathbb{V}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}}
does not contain any of the “special nodes”, i.e. the root
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
, any of the children nodes of
\mathfrak{r}_{j}
, the flower
\mathfrak{f}_{j}
, the parent and any sibling node of
\mathfrak{f}_{j}
, then any LF twist at this vine will not affect the property of belonging to
\mathscr{H}_{p+1}^{\mathrm{tr}}
. Clearly there are less than
20P
vines containing one of these special nodes; for each of them we can use Proposition
10.4
to treat its ladder parts, and sum and integrate trivially over the
k_{\ell}
and
t_{v}
variables for
\ell
and
v
outside of the ladders (there are at most
20
such variables per vine). This leads to a loss of at most
L^{20d}
per vine, and at most
L^{400dP}
in total.

(5)

Similarly, the property that all the nodes on the stem of each tree must belong to layer
p
, as in the definition of flower gardens in Definition
12.1
, is also preserved under LF twisting. For example, consider
\mathcal{G}
whose skeleton contains a Vine (II-a) in Figure
16
, while the skeleton of its LF twist
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
contains a Vine (II-b). We may assume the flower is in the tree
\mathcal{T}_{1}
or
\mathcal{T}_{2}
in the notation of Figure
16
(otherwise it is not affected by LF twisting); now if all stem nodes in
\mathcal{G}
are in layer
p
, then in particular
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}=p
, which implies that
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}}=\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}}=p
as
\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{2}}\leq\min(\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{3}},\mathfrak{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{4}})
. Thus all stem nodes in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
must also be in layer
p
, including those in the regular tree
\mathcal{T}_{(\mathfrak{u}_{2})}
, because the layer of
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
in the skeleton is given by the layer of the lone leaf of the regular tree at
\mathfrak{u}_{2}
in
\mathcal{G}
or
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(6)

Regarding the extra factor
M_{2}^{-P/9}
: if we insert the
M_{2}
cutoff, then for each
j
we fix a branching node
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{j}
and insert the cutoff such that
|\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}|\sim_{0}M_{2}
(otherwise these cutoffs play no role in the proof). Note for each
j
, the expression (
12.2
) involves an integration of the exponential factor
e^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}t_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}}
in the time variable
t_{j}
. By integrating in
t_{j}
before manipulating everything else, and arguing similarly as in the proof of Corollary 11.3 in
[
19
]
, we gain a factor
M_{2}^{-P/9}
from this integration. This also leads to a loss of at most
L^{20dP}
by fixing the values of
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
as in (2) , which is acceptable. We then proceed with the same arguments in Section
4
through Section
11
to treat the rest of the expression (
12.2
) involving the rest of the garden. All the possible loss (such as those described in (2)) that may come along the way again add up to at most
L^{100dP}
which is acceptable.

(7)

Regarding LF twisting in the context of (6): here we are assuming
|k|\geq M_{1}^{10}
. For any (CL) vine
\mathbb{V}
in
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{sk}})
that is SG, let the nodes
(\mathfrak{u}_{1},\mathfrak{u}_{11},\mathfrak{u}_{21},\mathfrak{u}_{22})
be as in Proposition
7.7
, then we have the following: if the flower is a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{1}
, then it must be a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{11}
. In fact, if this is not true, then the flower must be a descendant of
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
or
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
, which means that exactly one of the nodes
\mathfrak{u}_{21}
and
\mathfrak{u}_{22}
is on the stem. But this is not possible, as
|k_{\mathfrak{u}_{21}}-k_{\mathfrak{u}_{22}}|\leq 1
by the SG assumption, while the difference
|k_{\mathfrak{n}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}|
for any node
\mathfrak{n}
on stem and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
off stem is at least
|k|/2
since
|k|\geq M_{1}^{10}
. Therefore, see Figure
16
, we know that any LF twisting for
\mathcal{G}
will not affect the structure of the stem. In particular it will not affect the value of any
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
for any
\mathfrak{n}
on the stem of any tree, nor the smooth cutoff functions of these
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
involving
M_{2}
. The rest of the discussion for LF twisting is the same as in (4) and (5).

With the above discussions, we can then obtain the desired bound for (
12.6
): if we restrict to
\mathcal{G}
of order
n
, then this contribution is bounded by

\langle k-\zeta k^{\prime}\rangle^{-4\Lambda_{p+1}P}L^{(100d)^{3}P}\cdot\delta^{2\nu n},

with the corresponding extra factors if the cutoff functions involving
M_{1}
or
M_{2}
are present. By adding up all
n\geq 0
for
\mathscr{X}
, and adding up all
N_{p+1}+1\leq n\leq N_{p+1}^{4}
for
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
, this completes the proof of (
12.5
), as well as the variants with the cutoff functions.
∎

12.2.
Proof of Proposition
3.12

In this section we prove Proposition
3.12
.

Part 1: Proof of (
3.50
) and (
3.51
).
Recall that

a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t)

(12.7)

from (
3.35
) and (
3.36
), where the sum is taken over all trees of sign
+
and order
\leq N_{p+1}
. By repeating the same arguments in the proof of Proposition
3.9
in Section
3.7
, for each fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
t\in[p,p+1]
we can bound

\mathbb{E}_{p}|(a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}})(t)|^{2}\leq L^{2}\langle k\rangle^{-4\Lambda_{p+1}}.

(12.8)

In the same way, by (
3.39
), we can write
(\mathscr{L}_{0})_{k}(t)
in a form similar to (
12.7
), but with the restriction that the order of
\mathcal{T}
is
>N_{p+1}
, while the order of the subtree rooted at each child node of the root
\mathfrak{r}
is
\leq N_{p+1}
. The same proof can be carried out, and with the lower bound on the order of
\mathcal{T}
, using (
3.45
) and (
3.49
), we get the same bound (
12.8
) for
\mathscr{L}_{0}
but with extra factor
e^{-100N_{p+1}}
on the right hand side. In addition, we can treat the time derivative
\partial_{t}a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}
(which simply contains an extra
\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{r}}-t)
factor in
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}
similar to the one in (
12.1
)) using the same arguments in the proof of Proposition
12.2
, to get the same bound (
12.8
) with an additional
L^{100d}
loss. The same holds also for
\partial_{t}(\mathscr{L}_{0})_{k}(t)
.

Now note that, (
3.50
) and (
3.51
), compared to (
12.8
), requires large deviation estimates for the
supremum
of
\langle k\rangle^{(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}|a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)|
and
\langle k\rangle^{(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}|(\mathscr{L}_{0})_{k}(t)|
. This can be solved using the following trick in
[
19
]
: by Sobolev in
t
we have

\sup_{t,k}\big(\langle k\rangle^{(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}|a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)|\big)\lesssim_{0}\big\|\langle k\rangle^{(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)\big\|_{L_{k,t}^{2}}+\big\|\langle k\rangle^{(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}\partial_{t}a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}(t)\big\|_{L_{k,t}^{2}},

(12.9)

where the norm is taken for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
t\in[p,p+1]
(here
L_{k}^{2}
is with respect to the counting measure). Then, using summability in
k
, we can bound the second moment of the above supremum by the right hand side of (
12.8
) with an additional
L^{200d}
loss. The bounds (
3.50
) and (
3.51
) then follow from the standard Chebyshev inequality.

Part 2: Proof of (
3.52
).
Let
Z:=Z^{(5/4)\Lambda_{p+1}}([p,p+1])
, and recall the definition of
\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y},\mathscr{W}
in Section
12.1
. For any operator
\mathscr{K}
with kernel
\mathscr{K}_{kk^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)
as in (
12.3
), it is clear that

\|\mathscr{K}\|_{Z\to Z}\leq L^{d}\sup_{\zeta,k,k^{\prime},t,s}\langle k-\zeta k^{\prime}\rangle^{(3/2)\Lambda_{p+1}}|\mathscr{K}_{kk^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)|:=L^{d}\|\mathscr{K}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}.

Our goal is to prove large deviation estimates for
\|\mathscr{X}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}
; the cases of
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
are analogous.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}\geq L
. We insert smooth cutoff functions, which are just the ones described in Proposition
12.2
, to the
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}
terms defined in (
12.1
) that occur in (
12.4
). Denote the resulting contribution by
\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}}
(for
|k|\leq M_{1}^{10}
we do not insert the
M_{2}
cutoff, in which case we write
\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}}
instead of
\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}}
). The large deviation estimate we will prove is of form

\mathbb{E}_{p}\|\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}}\|_{\mathfrak{N}}^{2P}\leq L^{C_{2}}M_{1}^{-P}M_{2}^{-P/18}

(12.10)

(for
\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}}
there is no
M_{2}^{-P/18}
factor), and similar bounds for
\mathscr{Y}
and
\mathscr{W}
but with the extra factor
e^{-100N_{p+1}}
on the right hand side. The proof of (
12.10
) is very similar to the proof of Proposition 12.2 in
[
19
]
, where we apply Lemma
A.6
to represent
k
by a finite system and thus reduce to finitely many possibilities of
k
(under suitable cutoffs), and estimate the moment for each fixed
k
by Proposition
12.2
. Below we will only sketch the main points.

(1)

Like the cases of (
3.50
) and (
3.51
) above, if we fix
(k,k^{\prime},t,s)
, then the desired bound follows from (
12.5
); the main difficulty comes from the fact that we are taking
L^{\infty}
norms in
(k,k^{\prime})
and
(t,s)
.

(2)

For the
L^{\infty}
norm in
(k,k^{\prime})
, we only need to consider the case when
|k|\geq M_{1}^{10}
. In fact if
|k|\leq M_{1}^{10}
then
(k,k^{\prime})
has at most
M_{1}^{20d}
choices, so we may replace the
L_{k,k^{\prime}}^{\infty}
norm by the
L_{k,k^{\prime}}^{2P}
norm which allows to fix
(k,k^{\prime})
and apply Proposition
12.2
. The subsequent summation in
(k,k^{\prime})
loses at most
M_{1}^{20d}
which is acceptable in (
12.10
).

(3)

In the same way, we may also assume
|k|\geq M_{2}^{10}
(otherwise we lost at most
M_{2}^{20d}
from summation in
(k,k^{\prime})
, which can be covered by the
M_{2}^{-P/9}
gain in Proposition
12.2
now that we know
|k|\geq M_{1}^{10}
). Using the definition of
M_{2}
and the formula for
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
, this in particular implies that all branching nodes in the stem of
\mathcal{T}
must have the same sign (as, if two consecutive branching nodes in the stem have opposite signs, then the corresponding resonance factor will satisfy
|\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}|\geq|k|^{2}
which is not possible).

(4)

Note that the expression (
12.1
), hence
\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}}
, depends on
k
and
k^{\prime}

only via
the
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
for branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}
in the stems of the flower trees
\mathcal{T}
; moreover each such
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
has the form
\langle k,z\rangle+\widetilde{\Omega}
, where
z
is a vector depending only on the
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
variables for non-flower leaves
\mathfrak{l}
(in particular
|z|\leq M_{1}^{2}
), and
\widetilde{\Omega}
also depends only on these
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
variables (in particular
|\widetilde{\Omega}|\leq M_{1}^{4}
).

(5)

In addition, note from (
12.1
) and the
\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}-s)
factor that
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}
can be written as a sum of terms of form
e^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}s}\cdot{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}
, where
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}
does not depend on
\Omega_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
. This sum is taken over
(k_{\mathfrak{m}})
for siblings
\mathfrak{m}
of
\mathfrak{f}
(which has at most
M_{1}^{10d}
choices), and once these
(k_{\mathfrak{m}})
are fixed the value of
\Omega_{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}}
is also a fixed function of
k
and
k^{\prime}
. Thus, at a loss of at most
M_{1}^{10d}
(which is amplified to
M_{1}^{20dP}
with the
2P
power, but still can be absorbed by the
M_{1}^{-80dP}
factor in Proposition
12.2
), we may get rid of the exponential factor and replace
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}
by
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}
in the definition of
\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}}
, so now
\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}}
depends on
k
and
k^{\prime}
only via the
\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
for branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
in the stems of the flower trees
\mathcal{T}
.

(6)

Since
|\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}|\leq M_{2}
for all branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
on the stem, we know that also
|\langle k,z\rangle|\leq 2M_{2}
(and
|z|\leq M_{1}^{2}
) in the context of (4) above. By Lemma
A.6
, we can find
k_{*}\in\mathfrak{Rep}
, where
\mathfrak{Rep}
is a fixed subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
of at most
(M_{1}M_{2})^{C_{0}}
elements
17
17

17

This part of argument is easier than the irrational torus case
[
19
]
, as in
[
19
]
we also have
y\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and thus need an extra Sobolev argument in
y
.
, such that (
A.20
) holds. In this case, using the above bounds for
\langle k,z\rangle
, we conclude that
(\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}})_{kk^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)=(\mathscr{X}_{M_{1}M_{2}})_{k_{*}k_{*}^{\prime}}^{\zeta}(t,s)
with the
M_{2}
cutoffs, where
k_{*}^{\prime}
is such that
k_{*}-k_{*}^{\prime}=k-k^{\prime}
(we may also assume
k_{*}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{Rep}
by enlarging
\mathfrak{Rep}
, as
|k-k^{\prime}|\leq M_{1}^{2}
). We can then replace the supremum in
(k,k^{\prime})
by the supremum in
(k_{*},k_{*}^{\prime})
and control it by the
L_{(k_{*},k_{*}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{Rep}^{2}}^{2P}
norm, with a loss of at most
(M_{1}M_{2})^{C_{0}}
due to summation in
(k_{*},k_{*}^{\prime})
, which is acceptable in view of the gain
M_{1}^{-80dP}M_{2}^{-P/9}
in Proposition
12.2
.

(7)

Finally, for the
L^{\infty}
norm in
(t,s)
, we shall apply Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality in slightly more precise way than (
12.9
) (this applies to any of the cases considered above). Namely, concerning only the
(t,s)
part, we have

\sup_{t,s}|\mathscr{K}(t,s)|^{2P}\leq M_{2}^{P/100}\cdot\|\mathscr{K}\|_{L_{t,s}^{2P}}^{2P}+M_{2}^{-100P}\cdot\|\partial_{t,s}\mathscr{K}\|_{L_{t,s}^{2P}}^{2P}

(12.11)

for any function
\mathscr{K}
. Note that the bound in Proposition
12.2
without
\partial_{t,s}
derivative gains a factor
M_{2}^{-P/9}
which cancels the
M_{2}^{P/100}
factor in (
12.11
).

(8)

On the other hand, the term in (
12.11
) with
\partial_{t,s}
derivative can be estimated in the same way as in Proposition
12.2

after replacing

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}
by
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}
as in (5) above (here the
\partial_{t}
derivative introduces an extra
\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{r}}-t)
factor, while the
\partial_{s}
derivative introduces an extra
\bm{\delta}(t_{\mathfrak{n}}-s)
factor with
\mathfrak{n}
being the parent or a non-flower child of
\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}
, and all these factors can be treated just as the one in (
12.1
); the case when
\mathfrak{f}^{\mathrm{pr}}=\mathfrak{r}
only needs some trivial adjustments). This leads to a bound similar to (
12.5
) but with extra loss of at most
L^{(100d)^{3}P}
and possibly without the
M_{2}^{-P/9}
decay, but we still have the
M_{2}^{-100P}
weight in (
12.11
) which provides the needed decay.

By putting (1) through (8) together, we arrive at the proof of (
12.10
). Now, with (
12.10
) and a simple Chebyshev argument, it is easy to see that by excluding a subset
\mathfrak{G}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{p}
, which has gauge and space translation symmetry and has measure
\leq e^{-4N_{p+1}}
(so
\mathfrak{F}_{p+1}=\mathfrak{F}_{p}\backslash\mathfrak{G}
), we have that

\|\mathscr{Y}\|_{Z\to Z}+\|\mathscr{W}\|_{Z\to Z}\leq 1/2,\quad\|\mathscr{X}\|_{Z\to Z}\leq e^{2N_{p+1}}.

By Neumann series, this means that the operators
(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{X}(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})
are both invertible. Therefore
1-\mathscr{L}_{1}
has both left and right inverses, hence
1-\mathscr{L}_{1}
is invertible and

\|(1-\mathscr{L}_{1})^{-1}\|_{Z\to Z}\leq 2\|\mathscr{X}\|_{Z\to Z}\leq e^{3N_{p+1}}.

This proves (
3.52
).

Part 3: Proof of (
3.53
).
This is essentially trivial once we have (
3.50
)–(
3.52
), if we just use the easily established facts that

\|\mathscr{L}_{3}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}})\|_{Z}\leq L^{30d}\|\textit{{b}}\|_{Z}^{3},\quad\|\mathscr{L}_{2}(\textit{{b}},\textit{{b}})\|_{Z}\leq L^{30d}\|\textit{{b}}\|_{Z}^{2}\cdot\|a_{k}^{\mathrm{tr}}\|_{Z}\leq e^{4N_{p+1}}\|\textit{{b}}\|_{Z}^{2}.

This completes the proof of Proposition
3.12
.

Appendix A
Miscellaneous results

In this appendix we collect some useful lemmas needed in the main proof.

A.1.
Cumulants

We start with the properties of cumulants.

Lemma A.1

(Property of cumulants)
.

Let
S_{i}\,(1\leq i\leq r)
be pairwise disjoint index sets, and
X_{j}\,(j\in S_{i})
be complex random variables. Let
Y_{i}:=\prod_{j\in S_{i}}X_{j}
, then we have

\mathbb{K}(Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{r})=\sum_{\mathcal{P}}\prod_{B\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{K}\big((X_{j})_{j\in B}\big),

(A.1)

where the sum is taken over all partitions
\mathcal{P}
of
S_{1}\cup\cdots\cup S_{r}
, under the assumption that there is no proper subset
A\subset\{1,\cdots,r\}
such that
\cup_{i\in A}S_{i}
equals the union of
some of the sets

B\in\mathcal{P}
. The same results hold if the cumulants are taken assuming a particular event
\mathfrak{F}
.

Proof.

Note that by (
3.10
) we have

\mathbb{E}(Y_{1}\cdots Y_{r})=\mathbb{E}\bigg(\prod_{j\in X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{r}}X_{j}\bigg)=\sum_{\mathcal{P}}^{(*)}\prod_{B\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{K}\big((X_{j})_{j\in B}\big),

(A.2)

where the sum is taken over all partitions of
S_{1}\cup\cdots\cup S_{r}
, without the restriction made in (
A.1
). If we define those partitions satisfying the restriction in (
A.1
) as irreducible partitions, then any partition
\mathcal{P}
can be uniquely decomposed into irreducible partitions; i.e. there is a unique partition
\mathcal{P}_{0}
of
\{1,\cdots,r\}
such that
\mathcal{P}=\cup_{A\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\mathcal{P}_{A}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{A}
is an irreducible partition of
\cup_{i\in A}S_{i}
. This implies that

\mathbb{E}(Y_{1}\cdots Y_{r})=\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\prod_{A\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{A}}\prod_{B\in\mathcal{P}_{A}}\mathbb{K}\big((X_{j})_{j\in B}\big),

(A.3)

where
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{A}
is as above. Also by (
3.10
) we have that

\mathbb{E}(Y_{1}\cdots Y_{r})=\sum_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\prod_{A\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\mathbb{K}\big((Y_{i})_{i\in A}\big).

(A.4)

Note that both (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) remain true (with obvious changes) if
\{1,\cdots,r\}
is replaced by any subset
A\subset\{1,\cdots,r\}
. It then follows, by induction on
|A|
, that (
A.1
) also holds (with obvious changes) if
\{1,\cdots,r\}
is replaced by any subset
A\subset\{1,\cdots,r\}
, and lastly setting
A=\{1,\cdots,r\}
yields (
A.1
). The results assuming
\mathfrak{F}
follows in the same way.
∎

A.2.
Analytical lemmas

Next we prove some analytical lemmas concerning time integrations.

Lemma A.2
.

Consider the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\chi}F(t,s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\chi(t)\chi(t^{\prime})\chi(s)\chi(s^{\prime})F(t^{\prime},s^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

(A.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\chi}F(t,s,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{t^{\prime}}\chi(t)\chi(t^{\prime})\chi(s)\chi(s^{\prime})F(t^{\prime},s^{\prime},k)\,\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime},

where
\chi
is a smooth cutoff function. Then, for
complex valued

F
, we have

\|\mathcal{I}_{\chi}F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta}}\lesssim_{1}\|F\|_{L^{1+3\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\qquad\|\mathcal{J}_{\chi}F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{-\eta,0,0}}\lesssim_{1}\|F\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

(A.6)

Proof.

For the first inequality, from Lemma 3.1 of
[
24
]
we have

\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K(\xi,\sigma,\xi^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\widehat{F}(\xi^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\prime},

(A.7)

where the kernel
K
satisfies

|K|\lesssim_{0}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle\langle\xi-\xi^{\prime}\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle\langle\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}\rangle}.

(A.8)

By the definition of
\mathfrak{X}^{\eta}
norm (and assuming
F
has compact support), we then have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{I}_{\chi}F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{\eta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\langle\xi\rangle^{\eta-1}\langle\sigma\rangle^{\eta-1}\langle\xi-\xi^{\prime}\rangle^{-1}\langle\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}\rangle^{-1}|\widehat{F}(\xi^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})|\,\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\xi^{\prime}\rangle^{\eta-1}\langle\sigma^{\prime}\rangle^{\eta-1}|\widehat{F}(\xi^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})|\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}\|\widehat{F}\|_{L^{1/(2\eta)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim_{1}\|F\|_{L^{1/(1-2\eta)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim_{1}\|F\|_{L^{1+3\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

(A.9)

As for the second inequality, we can calculate

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{J}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-i(t\xi+s\sigma)}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{t^{\prime}}\chi(t)\chi(t^{\prime})\chi(s)\chi(s^{\prime})F(t^{\prime},s^{\prime},k)\,\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{t^{\prime}>s^{\prime}}\chi(t^{\prime})\chi(s^{\prime})F(t^{\prime},s^{\prime},k)\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\int_{t^{\prime}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{s^{\prime}}\chi(t)\chi(s)e^{-i(t\xi+s\sigma)}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\int_{t^{\prime}<s^{\prime}}\chi(t^{\prime})\chi(s^{\prime})F(t^{\prime},s^{\prime},k)\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{t^{\prime}}\int_{s^{\prime}}^{\infty}\chi(t)\chi(s)e^{-i(t\xi+s\sigma)}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t.

(A.10)

From this we easily see that
|\widehat{\mathcal{J}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma,k)|\lesssim_{1}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\langle\sigma\rangle^{-1}\|F(t^{\prime},s^{\prime},k)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}
for each
k
, which finishes the proof in view of the definition (
3.11
).
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Consider the following operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\chi}F(t,s,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{t_{1}}\chi(t)\chi(t_{1})\chi(s)\chi(s_{1})e^{i\Gamma_{1}(k)(t_{1}-s_{1})}F(t_{1},s_{1},k)\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{1},

(A.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\chi}F(t,s,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{s}^{t_{1}}\int_{s}^{t_{2}}\int_{s}^{s_{2}}\chi(t)\chi(t_{1})\chi(t_{2})\chi(s)\chi(s_{1})\chi(s_{2})

\displaystyle\times e^{i\Gamma_{1}(k)(t_{1}-t_{2})}e^{i\Gamma_{2}(k)(s_{2}-s_{1})}F(t_{2},s_{2},k)\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1},

then for complex valued
F
we have

\|\mathcal{U}_{\chi}F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1/4,0,0}}\lesssim_{0}\|\partial_{t_{1}}\partial_{s_{1}}F\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},\qquad\|\mathcal{V}_{\chi}F\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{1/4,0,0}}\lesssim_{0}\|\partial_{t_{2}}\partial_{s_{2}}F\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

(A.12)

Proof.

Assume
\|\partial_{t_{j}}\partial_{s_{j}}F\|_{L_{k}^{\infty}L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=1
, where
j
is
1
or
2
in the case of
\mathcal{U}_{\chi}
or
\mathcal{V}_{\chi}
. We may assume
F
is compactly supported, then a simple calculation yields that

|\widehat{F}(\xi,\sigma,k)|\lesssim_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\langle\sigma\rangle^{-1},\qquad|\mathscr{F}(F\cdot\mathbf{1}_{t_{j}>s_{j}})(\xi,\sigma,k)|\lesssim_{0}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1/2}\langle\sigma\rangle^{-1/2}

(A.13)

uniformly in
k
, where
\mathscr{F}
is the Fourier transform in
(t,s)
.

Consider first
\mathcal{U}_{\chi}
. By calculations similar to those for
\mathcal{J}_{\chi}
in the proof of Lemma
A.2
, we get

\widehat{\mathcal{U}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma,k)=\int_{t_{1}>s_{1}}\chi(t_{1})\chi(s_{1})e^{i\Gamma_{1}(k)(t_{1}-s_{1})}F(t_{1},s_{1},k)\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdot\int_{t_{1}}^{\infty}\chi(t)e^{-it\xi}\,\mathrm{d}t\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{s_{1}}\chi(s)e^{-is\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
+(\mathrm{similar\ terms}).

(A.14)

We may restrict to the case
|\xi|,|\sigma|\geq 1
(otherwise the proof is similar and much easier), and integrate by parts in the two integrals in
t
and
s
. The resulting bulk term involving
\chi^{\prime}
has the same form but has better decay in
\xi
and
\sigma
, and can be treated in the same way; for convenience we will only consider the hardest case, which is the boundary term. With this simplification we thus write

\widehat{\mathcal{U}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma,k)=\frac{1}{\xi\sigma}\int_{t_{1}>s_{1}}\chi^{2}(t_{1})\chi^{2}(s_{1})F(t_{1},s_{1},k)e^{i(\Gamma_{1}(k)-\xi)t_{1}}\cdot e^{i(-\Gamma_{1}(k)-\sigma)s_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\\
+(\mathrm{similar\ or\ better\ terms}).

(A.15)

Using (
A.13
) we get that

|\widehat{\mathcal{U}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma,k)|\lesssim_{0}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle}\cdot\frac{1}{\langle\Gamma_{1}(k)-\xi\rangle^{1/2}\langle\Gamma_{1}(k)+\sigma\rangle^{1/2}}\lesssim_{0}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle\langle\sigma\rangle\langle\xi+\sigma\rangle^{1/2}}

(A.16)

uniformly in
k
, which easily implies (
A.12
) for
\mathcal{U}_{\chi}
.

The estimate for
\mathcal{V}_{\chi}
is similar. We have

\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma,k)=\int_{t_{2}>s_{2}}\chi(t_{2})\chi(s_{2})F(t_{2},s_{2},k)\cdot G(t_{2},\xi,k)\cdot H(s_{2},\sigma,k)\,\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}s_{2}+(\mathrm{similar\ terms}),

(A.17)

where

\displaystyle G(t_{2},\xi,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{t>t_{1}>t_{2}}\chi(t)\chi(t_{1})e^{i\Gamma_{1}(k)(t_{1}-t_{2})}e^{-it\xi}\,\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}t_{1},

(A.18)

\displaystyle H_{j}(s_{2},\sigma,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{s<s_{1}<s_{2}}\chi(s)\chi(s_{1})e^{i\Gamma_{2}(k)(s_{2}-s_{1})}e^{-is\sigma}\,\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}s_{1}.

We will assume
|\xi|,|\sigma|\geq 1
. In the expression of
G
, we first integrate in
t
and may replace this integral by the boundary term
(i\xi)^{-1}\chi(t_{1})e^{-it_{1}\xi}
as above; this yields that

G(t_{2},\xi,k)=(i\xi)^{-1}e^{-it_{2}\xi}\int_{t_{2}}^{\infty}\chi^{2}(t_{1})e^{i(\Gamma_{1}(k)-\xi)(t_{1}-t_{2})}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}+(\mathrm{better\ terms}),

and notice that this remaining integral is bounded in Schwartz class with respect to
t_{2}
(for example by a change of variable
t_{1}-t_{2}:=u
), uniformly in
\xi
and
k
. The same happens for
H
, and putting together into (
A.17
) we get that

|\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{\chi}F}(\xi,\sigma,k)|\lesssim_{0}\frac{1}{\langle\xi\rangle^{3/2}\langle\sigma\rangle^{3/2}}

(A.19)

uniformly in
k
, which implies (
A.12
) for
\mathcal{V}_{\chi}
.
∎

A.3.
Combinatorial lemmas

Next we present some combinatorial results needed in the proof. Most of them are the same as in
[
19
,
22
]
, but we include them here for reader’s convenience.

Lemma A.4
.

Given a tree
\mathcal{T}
of order
m
.

(1)

We can order all the branching nodes of
\mathcal{T}
as
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{m}
, such that the following holds for any decoration
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
: for each
j
there exists
j^{\prime}<j
such that
k_{\mathfrak{n}_{j^{\prime}}}-k_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}
is an algebraic sum of at most
\Lambda_{j}
distinct terms in
(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
where
\mathfrak{l}
ranges over the leaves, and
\prod_{j=1}^{m}\Lambda_{j}\leq C_{0}^{m}
.

(2)

Moreover, consider the collections of all decorations
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
suc that
|k_{\mathfrak{l}}-k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0}|\leq 1
for each leaf
\mathfrak{l}
, where
k_{\mathfrak{l}}^{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
are fixed vectors for each leaf
\mathfrak{l}
. Then this collection can be divided into at most
C_{0}^{m}
sub-collections, such that for any decoration
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
in a given sub-collection, each
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
belongs to a fixed unit ball that depends only on
(k_{\mathfrak{n}}^{0})
,
\mathfrak{n}
and the sub-collection but not on
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
itself.

Proof.

See Lemma 6.6 of
[
19
]
and Lemma A.4 of
[
22
]
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Consider a given molecule with a given decoration. Suppose
\mathbb{U}
is either a DV, or vine-like object that is LG or ZG, and assume
\mathbb{U}
contains some disjoint SG vine-like objects
\mathbb{U}_{j}
as subsets. Then each
\mathbb{U}_{j}
is either one double bond or two double bonds or Vine (V), or the adjoint of a VC denoted by
\mathbb{U}_{j}^{\prime}
, which is either Vine (V) or two double bonds. In any case,
\mathbb{U}
still remains connected after removing all the
\mathbb{U}_{j}
(in case
\mathbb{U}_{j}
is a vine) or
\mathbb{U}_{j}^{\prime}
(in case
\mathbb{U}_{j}^{\prime}
is a vine).

Proof.

First assume
\mathbb{U}
is a VC or HVC, which is concatenated by one or multiple vine ingredients. Then each
\mathbb{U}_{j}
must be a subset of a single vine ingredient, because the gap of
\mathbb{U}_{j}
is not the same as the gap of
\mathbb{U}
, hence
\mathbb{U}_{j}
cannot be concatenated by any of the vine ingredients of
\mathbb{U}
. Therefore we may focus on a single vine ingredient, which is Vine (I)–(VIII) as in Figure
15
. The result is then self-evident by examining the structures of these vines. The case of DV is similar, since each
\mathbb{U}_{j}
must be a subset of one of the two vines (V) forming
\mathbb{U}
.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Fix
M\geq L
. Then for any
k=(k^{1},\cdots,k^{d})\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, there must exist
0\leq q\leq d
and nonzero orthogonal vectors
v_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}\,(1\leq j\leq q)
, and vector
y=(y^{1},\cdots,y^{q})\in(L^{-2}\mathbb{Z})^{q}
, such that
|v_{j}|\leq M^{C_{0}}
and
|y|\leq M^{C_{0}}
, where
C_{0}
is a constant depending only on
d
. Moreover the system
(q,v_{1},\cdots,v_{q},y)

represents

k
, in the following sense:

(1)

If
z=(z^{1},\cdots,z^{d})\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
,
|z|\leq M
and
|\langle k,z\rangle|\leq M
, then
z
is a linear combination of
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{q}\}
;

(2)

If
z=\gamma^{1}v_{1}+\cdots+\gamma^{q}v_{q}
, then we have
\langle k,z\rangle=y^{1}\gamma^{1}+\cdots+y^{q}\gamma^{q}
.

In particular, there exists a subset
\mathfrak{Rep}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
with at most
M^{C_{0}}
elements, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
there exists
k_{*}\in\mathfrak{Rep}
and

\mathrm{For\ any\ }z\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d},\,|z|\leq M,\mathrm{\ we\ have\ that\ }|\langle k,z\rangle|\leq M\Leftrightarrow|\langle k_{*},z\rangle|\leq M;\\
\mathrm{in\ this\ case\ we\ also\ have\ }\langle k,z\rangle=\langle k_{*},z\rangle.

(A.20)

Proof.

Upon multiplying everything by
L
, we may replace
\mathbb{Z}_{L}
by
\mathbb{Z}
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, consider the set
H
of
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that
|z|\leq M
and
|\langle k,z\rangle|\leq M
(clearly
0\in H
). Let
q
be the maximal number of linearly independent vectors in
H
, we may fix a maximum independent set
\{w_{1},\cdots,w_{q}\}\subset H
, and apply Gram-Schmidt process to get orthogonal vectors
(v_{1},\cdots,v_{q})
. Since each
w_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
|w_{j}|\leq M
, we can easily make
v_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
|v_{j}|\leq M^{C_{0}}
. Moreover, since
|\langle k,w_{j}\rangle|\leq M
for
1\leq j\leq q
, we know that also
|y|\leq M^{C_{0}}
where
y=(y^{1},\cdots,y^{q})
with
y^{j}=\langle k,v_{j}\rangle
. This means that
k
is represented by the system
(q,v_{1},\cdots,v_{q},y)
, as desired. Clearly, if both
k
and
k_{*}
are represented by the same system
(q,v_{1},\cdots,v_{q},y)
, then (
A.20
) holds by definition; this completes the proof as the number of choices of
(q,v_{1},\cdots,v_{q},y)
is at most
M^{C_{0}}
.
∎

Remark A.7
.

Lemma
A.6
is adapted to the current setting of square torus. A similar (and slightly more complicated) version was proved in
[
19
]
, which is adapted to generic irrational tori. If we consider more general (partly rational and partly irrational) tori, then the corresponding result can be stated and proved by combining the arguments here and in
[
19
]
. We omit the details.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
S
be any finite set of vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then there exist
q\leq d
vectors
X_{1},\cdots X_{q}\in S
such that any
Y\in S
is a linear combination of
(X_{k})_{1\leq k\leq q}
with coefficients bounded by
2^{d-1}
.

Proof.

Let
X_{1}\in S
be such that
|X_{1}|
is maximal. For
k\geq 2
, pick
X_{k}\in S
successively such that
|X_{1}\wedge X_{2}\cdots\wedge X_{k}|
is positive and maximal, if such an
X_{k}
exists. This process must terminate after at most
q\leq d
steps, which results in a subset
\{X_{1},\cdots,X_{q}\}
. Clearly they are linearly independent, and any
Y\in S
can be written as a unique linear combination
Y=\lambda_{1}X_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{q}X_{q}
.

Now we have
|X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{q-1}\wedge Y|=|\lambda_{q}|\cdot|X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{q-1}\wedge X_{q}|
; by the choice of
X_{q}
we have
|\lambda_{q}|\leq 1
. Next consider
|X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{q-2}\wedge(Y-\lambda_{q}X_{q})|=|\lambda_{q-1}||X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{q-2}\wedge X_{q-1}|
; since both
Y
and
X_{q}
belong to
S
, by the choice of
X_{q-1}
we have
|\lambda_{q-1}|\leq|\lambda_{q}|+1
. In the same way we get that
|\lambda_{k}|\leq|\lambda_{k+1}|+\cdots+|\lambda_{q}|+1
for all
1\leq k\leq q-1
, which gives the result.
∎

A.4.
Counting lemmas

Finally we recall some counting estimates from
[
22
]
.

Lemma A.9
.

Fix
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
r,v\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
, such that
|r|\sim_{0}P
with
P\in[L^{-1},1]\cup\{0\}
(with
|r|\gtrsim_{0}1
if
P=1
). Let each of
(x,y,z)
belong to
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
intersecting a fixed unit ball, assume
\xi\in\{x,y,x+y\}
and
\zeta\in\{x-v,x+y-v,x+y-v-z\}
, and define
\mathfrak{X}:=\min((\log L)^{2},1+\delta L^{2\gamma}P)
as in Proposition
10.5
. Then we have the following counting bounds

\displaystyle\big\{(x,y):|x\cdot y-\alpha|\leq\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma},\,\,|r\cdot\xi-\beta|\leq\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma}\big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}\mathfrak{X}^{-1},

(A.21)

\displaystyle\big\{(x,y,z):|x\cdot y-\alpha|\leq\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma},\,\,|\zeta\cdot z-\beta|\leq\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma}\big\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{1}\delta^{-2}L^{3(d-\gamma)-\gamma_{0}}.

(A.22)

Proof.

This follows from Lemma A.2 of
[
22
]
(the bounds (
A.21
)–(
A.22
) needed here are much weaker than in
[
22
]
).
∎

Lemma A.10
.

Let
r\in\{2,3\}
and
\epsilon_{j}\in\{\pm 1\}
, and
k_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
be in
\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
intersecting a fixed unit ball, that solve the following system

\sum_{j=1}^{r}\epsilon_{j}k_{j}=k,\quad\bigg|\sum_{j=1}^{r}\epsilon_{j}|k_{j}|^{2}-\beta\bigg|\leq\delta^{-1}L^{-2\gamma},

(A.23)

where
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{d}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
are fixed parameters. Let the number of solutions to this system be
\mathfrak{E}_{r}
, then we have

\mathfrak{E}_{2}\lesssim_{1}\delta^{-1}\min(L^{d},L^{d+1-2\gamma}),\quad\,\,\mathfrak{E}_{2}\lesssim_{1}\delta^{-1}L^{d-\gamma-\eta}\,\,\mathrm{if}\,\,|r|\geq L^{-\gamma+\eta};\quad\,\,\mathfrak{E}_{3}\lesssim_{1}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}.

(A.24)

Moreover, suppose we have four vector variables
k_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 4)
as above, that (
A.23
) with
r=3
holds for
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
, and that the same equation holds also for the three vectors
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{4})
with the same
\epsilon_{j}
and possibly different
(k,\beta)
, then the number of solutions is bounded by

\mathfrak{E}_{4}\lesssim_{1}\delta^{-1}L^{2(d-\gamma)}\mathfrak{X}^{-1},

(A.25)

where
\mathfrak{X}
is defined as in Proposition
10.5
from
P
, and
P\sim_{0}|k_{3}-k_{4}|
.

Proof.

This follows from (parts of) Lemma A.3 of
[
22
]
.
∎

Appendix B
Failure of a physical space
L^{1}
bound

Consider the reduced Fourier coefficients
a_{k}(t)
in (
3.2
). In the Gibbs measure case of
[
55
]
, these coefficients are not independent, but their cumulants satisfy the (discrete analog of) physical space
L^{1}
bound, namely

\int_{(\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d})^{3}}\big|\mathbb{K}\big(\check{a}(0,t),\overline{\check{a}(x_{1},t)},\check{a}(x_{2},t),\overline{\check{a}(x_{3},t)}\big)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\mathrm{d}x_{3}\leq C_{0}L^{-\gamma}

(B.1)

for
t=0
(and any
t>0
due to invariance), where
\check{a}(x,t)
is the inverse Fourier transform of
a
as defined in (
1.1
). The bound (
B.1
) is crucial in the derivation in
[
55
]
, see the discussions in Section
1.4
; however it is not true for
t>0
, in the off-equilibrium setting in this paper. To see this, just choose
t=1
, expand
a_{k}(1)
into Taylor series consisting of multilinear expressions of
a_{k}(0)
, and take lowest order terms. By using (
3.3
)–(
3.4
) and Lemma
A.1
, we get that the cumulant
\mathbb{K}\big(a_{k_{1}}(1),\overline{a_{k_{2}}(1)},a_{k_{3}}(1),\overline{a_{k_{4}}(1)}\big)
contains a leading contribution

\mathbb{K}_{0}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}):=\frac{-\delta i}{2L^{d-\gamma}}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k_{4}=0}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\pi i\cdot\delta L^{2\gamma}\Omega s}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
\times\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{1})\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{2})\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{3})\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{4})\bigg(\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{1})}-\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{2})}+\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{3})}-\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{4})}\bigg),

(B.2)

where

\Omega:=|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k_{4}|^{2}

and we have omitted the
\epsilon
factor in (
3.5
) (as (
B.2
) vanishes if
\epsilon\neq 1
). Note the other contributions contain either higher powers of
\delta
or more negative powers of
L
, so we may ignore these other terms and replace
\mathbb{K}
by
\mathbb{K}_{0}
.

Now, choose
k_{j}^{*}
such that
k_{1}^{*}-k_{2}^{*}+k_{2}^{*}-k_{4}^{*}=|k_{1}^{*}|^{2}-|k_{2}^{*}|^{2}+|k_{3}^{*}|^{2}-|k_{4}^{*}|^{2}=0
, but

\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{1}^{*})}-\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{2}^{*})}+\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{3}^{*})}-\frac{1}{\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}(k_{4}^{*})}\neq 0;

this is possible due to the off-equilibrium setting. Using the definition of
\check{a}
, we can calculate

\int_{(\mathbb{T}_{L}^{d})^{3}}\mathbb{K}\big(\check{a}(0,t),\overline{\check{a}(x_{1},t)},\check{a}(x_{2},t),\overline{\check{a}(x_{3},t)}\big)\cdot e^{-2\pi i(k_{2}^{*}\cdot x_{1}-k_{3}^{*}\cdot x_{2}+k_{4}^{*}\cdot x_{3})}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\mathrm{d}x_{3}\\
=L^{d}\sum_{k_{1}}\mathbb{K}_{0}(k_{1},k_{2}^{*},k_{3}^{*},k_{4}^{*})=L^{d}\mathbb{K}_{0}(k_{1}^{*},k_{2}^{*},k_{3}^{*},k_{4}^{*})\sim L^{\gamma},

(B.3)

which means that (
B.1
) cannot be true.

Appendix C
Schwartz solutions to (
WKE
)

In this appendix we prove Proposition
3.2
. Recall the equation (
WKE
) and its nonlinearity, which is given by the trilinear form (
COL
). We can decompose (
COL
) as

\mathcal{K}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})=\sum_{j=0}^{3}(-1)^{j}\mathcal{K}_{j}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3}),

where (note that
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{3}
are essentially equivalent up to permutation of input functions)

\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})(k)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\varphi_{1}(k_{1})\varphi_{2}(k_{2})\varphi_{3}(k_{3})\bm{\delta}(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k)\\
\times\bm{\delta}(|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3},

(C.1)

\mathcal{K}_{1}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})(k)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\varphi_{1}(k)\varphi_{2}(k_{2})\varphi_{3}(k_{3})\bm{\delta}(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k)\\
\times\bm{\delta}(|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3},

(C.2)

\mathcal{K}_{2}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})(k)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\varphi_{1}(k_{1})\varphi_{2}(k)\varphi_{3}(k_{3})\bm{\delta}(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k)\\
\times\bm{\delta}(|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3},

(C.3)

\mathcal{K}_{3}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})(k)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{3}}\varphi_{1}(k_{1})\varphi_{2}(k_{2})\varphi_{3}(k)\bm{\delta}(k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k)\\
\times\bm{\delta}(|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}k_{1}\mathrm{d}k_{2}\mathrm{d}k_{3}.

(C.4)

Clearly each
\mathcal{K}_{j}
is a positive trilinear operator. Next, in (
C.1
), by making the change of variables
k_{1}=k+\ell_{1}
and
k_{3}=k+\ell_{3}
(so
k_{2}=k+\ell_{1}+\ell_{3}
) we can rewrite

\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})(k)=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}\varphi_{1}(k+\ell_{1})\varphi_{2}(k+\ell_{1}+\ell_{3})\varphi_{3}(k+\ell_{3})\bm{\delta}(-2\langle\ell_{1},\ell_{3}\rangle)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{1}\mathrm{d}\ell_{3},

(C.5)

noticing also that
|k_{1}|^{2}-|k_{2}|^{2}+|k_{3}|^{2}-|k|^{2}=-2\langle\ell_{1},\ell_{3}\rangle
. By Leibniz rule it then follows that

\nabla\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})=\mathcal{K}_{0}(\nabla\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})+\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},\nabla\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})+\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\nabla\varphi_{3}),

(C.6)

where
\nabla
refers to
\nabla_{k}
, and clearly the same is true for each
\mathcal{K}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
. Moreover, the integral in (
C.1
) is supported in
k_{1}-k_{2}+k_{3}-k=0
, so similarly we get

k^{i}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})=\mathcal{K}_{0}((k_{1})^{i}\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})-\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},(k_{2})^{i}\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})+\mathcal{K}_{0}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},(k_{3})^{i}\varphi_{3}),

(C.7)

where
k^{i}
represents any coordinate for
1\leq i\leq d
. For other
\mathcal{K}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
a simpler version of (
C.7
) holds, with only one term on the right hand side that involves
k^{i}\cdot\varphi_{j}
, and the other two terms has coefficient
0
. Finally, both (
C.6
) and (
C.7
) can be iterated to get corresponding equalities for multi-indices
\alpha
.

Now we can start the proof of Proposition
3.2
. Fix any
s>d/2-1
and define

\|\varphi\|_{Z_{s}}:=\|\langle k\rangle^{s}\varphi\|_{L^{2}};\quad\|\varphi\|_{Z_{s,m}}:=\sup_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq m}\|k^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}\varphi\|_{Z_{s}}

for nonnegative integers
m
. It is proved in
[
36
]
, see Lemmas 4.1–4.3 of
[
36
]
(also Remark 2.6 of
[
36
]
), that each
\mathcal{K}_{j}
satisfies the trilinear estimate

\|\mathcal{K}_{j}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})\|_{Z_{s}}\leq C_{0}(s)\|\varphi_{1}\|_{Z_{s}}\cdot\|\varphi_{2}\|_{Z_{s}}\cdot\|\varphi_{3}\|_{Z_{s}}

(C.8)

for some constant
C_{0}(s)
depending on
s
. It then follows that (
WKE
) is locally well-posed in
Z_{s}
.

The main point in our proof is to establish the following: for any
T>0
and any
s
and
m
, if the solution
\varphi
to (
WKE
) exists in
Z_{s}
space until and including time
T
and

\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\|\varphi(\tau)\|_{Z_{s}}\leq A,

(C.9)

and if the initial data satisfies
\|\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{Z_{s,m}}\leq B
, and the solution
\varphi(\tau)\in Z_{s,m}
for all
\tau\in[0,T]
, then we have the a priori estimate

\sup_{\tau\in[0,T]}\|\varphi(\tau)\|_{Z_{s,m}}\leq C(s,m,T,A,B)

(C.10)

for some constant
C=C(s,m,T,A,B)
.

To prove (
C.10
) we induct on
m
; the case
m=0
is known. Suppose (
C.10
) is true for smaller
m
, then to control the
Z_{s,m}
norm of
\varphi(\tau)
, we only need to consider multi-indices
\alpha,\beta
such that
|\alpha|+|\beta|=m>0
. By iterating (
C.6
) and (
C.7
), and applying Duhamel formula, we get

k^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}(\varphi(\tau)-\varphi_{\mathrm{in}})=\sum_{j=0}^{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=\alpha\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{3}=\beta\end{subarray}}\iota\cdot\int_{0}^{\tau}\mathcal{K}_{j}\big(k^{\alpha_{1}}\nabla^{\beta_{1}}\varphi(\tau^{\prime}),k^{\alpha_{2}}\nabla^{\beta_{2}}\varphi(\tau^{\prime}),k^{\alpha_{3}}\nabla^{\beta_{3}}\varphi(\tau^{\prime})\big)\,\mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime},

(C.11)

where
\iota\in\{-1,0,1\}
is a coefficient depending on
j
and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}
etc. In each of these terms, either
\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}<m
for all
i
, or
\alpha_{i}=\alpha
and
\beta_{i}=\beta
for exactly one
i
, and
\alpha_{i^{\prime}}=\beta_{i^{\prime}}=0
for all other
i^{\prime}
. Now we apply (
C.8
) to (
C.11
), and use the induction hypothesis, to get

\|k^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}\varphi(\tau)\|_{Z_{s}}\leq B+4(m+1)^{6d}C_{0}(s)T\cdot C(s,m-1,T,A,B)^{3}+12A^{2}C_{0}(s)\cdot\int_{0}^{\tau}\|k^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}\varphi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{Z_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime}.

By Gronwall this then implies (
C.10
) with

C(s,m,T,A,B)=C(s,m-1,T,A,B)+Be^{12A^{2}TC_{0}(s)}\\
+4T(m+1)^{6d}C_{0}(s)e^{12A^{2}TC_{0}(s)}\cdot C(s,m-1,T,A,B)^{3}.

Now we can finish the proof of Proposition
3.2
. For
s>d/2-1
and
m
, consider the maximal time
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)}
such that (
WKE
) has a (unique) solution in
C_{t}([0,\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)})\to Z_{s,m})
. Since
\varphi_{\mathrm{in}}\in\mathcal{S}
and (
WKE
) is locally well-posed in
Z_{s,m}
(because the trilinear estimate (
C.8
) holds for
Z_{s,m}
with constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(s,m)
, which follows from the same proof as above), we know
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)}>0
. Moreover, if
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)}<\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,0)}
, then (
C.10
) implies that
\varphi(\tau)
is
uniformly bounded
in
Z_{s,m}
for all
\tau\in[0,\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)})
, so by applying local well-posedness in
Z_{s,m}
to
\varphi(\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)}-\epsilon)
for sufficiently small
\epsilon
, we can construct the solution to (
WKE
) that belongs to
Z_{s,m}
up to time
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)}+\epsilon
, which is impossible.

Now we know
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)}=\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,0)}
. But for any
s_{1}<s_{2}
, we can find
m
such that the
Z_{s_{1},m}
norm is stronger than
Z_{s_{2},0}
norm, thus
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s_{2},0)}\leq\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s_{1},0)}=\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s_{1},m)}\leq\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s_{2},0)}
. Therefore the values of
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}^{(s,m)}
are equal for all
(s,m)
, and define this to be
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}
. Then for
\tau\in[0,\tau_{\mathrm{max}})
the solution
\varphi(\tau)
belongs to all
Z_{s,m}
spaces, and hence is Schwartz; moreover if
\tau_{\mathrm{max}}<\infty
then the blowup criterion (
3.16
) has to hold, since otherwise we can extend the solution in
Z_{s,0}
. This proves Proposition
3.2
.
